# PART ONE: THE PIECES
By the Command of Her Imperial Majesty, Empress Aracelia Meson Mignonette, Guardian of the Imperial Legacy and Keeper of the Remnian Empire, let the following be known throughout the lands:
In accordance with the Treaty of Concord and the sacred traditions of our ancestors, it is hereby declared that the Chess Tournament of this year shall take place between the provinces of London Town and Old Prosperity. This announcement marks the beginning of an event that will test the mettle of our finest, whose fates will be written on the walls of our Empire’s history.
By law, all candidates between the ages of twenty to twenty-five from both provinces shall undergo the Province Aptitude Examination. This examination, designed to assess each competitor’s mind, body, and spirit, will determine which sixteen individuals shall be named Pieces – the champions chosen to represent their province on the Chessboard.
The rules of the Tournament, as handed down through generations, remain as follows:
Eight men and eight women shall be selected based on their performance in the Province Aptitude Examination. Each Piece will assume one of the following roles: King, Queen, Rook, Knight, Bishop, or Pawn. 
The goal of the Chess Tournament is simple: achieve Checkmate by capturing the opposing province’s King. 
The Tournament is divided into seven challenges, each designed to test the Pieces’ intellectual, physical, and emotional fortitude. Each challenge is time-bound, and success in each is crucial to advancing on the Chessboard.
The rules permit – nay, demand – that sacrifices be made. 
The strategies used, the alliances formed, and the sacrifices made in pursuit of this goal shall define the outcome of the Tournament. 
Once selected, the Pieces stand alone. No province, ruler, or ally may interfere. The outcome of the Tournament rests solely on the Pieces’ actions. Alliances formed within the board may last only as long as they serve the game.
Should a Piece fall, either through failure in a challenge or at the hands of their opponents, they shall be removed from the board, with no chance of returning or redeeming themselves. Only those who endure shall reach the final Checkmate.
There is no withdrawal, no escape. The moment a Piece steps onto the Chessboard, their fate is sealed. 
To the victor shall go honor, favor, and a year of influence in the form of a seat on the Remian Empire’s Provincial Leadership Board for their province. To the defeated… let them bear the weight of their loss in silence. May the chosen Pieces represent the strength and resolve of their provinces with dignity and valor.
By Order of Her Imperial Majesty, this decree stands.
Long live London Town.
Long live Old Prosperity. 
Long live the Remnian Empire.
To: Corporal Katherine Medina Celeste NicholasFrom: High Command of Old Prosperity
Subject: Tournament Orders 
Corporal Nicholas,
By mandate of the High Command of Old Prosperity and in accordance with the decree issued by H.I.M. Empress Aracelia, you are hereby notified of your eligibility for the upcoming Chess Tournament. At the age of twenty, you now fall within the designated range of candidates for consideration, although official selection has not yet been confirmed and will not occur until the Delegation Ceremony.
Your demonstrated abilities in the Frost Division and intellectual pursuits have placed you among those prepared for possible selection. As such, the following directives must be adhered to without delay:
You are required to report to Professor Aloysius, the head of the Remnian Historical and Cultural Studies department, by 1300 hours for immediate enrollment in Empirical History 300. This course is mandatory for all eligible candidates and will cover advanced topics including strategic warfare, imperial governance, and historical conflicts. Attendance is compulsory until the final assessments that occur before the official naming of the Tournament Pieces.
You are also required to undergo a physical assessment with Captain Atlas Tolliver. Because of the similarity in your scoring and abilities, you have been paired with Captain Tolliver for the duration of your combat evaluations and training exercises. It is important to note that working alongside someone of his caliber and standing within the division is a privilege not afforded to many. His command of the Frost element will serve as a critical benchmark against which your own abilities will be measured.  
As a member of the Frost Division of Year Eleven, your ongoing training in elemental control is a priority. You must attend weekly elemental control sessions to ensure your mastery of Frost techniques remains at the highest standard. Failure to do so will result in disciplinary action and may affect your standing within your unit.
While you are now eligible, your selection in the Tournament depends on your performance in the upcoming directives. Approach your academic and physical training with discipline, as each will be evaluated rigorously. Alongside preparing for the Tournament, you will also be assessed for promotion to Cadet Sergeant within your platoon. Evaluations with Captain Tolliver will guide this decision. 
This notice serves as your formal military order for advancement in at the University of Old Prosperity and participation in the Chess Tournament. 
May your actions reflect the honor and strength of Old Prosperity.
Signed,General Gareth ThorneOld Prosperity High Command
## Chapter 1: Katherine 
Katherine Nicholas had taken a beating before. 
Plenty of times really. 
There was the time in Year Nine when a Terrain thought it would be funny to change the dirt to mud during physical training, so Katherine threw ice in her face. There was also a time in Year Six when a Flamer tried to show how fast hair burned and wanted to use her as an example, so she caused a snowstorm in his bedroom. Hell, just last week, she managed to barely fend off a couple of Elites who thought it would be cool to show how fun shadow travel was, knowing damn well Frost couldn’t shadow travel. 
But this? This was damn near manslaughter.
She hauled herself off the mat, the taste of blood sharp where her teeth had sliced her cheek. The upperclassman – Daniels – stood across from her, not even winded, watching her with that same, tired disappointment every instructor wore lately. Like they were searching for something in her and kept coming up empty.
“Again,” Commander Reeves called from the sidelines like Katherine hadn’t just had her ass handed to her three times in a row already.
“Sir–”she started, but the look he gave her killed the protest in her throat.
Katherine forced herself to stand, spitting blood onto the mat as Daniels began to circle her. He had her beat in every category; height, weight, time at the University. She was honestly surprised he hadn’t cracked her ribs with that first hit.
This was supposed to be a “standard evaluation” – that’s what they called it when they wanted to see what you were truly capable of. But there was nothing standard about the way Daniels was coming at her. Since her mother stepped down from the En Guard two months ago, those standard evaluations had become an almost everyday thing. This week alone, they’d dragged her onto the mat six times. And it was only Wednesday.
He wasn’t evaluating her. He was hunting.
“Don’t just defend, Nicholas. Attack!” Commander Reeves barked from the edge of the mat, stylus hovering over his tablet.
Katherine wiped the blood from her mouth, eyes locked on Daniels as he bounced on the balls of his feet. The smirk on his face said everything – he knew she wouldn’t go full out. Knew she would hesitate. That’s why they’d picked him.
“C’mon, Nicholas,” Daniels taunted, frost already gathering at his fingertips, tiny ice crystals dancing along his knuckles. “Show us what the great Lieutenant’s daughter can do.”
Her frost responded before her brain could stop it, spiraling up her arms in protective layers. Defense. Always defense. That’s what she was good at. What her mother drilled into her. Keep yourself alive. Protect yourself. 
Daniels came at her like a storm, unexpected and hell-bent on doing damage. His first strike aimed straight for her already bruised ribs, but her shield formed instinctively, catching his fist before it could connect. The impact splintered across the shield’s surface, forcing her back a step.
“Still hiding behind your pretty little walls, Nicholas?” he sneered. “No wonder they’re disappointed.”
Something hot and angry flared inside her chest. Katherine dropped the shield and stepped into his space, sending a blast toward his face. He dodged, but not fast enough – ice crystals caught his right cheek, drawing a thin line of blood.
Surprise registered in his eyes, followed by fury.
For a split second, Katherine saw the opening – his stance was too wide, and his guard had slipped. One strike to his throat is all it would take. Her fingers tingled as ice gathered in her palm, forming a blade sharp enough to slice through skin, to stop breath, to – 
Would you kill to save a life?
The question made her freeze. 
Daniels recovered faster than she’d expected someone his size to be able to. Before she could reset, his leg swept beneath her, catching her ankles. She crashed to the mat, hard enough that air rushed from her lungs.
“Too slow,” he taunted, standing over her. “Always too fucking slow.”
His boot found her sternum, pressing down just enough to make breathing difficult. Katherine grabbed his ankle, frost crawling up his leg, but he only laughed and increased the pressure.
“Yield,” Commander Reeves called flatly.
Daniels leaned down, tendrils of ice snaking from his fingers toward her face. “You’re not her,” he whispered. “You’ll never be her.”
Katherine’s chest burned – from lack of oxygen, from rage, from the humiliation of being pinned like a butterfly to a board with everyone watching. She released his ankle, not trusting herself to hold back if she kept fighting.
“That’s enough, Sergeant Daniels.” Commander Reeves approached, tapping notes into his tablet. “Defensive capabilities adequate. Offensive capabilities insufficient.”
Insufficient. 
The word hit harder than any of Daniels’s punches.
Daniels stepped back, still smirking as Katherine rolled to her knees, one arm wrapped protectively around her ribs.
“That’ll be all, Nicholas.” Commander Reeves didn’t even look at her as he delivered the dismissal.
The academy bells chimed as Katherine limped toward the locker room, signaling the end of the training block. Cadets were already flooding the hallways, their conversations bouncing off the stone walls of the University of Old Prosperity as they headed to their next class. Some glanced her way as she passed, taking in her split lip and the way she favored her right side. None of them said anything. They didn’t have to. The judgment was clear enough in their eyes.
Maybe they were right. Every time she got close to landing a finishing blow, that same damn question froze her: Would you kill to save a life?
Katherine shoved through the locker room doors, silently praying it was somewhat empty – and for once, the universe actually listened. The few cadets still changing glanced her way before quickly averting their eyes. By the time dinner rolled around, half the University would know Daniels mopped the floor with her, and the other half would pretend they didn’t hear. 
She peeled off her training uniform, wincing as the fabric stuck to places where sweat had mixed with blood. Bruises bloomed across her ribs like storm clouds, dark and heavy under her skin. Nothing was broken – at least she was pretty sure nothing was – but tomorrow would hurt like hell.
The shower stalls were arranged in tight, crisp lines, just like everything else at the University of Old Prosperity. Eight identical units, white tile from floor to ceiling, with exactly five minutes of hot water allocated per cadet. Katherine stepped under the spray, setting the temperature as hot as it would go. Steam rose around her as the water washed away the blood and sweat, turning pink as it swirled down the drain.
Her com-band buzzed against the shelf where she’d placed it, the small screen lighting up with another notification. 
Shit. 
Three missed alerts. All from Captain Atlas Tolliver.
Katherine let her head fall back against the tile with a dull thud. Missing a meeting with any Captain was bad enough, but Atlas Tolliver? That was a special kind of screwed. He had become her mother’s First Mate in the En Guard the moment he graduated from the University before he was promoted to Captain of the Legion – Old Prosperity’s air force division. Now, he commanded the skies while her mother had ruled the seas. Atlas was known around campus for two things: his white hair and his ability to make cadets wish they’d never crossed paths with him. 
The water shut off automatically after her five minutes were up, leaving her dripping and shivering in the suddenly quiet stall. No matter how long she’d been at the University, she never got used to the timer. Like everything else about this place, it was designed to remind them that resources were measured, time was limited, and comfort was secondary to efficiency. Katherine dried off quickly and pulled on her standard-issue uniform – dark gray pants and jacket with a black shirt underneath, the Frost emblem stitched in silver thread above her heart. Her uniform pressed against her bruises – a reminder she’d failed. Again.
The University of Old Prosperity sprawled across the northern edge of the province, a massive complex of stone buildings and training fields designed to produce the military’s finest. From the locker room windows, she could see cadets moving across the grounds like ants, each with their assigned purpose, each part of the machine that kept their province secure.
Beyond the University walls lay Old Prosperity itself – industrial and sturdy, all factories and innovation centers, the manufacturing heart of the Empire. On clear days like today, you could just make out the smoke rising from the production district, where weapons and technology were crafted that kept Old Prosperity at the top of the provincial hierarchy.
“Damn, who did you piss off this time?”
Katherine turned to find Reign leaning against the row of lockers, arms crossed over her chest. Her roommate’s dark eyes narrowed as she looked Katherine up and down. 
“Daniels,” Katherine replied, shoving her training clothes into her bag. “Standard evaluation.”
“That wasn’t an evaluation; that was aa public execution.” Reign pushed off the lockers and fell into step beside her as they headed for the exit. “Reeves set you up.”
“Doesn’t matter.”
“Like hell it doesn’t.” Reign lowered her voice as they entered the main hallway. “You’d think they’d wait until the actual Tournament to try and kill us.”
Katherine snorted. “Why wait? Might as well weed out the weak ones early.”
Reign bumped her shoulder. “You’re not weak, Kat. You’re just too damn careful.”
Tell that to Daniels, Katherine thought but didn’t say.
Ahead of them, cadets poured into the mess hall, all talk of the Tournament humming beneath their conversations like static. Old Prosperity versus London Town. A matchup the Empire hadn’t sanctioned in decades – because when these two provinces fought, people didn’t just lose a game. They lost everything they’d ever stood for. 
The Tournament was supposed to be a solution.
That’s what they said.
A “controlled outlet” for provincial aggression. A way to stop the provinces from burning each other to the ground.
Funny how it never felt like that to the ones who got picked.
“I can’t even get through a standard evaluation without getting my ass handed to me.” Katherine sighed. “I think that constitutes as weak.”
“That’s bullshit and you know it,” Reign countered. “You’re one of the strongest Frost in our year. You just – “
“Hesitate,” Katherine finished for her. “Every time.”
Reign didn’t argue. She couldn’t. They’d been roommates since Year Seven, had trained together, studied together, bled together. Reign had seen Katherine freeze in combat simulations more times than either of them could count.
Their com-bands chimed simultaneously – the automated alert that signaled the start of mess hall hours. Katherine glanced at her screen, stomach dropping when she saw a new message from Atlas: My office. After dinner. No excuses.
“What?” Reign asked, noticing her expression.
“Nothing,” Katherine muttered, quickening her pace. “Just hungry.”
The mess hall was already packed when they arrived, the cavernous space filled with the clatter of trays and the low roar of hundreds of conversations. Cathedral ceilings arched overhead, stone pillars rising from the floor like ancient trees. The tall and narrow windows filtered the evening light into golden shafts that cut through the air.
Unlike the rest of the University, which was all uniform and depression, the mess hall walls were adorned with the banners of past Tournament victories – brilliant blue and silver fabric that hung from floor to ceiling, each embroidered with the names of the Captains who had led Old Prosperity to glory.
Katherine’s eyes automatically found her mother’s banner – Lieutenant Michelle Nicholas, undefeated Captain of the En Guard, the strategist who secured one of Old Prosperity’s greatest victories. The bright blue banner held her mother’s picture as she commanded her element.
“Come on,” Reign nudged her toward their usual table near the back. “Sebastian saved us seats.”
Katherine followed, trying to ignore the way conversations dipped as she passed. It’d been like this every day – whispers at her back, questions in the air, and Reign shooting daggers at anyone bold enough to speak too loud. She didn’t have to stand up for her, but deep down, Katherine was grateful that Reign was there. That’s what Terrains did – held everything together while the rest of them struggled to find their footing.
Sebastian Augustine was already sprawled across the bench when they reached their table, long legs stretched out like he couldn’t care less about taking up space. His plate sat untouched, and he was busy flicking through something on his com-band, barely glancing up as Katherine and Reign approached.
“You look like shit,” he said, finally looking at Katherine with those sharp hazel eyes. No sympathy, just a casual observation that somehow made it worse.
“Thanks,” Katherine slid onto the bench across from him, wincing as her ribs protested. “You always know exactly what to say.”
Sebastian smirked, pushing a tray toward her. “I got you extra protein. Figured you might need it after whatever the hell happened to your face.”
“Daniels happened,” Reign explained, dropping down next to Katherine. 
“And you didn’t,” Sebastian concluded, studying Katherine’s split lip with mild interest. “Shocking.”
Katherine shot him a glare. “Not all of us get to coast through evaluations on our name and family connections.”
“Please,” Sebastian rolled his eyes. “Like being an Augustine has ever done me any favors around here. If anything, it just means they expect twice as much.”
He wasn’t entirely wrong. Sebastian could have made Sergeant three times over by now if he wanted. Everyone knew it. The teachers knew it. The other cadets knew it. Hell, even the Nines, who’d only seen him in passing, knew it. But Sebastian Augustine had a particular talent for doing exactly what he wanted while making it look like he wasn’t trying at all.
It probably helped that his older brother was Captain Minho Augustine, the person who the University had given her mother’s role to. But that was only part of it. Sebastian was just... brilliant. The kind of brilliant that made Old Prosperity’s rigid system look like child’s play. He could solve complex tactical scenarios in his sleep, design new training protocols over breakfast, and still have time to rewrite half the engineering department’s curriculum because he was “bored.”
Katherine had known him since last year when they were partnered for a frost navigation exercise. He’d spent the entire time talking about theoretical applications for cross-elemental weapons while she was just trying not to freeze them both solid. Somehow, despite his arrogance (or maybe because of it), they’d become friends. Or whatever passed for friendship in a place like Old Prosperity.
“Tournament selections are next week,” Sebastian said, abruptly changing the subject. “Word is Minho’s already seen the preliminary list.”
Katherine’s heart skipped. “And?”
Sebastian shrugged. “And I still think the whole thing’s a glorified bloodsport designed to keep the provinces in line while making us think we’re competing for honor.”
“That’s not an answer,” Reign pointed out.
“It’s the only one I’ve got.” Sebastian leaned forward, suddenly serious. “Look, all I know is they’re looking for offensive capabilities this year. London Town’s got at least three dual-wielders on their candidate list.”
Katherine’s appetite vanished. Dual-wielders were rare – cadets born with the ability to control more than one element. They were almost always chosen for Tournament selection, their versatility making them invaluable in combat.
“And that’s why they sent Daniels after you today,” Reign said quietly. “They’re trying to push you past defensive tactics.”
“Well, it didn’t work,” Katherine muttered, pushing her food around her plate. “All I did was prove I can’t – “
She broke off as her com-band buzzed again. Fourth notification from Atlas. She wasn’t just in trouble; she was in deep shit.
Sebastian’s eyes narrowed as he clocked her reaction. “What did you do?”
“Nothing,” Katherine replied, too quickly.
“Bullshit,” Sebastian leaned closer, examining her face. “You’ve got that look.”
“What look?”
“The one you get when you’re about to be called into someone’s office for something you absolutely did.”
Reign glanced at Katherine’s com-band and winced. “Atlas Tolliver. Four notifications.”
Sebastian let out a low whistle. “What did you miss?”
“A meeting,” Katherine admitted. “This morning. I was too busy getting my ass handed to me by Daniels.”
“Atlas doesn’t like to be kept waiting,” Sebastian said, something almost like concern flashing across his features. “What was the meeting about?”
The mess hall went silent before Katherine could answer (not that she had an answer). The kind of silence that dropped like a stone, spreading from table to table until even the clatter of utensils ceased.
Katherine knew exactly what that silence meant. Every cadet did.
A Captain had entered the hall.
She didn’t need to turn around to know who it was. The sudden chill in the air, the way Sebastian’s eyebrows shot up, Reign’s sharp intake of breath – it all told her everything she needed to know.
Atlas Tolliver had come to find her himself.
Each table he passed went silent, cadets suddenly finding their food fascinating as he approached. Six feet two inches of cold calculation, his element practically radiating off him even from across the room.
Katherine forced herself to turn, meeting his gaze directly. The bastard didn’t even have the decency to look tired after running combat drills all day. His uniform was still perfectly pressed, white hair still perfectly styled, face still expressionless as his eyes locked on hers. The entire mess hall watched, hundreds of eyes tracking his movement across the room. Atlas Tolliver didn’t come to the mess hall. Ever. Captains had their own dining facilities, separate from the cadets. The fact that he was here, that he’d come looking for her, meant something was very, very wrong.
He stopped directly in front of their table, silver eyes assessing her injuries.
“You missed your meeting.”
Katherine straightened her spine, ignoring the sharp pain in her ribs. “I had an evaluation, sir.”
“With Sergeant Daniels,” Atlas replied. “I’m aware.”
Katherine sighed. Nothing happened at Old Prosperity without the Captains knowing.
“I apologize for missing the meeting, sir,” Katherine replied. “It won’t happen again.”
“No,” he agreed. “It won’t.” His eyes lingered on her bruised face. “Daniels hasn’t improved his right hook, I see.”
The comment caught her off guard. It was the kind of thing he would have said years ago, when he was still her mother’s First Mate and she was just Michelle Nicholas’s daughter tagging along during training sessions.
“His right hook was fine,” Katherine replied carefully, conscious of the cadets watching them. “It was his left that could use some work.” She rubbed her bruised ribs slightly, adding, “Though his timing was perfect.”
It was their old code from training days. Left side weak, timing perfect – Atlas would recognize it instantly: She’d seen the opening, could have struck a finishing blow, and chosen not to take it. 
Again.
“I’ll be sure to mention it during officer reviews,” he said dryly.
Without another word, he reached into his jacket and pulled out an envelope, the wax seal of High Command gleaming under the mess hall lights. He held it out to her, and when Katherine took it, their fingers brushed – just for a second. Katherine gripped the envelope tighter than she meant to, like that second of contact could shake her loose. She hadn’t been in the same room as him in months. 
“You’ll want to open that,” he said, already turning to leave.
“Anything else, sir?” The words escaped before she could stop them.
Atlas paused, glancing back at her over his shoulder. “Make sure this is the last time I have to track you down,” he replied. “The next time won’t be with words.”
The second the doors shut behind him, the whispers hit like a wave. 
“Holy shit,” Reign breathed, eyes locked on the envelope. “Are those – “
Katherine turned the envelope over, fingers trembling slightly as she traced the High Command seal. She didn’t need to break it to know what was inside.
Tournament Orders.
## Chapter 2: Melena 
Shadowmere smelled like smoke and bullshit, and Melena had been breathing both since before dawn.
The marketplace sprawled across what used to be a town square, vendors’ stalls built from salvaged stone and rotting wood that probably predated the border closures. Mismatched tarps stretched between broken pillars, creating pockets of shadow where people conducted business they couldn’t do anywhere else. The air hummed with a dozen different languages, most of them speaking in whispers.
She’d tucked her crimson hair under a hood that looked like it hadn’t seen a wash in weeks, slouched her shoulders until her royal posture disappeared, and let her accent settle into something that wouldn’t turn heads. Nine months of this, and she’d almost stopped feeling like she was playing dress-up.
Almost. 
The crowds moved with purpose here. No one lingered, no one made eye contact for too long, and everyone understood that what you heard in Shadowmere stayed in Shadowmere. Built in the ruins of some border town that got abandoned when Remnia closed her borders a century ago, Shadowmere was the one place you could go to get whatever you needed for a cost. 
Including answers about people who disappeared.
Melena made her way through the maze of stalls toward Maven’s usual spot, nodding to vendors who’d grown used to seeing her face. Strange how quickly she’d adapted to this world – the constant vigilance, the way conversations happened in code, the understanding that trust was earned in drops and lost in floods. Eight months ago, she’d been Princess Melena Lockwood, whose biggest concern was whether her fire control was tournament-ready. Now, she was just another information broker with calloused hands and a talent for finding things people wanted to stay hidden.
And still, somehow, Princess Melena Lockwood. 
She wasn’t sure which version of herself she preferred.
Maven’s stall sat in the shadow of what used to be a clock tower, the bells long since removed but the silver ones sewn into her sleeves providing their own quiet music. The woman looked up as Melena approached, and something that might’ve been affection flickered across her weathered features.
“Morning, girl,” Maven said, glancing up as Melena walked behind the stall to join her. “You look like you slept.”
“First time in a week,” Melena admitted, settling into the routine of examining Maven’s displayed wares while they talked. “Bad dreams.”
“About home?”
“About what happens if I never find him.”
Maven’s expression softened slightly – the closest she ever came to maternal concern. It had taken Melena weeks to realize that Maven’s gruff exterior hid someone who genuinely cared about the people caught in her web of information and favors.
“We’ll find him,” Maven said quietly. “But you know what I’m going to say about patience.”
“That it’s the difference between finding him and getting us both killed,” Melena recited, managing a tired smile. “I know. Doesn’t make the waiting easier.”
She pulled out the worn parchment from her pocket – her mother’s handwriting still legible after two years of handling: “If you ever need to disappear, my darling, there are places even Empresses cannot reach. Shadowmere exists in the spaces between maps. Find the woman with the silver bells. Tell her Lucia’s daughter needs passage to nowhere.”
Lucia’s escape route had become Melena’s lifeline.
Maven had tested her, made her prove she could handle herself in Shadowmere’s dangerous game of secrets and lies. The first real test had been when Maven sent her to verify intelligence about a trade route dispute. Melena had to break into a merchant’s private office, steal shipping manifests, and get out without being caught. When she’d succeeded, Maven had finally started looking at her like she might be useful.
But it was the second test that changed everything.
Maven had asked her to steal patrol schedules from the Imperial Guard barracks. “If you’re really who you claim to be,” she’d said, “you should have access.” It was a trap, obviously. Either Melena would fail and prove she was lying about her connections, or she’d succeed and reveal exactly how connected she really was.
Melena had done one better. She’d brought Maven the complete Imperial Guards security protocols for the next three months, including guard rotations, safe house locations, and emergency evacuation routes. Information that should’ve been impossible for a common information broker to access.
Information telling Maven exactly where she would be and when. 
That’s when Maven’s entire demeanor had shifted.
“Melena Lockwood,” she’d said matter of factly, not even bothering to make it a question. “Lucia’s daughter indeed.”
From that moment on, their relationship had been different. Maven had started teaching her things. Real things. Things Melena had a hard time understanding was real until Maven herself had taken her there. Maven told her almost everything she knew about the countries that officially didn’t exist. About Somnoria and its dream magic, about Thalara and its sky cities, about The Underside and its underground networks and how people could cross into any of the three of them. 
“Still nothing on your missing person,” Maven said now, looking down, organizing her latest intel. The silver bells in her sleeves chimed softly as she moved. “But I’ve got something else that might interest you.”
Melena’s pulse quickened. “What kind of something?”
“Tournament intelligence. Word is Old Prosperity’s been ramping up security protocols. Extra patrols, new communication codes, the works.” Maven finally looked up, studying Melena’s face. “Almost like they’re expecting trouble.”
“What kind of trouble?”
“The kind that comes from them getting too curious about other provinces’ plans.” Maven’s expression was carefully neutral. “Curious enough to send scouts to gather intelligence about Tournament preparations beyond their own borders. I hear they are sending the Captains to Zenithia this year.”
“How solid is this intel?” she asked.
“Solid enough that London Town should be watching their backs.” Maven’s tone carried a warning. “Old Prosperity’s always been aggressive, but this feels different. More organized. Nothing’s been the same over there since Nicholas stepped down, I’ll tell you that much. We don’t see too many from there out here anymore.”
“Think this will cause Imperial problems?”
Maven shrugged. “Don’t know. Is the Council saying anything?”
“No,” Melena shook her head. “Haven’t been to the daily briefings this week. Getting out here has been hard.”
“Sorry about that,” Maven responded. “When the province in charge of the military ups their shit, so do you. It’s been a crazy time here for information trading. Speaking of, I’ve got a potential contact for you. Someone who might know about cross-border movements from two years ago.”
“Who?”
“Take a guess.”
“No,” Melena shook her head. “Nope, not him.”
“Melena.”
“Maven,” Melena shot her a look. “I told you what I felt when I saw him last week when I was buying those crystals from Korven’s stall. No one – not even anyone in the Imperial family – has that much power. You could feel it in the air!”
“Which is exactly why you need to talk to him.”
“Or exactly why I should stay the hell away from him.” Melena lowered her voice. “Just because I know what he is doesn’t mean I need to know who he is.”
“He knows who you are,” Maven added, almost casually.
Melena’s stomach dropped. “What?”
“Last week. He knew you were there the whole time.” Maven’s expression was unreadable. “Didn’t seem bothered by it, though. If anything, he looked... curious.”
Great. So much for being subtle.
“He’s not going to kill you.”
“How can you be so sure?”
“Because,” Maven glanced over Melena’s shoulder, “he’s been standing three stalls down for the last ten minutes, waiting for you to stop being stubborn.”
Melena’s fire flared as she turned around. Sure enough, there he was – leaning against a vendor’s post like he had all the time in the world, dark eyes fixed on her with the kind of focus that made her skin crawl. When their gazes met, he straightened and started walking over.
Shit.
“Maven,” Melena said through gritted teeth, “next time maybe lead with that information.”
“You would’ve run.”
“Damn right I would’ve run.”
But it was too late now. He was already approaching, moving with that same fluid confidence that had caught her attention at Korven’s stall. She’d noticed it last week too, but up close, she could see just how much she actually had been blind to. The way he walked, his posture, his height. The clothes were expensive, which Melena had come to expect, but the cut of them was different than anything made in Remnia. She’d damn near run back to Maven’s stall, pleading for her to tell her just where in the hell someone got clothes like that. Maven had simply smirked and told her she was finally ready to learn more.
Up close, the power radiating from him was even more intense – like being near an open flame that could either warm you or burn you alive depending on his mood. The air around him shimmered slightly, the way it did around back in London Town when elementalists weren’t bothering to fully contain their abilities.
“Ladies,” he said, nodding to both of them, his accent confirming what she already knew. Pure Somnorian, educated, upper class. “Beautiful morning for... commerce.”
“Caelum,” Maven said evenly. “Meet my associate.”
His dark eyes swept over Melena, cataloging details with the kind of intensity that made her want to check if her disguise was still in place. 
“Just associate?” he asked, a hint of amusement in his voice. . “Here I thought she might be someone more... interesting.”
“Depends on what you find interesting,” Melena replied, forcing her voice to stay steady. She could feel her fire responding to the dream magic emanating from him, like flame recognizing a different but equally dangerous element. 
“Interesting would be finding out why you’ve been sneaking out here,” Caelum said, his attention never wavering from her face. “Haven’t your people done enough damage to the outer realms?”
Melena snarled as Maven raised her hand at them both. A ghost of a smile crossed his face, and for a split second, Melena caught sight of something that made her blood run cold. When he smiled, his canine teeth were slightly pointed – a side effect Melena learned quite literally last night was the effect of extended dream magic use that only happened to the most powerful practitioners. The kind of power that literally changed your body.
Fuck. What has Maven gotten me into?
“We’re not in Remnia anymore, Princess,” he said simply.
The bastard had a point.
“Princess?” Melena raised an eyebrow. “I think you’ve got me confused with someone else.”
“Do I?” He tilted his head, studying her with those unsettling dark eyes. “My mistake. It’s just that most information brokers don’t have the kind of posture that screams ‘I’ve been curtsying since I could walk.’”
“Maybe I’m just well-educated.”
“Maybe.” His smile widened. “Or maybe you’re exactly who I think you are, which would make this conversation much more... productive.”
Maven cleared her throat. “Perhaps we should take this somewhere more private?”
“No need,” Caelum said. “I’m sure your associate understands the value of discretion. After all, she’s been sneaking around Shadowmere for years gathering intelligence on every Remnia Tournament. Very thorough work, from what I hear.”
Melena’s blood ran cold. 
How the hell did he know about the Tournament?
“You seem to know a lot about my business,” she said carefully. “Question is, what do you want in exchange for keeping that information to yourself?”
“Who says I want anything?”
“Because you wouldn’t be here if you didn’t need something.” Melena forced herself to lean back casually, even though every instinct was screaming at her to run. “And you sure as hell wouldn’t be talking to me if you thought I was just some random broker.”
“Fair point.” Caelum glanced around the marketplace, then back at her. “Let’s say, hypothetically, that I have access to information about cross-border movements. People who might have entered Somnoria through... unofficial channels. And let’s say you have access to Imperial intelligence that could be very useful to certain interested parties.”
“What kind of interested parties?”
“The kind that prefer to remain anonymous.” His expression grew more serious. “The kind that have been watching tensions rise between the provinces and would like to avoid... unpleasant complications.”
“And in this hypothetical scenario,” she said slowly, “what would this exchange look like?”
“Private conversation. Neutral ground.” Caelum’s eyes flicked to Maven, then back to her. “Where we can discuss specifics without worrying about... interruptions.”
“When?”
“Tonight. There’s an old shrine about an hour’s ride north of here. Abandoned, quiet, perfect for the kind of conversation we need to have.” He paused. “Unless, of course, you’re not as interested in finding your missing person as you claim to be.”
The bastard. He was baiting her, and they both knew it. But if he really did have information about Elias...
“And how do I know you’ll actually show up?” Melena asked. “Or that this isn’t some elaborate trap?”
“You don’t.” Caelum shrugged. “But then again, I don’t know that you won’t bring half the Imperial Guard with you. I suppose we’ll both have to take our chances.”
He was right, and she hated him for it.
“Fine,” she said. “But if you’re wasting my time –”
“I’m not.” He responded. “Your missing person? He’s in more danger than you realize simply because he’s not in Remnia and to those inside Remnia, there’s nothing outside of Remnia. Know what that means, Princess? It means no one in your country is going to look for him if you don’t.”
Before Melena could respond, he turned and walked away, disappearing into the crowd as smoothly as he’d appeared. She stood there for a moment, heart pounding, trying to process what had just happened.
“Well,” Maven said quietly, “that went better than expected.”
“Better?” Melena stared at her. “He knows exactly who I am, he’s been watching me for months, and now he’s threatening my brother. How is that better?”
“Because,” Maven said, gathering her papers, “he didn’t kill you. Which means he needs you alive. And that, girl, gives you more power than you think.”
#
The shrine sat in a clearing surrounded by trees, their branches creating a canopy thick enough to block out most of the moonlight. What remained of the structure was mostly broken stone columns and crumbling walls, but Melena could still make out the intricate carvings that marked it as pre-war architecture. Before the countries decided they hated each other enough to seal their borders.
She’d arrived early, partly to scout the area and partly because sitting still back in Shadowmere had been impossible. Her fire had been restless all afternoon, responding to her anxiety about what information Caelum might have about Elias. Maven had sent her early after she’d burned a third whole into a stack of documents that had taken Maven a week to secure. 
The sound of some kind of vehicle made her turn. Caelum emerged from the tree line, stepping out of what looked like a sleek, dark transport that hovered just above the ground – definitely not Remnian technology. 
“You came,” he said, sounding genuinely surprised.
“You said you had information about my brother.” Melena stepped out of the shadows where she’d been waiting, her mind still reeling from all that she’d learned about Somnoria in the last week. 
Focus. Information first, existential crisis about other countries later. 
“I’m here to collect.”
“Straight to business. I respect that.” Caelum moved to the center of the ruined shrine, where a large flat stone that might have once been an altar provided a place to sit. “Though I should warn you, this conversation might not go the way you’re expecting.”
“What’s that supposed to mean?”
Instead of answering, he reached into his jacket and pulled out a folded piece of parchment. Even in the dim light, Melena could see it looked like official paper with some kind of seal, the kind used for important documents.
“Two years ago,” Caelum said, “a man matching your brother’s description crossed into Somnoria using forged documentation. He was traveling under the name Alexander Thorne, claiming to be a scholar from researching historical texts.”
Melena’s heart stopped. “Alexander” had been Elias’s childhood nickname for himself, back when he had his wisdom teeth pulled and he couldn’t remember his first name, only his middle name. No one outside their immediate family would know that.
“Where is he?” she asked, trying to keep the desperation out of her voice.
“That’s... complicated.” Caelum sighed. “He was granted residency and eventually ended up in Aetheria, teaching at the Dream Weavers Academy. He’s an amazing professor. Top of his career, quite literally. He’s taught and trained some of the best Dream Masters the Dream Guild has seen in the last two years. Everyone in Somnoria wants him on their payroll. But recently, he’s been showing signs of... instability. His research into dream mechanics has become erratic, dangerous even.”
“What kind of dangerous?”
“The kind that threatens the stability of the dream network itself.” Caelum paused, studying her face. “My cousin Cassius has been tasked with investigating him. If Cassius discovers who Alexander Thorne really is...”
He didn’t need to finish the sentence.
“Your cousin,” Melena said slowly, “what’s his last name?”
“Aether.” Caelum’s voice was careful. “House Aether, to be precise. One of the most powerful families in Somnoria.”
House Aether. Melena felt the pieces clicking into place. “And if they find out that Alexander Thorne is actually the Crown Prince of Remnia?”
“Then your brother becomes a political prisoner at best or disappears entirely at worst.” Caelum leaned forward. “There are factions in Somnoria that would see any member of Remnia’s royal family as a threat to be eliminated.”
“But you said he’s unstable. What’s wrong with him?”
“Dream magic affects people differently than elemental magic. Extended exposure can... change you. And your brother has been pushing the boundaries of what’s considered safe research for an Elementalist.” Caelum’s expression darkened. “He’s been having episodes. Breakdown moments where he warns people about things that haven’t happened yet, talks about a Tournament that hasn’t happened yet. I know because I cross-referenced it with Maven and Korven. The only Nicholas who has been in a Tournament is Lieutenant Michelle Nicholas. Not whomever your brother is rambling about.”
“What do you need from me?” Melena asked. It was the only question to be asked.
“Something that proves he’s not a spy but not the Crown Prince either,” Caelum replied. “And anything you can get your hands on about London Town’s Tournament preparations. The people who want your brother investigated? They’re the same ones pushing for Somnoria to interfere with the Tournament.” 
Caelum stood, beginning to pace. “They see instability in Remnia as an opportunity to force Remnia into recognizing and admitting the other countries exist. If they can prove that a member of your Imperial family is mentally unstable and conducting dangerous research in a foreign country, it gives them justification to…”
Melena felt like the ground had shifted beneath her feet. “You’re talking about invasion.”
“I’m talking about preventing one. But that requires keeping Cassius from discovering the truth about your brother which makes my job harder than I care for it to be.” Caelum stopped pacing, fixing her with an intense stare. “And it requires making sure the Tournament proceeds without interference. Meaning whatever you do, you must score high on your country’s exam and stand on that board,”
“And if I give you what you want?”
“I protect your brother. I make sure Cassius’s investigation leads nowhere. And I keep the warmongers in my country focused on internal politics instead of external conquest.”
Melena studied his face in the moonlight, her mind racing. Here she was about to make a deal with a devil and it wasn’t even the devil she usually made shady deals with. 
“Why should I trust you?” she asked.
“Because right now, I’m the only thing standing between your brother and either execution or a fate worse than death.” Caelum shot her a look. “And because you don’t have any other choice.”
He was right, and she hated him for it. But if Elias was really Professor Thorne, if he was losing his mind while conducting dangerous research under the nose of House Aether...
“I’ll get you what you need,” Melena said finally. “But I want proof that my brother is alive and as safe as you claim.”
“Fair enough.” Caelum reached into his jacket again, pulling out what looked like a small crystal. “This contains a recording from three days ago. Alexander Thorne conducting a lecture at the Academy. You’ll recognize him.”
Melena took the crystal with shaking hands. Her brother was alive. Broken, maybe, but alive.
“The Tournament intelligence,” Caelum continued, “I’ll need it before the selection ceremony. That’s when the political situation becomes too volatile to control.”
“That’s in two weeks!” Melena frowned. “How am I supposed to do that in two weeks?”
“Figure it out,” Caelum began to walk away from here. “Aren’t you going to be the next Empress anyways? Two weeks, Princess.”
Melena could only nod. There would only be two weeks to gather enough intelligence to save her brother’s life and prevent a war. While also somehow making sure she was selected for the Tournament.
Meaning it was time to stop playing in the shadows and go back to being a Princess. 
No pressure at all.
## Chapter 3: Katherine
Katherine lingered outside the lecture hall thirty seconds longer than she should have, staring at the polished plaque beside the door. Empirical History 300: Tournament History and Strategy. Her mother’s name used to be carved beneath it. Now it was a different professor – less conviction in his voice, same favorite lesson: win or die.
The stone under her feet felt colder than it should. She knew it was just nerves – or at least that’s what she told herself – but her stomach hadn’t settled since she’d seen the matchups printed on that morning’s decree. 
Old Prosperity versus London Town. 
A death sentence, if you would. 
“Welcome, everyone,” Professor Aloysius announced, his voice carrying throughout the tiered lecture hall. “We’re all here for the same thing, to learn how not to die when London Town decides to play with fire.”
A few nervous chuckles rippled through the room. “Now I’ve heard the whispers, and I will be the first to admit, I am just as surprised as you that Lieutenant Nicholas is no longer teaching this course.” 
He glanced around the room until his gaze landed on Katherine. “But if you paid attention during Rotation Theory last term, you’ll understand what I’m about to say. These matchups don’t happen by accident. Old Prosperity and London Town haven’t shared the board since the Crosspoint Era – back when tournament rotation followed patterns none of us alive today is allowed to keep studying. Lieutenant Nicholas’s resignation was a protest. Mine is a responsibility.”
“I want each of you to look around at the people near you,” Aloysius continued. “This room holds every eligible candidate between the ages of twenty and twenty-five. Apprentices who’ve returned from their posts. Officers who’ve put their commissions on hold. All of you, from Year Eleven to active duty, fall within Tournament age.” He paused, letting that sink in. “In two weeks, this number will be cut to sixteen. Eight men, eight women. Those sixteen will represent Old Prosperity as our Pieces.”
Katherine watched several students shift uncomfortably in their seats as Aloysius continued.  “Let me be clear. When you step into the Tournament as a Piece, you have one goal; secure a checkmate or die trying. There are no second chances, no honorable defeats. You either win, or you don’t come back at all.”
He turned to face the class, dust coating his fingers white. “Old Prosperity and London Town. The last time we went against them, we lost eight Pieces before we even reached the final challenge.” His eyes scanned the rows of students. “Do any of you know why this year will be particularly lethal for each province?”
Katherine’s fingers twitched under her desk. She knew the answer but Sebastian was answering before she could raise her hand.
“Because we’re too similar,” he said. “Both provinces think they’re the backbone of the Empire.”
“Continue,” Aloysius nodded at him. 
“Old Prosperity trains the military, helps Easton by running the military academy, and protects the Empire’s borders,” Sebastian continued. “London Town houses the government, makes the laws, controls the politics. Both think they’re more important than the other. Both think they know how to run things better than the other.” He shrugged. “Makes for a lot of bad blood when Tournament time comes around.”
Partially correct.” Aloysius began sketching a timeline on the board. “But you’re missing the crucial element that makes this year’s match different. Anyone care to elaborate?”
The silence stretched uncomfortably long. Aloysius’s chalk kept moving, each date a sharp click against the board. First was Old Prosperity’s first victory. Then a couple of years later, another win but at the cost of eleven Pieces. Seven years after that one, a lineup that left Old Prosperity and Astris both reeling for months. Then the following year came London Town’s first win against Old Prosperity.
“History shows us a pattern,” Aloysius said, pointing to each date. “Old Prosperity wins, but always in the final challenges, always with significant losses. And always,” his chalk tapped the board for emphasis, “when London Town thinks they have the upper hand.”
He turned back to face the class. “This year marks the first time these provinces have met during a succession year. So, someone,” Aloysius clasped his hands behind his back, “Try again.”
“Our combat training,” one of the voices in the front called out. “We start younger than they do.”
“Military discipline,” another voice added. “London Town’s nobles might have private tutors, but they don’t have our structure.”
Aloysius’s expression remained neutral. “Anyone else?”
“Numbers,” Reign spoke up from beside Katherine. “We have more eligible candidates of age than they do. Means we have a better chance of finding the strongest sixteen.”
“Wrong,” a voice said from the back of the room.
Dax Tolliver stepped forward, fire dancing across his knuckles like he’d been waiting to be invited into the conversation. The flames didn’t scare her – plenty of Flamers lit up when they got bored – but the ease with which he wielded them always made her skin prickle.
Light flickered across his face, catching on sharp cheekbones and the edge of a grin that never quite reached his eyes. His Vanguard jacket hung open like rules were optional, flame pendant pulsing steady against his chest. Even his curls looked intentional – messy in his signature I’m breaking curfew and your heart kind of way.
“Our advantage,” Dax continued, “is that we have experience they can’t match.” He gestured to where Atlas and Minho still stood. “Three Captains who fall within Tournament age. When’s the last time that happened?”
Katherine watched several students sink lower in their seats as if they could somehow hide from what Dax had said. Even some of the Apprentices looked pale. 
Reign leaned closer to Sebastian, her voice barely a whisper. “Did you know about this?”
“You really should have asked me how old my brother was.” Sebastian chuckled. “Come on, Nicholas. You can’t tell me you didn’t suspect something when Atlas showed up at the mess hall just to deliver his Tournament orders.”
Reign shook her head. “You are ridiculous.”
But Katherine’s hand was already moving. She reached into her jacket pocket and pulled out the envelope – still sealed, the wax of High Command unbroken, the weight of it suddenly heavier than it had been this morning. She hadn’t opened it. She’d been too busy reeling from having her ass handed to her and trying to finish lunch think straight. But now – 
She flipped it over. Read the sender’s code.
AT-06-L.
Atlas Tolliver.
“Shit,” she muttered.
She recognized the loops and curves of the letters anywhere. He’d written them. Crafted the entire schedule. The training directives, the assignments, the time blocks – they were all from him. And he hadn’t said a damn thing about it in the mess hall. Just handed it off like it didn’t mean anything.
But it did.
It always did, when it came to him.
Her fingers curled tighter around the envelope. She hated that he still had that power. Things had been... different since he made Captain last year. Not colder. Just – stricter. More formal. Like every sentence had to be measured and signed off before it left his mouth. Before, she could tell what he was thinking just by the way he looked at her. Now, she couldn’t even tell if he was looking at her. Whatever used to exist between them – whatever almost was – it didn’t survive the rank change.
Her com-band buzzed against her wrist. 
ATLAS TOLLIVER: We’ll discuss it after class.
She slid the order back into her jacket as Reign and Sebastian continued their back and forth. 
You could have told me yourself. 
Her com-band buzzed again.
ATLAS TOLLIVER: What do you think the meeting was for?
Heat rushed to her face, and for a second, she was glad the lecture hall lights were dim.
She didn’t answer. Couldn’t.
Not while Aloysius was still talking. Not while Sebastian was still smirking. Not while Atlas stood at the back of the room, acting like that message hadn’t just landed exactly where he aimed it.
She clenched her jaw and looked straight ahead, hands still in her lap.
Like nothing had happened at all.
“Captain Tolliver is correct,” Aloysius’s voice brought her attention back. “Old Prosperity’s advantage lies in the fact that our Captains are all within Tournament age for the first time in our province’s history.”
“Advantage?” a girl in the front row raised her hand. “You mean power vacuum. If they are all chosen, each of their branches is without its leader.”
“Not quite,” Minho spoke for the first time. “Each division has contingencies in place in case any vital members are called to the Tournament. That includes us.” 
Katherine hadn’t noticed him move, but there he was, now standing beside Dax. He looked like Sebastian, if Sebastian had aged ten years, cut off all his nonsense, and spent every morning making war with himself. Same bone structure. Same sharp jaw. But where Sebastian was reckless, Minho was contained energy waiting for the right moment.
His black hair was cropped short, military standard. His hazel eyes – too green and too blue at the same time – stood out even more against the severity of his face. He wasn’t as tall as Atlas or Dax, but at 6’2, he still managed to feel like the highest point in the room.
He didn’t posture like the other Captains. Didn’t need to. When Minho Augustine spoke, people shut the hell up.
And still, every time she looked at him, something twisted in her chest. He hadn’t stolen the En Guard from her mother – he wasn’t that kind of man. But he filled the space like he’d been born to stand there. Like the seat hadn’t still been warm when he took it.
Katherine didn’t hate him.
But she hadn’t forgiven him either.
“This brings us to an important point in Tournament history. The last time a Captain competed was over twenty years ago when Lieutenant Michelle Nicholas – “
The chalk snapped in Aloysius’s hand. Her name still made the room flinch. She didn’t have to look to know that Sebastian was watching. Or that Atlas had shifted too. 
“Led Old Prosperity to one of its most decisive victories against Durant. Youngest Queen Piece in history. One of the few to become a Captain after. She knew that Tournament matches against London Town require a specific approach.” He turned back to the board, grabbing a fresh piece of chalk. “Their Pieces tend to favor aggressive tactics, particularly their Flamers. Can anyone tell me the standard counter-strategy?”
“Ice walls,” one of the Apprentices called out. “Use Frost abilities to create barriers.”
“And when those melt?” 
“They won’t if they’re built properly,” another voice argued. “Old Prosperity’s Frost are trained specifically for – “
“Wrong,” Atlas cut in. “Ice walls aren’t enough. Not when they’ve been trained to melt through them.”
Flamers were fire wielders, yes – but trained Flamers were something else entirely. In London Town, they got full combat regimens. Drills. State funding. In Old Prosperity, they barely survived the cold weather. Unless you were born in Zenithia, you had to be built differently to hold a flame in the Eastern provinces.
“The heir presumptive to London Town’s throne,” Aloysius added. “is one of the most unpredictable Flamers the Empire has seen in decades. Princess Melena Lockwood. And she falls within Tournament age.”
“Which is exactly why,” Aloysius raised his voice slightly, “you’ll spend the next two weeks watching every recorded Tournament against London Town. Pay attention to their power sequencing. Their formations. And how they play when the board starts to turn against them. Your lives may depend on it. Class dismissed.”
Katherine’s com-band buzzed as she stood to leave the classroom. 
ATLAS TOLLIVER: I’m being called to Zenithia. I will find you in the morning. Before I leave. 
She didn’t respond. Instead, she grabbed her jacket and followed the current of cadets filing into the hallway. Sebastian caught up quickly as she tried her hardest to outpace him. She probably would have if the ribs didn’t feel like they were about to cave in. They walked in silence until the rest of the class disappeared around the far corridor. Then he pulled her by the elbow, just enough to turn her toward him.
“Katherine. 
She didn’t want to hear it. Whatever he was about to say – she didn’t want to hear it.
“You’re reading too much into it,” she said flatly.
“I’m not.”
He didn’t let go of her arm.
“You think I don’t recognize it? The way you looked when you opened those orders? The way he looked when he handed them to you? The way you look when you glance at that band?”
She yanked her arm free. “You’re wrong.”
Sebastian shrugged. “He could’ve told you in person.” His voice dropped. “But then you’d know how much it actually mattered.”
Katherine didn’t answer.
Didn’t look at him. Didn’t move.
Just stood there, jaw tight, pretending that line hadn’t landed exactly where it was meant to.
## Chapter 4: Melena
“Hold still, Your Highness.”
Melena bit back a sigh as Lorelei circled her like a vulture, tugging at invisible imperfections on her gown. The Imperial seamstress had been fussing over the same three inches of fabric for the past twenty minutes, muttering about “proper presentation” and “the eyes of the provinces.” Like Melena hadn’t been doing this her entire life.
Like any of this actually matters.
“Perfect is the enemy of done, Lorelei,” Melena caught the woman’s wrist as she reached for yet another pin. “And we are very much out of time.”
The seamstress stepped back with a reluctant nod, her eyes still cataloging every microscopic flaw that apparently only she could see. The gown was, objectively, a masterpiece – blood-red silk that caught the light like liquid fire, with gold embroidery tracing Imperial patterns across the bodice. Subtle but unmistakable flames licked up the skirt, delicate enough to pass as decorative to anyone not paying attention. But the message was clear to those who mattered: the Imperial Princess was a Flamer, and she wasn’t hiding it.
Not that she could, even if she wanted to. Her fire had been more unpredictable than ever since returning from Shadowmere, responding to every flash of anger about the lies she’d been raised on, every moment of frustration about Elias being trapped in a country that wasn’t supposed to exist.
Melena turned to the mirror, studying her reflection with the critical eye her grandmother had ingrained in her since childhood. Back straight. Chin lifted. Eyes sharp. The Lockwood look – regal, untouchable, and, to Lorelei’s credit, perfect. Her crimson hair was pulled back into an intricate crown of braids, with just enough left loose to frame her face. Or what would be visible of her face, anyway.
The obsidian mask sat on her vanity, gleaming darkly against the white marble. This year’s Zenithian Gala was a masquerade, which meant playing politics while pretending not to know exactly who stood behind each disguise.
A farce within a farce.
Perfectly Zenithian. And perfectly fitting for an Empire built on pretending three-quarters the world doesn’t exist.
“The mask is an insult,” she muttered, more to herself than to Lorelei. “Zenithia knows exactly who I am. Everyone does.”
“It’s tradition, Your Highness.” The response came not from the seamstress but from Lady Helena, her grandmother’s most trusted advisor, who had been silently observing from the corner. The woman stepped forward, lifting the mask with gloved hands. “And tradition is the currency of the Empire.”
Tradition. The word tasted bitter now. How many “traditions” were just convenient lies to maintain control? How many things had she accepted without question because that’s how it had always been done?
“Zenithia’s traditions are convenient excuses,” Melena said, allowing Helena to secure the mask. The obsidian was cool against her flushed skin. “A masquerade when they want to observe without being observed. A dinner when they want to know who is buying agriculture from whichever province they have an agreement with. It will never make sense to me.”
Just like everything else in this Empire.
“And yet,” Helena said, adjusting the final strap with practiced precision, “they host the most influential gathering of the season. One does not simply decline an invitation to the Crystal Palace.”
“Especially not now.” Melena met the woman’s eyes in the mirror. “Not with the Tournament looming.”
Helena’s expression never changed, but something in her posture shifted – a subtle tightening around the shoulders that most wouldn’t notice. But Melena had been trained to read these micro-expressions since before she could walk. Trained by Maven to notice even more. The Tournament was sensitive for reasons Helena couldn’t even imagine.
“Indeed.” Helena stepped back, examining Melena. “You understand what is required of you tonight?”
I understand more than you think. Melena’s fire prickled beneath her palms. “Smile. Charm. Secure alliances. Remind the provinces why London Town deserves their support in the Tournament.” She recited the words like a script she’d memorized but didn’t write. “Be the perfect Imperial Princess while my brother is... wherever he is.”
Teaching dream magic in a country you’ve never heard of while slowly losing his mind.
“Your Highness –” Helena’s tone carried a warning.
“I know.” Melena cut her off, flexing her fingers as heat gathered in her joints. The anger was always there now, simmering just beneath the surface. Anger at the lies, at the system, at having to perform. Anger at herself for never knowing better before. “I won’t mention Elias. I won’t ask about the succession rumors. I’ll be the picture of Imperial grace.” She turned from the mirror, the heavy skirts swirling around her legs. “I’ve been trained for this my entire life, Helena. I know my role.”
The spare. The backup. The just-in-case. The one who trades state secrets to save her brother while everyone thinks she’s just unstable.
But even as she thought it, her fire flared hot enough that Lorelei stepped back with a barely concealed flinch. Helena didn’t move.
Nothing rattled her grandmother’s shadow.
Nothing at all.
“Your fire, Your Highness,” Helena said quietly. “Remember your control.”
Control. As if the problem was technique instead of the rage burning through her every time she looked around the Palace. Melena closed her eyes, inhaling slowly as she pushed the flames back down where they belonged. When she opened her eyes again, her reflection showed exactly what the Empire wanted: poised, pristine, perfect.
A mask beneath a mask beneath a mask.
“Better,” Helena said, with the closest thing to approval she ever offered. “The delegation awaits you in the Grand Hall. The Imperial Guard has secured your route to Zenithia.” She paused, her eyes never leaving Melena’s face. “A reminder, Your Highness. Your grandmother expects results tonight. With London Town facing Old Prosperity, we cannot afford neutrality from any province, especially one as influential as Zenithia. Do you understand me?”
I understand that you have no idea what real stakes look like. “I’m aware of the stakes, Helena.” Melena moved toward the door, automatically scanning the room’s exits – a habit she’d picked up in Shadowmere that she couldn’t seem to shake. “I need to show I can do the job when my grandmother inevitably abdicates. You don’t think I’ve seen it coming.”
Helena said nothing, but her silence spoke volumes. It wasn’t a secret that her grandmother was thinking of abdicating. The rumors had been circulating through provincial courts for months now. What remained unspoken was whether the Empress would choose Melena or find another option. With Elias missing and Melena’s fire increasingly volatile, the line of succession was far from certain.
If only they knew why it was really volatile.
“Go,” Helena pushed her through the doors. “They’re waiting for you.”
The Imperial Guard stood at attention as she exited her chambers, falling into formation behind her as she made her way through the Palace corridors. The carpet beneath her heels was worn in the center from centuries of royal feet treading the same path. Portraits of her ancestors lined the walls, their eyes following her as she made her way to the car waiting outside.
All those generations of Lockwoods who never knew there were other countries beyond our borders. Or maybe they did know and chose to pretend otherwise.
The delegation moved through the grand entrance and down the palace steps, where a fleet of sleek, armored hover vehicles waited, their chrome exteriors gleaming under the evening lights. The Imperial insignia was etched into the doors – subtle enough to maintain diplomacy, prominent enough to remind everyone exactly who had arrived.
“Your Highness,” the Master of Protocol approached, looking somewhat concerned. “The journey to Zenithia will take approximately six hours, even with the new eastern corridor opened. We could arrange for the Imperial shuttle instead. It would reduce the travel time significantly.”
Melena paused, considering the options. The shuttle would be faster, but also more visible. More eyes watching her every move. And right now, she needed time to think. To plan. To figure out how to gather the intelligence Caelum needed without arousing suspicion.
“No,” she decided. “The vehicles will be fine. I could use the time to prepare.”
To breathe. To think. To be alone with my thoughts before I have to be Imperial Princess Melena Lockwood for an entire evening.
“As you wish, Your Highness.” He didn’t quite hide his disapproval as he bowed and stepped aside.
Melena slid into the lead vehicle, the smart-glass windows instantly adjusting to allow her to see out while preventing anyone from seeing in. As the doors sealed with a pressurized hiss, the cabin filled with the faint scent of dried apricots and black tea – her grandmother’s preferred interior.
Even here, surrounded by Imperial luxury, Caelum’s deadline loomed: two weeks to gather Tournament intelligence that could save Elias and prevent a war. Two weeks to somehow secure her place on the Tournament board while maintaining the perfect princess facade.
“Security has cleared the eastern routes, Your Highness,” Elliot, her driver, announced as the vehicle lifted silently from the ground. “Estimated arrival at the Crystal Palace is 8:45 PM, accounting for mountain terrain in the final stretch.”
“Thank you, Elliot.” Melena leaned back against the plush seating, feeling some of the tension drain from her shoulders now that she was alone. “I’d prefer minimal communication for the journey unless it’s necessary.”
“Of course, Your Highness.”
The other vehicles in their convoy arranged themselves in perfect formation as they accelerated away from the palace, gliding above London Town’s historic district before merging onto the elevated provincial highways. The capital was a study in contrasts below her – ancient architecture interwoven with cutting-edge technology, much like the Empire itself. Tradition and innovation, history and future, all compressed into a single sprawling metropolis that pulsed with light and power.
How many people down there lived and died never knowing that Somnorian dream magic was real, that there were floating cities in Thalara, underground networks in The Underside?
Melena watched the landscape transform through the tinted windows as the city gave way to the lush countryside of the inner provinces. Six hours of solitude stretched before her – perhaps the last peaceful moment she’d have before stepping into Zenithia’s web.
Six hours to figure out how to be the perfect Imperial Princess while gathering intelligence for a Somnorian noble to save her brother’s life. 
Six hours to reconcile the world she’d been raised to believe in with the truth Maven had shown her.
She reached up and removed the obsidian mask, setting it beside her on the seat. For a few hours, at least, she could be herself – whoever that was.
The Princess.
The Flamer.
The Spare.
The information broker who’d traded state secrets in shadowy markets.
The sister desperate enough to make deals with dangerous strangers.
The endless contradictions that made up her existence.
As they crossed the border into the Eastern Provinces, Melena closed her eyes. Six hours to prepare for battle.
It wouldn’t be enough, but it would have to do.
#
Forty-seven minutes.
They were forty-seven minutes late. Melena didn’t even know how, but they were.
“Imperial delegation arriving,” the Zenithian guard announced into his comm as their convoy finally pulled up to the Crystal Palace entrance. His tone made it clear exactly what he thought about royalty who couldn’t keep time.
Fuck you too, Melena thought, sliding her obsidian mask back into place before the vehicle doors unsealed with a soft hiss.
The Crystal Palace loomed above them, all angles and transparent walls that caught the moonlight like a thousand prisms. From inside, she could already hear music swelling – the kind of elegant rhythm that meant the formal dances had begun. Which meant everyone who mattered was already watching the dance floor. Which meant her absence had definitely been noted.
Melena tilted her head back to look at the spires, and her breath caught. The way they twisted toward the sky – she’d seen that exact spiral pattern in one of Maven’s books. Page forty-three of “Fundamentals of Dream Architecture.” The text had described how Somnorian buildings were designed to capture and channel stellar energy, and this palace was doing exactly that. There were stars was directly overhead whose light was funneled down through those crystal spires like a massive dreamcatcher.
A month ago, Melena would have never noticed such a detail. Now, it was all she could notice. 
Her security detail fell into step around her, but Melena waved them back. “Stay with the vehicles. I’m going in alone.”
“Your Highness – “ her chief guard began.
“This isn’t a negotiation,” she cut him off. “Six of you trailing me like armed shadows will only draw more attention.”
The last thing she needed was to storm in with an entourage when she was trying to slip in unnoticed. The whole point of a masquerade was anonymity, wasn’t it? Even if that anonymity was mostly pretend.
The Zenithian herald stationed at the grand entrance looked genuinely pained when he spotted her approaching alone. His silver mask couldn’t hide the way his eyes widened or how his fingers tightened around his ceremonial staff.
The staff. Melena stopped walking. 
The crystal orb at the top was positioned exactly like the dream-focusing tools Maven had shown her in her sketches of Somnorian Dream Master equipment. And the way the herald held it like he was unconsciously channeling energy through it... Christ, was he actually using dream magic right now?
“Your Imperial Highness,” he stammered, glancing frantically at the entrance and then back at her. “We weren’t certain... that is... would you prefer a formal – “
His accent. Maven had made her listen to recordings of Somnorian nobility speaking, and this herald had the same subtle elongation of vowels, the same musical quality. But that was impossible. Zenithia was a Remnian province. How could their people speak with Somnorian inflections unless...
“I would prefer,” Melena interrupted, already moving past him, “that no one knows I’ve arrived just yet.”
His relief was so palpable she could practically taste it. “Of course, Your Highness. The masquerade is underway in the Northern Crystal Hall. Your delegation has already been – “
She didn’t hear the rest. She was already gone, navigating the familiar labyrinth of crystal corridors that reflected her gown in fractured patterns across the walls. She’d been in the Zenithian Palace more times than she cared to admit growing up. The palace was designed to disorient visitors. All that transparency created an illusion of openness while actually controlling exactly where and how people moved through the space.
Melena pressed her palm against one of the walls and nearly jerked it back when she felt the subtle vibration. These crystals were active, channeling energy in pathways that formed a network throughout the entire palace. She’d practiced this exact technique three nights ago in her room – Maven had taught her how to sense dream energy flows by placing her hands on crystalline surfaces and calling her own fire forward. Except Maven had called it “advanced Somnorian sensing” and said most dreamers took years to master it.
So how the fuck was a Remnian palace using advanced Somnorian techniques?
The music grew louder as she approached the Northern Crystal Hall and Melena found herself humming the tune. The Zenithians would have definitely noticed her absence by now, which made slipping in undetected her only real option. For once, she was actually grateful for this ridiculous masquerade.
She eased through a side entrance, the cool air hitting her flushed skin as she took in the spectacle. Crystal walls turned moonlight into rainbows, throwing color across the dance floor. The Crescent Waltz was in full swing. The most visible, most formal dance of the night, and she’d arrived right in the middle of it.
Melena skirted the edge of the room, keeping her head down. If she could just reach the far corner where the Zenithian diplomats typically – 
“You! Red gown!”
Fuck.
A dance master in silver and white was gesturing frantically at her. “Quickly! We need one more!”
She ignored him, pretending to be fascinated by an ice sculpture.
“My lady!” He appeared at her side, surprisingly fast for someone in ceremonial robes. “The formation requires even numbers. We’re one short.”
“I’ve only just –”
“Perfect timing then!” His hand was already on her elbow, steering her toward the dance floor with shocking audacity. “The Exchange begins in mere moments!”
Making a scene would only draw more attention. With everyone masked, she could at least maintain some anonymity. Better to dance for five minutes than have the entire hall watching her argue with this persistent bastard.
“Fine,” she muttered, allowing herself to be positioned at the end of the female line.
She didn’t even have time to protest before the music shifted and a masked figure stepped into place across from her.
His height was the first thing she noticed. He was taller than the other men in the line. His tux hugged his body, muscles evident under the soft glow of the room. Melena tilted her head slightly. 
She’d never seen someone with violet eyes before. 
As the music began, the man across from her moved as if he knew what he was doing, as if he had performed this exact dance in a dozen different ballrooms. But she had been in every ballroom that mattered. And she had never seen him. His posture was too rigid to be a commoner and too fluid to be anyone of noble blood. Not military, either. There were no visible ranks or insignia. Her fire prickled again, coiling at the base of her spine. 
She wanted to say something but the sound of her voice would give her away instantly. Instead, she followed his lead as the couples began to dance around each other, humming softly under her breath. The Crescent Waltz had always been her favorite and all that time in Shadowmere made Melena forget dancing was her favorite part of being an Imperial Princess. For mere minutes, she could just exist. 
He stepped in close, hand resting low at her back as the men and women around her began to find their partners – and her fire surged like it wanted out. Melena tightened her jaw and focused on the steps, still humming along to the familiar melody. If she lost focus now, she wouldn’t just scorch her dress. She’d set the entire floor ablaze. And that was the kind of attention she couldn’t afford.
Melena looked down at the floor, over the man’s shoulder, at the other couples – hell, anywhere that didn’t require her to look at him. The sound of the violins’ crescendo filled the air around them as he weaved them through the other dancers, and she found herself following his lead with ease. He was a good dancer. Too good, actually.
