She had already determined that he couldn’t be from Meridion or Little Villa – the two closest provinces. His footwork almost rivaled her own. He couldn’t be from Durant or Rovencia two hours north – they didn’t learn proper dances until they were almost marrying age.
That left Easton, Astris, or – Gods help her – London Town itself. Which meant he was either someone she should know or someone who wanted her to think she didn’t. His hand adjusted at her waist, guiding her into the next turn. Her heel nearly caught the edge of her gown, but she recovered before anyone noticed. Not him, though. His thumb pressed once – barely a touch. 
Before she could speak, heat surrounded her in an invisible cloud. He hadn’t caught her – his fire did.
The shock of it made her humming stop dead.
Those violin strings, that piano progression... 
She’d heard them somewhere else. 
With someone else. 
With Maven. 
Maven hummed this exact sequence while they worked late into the night in Shadowmere. Maven’s voice carried while she organized intelligence files, while she taught Melena to recognize other kinds of magic, and while she casually dismantled everything Melena thought she knew about the world.
This wasn’t Remnian music. 
Melena forced herself to look at the man in front of her. Looking into his eyes, was like looking at the night sky and seeing comets shoot across it. He didn’t flinch under her stare. Just kept guiding her like her confusion didn’t matter. Melena hated how calm he looked while her element refused to settle. She searched his eyes again, trying to find something, anything. But all she got was that same impossible violet.
She let him lead her into the next sequence, but her breath was getting harder to control. Not because of the steps but because of the heat. It wrapped around her legs, her waist, up her sides like a second skin. Like it was searching. His fire didn’t burn. It lingered.
As if it were trying to memorize her.
Melena’s pulse slammed in her throat. No one had ever touched her like this without touching her. No one had ever dared.
She leaned in, lips brushing the edge of his mask. “Who are you?” It came out lower than she meant. “And don’t lie.”
He didn’t answer right away. Just tightened the space between them like he had every right. She was about to ask again when he leaned in. 
“Tiran Blackwood.”
Her flame erupted so fast it nearly lit the air between them. 
“Are you surprised, Princess?”
“I’m disgusted,” she said. “Surprise would require respect.”
He didn’t flinch at her words. If anything, his grin widened – just enough to piss her off. The final note of the waltz echoed through the hall, and the dancers around them broke formation with quiet bows and scattered applause.
Melena should’ve walked away. Should’ve disappeared into the crowd and went to get started on the mess Caelum was going to get her into. But curiosity killed the cat as she held her position on the dance floor. “Dance with me again.”
He smirked – actually had the audacity to smirk at her. “I thought I was your enemy.” 
“I never said you were my enemy.” Melena held out her hand for him to grab it. He took her hand, his fingers warm in a way that made her nerves hum.
“You didn’t have to,” he said. “Your fire said enough.”
“Observing me, Lord Blackwood?” She said letting him guide her once more. “How pathetic.”
“Reading you,” he corrected, a slow grin tugging at his lips. “There’s a difference.”
The music shifted, and so did the distance between them. His hand dropped lower on her waist, pulling her closer than protocol allowed. Their feet moved together through the sharper turns of the tango, this time moving less like partners and more like rivals in a practiced duel.
“I know your favorite color is crimson like your hair. You prefer your meat rare. You hate the smell of lavender but wear it anyway because Helena says it softens your image.”
Melena arched a brow. “Congratulations. You can gather basic intelligence.”
He smiled and continued much to her dismay. “I watched you flinch when a minister gave you lavender soap last winter. You said thank you, smiled, and dropped it the second he left.”
“And?”
“You eat around the onions in your grandmother’s stew even though you know she notices. You tap your left thumb when you’re lying. And you’ve been gone for nine months on some diplomatic mission.”
“Still not impressed.”
“We both know, you weren’t on a diplomatic mission,” he spun her before pulling her back in.
Her step faltered. “You don’t know anything.”
“Then why does your fire taste like marketplace smoke?” he murmured, guiding her hand until it was laced with his in front of them.
She could feel his flames wrapping themselves around her hand, and there was something else there too – something that pulled at her like gravity. Melena closed her eyes, and when Tiran tugged at her hand, forcing her to look down, she did.
There were no visible flames.
But Melena could feel them there.
For a heartbeat, she stopped thinking and let it happen. It was the most intimate thing she’d ever experienced and it was happening on a dance floor full of people too blind to see it.
“Starlight,” she breathed. 
His eyes darkened to deep indigo as Melena tried her hardest not to panic. “Starlight?”
Then the music ended.
Again.
“Clever trick,” she said, lifting her chin. “But it changes nothing.”
“Doesn’t it?” He let go of her hand, but the warmth still clung to her skin. “You felt it.”
“I felt nothing.”
“Liar.” He chuckled. “Your left thumb is tapping.”
She stilled her hand. “I have better things to do than play games with you.”
“Then why are you still here?”
The question hit her like a slap. Because she didn’t know. Because something about him made her want to stay, made her want to keep circling each other like this until one of them broke. Not that she could ever say that to him though.
“Stay out of my way tonight, Lord Blackwood.”
He bowed, mockingly formal. “Of course, Your Highness.”
She turned to leave.
“Princess.”
She stopped, hating herself for it.
“Try not to hum any more interesting melodies,” he said quietly. “People might start to wonder where you learned them.”
Her blood turned to ice, but she didn’t turn around. “I don’t know what you mean.”
“Of course not.” She could hear the smile in his voice. “Just like I don’t know why you remind me of someone I met in a stall that doesn’t exist.”
And then he was gone – swallowed by the ballroom, like he’d never been there.
But her fire knew better. Her skin still burned with the echo of his. And for the first time since Elias vanished, Melena wasn’t worried about surviving a diplomatic even.
She was worried about inciting one. 
## Chapter 5: Katherine
Katherine had come to learn that there were exactly three types of people who woke up before dawn in Old Prosperity: the overachievers who thought sleeping past sunrise was a sin, the punishment crews who had no choice, and the idiots who somehow had been given Tournament orders for early morning training.
Somehow, she’d become the third type.
The north training grounds were already alive with activity when she arrived–not with other cadets, but with the steady sound of ice forming and cracking as Atlas trained. He stood in the center of the practice area, surrounded by training dummies that now wore transparent armor. Each dummy bore the scorch marks of previous Flamer training sessions, their surfaces scarred black beneath the fresh layer of frost.
Katherine continued to watch him; even from a distance, his control was impressive. Each layer of ice formed perfectly, with no energy wasted or power leaked. The kind of mastery that made her own abilities feel clumsy in comparison.
She checked her com-band. Fifteen minutes early and still somehow late by Atlas Tolliver’s standards.
“I was wondering when you’d show up,” Atlas said without turning around. “Your footsteps give you away, Corporal. You’ll need to work on that before the Tournament.”
If I make it to the Tournament, Katherine thought. But she kept that to herself as she stepped forward, boots crunching against the frost-covered grass. “Would you prefer I float, sir?”
Atlas finally turned to face her, and Katherine immediately regretted the comment. His silver eyes held the gleam that usually meant someone was about to have a very bad day. She’d seen it enough times during training sessions – right before he made a cadet run laps until their legs gave out.
“Actually,” he said “I would prefer you take this seriously. Unless you think London Town’s Flamers will be impressed by your wit?”
Katherine bit back another comment. He had a point, even if she hated admitting it. The matchups weren’t just about skill – they were about image. And London Town had perfected theirs. While Old Prosperity trained its Pieces in open combat, the capital specialized in presentation. Controlled media. Flawless optics. Which meant whatever power their heirs had, it wouldn’t show up in some leaked training report. It would hit like a blade – sharp, accurate, and too late to dodge.
“No, sir,” she said, forcing her voice to stay level. “What’s the plan for today?”
“Simple.” Atlas gestured to the ice-covered dummies. “You’re going to stop playing defense.”
Katherine felt her stomach drop. It would be that. Not agility drills. Not dummy strikes. No – he wanted the one thing she’d spent years avoiding. She straightened her spine and said nothing, but her fingers were already twitching for a shield. 
“With all due respect – “ she began, but Atlas cut her off.
“Save it,” he said. “I’ve read your file. I know about the lake.”
Katherine’s breath caught. The temperature around her dropped several degrees before she could catch herself, frost spreading from where she stood. Her fingers curled into fists at her sides, and the frost crept higher than she intended – up her boots, over her wrists, tight enough that her joints ached. She knew that he knew. Everyone knew, even if they never talked about it. That’s how Old Prosperity worked – nothing stayed a secret, but everyone pretended they didn’t know a thing. 
“That was different,” she muttered. “I was seven. I didn’t know how to control –”
Before Katherine could respond, Atlas moved. One moment, he was standing still; the next, a barrage of ice shards was hurtling toward her face. Her shield snapped up automatically, the projectiles dissolving harmlessly against it.
“Stop talking,” Atlas ordered, already preparing another attack. “Show me what you can really do.”
“I am showing you – “ Katherine started but had to duck as a wave of freezing air whistled past her ear. The grass behind her froze instantly.
“No,” Atlas replied. “You’re hiding. There’s a difference.”
He struck again, this time from multiple angles. Katherine’s shields held, but she could feel the strain of maintaining them against him. They both knew Atlas was holding back, but his attacks were relentless even at half-strength.
“Fight back,” he demanded, punctuating each word with another assault. “Stop reacting and start acting.”
Katherine gritted her teeth, feeling the familiar surge of her element under her skin. It would be so easy to release it, to show him exactly what she could do. But every time she thought about attacking, she remembered the sound of ice cracking, of someone screaming her name as the lake froze – 
Atlas’s next attack came from below, ice spreading across the ground like lightning. Katherine jumped, barely avoiding being frozen in place. Her landing was awkward, and she had to roll to avoid the follow-up blast that would have caught her in the chest. Her ribs protested the movement, a sharp reminder that her body hadn’t caught up to her adrenaline. Her breath came shorter now – tight in her throat, heavy in her chest. She forced her hands to stay steady.
“In the Tournament,” Atlas said, not even breathing hard, “you won’t have time to overthink. You won’t have time to question yourself.” Another wave of attacks, each one forcing her further back. “You’ll have seconds to decide between killing or being killed.”
“There are other ways,” Katherine argued, throwing up another shield. “Defense can be just as effective – “
“Can it?” Atlas’s voice sharpened. “Tell that to the last three defensive specialists who entered the Tournament. Oh wait, you can’t. They’re dead.”
A blast of cold caught her shoulder, spinning her halfway around. It hadn’t hurt – Atlas’s control was too precise for that – but the message was clear. In a real fight, that would have been the killing blow.
“That’s twice you would have died,” Atlas said, his voice eerily calm. “Want to try for three?”
Katherine’s frustration snapped. She flung her hands, pulling moisture from the air so fast it stung her skin. Frost curled down her arms in twisting ribbons, each one meeting at her palms. It wasn’t a shield she was going for this time. 
Ice needles formed in midair, dozens of them glinting like glass under the morning light. Thin as hair. Razor-edged. Floating in perfect suspense around her like a crown of blades. 
Atlas raised an eyebrow. Just one. But that alone felt like victory. 
Katherine didn’t hesitate this time. 
With a flick of her fingers, the needles launched – fast, wide, in an arc that would have torn through any static defense line. If it was defense Atlas wanted, it would be defense he – 
Atlas didn’t move. 
Every single shard stopped mid-flight, frozen between them like stars caught mid-blink. 
“Better,” he said, studying the deadly array. “But you’re still holding back.”
“I’m not – “
“Yes, you are.”
Atlas closed his fist. Her ice needles shattered instantly – no warning, no effort. Just gone.
“You have the power to be lethal,” he said, not even looking at the broken pieces. “But you’re still afraid of what that means. And that fear?” He gestured once, and the shards lifted, reforming midair like he’d never needed her to make them in the first place. “That fear gets people killed.”
Katherine didn’t answer. Mostly because she wasn’t sure if what she was feeling was shame or rage.
“You know,” a voice called from the edge of the field, “most people wait until after breakfast for death threats.”
She turned, already knowing who it was. Sebastian, leaned against one of the dummies like he hadn’t just walked into a frostbitten war zone. His presence should’ve been a break. But the way he looked at her?
Too calm.
Too careful.
She didn’t like it.
“Lord Augustine,” Atlas all but rolled his eyes. “Or should I say, Corporal Augustine? Wouldn’t want to confuse you with your younger brother, Zephyr, would we?”
“If you’re going to use my title,” Sebastian pushed off the dummy, hands sliding into his pockets, “use the right one. That would be Prince Augustine to you.”
“Sebastian…” Katherine warned.
“What?” he shrugged. “Just ensuring the Captain keeps things politically correct. Wouldn’t want word getting back that he offended my mother and London Town’s favorite son.”
“I thought that was Minho.” Katherine rolled her eyes.
“Prince. Lord.” Atlas cut in. “Why are you here? I wasn’t informed that Minho had authorized your participation in morning drills.”
“My brother commands the En Guard, not me,” Sebastian replied coolly. “But if you must know, I’m here as a favor. Told him I was checking on Corporal Nicholas – he told me to make myself useful.”
“And what does the older Augustine define as useful?”
“You heard Aloysius. The London Town Pieces will be Flamer heavy. You’re both Frost. You need to train with a Flamer. Lucky for you, I’m available.”
“There are only four of you in the entire province.” Atlas folded his arms across his chest. “I wouldn’t call that luck.”
“Considering the other two are twelve-year-old twins in Year Two, and the other is your brother who, need I mention, is a Captain, I’d be a little more grateful,” Sebastian said with a shrug.
Katherine couldn’t believe how casually he was speaking to Atlas – but then again, this was Sebastian. The same person who didn’t care that Minho was one of the most powerful figures in both London Town and Old Prosperity, even if he’d forfeited his place in the succession line to take a Captain’s rank.
“Let’s make this interesting.” Sebastian stepped forward, rolling up his sleeves. “Katherine needs offensive techniques against Flamers? Fine. But she needs to understand their mindset, too. How they move. How they think – “
“How they fight when they’re desperate?” Atlas cut in.
Katherine didn’t miss it – how Sebastian’s smile slipped, just slightly.
“Especially then.”
Katherine stayed still, arms crossed, trying not to show just how closely she was watching. Sebastian’s stance had changed – weight shifted, spine looser, arms tucked close like he wasn’t preparing to strike but react. It wasn’t Old Prosperity form. Too fluid. Too fast. Had to be London Town. Which meant he was about to give help a new meaning. 
“Rules?” he asked, but it was mostly for show.
Katherine didn’t even try to hide her snort.
As if Atlas Tolliver ever followed a rule he didn’t write himself.
“None,” Atlas said. Ice was already forming at his fingertips. “Show her what a real Flamer fight looks like.”
Sebastian didn’t crack a joke. He didn’t smile. His flames didn’t spark – they snapped to life, fast and focused, coiling tight around his forearms like armor. Not a display. A warning. Katherine blinked. That was not the lazy heat he tossed around in drills.
“First thing you need to know about Flamers?” he said, eyes locked on Atlas. “They’re predictable. If you know where to look.”
Then he moved. No windup. No warning.
The fireball missed Katherine’s face by inches. She dropped down out of instinct, not fear – but she wasn’t thrilled about being used as a test dummy either.
Wait.
The angle. The speed. The way it forced Atlas to pivot – 
Atlas fired back immediately, his signature frost grid lighting up the field in a perfect three-point pattern. Katherine had seen it a hundred times. She’d studied it. Countered it.
But Sebastian didn’t counter it. He ignored it.
His flames cut through the attack like it was routine.
Like he’d practiced that exact combo before.
Not once. Not twice. But enough times to know how it worked. 
Katherine’s eyes narrowed. She counted the rhythm in her head – three-second burst, six-second reset, nine-second full combo. The exact timing used in advanced combat rotations. Standard issue for Old Prosperity.
Except Sebastian hadn’t been there long enough to master that.
“Your left side’s still open,” Atlas said, launching another flurry. 
Sebastian dodged them with ease. “And you’re still hiding behind that grid pattern. Some tactician you turned out to be.”
Atlas’s face didn’t shift, but Katherine caught it anyway – the frost crawling across the floor, wider than it had been with her. Reaching for the ground like it wanted more room to breathe.
“Better a tactician,” Atlas muttered, “than a deserter.”
Oh. 
Oh.
So that’s what this was.
This wasn’t training. It wasn’t even about her anymore.
She didn’t say anything – mostly because she wasn’t sure it would help. The air around them dropped again, enough to make her ears pop. Both of them were drawing power now. The kind that ended with med wing reports and a full disciplinary review.
She shifted her weight, just in case she had to jump in – 
But Sebastian caught her eye and gave her that look.
Don’t.
So she backed off.
Fine. If they were gonna go full ego war, the least she could do was take notes.
Sebastian launched straight heat; tight bursts, each one stacking harder than the last, too hot to even recognize the color of.  Katherine didn’t need a thermometer to know he was turning up the heat with each strike. She could feel it in her teeth.
Atlas matched him strike for strike, but his patterns were slipping. Gone was the textbook lessons they’d learned at the University. Now it was… messy. Off-rhythm. Like he was fighting memory, not muscle. The temperature shifted with every blow – frost creeping up the walls, fire sparking low across the ground. The entire field felt unstable.
“Getting sloppy, Captain?” Sebastian called, flames flicking behind his knuckles. “Or just distracted?”
Atlas didn’t take the bait. Just dropped a full barrage, thick enough to cut through steel.
Sebastian answered with an arc of flame that sliced through it like it was nothing.Katherine blinked.
She’d only ever seen that move once – and it was locked behind three layers of clearance. Something you weren’t supposed to learn unless you were wearing stars on your collar.
The only Flamer in Old Prosperity with that kind of clearance was Dax Tolliver. 
“If you two are done using me as a narrative device,” she called, arms crossed, “you could at least let me know when to duck.”
The ice hissed. The flames flickered. But both men paused.
“Your point, Corporal?” Atlas asked without looking at her.
“My point,” Katherine said, fully aware she was talking to two egos with murder magic and potentially kissing her promotion goodbye, “is that this stopped being about my training the second he pulled out that counterstrike.”
She nodded at Sebastian. “Which, unless I missed something, is still classified.”
Atlas didn’t move. But his hands were still clenched. Sebastian’s fire dipped just enough to say he’d heard her.
Cool. Maybe they’d kill each other first. Save her the trouble of writing the field report.
Then Sebastian laughed. Actually laughed. “She’s got us there, Captain.”
Atlas exhaled like it physically hurt him to end this. “Training’s over.” He didn’t even glance back. “Corporal Nicholas?”
“Sir?”
“Next time, try shutting up five seconds earlier.”
“Noted,” she muttered, watching him storm off like the frost hadn’t already said everything for him. She waited until he was out of range, then turned to Sebastian. “So.”
“Don’t.” His grin was back, but it was a little too neat. “Some questions don’t have answers.”
“Like how you know Captain-only combat sequences?”
“Exactly like that.” He dragged a hand through his hair, fire dancing along his fingers like it had opinions. “Focus on the Flamer tactics. The rest isn’t in your syllabus.”
“You mean our syllabus,” Katherine corrected. Sebastian sighed. “I do have one question though.”
“What?”
“Do you know Atlas?”
“No.” A pause. “Not anymore.”
## Chapter 6: Melena
So far, she’d been able to avoid Tiran for the most part. 
Much of it had to do with the pompous delegates from the Southern Provinces that kept vying for her attention every time she moved, but most of it was because of the look Helena had given her when she stepped off the dance floor. She was supposed to sneak in, not become the topic of discussion. Helena was not pleased. 
Melena adjusted her mask, fighting the urge to rip the damn thing off her face. Her skin still burned where Tiran’s fire had touched her like her body couldn’t decide if it was pissed or impressed.
She chose pissed. Definitely pissed.
The first of the diplomats to intercept her wore Rovencian gold like a second skin – loud, glinting, and just subtle enough to be smug about it.
“Your Highness,” he said with a bow that lingered too damn long. “It’s been far too long since we’ve seen a Lockwood heir in motion. We were starting to think your grandmother wouldn’t send any of you this year after Elias didn’t show last season.”
Melena didn’t blink. “My grandmother would never miss a chance for us to speak to the people our family serves.”
He laughed – fake, of course – and stepped closer, lowering his voice. “Rovencia’s praying for peace this Tournament. Though I imagine you’ve heard we’ve rerouted our agricultural trades through Old Prosperity. Their supply lines are much more… disciplined.”
Ah. There it was.
She smiled again. “Discipline is easy to praise when you know nothing about peace.”
He inclined his head and disappeared, clearly satisfied he’d said what he came to say.
Before the flames under her heels could even think about catching, another diplomat was already on her – Highcrest this time, robed in navy and arrogance.
“Princess Melena.” He lifted her hand without asking. “I must say, your presence tonight nearly distracted from your brother’s absence.”
Melena pulled her hand back delicately. “Nearly?”
“Well, it’s hard to forget a legacy like his.” His smile tightened. “Should we expect an address soon? Something public? Something just for us leaders, perhaps?”
“I have no news of the Crown Prince,” she replied. 
“Highcrest will be looking forward to any news you do have,” he bowed out before she could remind him who invented the crown he bowed to.
Three conversations, three pieces of intelligence. This was easier than she’d expected.
Melena moved toward the refreshment table, mentally cataloging what she’d gathered so far. Rovencia had rerouted their agricultural trades through Old Prosperity – that was valuable. It meant Old Prosperity was strengthening supply lines, probably preparing for a lapse. Highcrest was fishing for information about Elias’s whereabouts, which confirmed they didn’t know about his situation. And they were all clearly nervous about the upcoming Tournament.
Maven would be proud. The techniques she’d learned in Shadowmere were working perfectly here. Read the subtext, note what they’re not saying, watch their body language. These diplomats thought they were being subtle, but to someone trained in information extraction, they might as well have been shouting their secrets.
This is actually going to work, she realized with a surge of satisfaction. I can get Caelum everything he needs without risking my neck. Two weeks? I’ll have a complete intelligence package in two hours.
The confidence felt good. For the first time since making that deal with Caelum, she felt like she had control over the situation. Like she could save Elias without throwing herself into the same arena that had broken him in the first place.
She was barely halfway toward the drinks table when a third noble caught her – Celestian – from his caught her with a bow of his head. His mask was fox-shaped and red-gold, matching the wine he handed her as if he were drinking it for that simple fact. 
“You wear your fire well, Princess. I wasn’t expecting you to be so composed…”
Melena sipped the wine without looking at him. “This wine is wonderful, don’t you think?”
He blinked, clearly expecting something else. She wanted to tell him how she could tell his accent was fake – that real Celestians didn’t roll the letter R but then…
“Don’t say what you’re thinking. It’ll cost you three provinces and Helena’s last nerve.”
Melena stiffened.
Only one person could’ve gotten through her shields like that.
She didn’t turn her head. Just adjusted her grip on the wineglass and scanned the upper gallery until she saw him.
Minho Augustine stood in the upper tier, champagne in one hand and that smug-ass smirk he’d had since they were teenagers plastered across his face. 
“You’ve lost Durant,” he continued. “Rovencia’s bluffing. Highcrest is fishing for a crack in your face. Tiran’s off making promises he can’t keep getting all three of them to buy into whatever the hell Zenithia is selling. Do you want my help, or do you want to accidentally burn the whole floor down?”
“That’s not off the table.” she thought back, knuckles tightening around her glass.
“Then at least wait until Alleia commits first. The optics are better when you torch someone after Frost pledges support.”
She hated how much sense he made.
“Hello, Minho,” Melena sighed as she continued her walk around the room. A nod here. A wave here. She was doing her Imperial duty to everyone looking – paying attention to everyone and nothing all at once. 
“They were expecting Elias.”
“It’s been years.”
“That doesn’t matter to them,” Minho replied. “You’re in the way of their money.”
“Money?”
“Bets, Princess. What did you think the provinces did during the Tournament? Just watched?”
Melena’s stomach turned – not because she was surprised. But because she wasn’t. Not really. Minho wouldn’t lie to her about that. 
“You mean to tell me they’re placing wagers on which province wins?”
“I mean to tell you they’re betting on who dies first, who makes it to the final challenge, and whether or not London Town’s princess burns out before she ever gets to make a move.”
Melena didn’t respond. She didn’t need to. The flick of heat behind her teeth said enough.
“And right now?” Minho added, “The odds are better on Old Prosperity.”
Melena pressed further, keeping her mental voice casual. “What about their actual strategy? Training methods? Are they changing their approach from last time?”
“Why would you want to know that?”
“Because I need to understand what London Town is up against. If I’m going to advise my grandmother on alliances, I need real intelligence, not just gambling gossip.”
Minho was quiet for a moment, and she could feel him considering. “Training’s been intense. More focused on individual combat than team tactics. But that’s all I’m hearing through normal channels.”
Normal channels. That was the problem, wasn’t it? Everything she was getting tonight was surface-level intelligence – the kind of information that filtered through social networks and diplomatic gossip. But what Caelum needed was classified military intelligence. Tournament preparation details. Strategic planning that would never be discussed at a masquerade ball.
“What about their new weapons? Equipment upgrades? Anything about how they’re adapting their elemental training?”
“Melena.” Minho’s mental voice carried a warning. “That’s not cocktail party conversation. That’s the kind of intel that gets people court-martialed for sharing.”
Her stomach sank slightly, but she pushed the feeling down. There had to be another way. Someone here had to know more than betting odds and supply routes.
Melena grabbed another glass of wine from a passing tray, swirling it without sipping. “You don’t get to do that.”
“Do what?”
“Talk about Old Prosperity like you didn’t give up your place in succession to be there.”
Minho didn’t answer at first. “Melena…”
“Minho,” she cut in. “You were second in line to the second greatest legacy this Empire has ever seen. And you gave that up for what?” She took a sip of the wine. “A uniform and a curfew?”
Minho’s voice dropped, just enough to let her know he’d felt that.
“I gave it up because I wasn’t about to live in Maxim’s shadow and wait for someone to tell me when I mattered. At least as Captain, I make my own rules. My own standards. Hell – my own curfew. So, I will ask you again, do you want my fucking help or not?”
“Yes,” Melena sighed. “I do.”
“Then turn to your left and put the wine glass down. You aren’t going to want to have that in your hand when they walk in.”
“When who – “
Silence fell across the room and Melena could have sworn the air around them got colder.
She knew that chill. Had felt it before – years ago, when she was fifteen standing behind her brother at official functions. When both of them were still young and naïve enough to believe that they’d actually had choices about where they went and what they did. 
The crowd parted and there they stood – the Tolliver brothers. 
Atlas now carried his rank with a different bearing – his back straight, shoulders firm, white hair neatly pulled back. Gone were the loose curls from his days sparring with Elias in the private training yards. His silver eyes surveyed the room with an air of detachment, yet she noticed how they paused on the exit points. 
Old habits turned into soldier habits.
He’d been handsome before. Now he was fucking gorgeous, and that was just the only word Melena could come up with at first glance.
And Dax – hell. The younger Tolliver hadn’t just grown into his power; he’d finally gained control over it. Flames no longer flickered nervously at his fingertips like they had during his first official appearances. Now they coiled confidently around his wrists like living jewelry. His dark curls still fell across his forehead, but the boyish grin he’d worn at seventeen had sharpened into something that should not have made Melena blush. 
“Well, I’ll be damned,” Melena said quietly, placing her wine down before her own fire could boil it. “They’re actually here.”
“Who else would we send?” Minho sounded amused in her head. 
She sighed. “I just assumed you’d be the only one.”
Five years. That’s all it had taken for awkward boys trailing after her brother to become men with stars on their collars. Five years for Atlas to go from First Mate to Captain of the Legion. Five years for Dax to transform from cadet to whatever the hell he was now.
“Captain Atlas Tolliver and Captain Dax Tolliver of Old Prosperity,” the herald announced, voice echoing unnecessarily.
Atlas looked exactly where she knew he would – straight at her. No emotion crossed his face, but she saw the flicker of recognition in those eyes. When they’d first met, he could barely maintain eye contact with her. Now he held it like he owned the right.
Dax didn’t bother hiding his reaction. His smile broke into something almost genuine when he spotted her. Melena watched their approach with a calm she didn’t feel, suddenly aware of how much time had passed since they’d all been in the same room together. How much had happened.
How much hadn’t.
There was a long list of things that could happen in five years. 
The Tollivers were halfway across the ballroom now, and Melena’s stomach knotted. She remembered them flanking her brother at formal events. Remembered how they’d laughed when they thought no one could hear. Remembered the way Elias had trusted them when he wouldn’t even trust his own family.
“Minho…”
“Sorry, Princess,” she could feel Minho exhaling. “This is not how we wanted to meet again.”
“But duty calls?”
Minho nodded. “Duty calls.”
“You said to wait until Alleia pledges their support.”
“Hm?”
Melena locked eyes with Atlas as he made his way over to her. “They’re going to place their bets on Old Prosperity… aren’t they?”
Minho was silent for a moment as Melena felt him move around the room. 
“Dax is,” Minho replied. “But Atlas? His money is on you.”
Perfect. If Atlas was betting on her, maybe she could leverage that into getting some real information. The Tolliver brothers had always been more direct than the smooth-talking diplomats. Less political, more military. They might actually give her something useful.
As they approached, Melena straightened, shifting into the diplomatic mode she’d perfected over years of these functions. But underneath, her information broker instincts were already working, taking note of everything she could observe about Old Prosperity’s newest Captains.
“Your Highness,” Atlas said, offering a respectful bow that was perfectly measured – formal enough to show proper deference, casual enough to acknowledge their history. “It’s been too long.”
“Captain Tolliver,” she replied, noting how he held himself. Command presence. Real authority, not just borrowed rank. “Both of you. Congratulations on your promotions. Old Prosperity must be very confident in your abilities.”
“We’ve been fortunate,” Dax said. “Good timing, better mentors.”
“And intensive training, I’d imagine.” Melena nodded. “The Tournament preparations must be quite different from what you experienced as cadets.”
She watched for reactions, any tells that might give her insight into Old Prosperity’s actual readiness levels. But both brothers’ expressions remained pleasantly neutral.
“Every Tournament brings new challenges,” Atlas replied. “We’re focusing on fundamentals. Adaptability.”
Fundamentals. Adaptability. The same non-answers she’d been getting all night. Melena tried a different approach.
“I remember how you used to train with Elias. He always said Old Prosperity’s methods were... unique. Are you still using those same techniques?”
“Some things change, some things don’t,” Dax said easily. “Though I imagine London Town’s approach has evolved as well.”
Another deflection. Melena felt her frustration building but kept her expression pleasant. These weren’t the awkward boys who used to follow her brother around anymore. They were seasoned military officers who knew exactly what information was classified and what wasn’t.
“Of course,” she said. “Innovation is key to staying competitive.”
“Absolutely,” Atlas agreed. “Though I’m sure you understand the importance of maintaining certain... operational discretions.”
There it was. The polite but unmistakable message: We’re not telling you anything useful, and you should know better than to ask.
Minho, she thought, keeping her smile in place. Are they always this tight-lipped at formal events?
What did you expect? came his amused reply. They’re Captains now, not gossipy cadets. They’ve been trained not to leak intelligence to pretty princesses, even ones they used to know.
The casual dismissal stung more than it should have. But worse than that was the growing realization that she wasn’t going to get anything substantial from them either. They were too professional, too well-trained in operational security.
How the hell am I supposed to get real intelligence if everyone with actual access is trained not to share it?
The thought hit her quicker than she’d expected and Melena had to focus on keeping her expression neutral as the Tolliver brothers made their polite excuses and moved on to other conversations. She stood there watching the swirl of diplomats and officials around her with new eyes.
This was all surface level. Every conversation tonight – the trade route information, the betting odds, the political maneuvering – it was all just the tip of the iceberg. The real intelligence, the kind Caelum actually needed, wasn’t shared at social events. It was locked away in classified briefings, restricted military sessions, confidential strategy meetings.
Places she could never access as the spare. 
No matter how good she’d gotten in the information game.
“You must score high on your country’s exam and stand on that board,” Caelum’s words echoed in her memory. At the time, she’d thought he was just being dramatic. That there had to be other ways to get the information he needed than entering the one thing that had almost killed her brother. 
But there weren’t, were there?
Fuck.
The word formed in her mind with crystalline clarity as the full scope of her situation became undeniable
Caelum was right. The admission tasted bitter, but she couldn’t deny it anymore. He was right, and I was naive.
If she wanted intelligence that could actually save Elias, if she wanted access to the kind of information that might prevent a war, there was only one way to get it.
She had to stop being the princess watching from the sidelines.
She had to get on that board.
Two weeks, she thought, feeling the weight of Caelum’s deadline settling over her like a shroud. Two weeks to score high enough on the Provincial Aptitude Examination to earn a spot in a Tournament.
Two weeks to become the very thing I’ve been trying to avoid.
Melena grabbed a new wine glass off a passing tray. 
Fuck it. Now we do it your way, Caelum. 
## Chapter 7: Katherine
Would you kill to save a life?
Big. Bold. Annoying. 
Katherine didn’t roll her eyes this time, but only because she didn’t want the proctors to dock her for being disrespectful. Again. The first time was for muttering about the bright blue Frost uniform they’d shoved her into. They hadn’t taken away points – people expected Old Prosperity cadets to be a little bit of a pompous ass during testing. If she hadn’t said anything, they probably would have looked at her a little bit funny. 
Would you kill to save a life?
The question stared back at Katherine from her console screen, exactly as unforgiving as it had been two weeks ago when Aloysius had written it on the board. It was mind-blowing to think they’d started Empirical 300 two weeks ago and now they were taking the Examination itself. 
She stared at the words a second longer than she meant to; not because she didn’t know the answer, but because it was the wrong question.
Katherine could kill. Hypothetically. If she had to. She’d been trained for it since she could walk without tripping over her own boots. The better question was: could she live with it? Could she stand over a smoking battlefield and say, I did that, and sleep through the night after?
Apparently, that was too complicated for the Testing Committee.
The console flickered and the question disappeared. In its place: a countdown.
Simulation Loading: 10… 9… 8…
Katherine cracked her neck, flexed her fingers, and pretended her heart wasn’t flipping over itself. The silence in the testing chamber got heavier, like the walls themselves were holding their breath.
By the time the screen read 3, she already knew what was coming.
They’d briefed them last week – urban warfare scenarios pulled straight from Remnian history. Last year this part of the examination had sent three cadets to the medical wing before the simulation had even ended. 
“They’ll drop you into chaos,” Aloysius had warned during drills. “Your job is to bring the order. Fast.”
2… 1…
The floor panels beneath her boots vibrated, and the light in the room dimmed. Privacy walls rose between candidates as the air around them changed. 
Simulation Engaged.
The world shifted before she could blink.
White walls dissolved into heat and smoke, replaced by a war-torn city center’s cracked stone and steel. Old Prosperity’s Central Square – but warped. Wrong. Too many shadows, too much dust. Like someone had dragged her favorite childhood memories through a blender and expected her to recognize the pieces.
The air smelled like fire and oil. The kind of mix that meant something had already exploded – or was about to. Shouts echoed from around the block. Screams too. They sounded so damn real that for a second, Katherine forget they were in a simulation. In front of her, civilians darted between alleys, glass shattered in a storefront and somewhere to her left, a child cried.
They didn’t ease you into it.
Katherine had watched enough Tournaments growing up to know that they never would. Shock value is what Aloysius called it when some Nine asked him about it last week. They needed real reactions. They needed to know what moves you’d make under pressure. 
A fire ball detonated three shops down. 
Katherine flinched. Not from the blast – the last two weeks had been full of those from Sebastian – but from the way the heat moved. She’d never felt fire that … deadly before. It was almost as if whomever had programmed it set it to maximum destruction.
She didn’t waste time trying to figure out what was going on. She moved.  
The first shield went up without thought – a narrow arc of ice curling around the front of a storefront where two civilians had ducked for cover. She shaped the wall to bend, to let the fire roll around them, instead of slamming and shattering. The second shield came faster, a sheet of frost across the alley floor causing the attacker to slip and fall giving the two civilians time to run away. 
Another scream forced her attention to her right. 
Katherine twisted toward the sound and caught a flash of red – a simulated child, maybe eight years old, frozen in the middle of the street with fire gathering behind him.
She sprinted forward, frost blooming with every step. A bridge formed under her boots, curving up and over the fire’s projected path, frost hardening the air itself. She slid down the other side, grabbed the kid, and spun as fire tore through the street behind them. Heat scorched the edge of her jacket.
She didn’t stop.
“Go!” she shouted, placing the child behind her shield wall. “Stay low, move fast.”
Another blast came at her – this one aimed directly at her chest.
Katherine turned, just in time to see a woman sprinting toward her and from the look on her face, Katherine could tell it must have been the child’s mother. The sim looked frantic screaming in a language the examination hadn’t even bothered to subtitle.
Her hair was singed. Her arm was bleeding. And her eyes?
Terrified.
But she wasn’t looking at the fire surrounding them. 
She was looking at Katherine. 
“No –” Katherine raised her hands, stepping back from the child. “I’m not the one who – “
Too late.
The woman launched at her, fists flying, nails dragging across Katherine’s arm as her frost instinctively surged. She threw up a barrier – not anything that would hurt the woman, just a wall between them. The simulation didn’t care. It read the woman’s panic, amplified it, and threw in two more civilians sprinting from the alley as her backup.
One shouted something about her being a traitor.
Another reached for a weapon.
Shit. Shit. Shit – 
She backed up too fast and slipped on the frost she’d laid down earlier. Katherine’s head snapped back against the cobblestone with a crack, and stars exploded behind her eyes. A fireball screamed from the sky. Someone – maybe the child’s mother, maybe a new attacker – was throwing shots now. Katherine rolled, threw up a shield half a second too late. It caught the blast, but not the heat. Her jacket went up in flames at the shoulder. Pain seared down her arm.
Would you kill to save a life?
The words echoed, too loud in her skull. Except this time, the life in question wasn’t some theoretical civilian.
 It was hers.
Her frost pulsed, begging to be unleashed.
To strike back. To end the threat.
To kill.
She screamed from the pain of the flames, the sound erupting from her chest like a shatter pulse, slicing the air in all directions.
Everything froze.
The mother. The civilians. The fire. Even her own screams.
Then – 
SIMULATION TERMINATED.
PULSE SPIKE DETECTED. CANDIDATE FORCEFULLY REMOVED.
The first thing she felt was the pain.
Not heat – pain.
A slow, biting throb that pulsed behind her shoulder and down her arm like something was still trying to set her nerves on fire. Her eyes cracked opened to too much light, the kind that made you squint even if you weren’t in a med bay.
But she was. She could tell by the way the air smelled – sterile, metallic, as if someone had tried to clean blood with bleach and decided that was good enough.
She tried to sit up.
Bad idea.
Pain lanced through her ribs, her bicep, her everything. The machines she hadn’t noticed before started beeping louder, one of them flashing something about elevated vitals.
“Easy.”
The voice came from the corner and Katherine turned her head too fast, instantly regretting that too. 
Atlas sat in the chair by the wall, blue uniform without a single wrinkle, arms folded as if he had fallen asleep there. His eyes met hers but not in the Captain Tolliver kind of way. 
The man before her was just Atlas. 
“What are you doing here?” her voice cracked. 
Atlas didn’t answer right away. He just shifted forward, elbows on his knees, gaze steady. 
“Candidate assessment,” he said eventually. “I was sent in Minho’s place to observe your condition after the simulation. He would have come but he decided he had to be the one to tell your mother. So here I am. Unofficially. He’s still your commanding officer.”
Her mother. 
Katherine had written her mother’s information down as her emergency contact before the examination began without a second thought. The chances of being pulled out of a simulation were common but she hadn’t expected them to actually follow through with alerting her emergency contact. She was fine. Really. 
“She’s not thrilled,” Atlas continued. “Seeing as you almost got third degree burns.”
“I can imagine,” Katherine looked away from him. The bandages on her arm itched but she didn’t move. She didn’t want to give him the satisfaction. “I’m healing. I’ll be fine.”
“I never said you wouldn’t be.”
“You never said I would be either,” Katherine mumbled. “Just went straight to telling me about my mother.”
She heard him exhale as if he were trying to figure out what he should say – if he should say anything at all. 
“Why are you here, Atlas?”
“They showed me the feed,” he replied. “I know what you’re thinking. I took the exam but I’m their commanding officer until the Tournament so they had no choice.”
“And?”
“And you froze the entire square.”
“Didn’t mean to,” she muttered. 
“I know.” Atlas looked away. His voice didn’t shake. But his hands had – just a little  – when he sat down.
“I heard you scream.”
Katherine didn’t respond; she didn’t know if she hated that he’d heard her – or if she was grateful, but she knew the look that had spread across his face. 
Relief. 
“Minho wouldn’t have come,” he added after a moment. “He has a love hate relationship with med bays.”
Katherine blinked. Atlas’s jaw flexed, like he regretted saying it aloud.
“Three years ago, the simulation didn’t exist. We were barely twenty, hadn’t even started our Year One Apprenticeship. Back then they determined your strength by hand-to-hand combat. Before I knew it, Minho and I were up against each other. I thought it would be stopped before things got too rough so, I didn’t hold back. Then I heard his scream, and I knew – I’d almost killed him.”
“Took three healers to save him,” he continued. “They called my brother in. Didn’t tell our father what I’d done. Someone just went and grabbed Dax from the other room. He had to melt ice from Minho’s chest while an Elite recited life incantations over him so he wouldn’t shapeshift from the pain. And you know what I remember most?” He looked back at her. “Being proud of how good my control had become.”
Atlas exhaled hard and closed his eyes. “Sometimes I wish I didn’t have control over my frost. Then I could say it was an accident.”
“You don’t mean that.”
“What you did in there,” Atlas sighed. “Was an accident. That’s all. If we’re being honest, it won’t even cost you that many points on the exam. They’ll let you take the written and psychological parts this evening with the oldest candidates if you’re up for it. There aren’t that many twenty-five-year-olds this year, so you won’t be hindering them.”
“I thought you weren’t here as my commanding officer,” she raised an eyebrow. “Anything else you need to report to me since you’re back at it?”
“I’ve always been your commanding officer.”
“Not always,” Katherine whispered. 
Atlas stood up, but didn’t move towards the door. He just stood there, watching her like he had in the middle of Aloysius’s classroom. 
“You know I wasn’t allowed to talk to you after your mother stepped down, right?” he asked suddenly.
Katherine blinked.
“They said it’d be…inappropriate,” he continued.
“Because of the chain of command?”
“No.” He shook his head.
“Then why?”
For a second, neither of them spoke. 
Then – 
“I never wanted to be your commanding officer,” he said, voice low. “But how was I supposed to say no to Michelle Nicholas – when she asked me to protect the one person I never should’ve gotten close to?”
## Chapter 8: Melena
Her hands were still shaking from the altercation.
Everything had gone exactly as she expected.
Except for one thing.
Cordelia.
Fourteen days. That’s how long it had been since Caelum cornered her in that marketplace, since he’d handed her that crystal with Elias’s voice on it, since he’d given her two weeks to gather enough Tournament intelligence to save her brother’s life and prevent a war.
Fourteen fucking days of sneaking around the palace, stealing diplomatic files, memorizing troop movements, recording conversations between Helena and the Council members. Fourteen days of perfectly executed intelligence gathering while maintaining the facade of a reluctant heir being dragged into a Tournament she didn’t want.
And now? Now her seventeen-year-old sister was about to blow the whole goddamn thing to the Outer Realms and back. 
Unlike Old Prosperity who would likely be facing some dangerous challenge forcing them to use their elements in battle, London Town sent their candidates through rigorous decision making in the best way it knew how: sitting them in front of the Elders of Remnia and forcing each of them to take their psychological exam in front of everyone – secrets be damned.
Which meant if Cordelia got called in there, if they started digging into why a Lockwood princess was suddenly interested in the Tournament…
Melena had been all but prepared in her burnt orange jumpsuit that all Flamers wore, waiting for her name to be called. Within the walls of the Tournament Hall, it didn’t matter who she was on the outside. She was one of almost three thousand candidates that fell within testing age that year and with her usually curly hair straightened, Melena could almost pass for someone else.
It was almost a gift.
There were no stares, no whispers, no one begging for pictures. Because Elias was always in the spotlight. Most people hadn’t seen Melena’s face in years – not since their mother’s funeral, and even then, she had been shielded from the cameras by a black veil. Elias had been the only person that day to show his face. Looking back, Melena should have been grateful for that moment. It was probably the only thing that allowed her to stand in line and be able to just breathe.
The only thing keeping Caelum’s plan intact.
Then she saw her.
Same crimson hair.
Same amber eyes.
Bright blue candidate uniform marking her as a Frost.
Standing four lanes over, laughing with the girl behind her as if she’d known her all her life.
Cordelia.
“You have to be fucking kidding me,” Melena’s hands curled tight, knuckles cracking. She almost dashed across the room – then stopped. Drawing attention would only make it worse. Candidates were not allowed to leave their line once they were in them. And Cordelia knew that.
But knowing her little sister, she was there for shits and giggles. Seventeen. Three years too early for the Tournament. And still moving up like it didn’t matter.
To Cordelia, it probably didn’t.
But to Melena, it was everything. 
Melena could’ve burned the ground under her sister’s feet because of all the days; why today?
Today was her birthday. 
Melena turned twenty that morning, which made her technically eligible for the Tournament thanks to some loophole rule that had been enacted in the last six years that allowed candidates to test if their birthday fell within the Tournament window. The whole reason she could be here gathering intelligence for Caelum without raising suspicion.
But Cordelia? Cordelia was seventeen and had no business being here at all. She was here for shits and giggles, probably to mess with Melena because all of a sudden her big sister gave a damn about the Tournament when she’d never shown interest before. Cordelia wasn’t stupid – she’d definitely noticed Melena’s sudden change in attitude about the whole thing. And knowing her little sister, she was probably suspicious as hell about why Melena actually wanted to be here.
Smart girl. Too smart for her own good.
Cordelia wasn’t even paying attention to her surroundings. She just kept laughing with that same charm that she used on the palace guard whenever she had missed curfew.
Four more people moved ahead.
Three.
Two.
This is happening. This is actually fucking happening.
“Cordelia Lockwood.”
Three things happened in that moment.
The first was five people turned to the voice, likely because both Cordelia and Lockwood were common names in London Town.
The second was Cordelia – sweet, reckless Cordelia – had the audacity to smile like she was proud of herself.
And the third?
Melena realized no one recognized them.
Yet.
Not the girl holding Cordelia’s hand.
Not the guards at the perimeter.
Not the attendant logging names into the system.
No one moved like they knew they were standing in the presence of royal blood. No one blinked. No one bowed.
Cordelia Lockwood was just another girl in the line.
And that meant, for right now, Melena was too.
For now. But the second those Elders start asking questions...
Melena knew she had to move and fast. Blending in was the only thing saving both of them – and her mission. She didn’t even realize she’d broken formation until she’d accidentally stepped on someone’s feet sliding into line behind Cordelia.
“Do you want to die?” Melena whispered, her mind racing through every possible disaster scenario. 
Cordelia tilted her head back just enough to meet her eyes. “Hi, Lena.”
“I’m serious.”
“I know.” Cordelia shrugged. “They won’t even do the blood check until the physical evals meaning I can make it through the first section of the test.”
Melena felt the heat in her body moving towards her fingers. Two weeks of perfect espionage and she’s going to destroy it all for a psychology exam. “You’re a kid.”
“And I’m smarter than half the people in this hall.” Cordelia frowned. “I’m tired of them picking boring candidates every year. Do you know how many Pieces we could keep alive if they had someone with an ounce of my knowledge?”
Melena stared at her.
Cordelia wasn’t scared.
She was out of her fucking mind.
“This isn’t a game,” she hissed, thinking of Caelum’s warning about how many people wanted Elias dead. “Are you trying to have our heads on a fucking platter? It’s bad enough I have to be here. I can blend in for the most part.”
“I will give you that,” Cordelia agreed. “But you’re the heir. There’s a difference. You won’t blend in for long.” She leaned closer. “What is it that you’re so afraid of, big sister?”
Where do I even start? The psychological exam. The Elders digging into their backgrounds. Someone recognizing them. Caelum finding out she’d been compromised. Losing her only chance to save Elias. Starting a war.
Before Melena could respond – before she could pull her sister out of the line and drag her back to the Imperial hover, back to safety, back to where Melena wouldn’t have to worry about her finding out the truth and doing something stupid like trying to help – Cordelia stepped forward.
“Present.” She called out.
Fuck.
The doors to the Elders’ chamber hissed open, and Melena barely had time to curse under her breath again before a pair of guards began moving in their direction – armor standard issue, expressions unreadable, and absolutely no recognition in their eyes.
Melena didn’t move. Her fingers tightened around Cordelia’s arm, pulling her hard enough to make her stumble. “She’s not a candidate. Her name isn’t on the list – “
“Doesn’t matter,” the second guard snapped, already moving closer. “She’s in uniform. She’s in line. She’s up.”
“She’s seventeen!” Melena’s voice cracked. “She lied to get in here – “
“And you think that’s new?” the first one cut her off. “You think you’re the only ones playing games today?”
The irony would’ve been funny if it wasn’t so terrifying. If only he knew what games she’d actually been playing. 
He reached for Cordelia again.
“You need to back away,” the taller guard ordered. “This is your final warning.”
Final warning. Like the one Caelum had given her. Two weeks, Princess. 
“No,” Melena said before she thought better of it. “She’s not going in there.”
“Then you’re interfering with the Tournament Selection Process, and the Sanctum will have to be notified,” he replied, reaching for something at his hip – a small rod with glowing edges.
Elemental suppressor.
Melena’s blood turned to ice. The Sanctum. Notification. Official records. Everything she’d worked to avoid was about to be documented and filed and investigated.
Caelum will think I’ve been compromised. The deal will be off. Elias will disappear.
She shifted, sliding in front of her sister. “I said no.”
“What’s the hold-up?” A voice from behind the guards caught Melena’s attention.
A line attendant – low-ranking, clipboard in hand, clearly annoyed. She didn’t bother approaching. Just leaned to the side to see what the delay was. One look at the guards, then at Melena, and her frown deepened.
“You,” she pointed at Cordelia. “Did you say present?”
“I did,” Cordelia replied.
“Then let her through,” the attendant snapped. “We’ve got two thousand more to process today.”
No no no no NO.
The taller guard moved, and Cordelia yelped as he jerked her from Melena’s grip, one arm twisted behind her back, the other holding her waist as she struggled. “Wait! Stop – stop! Let go of me!”
And that’s when Melena’s control snapped.
She reached before she could think – then hands were everywhere. Shoulders. Arms. Wrists. There were only seconds between her initial movement and the suppressor rod making contact with her shoulder.
This is it. This is how it ends. Not because I got caught stealing files or recording conversations, but because of my sister playing fucking detective. 
It felt like drowning – except her lungs weren’t the problem. Her flame was. One second, it burned hot in her chest, her arms, her spine – ready to defend Cordelia, ready to fight for her mission, ready to do whatever it took to keep Caelum’s deal intact. And then?
Nothing.
Like a match snuffed out mid-strike.
Like hope dying.
The tile was cold against her palms.
Happy twentieth birthday to me.
“Get up.”
The voice came from somewhere above her, filtered through the ringing in her ears and the hollow ache where her fire used to live. Melena tried to push herself up, but her arms shook like she’d been training for hours instead of lying unconscious for – how long had it been?
“I said get up.” A boot nudged her ribs, not gentle. “Unless you want to forfeit your candidacy here and now.”
Candidacy. Right. That’s what this was supposed to be about. Not saving Elias. Not preventing a war. Just a girl taking a test.
Melena forced herself to her knees, then to her feet. The world tilted slightly, and she had to brace herself against the wall. The suppressor’s effects were wearing off, but slowly. Like waking up from anesthesia.
The hallway was different now. Emptier. Most of the candidates had been processed while she was unconscious. She could hear voices echoing from the testing chambers – muffled questions, nervous laughter, the scratch of styluses on tablets.
“Where’s my sister?” Her voice came out rougher than she intended.
The guard – a different one now, older, with scars across his knuckles – looked at her like she was an insect. “Already processed. Passed the initial screening.”
Of course, she did. Cordelia was probably charming the Elders right now, answering their questions with that same easy confidence she used to talk her way out of everything. The same confidence that was going to get them both killed.
“Can I – ”
“You can take your test or you can leave.” He gestured toward a door marked with the number seven. “Chamber Seven. They’re waiting.”
They’re waiting. Like she was late to her own execution.
Melena walked toward the chamber on unsteady legs, her mind trying and failing to understand what had been said to her. 
The chamber door slid open with a soft hiss.
Inside, three Elders sat behind a curved table, their faces hidden by ceremonial masks – silver for Memory, gold for Truth, copper for Judgment. Ancient tradition, supposedly neutral. In reality, Melena knew exactly who was behind each mask. Elder Galeah, Elder Dali, Elder Black. She’d memorized their files, their voting patterns, their weaknesses.
Intelligence gathering. Even now.
“Candidate Lockwood,” the golden mask – Truth – spoke first. Elder Dali’s voice, though the mask’s modulation made it sound otherworldly. “Please be seated.”
Melena sat in the single chair facing them, trying to look nervous in the right way. Nervous about the test, not nervous about being exposed as a spy.
“You understand this is a psychological evaluation,” Memory continued. Elder Galeah. “We’re not here to judge your elemental abilities. We’re here to assess your mental fitness for Tournament participation.”
Mental fitness. If they only knew.
“I understand,” Melena replied.
“Good.” Judgment leaned forward – Elder Black, the hardest to read even without a mask. “Let’s begin with a simple question. Why do you want to compete in the Tournament?”
Because a dream magic user from Somnoria is blackmailing me with my brother’s life.
“To serve my country,” she said instead. “To prove that I’m worthy of the trust placed in me by blood and by the crown.”
Truth tilted her head. “And yet, according to our records, you’ve shown little interest in Tournament competition until very recently. Your training scores are adequate but not exceptional. Your instructors describe you as... reluctant. What changed?”
Everything. Melena felt the phantom weight of Caelum’s crystal in her pocket. Proof that her brother was alive but broken. Evidence that she was running out of time.
“I matured,” she said carefully. “Turned twenty today, actually. Realized that privilege comes with responsibility.”
“Today?” Memory’s voice sharpened with interest. “Your birthday is today?”
Shit. She shouldn’t have mentioned that. Birthdays were tracked, recorded, cross-referenced. They’d know she was only eligible because of the loophole rule. They’d wonder why she’d waited until the last possible moment.
“Yes,” she said, because lying now would only make it worse.
“Interesting timing,” Judgment mused. “Most candidates register months in advance. You submitted your application two weeks ago.”
The day after I met Caelum.
“I wanted to be sure I was ready,” Melena said, which was technically true. She’d wanted to be sure she could gather enough intelligence to satisfy Caelum’s demands before committing to the Tournament. 
The Elders exchanged glances – or at least, their masks turned toward each other in a way that suggested communication.
“Tell us about your family,” Truth said finally.
There it is.
This was the question Melena had been dreading. “What about them?” she asked, stalling for time.
“Your relationship with them. Your responsibilities. Your loyalties.”
My loyalty is to keeping Elias alive. But she couldn’t say that.
“I love my family,” she said instead. “I’d do anything to protect them.”
Which was also technically true.
“Anything?” Judgment’s voice carried a weight that made Melena’s skin crawl. “That’s a dangerous word, Candidate Lockwood. In the Tournament, you might face situations where protecting your family conflicts with protecting your teammates. Or your country. What then?”
What then indeed.
Melena thought of Cordelia, somewhere in this building, probably answering similar questions with far less preparation. Thought of Elias, half a world away, slowly losing his mind while she played word games with masked bureaucrats. Thought of Caelum’s warning about factions who wanted her brother dead.
“Then I’d make the choice that saves the most lives,” she said.
It was the right answer. The one they wanted to hear.
It was also the truth.
## Chapter 9: Katherine
She didn’t flinch when she saw the gauze. That was progress.
It wrapped from her wrist to her shoulder blade – “just a precaution,” the nurse had called it, despite the fact that Katherine had already started healing. Frosts recovered quickly thanks to their healing ability. Everyone knew that. But apparently, protocol didn’t give a damn what elemental blood ran under her skin.
Atlas had been right. She’d finished the rest of the PAE with the twenty-five-year-olds – back straight, pain buried, pretending like she hadn’t screamed herself hoarse in the simulation hours before. There hadn’t been time to rest before the rankings dropped. It wasn’t like there was a point in waiting anyways. 
She didn’t run – that would have looked desperate – but she didn’t drag her feet either. 
Everyone would be gathered at the board in the center of the Tournament Hall, elbowing for space, craning to see if their name had made the sixteen.
The hallway opened up into the central chamber, and Katherine froze.
The hall had transformed. What was this morning just a sparse clinical testing facility was now crawling with cameras, lights, and broadcast crews. Katherine blinked against the sudden brightness. How had they set all this up so quickly? Giant screens hung from the ceiling, each one displaying the Imperial emblem while a countdown timer ticked away the seconds to the official announcement. It wasn’t just the media equipment that caught her off guard – it was how normal everyone else seemed about it.
A woman with a clipboard and headset rushed past her, shouting orders at a team hauling equipment. “I need reaction shots when the names drop! Get your lenses ready!”
Katherine took an instinctive step back, bandaged arm pressed against the wall. This wasn’t right. Testing had just ended – literally just ended. She could still feel the sting of the simulation burns under her gauze. And yet here they were, cables snaking across floors that had been empty hours ago, lights mounted where there’d been nothing but blank walls.
“Move it!” someone barked, and Katherine barely dodged a hovering camera that zoomed past her face, its lens adjusting with a mechanical whir.
It paused, then swiveled back towards her. A red light blinked on.
“Hey – “ she started, then stopped herself. Starting a fight with a camera wasn’t going to help anyone.
Katherine turned away from the drone, trying to get a better look at the crowd. That’s when she realized; it wasn’t just University cadets. The hall was packed with people she’d never seen before. Men and women in uniforms from every branch of Old Prosperity’s military and civil service. Factory workers with regulation haircuts. Engineers with Old Prosperity insignias stitched onto their sleeves. People from the Northern Quad, Eastern District, and even the remote mining settlements.
All of them between twenty and twenty-five.
All of them here for the same reason.
“Where did all these people come from?” she whispered, more to herself than anyone else.
“You didn’t think it was just University candidates, did you?”
Katherine turned to find Reign pushing through the crowd toward her, eyebrows raised like Katherine had just asked if water was wet.
“I mean – “ Katherine started, but the words died in her throat. 
The Tournament pulled from the entire province. She’d known this. Had read it in Aloysius’s textbooks. Had heard it whispered during her mother’s debriefings. But knowing something and seeing it were two different beasts entirely.
“There’s gotta be at least a thousand people in here,” Katherine said, trying to keep the panic from her voice.
“Three thousand, actually,” Reign corrected her. “Give or take a few who didn’t pass the physical screenings.”
Three thousand. 
Three thousand candidates.
Sixteen spots.
The enormity of it hit her like a blast of cold air. All those lives, all those potentials, narrowed down to sixteen names on a board. And majority of those sixteen wouldn’t make it back. A static crackle cut through the noise as the main screen flickered to life. The Imperial emblem filled the display, glowing blue against black. A hush fell over the crowd.
“Ten minutes until Delegation begins!” a voice announced through the speakers.
Katherine’s heart slammed against her ribs. Another drone zipped past, this one trailing a holographic mic that captured snippets of conversation from the crowd. It paused near a group of miners, recording their nervous laughter before moving on to a cluster of office workers huddled together in silent prayer.
“I don’t understand,” Katherine said. “How did they set all this up so fast? We were just taking the exams this morning.”
Reign gave her a strange look. “Kat... they’ve been building this set for weeks. Did you think they’d wait until the last minute?”
“But that would mean – “
“That the selections were predetermined?” Reign’s laugh had an edge to it. “Welcome to the Tournament, Nicholas. The PAE isn’t about finding the best candidates. It’s about giving the public a show while the Empire confirms what High Command already decided. All of Old Prosperity’s Pieces come from the University. Were you seriously not paying attention in Aloysius’s class? When was the last time you talked to your mom?”
“But the simulation,” Katherine argued. “The scores – “
“I mean they are valid, don’t get me wrong. But they are only looking at those of us who attend the University,” Reign finished. “Take you for example. You’re Michelle Nicholas’s daughter. You really think you aren’t going to be on that board?”
The camera drones seemed to multiply, sweeping through the crowd like hungry birds. Katherine felt exposed under their mechanical gazes as she tried to come to terms with what Reign was saying. 
“Five minutes to selection!” the announcer’s voice boomed.
The crowd pressed tighter. Bodies against bodies. Heat against cold. Elementals didn’t naturally mesh together  –  Flamers running hot, Frosts running cold, Terrains heavy with the weight of their element, Elites crackling with energy that made the hair on your arms stand up. It was like trying to mix oil and water, dirt and lightning, and expecting harmony.
Katherine craned her neck, trying to see the board through the sea of heads. Another camera drone buzzed, stopping to hover just above her. 
“Ignore them,” Sebastian stepped in front of, his shoulder blocking the drone’s view. 
“They might be the easiest part of this whole mess,” Katherine muttered. “Where have you been?”
“Around,” he shrugged, too casual to be believable. “Scoping out the competition.”
“Three thousand people isn’t competition.”
“Only sixteen of them matter.”
“And you’re so sure I’ll be one of them?” she asked, trying to sound like she wasn’t desperately hoping for reassurance.
Sebastian didn’t answer right away. Instead, he gestured to a group of candidates huddled near the western wall. Most wore the traditional copper pendants of the Engineering Corps. Their uniforms were clean but worn, many patched at the elbows and knees. They stood together, shoulders touching, as if they were bracing for impact.
“See them?” Sebastian’s voice dropped. “They’re here because they have to be. Blood type. Element compatibility. Genomic sequencing. Half of them were ruled out before they ever stepped into an examination room.”
Katherine frowned. That wasn’t in any of the textbooks. “What are you saying?”
“I’m saying there’s a reason why Old Prosperity’s Pieces come from the University.” His eyes met hers, unusually serious. “We’re the healthiest people in the entire Province.”
“Tried to tell her that,” Reign mumbled. 
Before Katherin could process what he meant, the lights dimmed dramatically. The crowd hushed, all eyes turning toward the enormous screens in front of them. 
“Citizens of the Remnian Empire,” a voice echoed through the hall, “Today we honor the tradition of the Tournament, a sacred challenge that tests not just the strength of our provinces, but the character of our people. You are about to witness history as sixteen Pieces are chosen. Per Imperial law, their exact placements on the board will not be revealed until Delegation.”
Katherine barely registered the words. Her focus was on Sebastian, whose expression shifted from serious to something else. His gaze fixed on something – or someone – across the crowded hall, jaw tight enough to crack teeth. She tried to see what had caught his attention but only saw a sea of heads and camera drones.
“What are you looking at?” she whispered, nudging him.
Sebastian didn’t answer. Didn’t even blink.
“Reign,” Katherine turned. 
Reign stood on her tiptoes, straining to look over the crowd. “Too many damn Blockers blocking the view,” she muttered, dropping back down. “Why? What’s happening?”
“Nothing,” Sebastian cut in as he turned away from whatever he’d been fixated on.
“By the power vested in the Sanctum,” the voice continued, “we present the sixteen the men of Old Prosperity!”
The screens flickered, and names began to appear, one by one, in glowing white text.
The first three names shocked no one: 
Augustine, Minho E.
Tolliver, Atlas A.
Tolliver, Anatolios D. 
Katherine blinked, staring at the third name. “Anatolios?”
“That’s Dax’s real name,” Sebastian said, a hint of amusement cutting through the tension. “Anatolios Daxamari Tolliver. Atlas Arcadius Tolliver. Their parents had a thing for A names, and Dax was never a fan.”
“How did I not know that?” Katherine frowned. “I’ve known them for years.”
“Because no one calls him that except personnel officers, your mother, and his father when he’s pissed,” Sebastian smirked. “Would you introduce yourself as Anatolios if you had a choice?”
Reign suddenly grabbed Katherine’s arm, her fingers digging in hard enough to bruise. “Oh shit.”
“What?”
“Look,” Reign pointed at the screen as a fourth name appeared.
Augustine, Sebastian B.
The murmur became a confused buzz.
“By the Gods,” Katherine whispered, turning to Sebastian. His face was a perfect mask of indifference, but his hands had curled into fists at his sides. “You… you…What the hell did you go in there and do?”
“I tanked my application,” he corrected her. “Or tried to.”
“You what?”
“Missed half the psychological evaluation. Showed up drunk to the combat simulation. I even wrote my name backward on the entry forms.” He laughed, but there was no humor in it. “I did everything short of setting the test center on fire.”
Katherine couldn’t process what she was hearing. “Why would you…Why would you sabotage yourself like that? Do you know how many people would kill to be on that list?”
“That’s exactly the problem,” Sebastian snapped. “They will kill. And some of them will die. And for what? So, the provinces can pretend they’re settling their differences with a game instead of a war?”
“It’s tradition – “
“It’s a fucking death sentence wrapped in ceremony!” His control slipped, flames licking up his forearms. A nearby Frost shifted away nervously. “You think I want to watch my brother play general with sixteen lives like they’re just pieces on a board? You think I want to be one of those pieces? You think I want him to have to decide if he is going to save my life?”
Katherine stared at him, speechless. Sebastian Augustine wasn’t afraid of anything. Not failing exams, not breaking the rules, not pissing off Captains. He walked through life like consequences were theoretical concepts that applied to other people.
But now?
Now he looked scared.
“What is it?” she asked, voice dropping. “What do you know that I don’t?”
He opened his mouth to speak, but a drone swooped between them, its lens adjusting to capture their faces in the wake of the announcement. The red recording light pulsed steadily. Sebastian’s expression smoothed over instantly, all traces of fear vanishing behind his usual careless smile. He threw an arm around her shoulders, carefully avoiding her bandages as he hugged her.
“Congratulate me,” he whispered in her ear. “Now.”
“Look at you, Bash!” she announced loud enough for the microphone. “Congratulations.”
The crowd around them laughed, the tension broken.
But Katherine felt it – the tremble in his fingers where they rested on her shoulders. The slightest catch in his breath that no one else would notice.
“We need to talk,” she said under her breath. “Privately.”
“Later,” he replied, equally quiet. Then, louder: “Time to celebrate! Where’s that champagne they’re always showing on the broadcasts? Don’t tell me that’s just for Delegation…”
As he pulled her through the crowd, Katherine couldn’t shake the feeling that she’d just glimpsed something she wasn’t supposed to see. A crack in the carefully constructed veneer of Sebastian Augustine.
And if Sebastian was afraid, Katherine knew in her heart of hearts, if he thought it was better to lose a little face than step onto that board?
Then there was no way in hell she was going to make it out of there alive. 
## Chapter 10: Melena
Melena winced as her fingers brushed against the bruise blossoming across her shoulder blade. The skin there was tender – an angry mix of purple and blue that spread like spilled ink beneath her skin. Six hours. That’s how long it had been since the suppressor rod had made contact.
Six hours since her fire had been snuffed out like it was nothing.
London Town’s preparation chamber was all polished obsidian and sharp angles, designed to intimidate as much as to prepare.
