Mirrors lined every wall, forcing candidates to confront themselves one final time before stepping onto the Chessboard. Melena stared at her reflection, barely recognizing the woman who stared back.
They’d assigned her a black formal dress with crimson trim – London Town’s Tournament colors – but the fabric felt wrong against her skin. Too heavy. Too restrictive. Or maybe that was just the hollow ache where her element should be.
Melena slid the sleeve up her arm, examining the marks there – thin red lines where the suppressor’s energy had traveled through her body, seeking out every trace of her fire and smothering it. She’d always hated her flames for being so wild, so uncontrollable. But their absence was worse. So much worse.
She traced one of the marks with her fingertip, trying to coax even the faintest warmth to her skin. Nothing. Not even a spark.
“Fuck,” she whispered, letting the sleeve fall back into place. The Tournament was about to begin, and she couldn’t even light a candle.
How am I supposed to gather intelligence when I can barely function? 
The psychological evaluation had been a disaster - who knew what Cordelia had revealed to those Elders? For all Melena knew, they were already suspicious, already watching her too closely. And now she was supposed to step onto that board, suppressed and compromised, while somehow completing Caelum's task.
The creak of the door made Melena yank her sleeve down, instinct rather than modesty. She didn’t turn around  –  she didn’t need to. The reflection in the mirror showed everything: Tiran Blackwood, who looked entirely too comfortable.
“Get out,” she said.
He didn’t. Instead, he closed the door behind him with a soft click that felt too final for her liking. The black formal wear of a London Town candidate suited him – charcoal fabric offset by crimson embroidery at the collar. His eyes were the same violet she remembered from the masquerade, watching her with the same unsettling intensity.
“Not happy to see me, Princess?” His voice was almost teasing as he moved to stand a few feet behind her. Close enough to talk, far enough to dodge if she decided to lash out. Smart man. Very smart man. 
“Why would I be?” Melena turned to face him, one hand instinctively moving to the small of her back where the suppressor had hit her hardest. “The last time we spoke, you practically threatened to take my crown.”
“I merely suggested that if you weren’t fit to wear it, someone else would.” His gaze traveled from her face down to where her hand rested against her lower back, then back up to meet her eyes. “Is that where they hit you?”
The question caught her off-guard. “What?”
“The suppressor rod.” Tiran stepped closer, and she found herself backing against the mirror. “They’re designed to connect with the central pathway of an elemental’s energy. For Flamers, that’s usually here.” He touched his own lower back, somewhere near the base of his spine. “It’s where the fire first manifests in the body.”
Melena’s eyes narrowed. “How would you know that?”
Too much. He knows too much. The masquerade, the marketplace smoke comment, now detailed knowledge about suppressor rods. Either Tiran Blackwood was extremely well-informed about things no London Town heir should care about, or…
Or he's connected to whatever compromised me in the first place.
“I make it my business to know things.” He tipped his head slightly. “Does it still hurt?”
Just like Caelum made it his business to know things. The thought made her stomach turn.
“I don’t need your concern,” she snapped, but even as the words left her mouth, she felt the ache pulse through her body.
Tiran studied her for a long moment. “You can’t access your fire, can you?”
The direct question made her jaw clench. “Delegation is in fifteen minutes. Whatever you came to say, say it.”
“I came to offer help.” He reached into his jacket and pulled out what looked like a small vial filled with liquid fire  –  golden-red, with flecks of something that reminded her of embers. “This can temporarily restore your element until the suppressor’s effects wear off.”
Melena stared at the vial with equal parts suspicion and desperate want. “And what do you want in return? My undying gratitude? A lock of my hair for whatever twisted spell work you’re collecting?”
“I want the Bishop position.”
Melena laughed, the sound filling the quiet room. “That’s not my decision to make.”
“It is,” Tiran countered, stepping closer. “They haven’t announced the positions yet, but I know your grandmother sent Helena to consult with the Delegation committee an hour ago because the Queen’s Bishop went to your sister.”
That made Melena’s breath catch. “Cordelia? That’s – no. She’s seventeen. She’s not even officially eligible.”
“And yet,” Tiran said, “She made the cut. Quite impressive. The committee had to make a few... exceptions, but with her bloodline and her scores, she’ll be on that board.”
“How do you know that?”
“The same way I know Zephyr Augustine made Old Prosperity’s sixteen. The same way I know their King’s Bishop is going to Sebastian, despite how he tried to sabotage his own exams so he would push his younger brother’s scores out of the running. Minho? He’s going to be their King and don’t even get me started on the oldest Augustine. Do you really think he’s going to be spared after the shit Sebastian pulled? Nope, they’re coming for him too.” 
This is exactly what Caelum needs. Detailed knowledge about Tournament participants, their positions, their capabilities. And here was Tiran, offering it freely like some kind of gift.
Tiran held the vial up between them. “I survive on information and in the Tournament, information will keep you alive.”
Almost exactly what Caelum told me. The coincidence made her skin crawl.
Her gaze was fixed on the vial now, on the promise of fire when she needed it most. “And all you want is to replace my sister as Bishop?”
“I’m offering you a choice,” he said. “Let Cordelia step onto that board, where she’s almost certainly going to die in the first challenge. Or give her position to someone who might actually survive it. Either way, I’ll still give you this.” He held out the vial.
Melena’s fingers trembled slightly as she reached for it. “Why would you help me if I say no?”
“Because I don’t want to see you die on that board,” he said. His voice had lost some of its edge, sounding almost sincere. “And without your fire? That’s exactly what will happen.”
She took the vial, examining it more closely. It was warm to the touch – not hot enough to burn, but warm enough to remind her of what her own element felt like.
This is insane. But what choice do I have?
Without her fire, she was useless. She couldn't survive long enough to complete Caelum's task. And if she failed…
Elias disappears forever.
“How do I know this won’t kill me?” she asked.
“You don’t.” He stepped back, giving her space. “But if I wanted you dead, Princess, there are far more efficient ways than poison.”
“Not exactly reassuring.”
“It wasn’t meant to be.” For the first time since entering the room, Tiran smiled. It wasn’t the confident smirk from the dance floor, but something she could accept. “The vial will burn going down. It’ll feel like swallowing a star. But when it settles, your fire will respond again. Not at full strength, but enough to defend yourself until your natural element returns.”
Melena uncorked the vial and sniffed it cautiously. It smelled like cinnamon and ash  –  like her own fire but distilled.
“Ten minutes until Delegation,” announced a voice through the chamber speakers.
“Time’s running out,” Tiran said softly. “What will it be, Melena? Your sister or your survival?”
The vial was warm in her palm, getting warmer with each passing second.
“Eight minutes until Delegation.”
Melena closed her eyes, suddenly aware of how many lines she was about to cross. “If you’re lying to me, Blackwood…”
“I know,” he said, his voice barely above a whisper. “You’ll burn me from the inside out.”
Melena uncorked the vial and tipped it back before she could second-guess herself. The liquid burned a path down her throat  – not painful, but intense, like swallowing a star just as Tiran had warned. Heat bloomed outward from her chest, spreading through her limbs like wildfire reclaiming scorched earth. It wasn’t her fire, not exactly. This felt older. Jagged. But it was fire all the same, and she wasn’t about to reject it.
“The sensation will pass,” Tiran said. “Give it a moment.”
She wanted to snap at him; to tell him she didn’t need his guidance, but the rush of heat stole her words. Sweat beaded along her hairline as her body adjusted to the foreign element. When she finally caught her breath, she felt the familiar weight of flames at her fingertips. She extended her hand, and a small flame danced across her palm, weaker than her natural fire but present.
“Four minutes to Delegation,” the chamber speakers announced.
“You better hope this works,” Melena said, pulling her com-band from her pocket. Her fingers hovered over the screen.  She found the name she was looking for. Flight Commander Julian Voss. 
Tiran stepped closer. “The Flight? What could you possibly have done to earn a favor from them?”
“That’s not your concern,” Melena said, pressing the call button before he could ask more questions. “You might have been right when you said there was something I could do. Now shut up before he answers.”
The com-band connected on the second ring, and a stern male voice answered. “Princess. I didn’t expect to hear from you today of all days.”
“Commander Voss,” Melena said. “I’m calling in that favor you owe me.”
A pause on the other end. “I assumed that debt would be collected in a more… official capacity.”
“This is as official as it gets.” She glanced at Tiran, who was watching the exchange with undisguised fascination. “I need Cordelia Lockwood’s scores adjusted. Immediately.”
“Princess –” Voss’s voice dropped to a whisper. “What you’re asking is... highly irregular. Helena Rivington herself was just here arguing on behalf of the Empress, and even she couldn’t –”
“I’m not asking as the Empress’s granddaughter,” Melena cut in. “I’m asking as the person who ensured the Core Flight assignments remained outside Imperial jurisdiction three years ago. The person who convinced my grandmother that the Flight should maintain its own command structure rather than answer to the provincial governors.”
Silence stretched between them. Then a heavy sigh.
“How much of an adjustment?” Voss finally asked.
“Enough to place her below the selection threshold.” Melena closed her eyes, telling herself this was for Cordelia’s protection. “Replace her with Tiran Blackwood for the Bishop position.”
“You’re asking me to commit what some might consider treason, Princess.”
“And I’m asking because I know you’ll do it anyway.” Melena’s voice hardened. “My sister is seventeen, Commander. She won’t survive the Tournament.”
More silence, then the sound of fingers against a keyboard. “It will take a few minutes to access the final scores.”
“You have three,” Melena said, glancing at the countdown timer on the wall. “After that, they lock the positions.”
“Understood.” A pause. “Princess? This makes us even. Whatever happens after this...”
“Is on me,” she finished for him. “I know the price, Commander.”
The call ended, and Melena slipped the com-band back into her pocket. 
Another favor used. Another debt called in. Another step further from the princess I was supposed to be.
But that princess wouldn't save Elias. That princess wouldn't prevent a war. That princess would sit safely in the palace while her brother lost his mind in a foreign country.
This is who I have to be now. The spy. The manipulator. The sister who would burn down empires to save the people she loved.
Even if it meant making deals with people who knew too much. 
Even if it meant committing treason. 
“That was impressive,” Tiran said, breaking the silence. “I had no idea you had the Flight in your pocket.”
“I don’t,” Melena replied. “I did them a favor once. A significant one. They’re repaying it by doing something that could get us all executed if the Sanctum discovers it.”
“And yet you did it without hesitation.”
“For my sister?” Melena looked at him directly. “I’d burn down the entire Empire.”
And for my brother? I might just have to.
The chamber speakers crackled once more. “Two minutes to Delegation. All Pieces, prepare to enter the hall.”
Tiran extended his hand. “Shall we, Princess?”
She ignored his offered hand, moving past him toward the chamber door. But before she reached it, she turned back. “If you die out there, Blackwood, I’ll bring you back just to kill you myself. You understand me?”
The curve of his smile only pissed her off more. “I wouldn’t expect anything less.”
## Chapter 11: Katherine
Katherine had expected a stadium.
Something loud and crude, maybe. Bodies packed into tiered seating, provincial banners hung from every available surface. The kind of place that invited shouting and stomping and the primal thrill of watching other people suffer for entertainment.
Instead, she found herself walking into a cathedral.
The Delegation Hall rose around her like something grown rather than built, all sweeping arches and impossible height. Black marble gleamed underfoot, veins of gold catching the light from crystal fixtures suspended in the air as if by magic. The ceiling curved inward, adorned with ancient mosaics depicting Tournaments from centuries past  –  Pieces frozen mid-victory or defeat, their faces rendered in tile and stone.
It was beautiful, in the same way a well-crafted blade was beautiful. Designed for a specific and terrible purpose.
“Move your ass, Nicholas,” Sebastian muttered from behind her, shoving her shoulder lightly. “You’re blocking the damn door.”
Katherine hadn’t realized she’d stopped walking. She forced her legs to move again, following the stream of white-uniformed candidates toward their designated staging area.
Everywhere she looked, there were cameras. Not just the expected broadcast drones hovering near the ceiling but smaller, more discreet units strategically placed throughout the hall. Some were focused on the massive Chessboard at the center of the room  –  sixty feet of alternating black and white squares, each large enough to hold several people. Others were trained on the gathered crowd of Imperial officials, provincial leaders, and Tournament patrons seated in private viewing galleries around the perimeter.
“Shit, they’ve got more cameras than Year Eight graduation,” Sebastian said, eyeing a drone that zoomed past their head.
“Yeah, well, more people watch this than watch us graduate,” Reign said from in front of her, tugging at her too-tight collar. “Pretty sure my parents have a betting pool at work.”
“On what, how fast we die?” Katherine stammered.
“Probably,” Reign shrugged. “My dad had money on that kid Quinn last year. Lost his whole paycheck when he got taken out in the first challenge.”
The conversation died as the reality of their situation settled over them again. Katherine had spent so many hours studying past Tournaments, dissecting the strategies of Pieces who’d won  –  and the fatal mistakes of those who hadn’t. But those had been names on a page. Numbers in a statistic. Not flesh and blood people standing all around her, adjusting collars and talking in hushed tones about the families they hadn’t seen since summer break.
“Pieces, you will now be seated,” announced a voice from overhead speakers. “Please proceed to your designated area and await further instruction.”
The sixteen candidates from Old Prosperity moved toward a section of tiered seating draped in white and silver, opposite an identical section in black and crimson where London Town’s candidates were being directed. Katherine followed, hyperaware of the fact that their every movement was being recorded and transmitted across the Empire. 
As she took her seat, Katherine scanned the viewing galleries, searching for familiar faces among the sea of Old Prosperity’s civilians. Atlas had told her that her mother would be here  –  not that it mattered. Protocol dictated no contact between Pieces and observers once Delegation began. Still, some childish part of her wanted to know her mother was watching.
There  – on the western side. Her mother sat with damn near perfect posture, her civilian clothes still somehow conveying the authority of her former rank. Beside her was her father, Ethan, looking painfully out of place, his academic frame dwarfed by the military and political figures surrounding them.
Katherine hadn’t realized how much she’d missed seeing them together until that moment. When was the last time she’d had dinner with them? Six months ago? Eight? She hadn’t bothered going home for summer break, thinking she’d at least have the fall holidays. Katherine tore her gaze away, focusing instead on the other candidates filing into their seats as she took her place in between Reign and Sebastian.
She kept her hands fisted in her lap, forcing herself to stay still even as her nerves screamed otherwise. She could feel Reign bouncing her knee beside her, the way Sebastian flexed his fingers against the seams of his uniform. She was too distracted to say anything as the Hall doors opened and every voice fell silent. 
The Sanctum had arrived.
They emerged from sixteen equidistant entrances around the circular hall, each robed in gray fabric that marked their neutrality. Their faces remained obscured by the trademark veils of their order  –  shimmering material that moved like smoke, concealing everything except the vague impression of human features behind them. Sixteen Sanctum members for sixteen Pieces. The symmetry was intentional, another reminder that everything in the Tournament had meaning.
The lead member approached the center of the hall, where a raised platform held a miniature version of the Tournament Chessboard. No bigger than a dinner table, its dark and light squares shimmered with embedded tech  –  waiting to receive the Pieces’ designations.
“Citizens of the Empire,” the Sanctum leader began. “We gather today for the sacred tradition of Delegation. For centuries, the provinces have settled their differences through strategy rather than warfare. Today, we honor that tradition as we declare the Pieces who will represent Old Prosperity and London Town.”
“As dictated by Imperial law,” the Sanctum leader raised their hands, “we shall proceed from Pawn to sovereign. When your name is called, step forward onto the designation tile where your position shall be confirmed. We will begin with the Pawns of Old Prosperity.”
The Sanctum member lifted a single hand. The black and white Chessboard shimmered once, then dimmed. “Pawn One, Old Prosperity, Sergeant First Class Tegan Morrows.”
Tegan, a girl Katherine vaguely recognized from the Engineering Club, rose stiffly from her seat. Cameras swung toward her, catching the exact moment her mouth tightened into a prideful smile. She crossed the hall and went to stand on her square without looking around her. Then came the flash of light from her picture being taken. Tegan’s face appeared on the hologram in midair, locking her position in. 
The next name was called before the photo of her even faded.
“Pawn Two, Old Prosperity. Cadet Griffin Hale.” 
Griffin moved faster  –  almost too fast. His boots slipped against the marble, but he caught himself, squaring his shoulders before stepping onto the board. Another flash. Another picture. Another name called. By the time all eight Pawns were called, the knot in Katherine’s stomach had grown. Everything after the Pawns was a serious deal. 
The Sanctum leader shifted their weight, just enough that the ceremonial robes rustled against the marble floor. Something about the sound made Katherine’s shoulders tighten. Her mom used to make that same noise when she’d come home from En Guard meetings  –  fabric sliding against stone as she’d drop her formal jacket by the door, sighing like the weight of an entire province had been sitting on her chest all day.
“The Knight,” the Sanctum member announced, “moves unlike any other. They are as unpredictable as they are essential.”
Katherine resisted the urge to search for her mother’s face again. She’d taught her how to play when she was six, sitting cross-legged on the floor of their quarters with pieces that were too big for her hands. “Knights don’t play by the same rules, baby girl,” she’d said, demonstrating the L-shaped movement. “That’s why the other pieces fear them. Why you always protect your own.”
“King’s Knight, Old Prosperity.”
Reign nudged her with an elbow. The entire hall seemed to hold its breath.
“Captain Anatolios Dax Tolliver.”
No one actually gasped – Old Prosperity had too much discipline for that – but the collective straightening of spines throughout the hall said enough. Dax stood from his seat near the front row, tugging his jacket straight with a single motion. The cameras circled him like vultures, hungry for some reaction, but his face was pure Tolliver: blank as freshly fallen snow.
Katherine watched him cross to his position and remembered the first time she’d seen that same expression. Dax had been what, fifteen? Standing beside Atlas at a High Command dinner, trying so hard to look like he belonged there while his father shook hands with her mother. Now Katherine stared at him  –  Captain of the Vanguard, King’s Knight, second most valuable offensive piece on the board.
“Queen’s Knight, Old Prosperity.” The Sanctum leader’s voice pulled her back to the present. “Private Zephyr Augustine.”
Sebastian stiffened so fast that he probably would have set something on fire if he’d been anywhere but the Delegation Hall. Every muscle in his body locked tight, his fingers digging into his thighs until the seams of his uniform stretched under the pressure. But it wasn’t the way he moved that scared her  –  it was the sound he made. Low. Broken. Like something cracking from the inside out.
“No,” he muttered first. Then louder. “Fuck no.”
Zephyr was already on his feet, moving toward the board like someone had wound him up and let him go. Shoulders squared. Head high. Face blank in that way all cadets at the University learned to master when they’d been called to do something they didn’t want to do. Sebastian shoved out of his seat before Katherine or Reign could react. Cameras swung toward him immediately, eager to catch whatever was about to happen. He didn’t seem to notice. He moved straight for the front row, straight for Minho, crossing the marble like he had a right to tear the whole place apart.
“You said you wouldn’t let them do this,” Sebastian hissed, grabbing Minho by the shoulder hard enough to bunch the fabric of his jacket.
Minho didn’t flinch. Didn’t turn. Didn’t so much as blink.
“Sit down before I make you,” he said quietly enough that only the first two rows could hear. 
“You said – “ Sebastian started, the words cracking. 
“I said,” Minho interrupted, still not looking at him, “sit your ass down.”
Sebastian froze. He didn’t let go at first  –  his hand was still locked around Minho’s jacket  –  then slowly, he stepped back like he was prying himself off a ledge. His hands dropped. His shoulders heaved once. Twice. And the audience said nothing. No gasps. No whispers. Just six thousand people pretending they hadn’t seen a brother almost unravel on the Empire’s front porch.
Zephyr reached the board by the time Sebastian had sat back down. His hologram flickered to life above him  –  wide-eyed, proud, and all the things Sebastian never wanted him to be.
The chime sounded and without another word, Zephyr Augustine was locked in place.
“The Bishop,” The Sanctum leader continued, unfazed by Sebastian’s outburst, “extends the reach of the province. Where a Knight may cross enemy lines, a Bishop penetrates them. Queen’s Bishop, Old Prosperity. Captain Atlas Arcadius Tolliver.”
Atlas rose from his seat without fanfare, not a single crease in that damn uniform. While everyone else fidgeted with buttons or tugged at sleeves, he just... stood. Like he’d been waiting his whole life for this moment, for this exact position.
Queen’s Bishop.
Suddenly, Katherine was thirteen again, sitting on the cold floor of her mother’s quarters, face-burned with frustration as fifteen-year-old Atlas reset the board. She’d lost. Again. Michelle was stuck in some endless meeting with High Command, so Atlas had been assigned to “keep an eye on her.” As if she needed a fucking babysitter.
“You’re not paying attention to the bishops,” he’d said, pointing to the diagonal line her pieces had left open. “Knights get all the attention because they’re unpredictable. But bishops...” He’d lifted the piece, letting it hover over the square before setting it down with a click. “Bishops win games because they move in the shadows.”
She’d snorted then. “Is that why you follow my mother around like a shadow?”
Atlas didn’t say anything. He just moved  –  Pawn to E4. She mirrored  –  Pawn to E5.
He brought out his knight, pressured her center, and when she scrambled to defend, he slid his Bishop to B5. She tried to fight back and shoved a pawn up to F5, leaving her King’s side wide open without even realizing it. Atlas didn’t blink. He captured her Pawn at C6 and when she panicked and moved her knight up to F6, he slid his Bishop forward to D7.
Check.
Katherine had no choice but to drag her King out to F7, exposed and staggering across the board. Atlas just chased her, sliding the Bishop back to E8  –  check again  –  then watched as she stumbled to E7, desperate to hide behind her own pieces.
He didn’t even need his Queen.
All he did was push a Pawn up the center, bait her into grabbing it  –  and when she did, when she thought maybe she’d caught up  –  he moved his Queen’s Bishop out from C1 to G5.
Third check.
Three minutes, thirty seconds.
Game over.
She’d lost that game in under twelve moves. Lost another four games after that. 
She wasn’t thirteen anymore.
She knew what the Pieces did now. Knew how to play Chess like back of her hand. Even beat Atlas a couple of times before her mother stepped down. He’d taught her what it looked like when someone didn’t come for your Queen or your Knights or your Pawns  – when they came straight for the King and didn’t stop until there was nowhere left to run.
That’s who Atlas was.
The person who you didn’t see coming until you were already out of moves.
And this time?
There wasn’t a clock ticking down.
There wasn’t her mother coming home and ending the game.
This time when Atlas moved, people were going to die.
And maybe that’s what she should’ve been thinking about. The bodies. The blood. The way the Chessboard wasn’t just a pretty metaphor anymore  –  it was a kill order, written in real names and real lives.
Instead, she was thinking about Atlas. Standing there. Bishop to C1. Taking his place like he already knew how this whole damn thing would end.
Knowing Atlas, he probably did. 
## Chapter 12: Melena
Melena always wondered what Elias had been thinking when his name was called.
Was he nervous? Scared? Did he already know that he was going to be chosen? Did he already know he was walking into a shit show?
A million questions ran through Melena’s mind as she watched the London Town Pawns take their place on the board. Part of her was upset. No, not upset. Fucking furious because she’d given into Tiran. The other part of her was relieved that Cordelia was back at home, probably giving the guards hell, but at least she was safe. Angry but safe.
The banners shifted overhead, the heavy black and crimson folds catching the gold light as the Sanctum leader called the next name. Melena barely heard it. Her blood was rushing too loud in her ears. She shifted her weight and felt the burn still sitting low in her body – the fake fire Tiran had given her. It wasn’t hers. It didn’t hum under her skin like it used to. It sat there like a loaded weapon she didn’t fully trust but needed anyway.
Maven had never prepared her for this. But then again, neither had London Town. All those months in Shadowmere learning espionage while her peers learned parliamentary procedure and diplomatic protocol. None of them had trained for combat – that wasn't what London Town heirs did.
But Old Prosperity? They've been preparing for war since they could walk.
“King’s Knight, London Town.” The whole hall went quiet. Melena felt her jaw tighten without meaning to. “Lord Felix Harrington.”
One of Helena’s favorites. A boy who’d spent more time with his nose in the air than his feet on the ground. Felix looks nervous. Good. He should be. They all should be. This wasn't a parliamentary session where charm and connections could save you. Melena swallowed hard, watching as he took his place on the board. Ten spots had been filled. Two more before they got to the big guns. Suddenly, she couldn’t ignore what was happening on the Old Prosperity side anymore.
She’d been trying not to look. Trying to tell herself that she didn’t care. But her eyes kept sliding over there like they couldn’t help themselves.
Three Augustine brothers on Old Prosperity’s board already. Minho as King. Sebastian clearly fuming after his outburst over Zephyr’s selection. And Zephyr himself, looking way too young to be standing there with his brothers.
Her gaze caught Minho’s across the hall, and she didn’t hesitate to reach out this time. 
You look like shit, she sent.
Always a pleasure, Princess. Even his mental voice sounded strained.
Headache?
He didn’t need to elaborate. They’d been through this before when Elias had been chosen – the Tournament Hall was like a megaphone for emotions. Minho’s telepathic abilities made him a receiver for every scream, sob, and prayer bouncing off these walls. It was why she wasn’t surprised by how cold he’d been with Sebastian earlier. When the noise got too loud, Minho shut everything down – emotions, connections, even family.
Like standing in the middle of a riot, he confirmed. You doing okay with that bargain-basement fire Tiran slipped you?
Melena nearly choked. How did you – 
Please. Your real fire is like a signature. This one feels like a cheap knockoff.
Something in the way he said it – that familiar blend of arrogance and actual concern – made her fire pulse hotter beneath her skin. It was the same tone he’d used when they were fifteen and she’d accidentally set his favorite history book on fire during a particularly heated argument about provincial borders.
Speaking of signatures, she redirected, I see the Sanctum’s keeping the Augustine boys together. Except one.
She didn’t ask directly. Didn’t need to. Minho would know what she was fishing for, and she wasn’t about to add to the pressure already crushing his temples.
Fuck, Mel, Minho sighed, and just that small slip – using her nickname instead of “Princess” – told her how much pain he really was in. Don’t make me look.
I wouldn’t if I didn’t need to know. She sighed. Someone told me something earlier, and I don’t want them to be right. 
A wave of energy behind her made the hair on the back of her neck stand up. It wasn’t steady like Minho’s telepathic hum, Sebastian’s explosive heat, or Zephyr’s eager-to-please vibe. 
This one was older. 
Calmer. 
Almost like Elias.
Maxim? she asked, not turning around.
Minho’s mental voice cracked. Shit. I didn’t feel him come in.
That’s impossible. He’s supposed to be – 
At the Celestian Embassy? Yeah, that’s what I thought too.
Melena resisted the urge to turn around. She didn’t need to see Maxim Augustine in Tournament clothes to know what he looked like. The oldest Augustine brother had been a fixture at palace functions since before she could remember – always polished, always proper, always exactly where he was supposed to be in the line of succession. 
Right below her family.
This doesn’t make sense, Melena shook her head. Maxim refused to take the PAE three times now. Said he wouldn’t risk the line of succession.
Well, looks like he changed his mind, Minho replied. 
Why now? Melena asked. 
You think I know? The frustration in Minho’s voice was palpable. If I’d known Maxim was planning this, I would’ve  –  He cut himself off, and Melena could sense him retreating further behind his mental shields.
Minho?
Tiran is about to take his place on the board, he said after a moment. And Sebastian’s about to lose his shit if they announce Maxim as… 
“King’s Bishop, London Town.” Her entire body went rigid. “Lord Tiran Blackwood.”
There he was. Right on cue. Walking across that marble floor like he already owned the whole damn place. His face didn’t change, but she could’ve sworn there was a flicker of something in those violet eyes when he passed her seat – satisfaction, maybe. Or relief.
Whichever one it was, it just pissed Melena off. 
“You know what nobody tells you?” Elias had slurred, three bottles deep the first night home after he returned from his Tournament. “That fucking floor is freezing. Like, ice cold. But you’re sweating anyway because there’s so many goddamn lights. And everyone’s watching you, and you have to try not to fucking trip, Mel. If you look down, they’ll talk about how you’re not confident.”
Melena didn’t know what to say then. Just sat with him while he drank himself stupid trying to forget.
She wasn’t confused now. The marble was cold. And the room was burning. The Sanctum leader kept announcing names, but they blurred together in Melena’s head. Her focus kept slipping between now and then  –  between watching Tiran and remembering Elias sobbing in her bathroom at 3 AM. two weeks after his Tournament ended. 
Minho? She tried again, but he was locked down tight, his shields at maximum. 
“You know why they put us in the sovereign positions?” Elias had laughed once, that hollow sound that made her chest hurt. “Not ‘cause we’re special. Not ‘cause we earned it. It’s ‘cause we’re the ones people want to see broken. The pampered princes and princesses brought low. Makes for better ratings when Royal Blood spills, Mel. Trust me on that.”
She’d thought he was being paranoid. Overdramatic. Now, sitting here, feeling the eyes of the two most powerful provinces on her back, she wasn’t so sure.
“King, London Town.”
Melena’s breath caught as she felt Maxim rise from his seat behind her but she refused to turn. Her eyes were fixed on the black throne at the center of the board, the position that had nearly destroyed her brother.
“Prince Maxim Augustine.”
Whispers erupted like someone had lit a fuse, and suddenly Melena was thirteen again, watching Elias get called to the board. She’d been so goddamn proud that day. So fucking clueless about what it really meant to wear that crown.
Maxim moved past her row, his arm brushing against hers for just a moment. Their eyes met, and she saw nothing gentle there  –  only focus and intensity, as if he had made peace with whatever was coming.
“From the very first day, I could tell who would fail,” Elias had once said to her, his tone monotone, eyes fixed on an invisible point. “It wasn’t about their scores or potential. It was all in their walk. The ones who scan for exits? Already dead. The ones who gauge the competition? Might make it halfway. But there’s always someone there to hasn’t made a single move, and those people? They’re the real threats, Mel. They’re the ones who know what Pieces they’re prepared to sacrifice.”
Maxim looked straight ahead as he took his position. The hologram of his face appeared above the board, locking him in place. Tiran’s fake fire flared in her chest like it was trying to burn away the memory of her brother’s broken voice. She’d never understood why he disappeared for weeks at a time, why he’d come back with that distant look in his eyes. Why he’d stopped being the brother she knew.
Until now.
“Queen, London Town. Princess Melena Lockwood.”
She rose from her seat, Elias’s tear-streaked face flashing behind her eyes. “Just walk, Mel,” he’d sobbed at their mother’s funeral, fingers digging into her arms hard enough to bruise. “Don’t look at them. Don’t look down. Don’t let them see you’re scared. Just fucking walk.”
So, she did.
One foot in front of the other across that ice-cold marble.
As she took her place on the Queen’s throne, as the hologram of her face appeared above the board, as the Sanctum leader declared the Tournament officially begun, Melena felt something shift inside her. The fire Tiran had given her sat on top, but deep in her core, where a suppressor rod couldn’t reach, a spark of her own flame flickered to life.
Weak. Barely there. But hers.
And maybe  –  just maybe  –  it would be enough to keep her from becoming whatever had broken her brother.
## Chapter 13: Katherine
Katherine had never been beyond Old Prosperity’s borders before.
That realization hit her as their transport crossed over into Havenwood, the sleek hover vehicle humming beneath them like a sleeping cat. All her life, she’d studied maps of the Empire. Could recite the major exports of each province. Had memorized the historical conflicts and alliances that shaped their territories. But knowing something from a textbook wasn’t the same as seeing the gradual shift of landscape with your own eyes  – how Old Prosperity’s rigid industrial architecture gave way to Havenwood’s sweeping curves and vibrant colors.
“First time out?” Sebastian asked, catching her wide-eyed stare as she pressed her face closer to the window.
Katherine didn’t answer right away. Didn’t want to admit that she  – Michelle Nicholas’s daughter –  had never set foot outside her home province. She’d always had a reason: summer training programs, winter academies, extra credit opportunities that kept her tethered to the University and its cold walls.
“It’s different than I expected,” she finally replied, watching as they passed a massive digital billboard showcasing their delegation ceremony from earlier. Her own face flashed briefly across the screen alongside fifteen others. Sixteen potential sacrifices for the entertainment of millions.
Sebastian leaned forward from the row behind them. “Havenwood exists for two things,” he said, voice surprisingly steady for someone who had nearly lost his damn mind at Delegation. “Entertainment and excess. The Tournament’s their biggest moneymaker.”
“Hear that?” Reign nudged Katherine’s arm. “We’re selling points for the bookies.”
The transport curved around a hillside, and suddenly, the Tournament Houses came into view. Three massive structures rose from the valley floor, gleaming in the late afternoon sun. Unlike Old Prosperity’s blocky efficiency or London Town’s stiff formality, these buildings seemed designed purely for aesthetic impact  –  soaring glass panels catching light at impossible angles, creating prisms that scattered rainbows across the surrounding landscape.
The Houses grew larger as they approached – three distinct structures arranged in a perfect triangle. The white steel and frosted glass building on the right flew Old Prosperity’s banners from every available surface. The obsidian structure on the left displayed London Town’s crimson and black. Between them stood the tallest building  –  the Sanctum House  – a perfect blend of both styles, with neither province’s colors displayed.
“You’d think they’d be more subtle about the symbolism,” Sebastian said with a snort. “What’s next, actual chess pieces on the roof?”
Minho glanced back from the front row. “Do you ever do anything other than complain?”
“Why would I when I have you around?” Sebastian laughed. 
As their transport slowed, Katherine took in the compound’s surroundings. The entire area was cordoned off from the rest of Havenwood by a shimmering barrier  –  nearly invisible except for the occasional ripple when the light hit it just right. Beyond that, she could see the dense crowds already gathering around the perimeter. Fans with banners. Camera crews setting up equipment. Vendors hawking merchandise with their faces on it. The transport came to a smooth stop at the entrance to the compound. 
The man waiting for them had the kind of face that said he’d seen too much shit to be impressed by anything  –  least of all sixteen fresh Pieces who still thought they had a chance.
“Welcome to Havenwood,” he said, not bothering to make it sound welcoming. “Old Prosperity, I presume.”
Katherine studied him as they filed out of the transport. Early thirties, maybe. No visible element markings, which was unusual. Most handlers wore their elements proudly, but this one? Nothing but that pristine gray uniform with white trim that matched their House.
“I’m Nico,” he continued, eyes sweeping over the group. “Your handler for the duration of the Tournament. Not your friend. Not your therapist. Not your confidant. I’m here to make sure you understand the rules, follow the schedule, and don’t do anything stupid enough to get yourselves disqualified before the fun begins.”
“Define stupid,” Sebastian said, earning a sharp look from Minho.
Nico’s expression didn’t change. “Anything that violates Sanctum protocols. Attempting to leave the compound. Damaging Tournament property. Engaging with London Town Pieces outside of official challenges.” He paused. “Or killing each other before the Sanctum says you can.”
Katherine’s stomach turned. The casual way he said it  –  like he was listing grocery items rather than regulations on when they were allowed to die  –  made it worse somehow.
“Let me be clear,” Nico continued, gesturing for them to follow him toward the White House. “The moment you walked out of Delegation, you stopped belonging to Old Prosperity. You now belong to the Tournament. To the Sanctum. Your provincial identity exists only as a team designation. Nothing more.”
The entrance to their House slid open at his approach, revealing an interior that somehow managed to be both expansive and sterile at the same time. The main floor was one massive open space with high ceilings and too much light. Clean lines. Neutral colors. Furniture arranged in conversational clusters that didn’t actually invite conversation.
“This is the common area,” Nico said, not slowing his pace. “All meals are taken here, all group meetings, all strategy sessions if you decide to have any. The entire space is monitored 24/7.”
Katherine glanced up, noting the discreet cameras embedded in the ceiling at regular intervals. No blind spots. Nothing hidden. Like they wanted the Pieces to know they were being watched every second.
“What about private conversations?” Dax asked, eyeing the cameras.
Nico’s laugh was short and humorless. “There’s no such thing as private in the Tournament Houses, Captain Tolliver. Every inch of this place is designed for maximum visibility for the Sanctum and for the viewing audience.”
“The audience?” One of the Pawns  –  Tegan, Katherine thought  –  sounded surprised. “They can see inside the Houses?”
“Not live,” Nico clarified. “But the Sanctum reviews all footage. Selected clips are broadcast during intermissions. The public loves seeing what happens behind the scenes.” His smile didn’t reach his eyes. “Makes them feel like they know you before you die.”
No one had a response to that.
Nico continued the tour, leading them through a set of double doors into a state-of-the-art training facility. Unlike the common area, this space hummed with energy. Combat rings. Simulation chambers. Element-specific training stations. The floor itself seemed adaptable, panels shifting subtly as they walked across them.
“Training facilities are available from 0500 to 2200 hours,” Nico said. “All sessions are monitored and analyzed. The Sanctum uses this data to design the challenges, so I suggest you be strategic about what you reveal.” He nodded toward a sealed door at the far end. “That leads to the medical bay. Let’s hope you don’t need to see it, but you probably will.”
Katherine watched as Atlas studied the training equipment, noting the same look in his eyes that she’d seen a thousand times during University drills. Cataloging. Assessing. Planning. Even here, he was still a Captain.
“The back wall is one-way glass,” Nico continued, gesturing toward what appeared to be a solid wall at the rear of the training area. “It looks out onto the neutral zone between Houses. You’ll be able to observe London Town’s Pieces when they train, just as they’ll be able to observe you.”
“Psychological warfare,” Minho noted.
“Everything in the Tournament is warfare, Captain Augustine,” Nico replied. “The sooner you accept that, the longer you’ll survive.”
As they moved back through the common area toward a curved staircase, Katherine caught glimpses of other spaces  –  a dressing room with frosted glass walls, a small library area with screens instead of books, and several doors marked with Sanctum symbols that Nico pointedly did not explain. There were too few Frosts on their side, she realized. Just her, Atlas, and Houston  –  the Queen’s Rook  –  a thin line of water against whatever fire London Town was building across the compound.
“Sleeping quarters are on the upper levels,” he said as they ascended. “Pawns on the second floor. Knights and Bishops on the third. Rook, King, and Queen on the fourth. Your uniforms and personal effects have already been delivered.”
The second floor was a circular hallway with eight identical doors, each bearing the name and chess designation of its occupant. The Pawns peeled off, some excited, some terrified, all of them suddenly looking too young. Zephyr hesitated, glancing back at Sebastian before Minho gave him a small nod, sending him to his assigned room.
The third floor followed the same layout but with only four doors  –  two marked for Knights, two for Bishops. Katherine felt a small twinge of relief knowing she’d at least have some buffer between herself and Minho as King. Atlas and Sebastian would be on this floor with Zephyr and Dax.
“The fourth floor is restricted to those assigned there,” Nico said as they continued upward. “Meaning unless you’re the King, Queen, or Rooks, you don’t go up there without permission.”
By the time they reached Katherine’s door, her head was spinning with rules and restrictions. Her name was etched into a small plaque beside the entrance: Katherine Nicholas  –  Queen, Old Prosperity.
She’d known her designation since the delegation, of course. Had felt the weight of it settle over her when the Sanctum leader called her name, watched her mother’s face as she took her position on the board. But seeing it here  –  permanent, official  –  made it real in a way the ceremony hadn’t.
“Code is your birthdate,” Nico said, nodding toward the door panel. “Change it once you’re inside. Only you and the Sanctum will have access.”
The door slid open at her touch, revealing a space that was surprisingly... normal. A bed with crisp white linens. A desk beneath a window overlooking the compound’s central courtyard. A small seating area. A private bathroom. Nothing extravagant, but not the prison cell she’d half-expected either.
Her duffel bag sat at the foot of the bed, looking out of place against the pristine surroundings. Beside it was her Tournament uniform  –  white with silver accents, the Queen’s crown emblem emblazoned on the shoulder.
“Rest while you can,” Nico said from the doorway. “First challenge starts at 0900 tomorrow. Breakfast is at 0700. I suggest you don’t be late.”
Before she could ask any of the dozen questions fighting for priority in her mind, he was gone, moving on to take Minho to his room next door. 
Katherine stood in the silence of her room, suddenly exhausted. The adrenaline that had carried her through Delegation had drained somewhere between Old Prosperity and here, leaving her hollow in places she didn’t know could be hollow.
She crossed to the window, looking out at the compound bathed in the fading light of day. From here, she could see the London Town House. Somewhere in that building, sixteen other Pieces were going through the same orientation. Learning the same rules. Preparing for the same challenges.
The thought should have made her anxious. Instead, it just made her tired.
Tomorrow, the real game would begin. Tomorrow, they’d stop playing chess and start playing for their lives.
But tonight? Tonight she just needed to breathe.
Katherine turned from the window and reached for her duffel, wondering which pieces of her former life the Sanctum had deemed important enough to pack.
#
Sleep refused to come.
Katherine had tried everything  –  counted backward from a thousand, regulated her breathing like they taught in combat meditation, even pressed her face into the pillow until the pressure made her bones ache. Nothing worked. Her mind kept spinning with fragments from the day: Sebastian’s broken sound when Zephyr’s name was called, Atlas’s face as he took his place on the board, the way her mother looked from across the hall  –  proud and terrified all at once.
Three hours of staring at the ceiling was her limit.
She slid from the bed, careful not to disturb the sheets too much  –  a habit from University inspections she couldn’t seem to shake. The Tournament-issued sleepwear felt foreign against her skin like everything else in this place.
Katherine crept to the door, hesitating with her hand over the sensor. Nico’s words echoed in her head: Every inch of this place is designed for maximum visibility – for the Sanctum and for the viewing audience.
She’d be recorded leaving her room. Recorded walking down the hallway. Recorded knocking on Atlas’s door at  –  she checked the soft glow of the clock  –  2:37 in the morning. Her bare feet made no sound on the floor as she moved through the darkened hallway. She could have found his door blindfolded if needed  –  third on the left, Queen’s Bishop. Her fingers hovered over the panel beside it, suddenly unsure. They weren’t friends anymore. Hadn’t been for years. What right did she have to wake him up just because her brain wouldn’t shut down?
Before she could decide, the door slid open.
Atlas stood in the doorway, silver eyes reflecting the dim light from the hallway. No surprise on his face. No confusion. Just calm acceptance, like he’d been expecting her to show up at some point.
“Nico lied,” he said quietly, stepping back to let her in. “They don’t record at night.”
Katherine blinked. “What? How do you – “
“I’ve been in Tournaments before,” he replied. “Not as a Piece, but security detail. They run on a skeleton crew after midnight. Too much data to process if they record everything.”
She stepped into his room, trying not to show her relief. It was identical to hers in layout but already showed signs of Atlas’s presence  –  tablet open on the desk, Tournament uniform hung up in separate pieces, bed made so tight you could bounce coins off it.
“Couldn’t sleep?” he asked, closing the door behind her.
Katherine shook her head. “Too quiet. Too loud. I don’t know.”
He nodded once, understanding without her having to explain. That was always their thing, even before  –  this silent language that ran beneath their words, saying all the things they couldn’t.
“Remember when I used to sneak into the common room at the University?” Katherine asked, suddenly needing to fill the silence. “After my nightmares?”
“You mean when you’d claim you were ‘studying’ at three in the morning?”
“I was.”
“With your eyes closed and drool on your textbook.”
Katherine felt herself smile despite everything. “I never drooled.”
“Twice.” Atlas held up two fingers. “Once during Tactical Theory finals, once after your mother’s promotion ceremony.”
She’d forgotten that  –  how he kept track of small things that most people wouldn’t notice. How he’d wake her up before anyone else found her there, sliding a cup of tea across the table without comment before sliding into a chair next to her and asking her about what she’d been reading. 
“You always knew,” she said softly. “When I couldn’t sleep.”
Atlas moved to the small table by the window, where a half-finished cup of something steamed. “You get this look,” he said, pushing the cup toward her. “Right between your eyebrows. Like you’re trying to solve a puzzle with missing pieces.”
Katherine touched the spot reflexively, then stopped herself. “I do not.”
“You’re doing it now.”
She rolled her eyes, but took the offered cup – tea, by the smell of it. Some herbal blend that reminded her of those late nights when she was fifteen and he was eighteen, both of them pretending they weren’t terrified of what came next.
“Your mom used to make this for you,” Atlas said as she took a sip.
“Valerian and lavender. You remember that?”
“Kind hard to forget how the great Medina takes her tea.”
Medina. Her middle name. The first one that is. The one her mother never used, and her father only pulled out on official documentation. Atlas was the only person who’d ever called her that. 
“That was a long time ago,” she said softly. “When you started calling me that.”
“You know why I started calling you Medina?” Atlas asked softly.
Katherine shook her head. She’d always assumed it was just to annoy her in the same way she and Reign called Sebastian by his middle name. 
“Your middle name means ‘city’ in one of the old languages,” he said. “I learned that during Cultural Studies, right before my first deployment.” His eyes stayed on hers. “Made me think about how Old Prosperity was my home – the city I’d always return to.”
Katherine felt heat rise in her cheeks. “That’s surprisingly poetic for a Frost.”
“Don’t tell anyone,” he said with the ghost of a smile. “I have a reputation.”
“But you didn’t,” she said quietly.
“Not for your eighteenth birthday. Not when I said I would.” He pulled a chair out for her at the table before taking his own across from her. “Spent three days arguing with Minho about it. He wouldn’t authorize the leave.”
Katherine blinked. “You never told me that.”
“Wouldn’t have changed anything.” Atlas shrugged one shoulder. “I still missed it.”
“Why was Minho even in charge of your leave requests?” Katherine asked. “You were both barely out of training.”
Atlas’s laugh was soft and humorless. “Fast-tracked graduation, remember? Minho had just turned twenty, I was twenty-one. They pushed us through Year Ten three months early, skipped our Apprenticeships completely.” His jaw tightened. “Said the Empire needed leaders more than it needed us to finish University. Really, they just needed bodies with rank on the Northern Border.”
“I remember my mom being pissed about it,” Katherine said. “She said it was reckless.”
“It was.” Atlas looked down at his hands. “Put Minho in charge of a unit he wasn’t ready for. Put me under his command when six months earlier we’d been equals.”
Atlas rarely talked about those early days after graduation. How he and Minho went from friends to superior and subordinate overnight. How the rank change had fractured something that never quite healed, even after Atlas had become Captain in his own right. 
“When I asked for leave for your birthday,” Atlas continued, “Minho saw it as me still thinking we were at the University. Like I didn’t respect the chain of command.” His voice softened. “I reminded him we’d been planning it for months. Told him I’d promised you we’d go to that bakery in the Eastern District – the one with those ridiculous sugar rolls you liked.”
Katherine remembered those plans. Remembered counting down the days, waiting for Atlas to come back and take her to the one place her mother never allowed because it was “a waste of a perfectly good carb allocation.”
“He said no.”
“Three times,” Atlas confirmed. “Said a first-level Frost attack meant all leaves were canceled. When I pointed out that four other officers still got approved that week, he – “ Atlas stopped, jaw working.
“He what?”
Atlas looked out the window, the faint red glow from London Town’s House catching in his eyes. “He said maybe if I cared as much about my duty as I did about ‘babysitting Michelle Nicholas’s daughter,’ I’d understand why the request was denied.”
Katherine sucked in a breath. “He really said that?”
“That and worse.” Atlas turned back to her. “I punched him.”
“You – what?”
“Broke his nose.” Atlas flexed his right hand like he could still feel the impact. “He demoted me on the spot. Two months probation. My father nearly killed me when he found out.”
Katherine stared at him, trying to process this new information. For years, she’d carried that missed birthday like a badge – proof that Atlas had forgotten her once he graduated and became important. She’d been so hurt, so convinced he’d moved on.
“Why didn’t you tell me?” she asked quietly.
“What was I supposed to say? ‘Sorry I missed your birthday, I was on probation for assaulting my superior officer because he insulted you’?” Atlas shook his head. “You had enough drama with your mother. Didn’t need mine too.”
“You and Minho...” she started.
“Got past it,” Atlas said, but his tone suggested otherwise. “Had to. We were trapped on that base for months. He apologized eventually after your mother demoted him and made me her First Mate because he ignored me for six more weeks.”
“Did he mean it?”
Atlas’s smile was sharp. “Minho never says anything he doesn’t mean. Whether he believes it is another question entirely.”
Katherine thought about the Delegation ceremony  –  how Minho had shut Sebastian down without even looking at him. The coldness in his voice. The absolute certainty that he would be obeyed.
“And now he’s your King,” she said. “Again.”
Something flashed in Atlas’s eyes  –  not quite anger, but close. “Not by choice. Not for either of us.”
Katherine knew he was right. She yawned suddenly, the tea doing its work faster than she expected. Atlas noticed and straightened up.
“You should get some sleep,” he said quietly. “First challenge is tomorrow.”
Katherine nodded but didn’t move right away. “I don’t want to kill you,” she blurted out, the words slipping past her exhaustion filter.
Atlas stilled, surprise flickering briefly across his face before settling back into careful neutrality. “That’s... good to know.”
“I’m serious.” She sighed. “Whatever happens tomorrow, or the next day, or whenever this nightmare really starts... I don’t want to be the one who takes you off the board.”
Something in his expression softened. “Is that what you’re worried about? That they’ll pit us against each other?”
“Isn’t it what you’re worried about?”
He didn’t answer right away. When he did, his voice was lower, almost gentle. “The Sanctum won’t put us against each other. Not at first. They need the drama to build. We’re more valuable to them as allies with a complicated history than as early casualties.”
“How can you be so sure?”
“Because that’s how I’d play it,” he said simply. “And they’re better at this game than I am.”
Katherine felt a strange mix of relief and dread at his assessment. Relief because she believed him  –  Atlas saw patterns where others saw chaos. Dread because he was already calculating their value as entertainment.
“You should go,” Atlas said after the silence had stretched too long. “While they’re still between camera shifts.”
Katherine nodded, rising from her seat. She moved toward the door, then paused to face him. “I meant what I said earlier. About having each other’s backs.”
“I know.” He was standing now too, silhouetted against the window. “As long as we can.”
It should have made her feel better, but as Katherine stepped back into the empty hallway, it felt like the closest thing to an alliance she was going to get.
# PART TWO: THE TOURNAMENT
## Chapter 14: Melena
They weren’t ready. 
Melena knew it the minute she walked into the breakfast hall and felt the silence engulf her. Sixteen Pieces, sitting at a table meant for thirty-two, spread out like they were afraid one of them might have something contagious. No one touched the food. No one moved. No one looked up when she entered. Plates of fresh fruit, warm bread, cooked meat, and protein packs arranged in perfect symmetry  –  untouched. Sixteen cups of steaming liquid  –  untouched. Cutlery laid out at angles that would make her grandmother proud  –  untouched.
Just sixteen bodies, wearing identical black and crimson, holding their breath.
Broken before we even begin. Melena cataloged the scene automatically - who looked ready to bolt, who was angry, who was already defeated. Ellis kept glancing at the door. Viv's hands were shaking slightly as she reached for her cup. Marcus hadn't even attempted to sit properly.
Caelum would want to know this. London Town's psychological state, their breaking points, how quickly they crumbled under pressure. Too bad knowing it wouldn't help her keep them alive.
The only one who moved was Raven, London Town’s handler, standing off to the side like a sentinel. Arms crossed. Spine straight. Eyes fixed somewhere above their heads. She’d worn her Tournament jacket today  –  midnight black, lined with medals and ribbons, proof she’d survived as a Piece at some point before she’d earned the right to guide others to their deaths. Raven didn’t acknowledge Melena’s entrance either, not that she was expecting her to. 
Raven made that clear after what happened last night. 
Melena could still hear the sound of it.
The cuffs locking into place with a metallic snap that echoed through the common area. The crack of electricity hitting the floor as the Dawner from their team – Olivia – dropped to her knees. Not from pain, though there had been plenty. Not from fear, though that had been there too.
From shame.
The kind that burned hotter than any fire Melena could conjure.
“You stand when Her Highness enters the room,” Raven’s voice cut through her thoughts. 
Chairs scraped against stone as fifteen bodies rose in unison. Still not looking at her. Still silent. But standing.
Protocol.
Rules.
That’s what had gotten them in trouble to begin with.
Information versus leadership. Maven had taught her to observe, to analyze, to extract intelligence and disappear. But she couldn't disappear from this. These people needed her to actually do something with what she saw, not just collect it and report back to someone else.
How do you lead people when you've been trained to use them?
Melena crossed to the head of the table, every step echoing in the hall. She hadn’t asked for this. Hadn’t asked to be their leader. Hadn’t asked for the crown or the throne or the weight that came with being a Lockwood.
But here she was. 
Nobody sat until she did.
Nobody spoke until she broke the silence.
“The food’s getting cold.”
It was a simple sentence, a simple observation, yet it stretched between each of them as if Melena had asked them to do something morally wrong. Tiran was the first one to move, reaching for a piece of bread from the plate closest to him as if nothing happened. As if they hadn’t spent the night watching Pieces be punished.
He met her gaze as he took a bite, his expression unreadable. He’d been different since Delegation. Still an asshole. Still too smart for his own good. But the edge was gone from his voice when he spoke to her. His usual mockery was replaced with something she couldn’t name. He’d actually checked on her when it was all said and done. Waited outside her room as Raven escorted her back, not saying anything until the handler was gone. Then just a simple question: “Are you alright?”
Like he cared. Like it mattered.
At least someone isn't falling apart. Tiran's composure was either genuine confidence or excellent acting. Either way, it was useful. If she could figure out how to channel that stability to the rest of the team...
Stop thinking like a handler. Start thinking like the Queen Piece. Start being a Lockwood damn it. 
One by one, the others followed his lead. Reaching for food. Pouring drinks. Pretending normalcy while the weight of last night pressed down on all of them.
They broke the rules. 
Imperial law. 
The Empress’s law. 
Her law. 
Not that it mattered when it was all said and done. 
Melena picked up her fork and knife, cutting into the meat in her own silence. She didn’t taste what she put in her mouth. Didn’t feel the warmth of the tea as she swallowed. All she felt was their resentment, their fear, their doubt.
And beneath it all, her own flames. Not the borrowed fire Tiran had given her days ago, but her own element  –  returned full force after the punishment, after the suppressor’s effects had worn off. It rolled beneath her skin, eager and angry, hungry for something to burn.
Last night, it had almost been Raven. 
They’d been in the common area. Everyone was either too wired to sleep or too cautious to really talk after Raven had laid out the rules to them. Just sixteen strangers in matching uniforms pretending they weren’t terrified of what tomorrow would bring.
That’s when she’d heard it  –  hushed voices from the far corner, urgent whispers that cut through the white noise of the room.
“Just try it,” a voice urged. “No one’s watching, and it’ll be out of your system before the challenge tomorrow.”
“We’re not supposed to – “
“Since when does London Town follow Old Prosperity’s rules?”
Melena hadn’t meant to eavesdrop. She was too busy trying to figure out which of her Pieces Tiran was going to try to con next when she felt the hair on the back of her neck stand up. She’d grown up in a palace full of secrets, of whispers that turned to scandals, of choices that cost more than the people making them realized.
By the time she’d turned, it was too late.
Olivia, one of their Pawns – an Elite with high-level shadow-travel capabilities  –  stood with her arms outstretched, frost crystallizing around her fingertips. Melena was too far away to tell her to stop, to warn her. She watched as Jarom, their King’s Knight slipped the ice to her from a vial in his pocket, watching in fascinated horror as she managed to manipulate the element. There was a name for the Elementalist who could control two elements – Dyad. Being a Dyad wasn’t illegal, but using an argumenter to become one was. 
Yet, they stood laughing and giggling where anyone – everyone – could see, including Raven, who’d materialized from the corner of the room as if she’d been waiting for someone to make a mistake. 
“Well,” Raven’s voice had been calm. Too calm for Melena’s liking. “What do we have here.”
The frost had instantly melted from Olivia’s hand, but the evidence was still there – the faint blue glow lingering on her palms, the joy sparkling in her eyes. 
“Reporting a breach at the London Town House,” she reported. “Two Pieces in violation of Code Sixteen. Awaiting instructions.”
The room had gone silent then. Every Piece suddenly very aware of what this meant. Code Sixten – use of an unassigned element from birth – carried one of the harshest penalties in the Tournament rulebook.
And they all knew why.
Eighty years ago, a London Town Piece had smuggled in augmenters for the entire team. It enhanced each one beyond their natural abilities. Six Old Prosperity Pieces died in the First Challenge, all slaughtered by opponents with artificially amplified powers they couldn’t match.
The ensuing scandal had nearly ended the Tournament altogether. It brought so much shame to London Town that there had no choice but to act. Six months after the Tournament, there was an execution and a new rule carved into Imperial law. 
No Piece shall enhance, augment, or artificially alter their elemental abilities by any means. The penalty for such actions shall be immediate removal from the Tournament and Imperial discipline.
A law signed by Empress Caliane Azira Hastings – Melena’s great-grandmother. 
And now I'm watching history repeat itself. Not augmenters this time, but the same fundamental problem - London Town Pieces too desperate, too unprepared, making stupid choices that would get them all killed.
Raven hadn’t waited for a response from the Sanctum. The spot on Melena’s back where the suppressor rod had hit her started to tingle as Raven produced a set of neutralizing cuffs and approached Olivia and Jarom like this was a buffet line and they were in the way of her meal. 
“Wait,” Melena had said, stepping forward before she could even think about what she was doing. “They’re just playing. It’s the first night. Everyone is getting to know each other.”
Raven’s gaze snapped to her. “You don’t make the rules here. I do.” 
Something in the way she said it pissed Melena off. 
“I think you’re missing something at the end of that sentence,” Tiran appeared beside her. “Begins with Your and ends with Highness.”
“She’s a Piece now,” Raven responded. “Be lucky I’m not calling her by her first name.”
Before Melena could say another word, Raven had moved. 
The cuffs snapped into place with a faint surge of power that spread across the room. Olivia didn’t have the time to scream before her knees buckled beneath her. Frost spread from the point of impact  –  the last remnants of the borrowed power leaving her system – as tears fell down Olivia’s face. 
Raven looked up at Melena from where she had Olivia pinned down. “If this is how your team handles one unstructured night, I suggest you pray to whatever gods are left before the first challenge begins.”
Jarom took a step toward her and found himself facing two Sanctum guards who’d entered the room, weapons drawn.
“By order of the Sanctum,” the taller guard announced, “Piece Jarom Fairchild and Piece Olivia Rembrandt are hereby placed on probation. Their participation in the first challenge is suspended. Their status will be reevaluated following completion of the event.”
Melena watched as they led her teammates away. Watched the shock on the others’ faces dissolve into the same look she’d seen on tours of the provinces.
Hatred. 
Maybe not for her directly, but definitely for the family she’d been born into and the ones that’d come before them. 
A knife scraped against porcelain, pulling Melena back to the present. Across the table, Ellis – their King’s Rook – had abandoned the pretense of eating and was now just moving food around his plate. His eyes kept drifting to the empty seats where Olivia and Jarom should have been.
“This is bullshit,” Ellis finally muttered, breaking the silence. “First challenge hasn’t even started and we’re already down two Pieces.”
“Better learn to whisper,” Tiran replied without looking up. “Or you’ll be the third.”
Ellis’s jaw tightened, but he said nothing more. Just stabbed his protein pack with unnecessary force.
Melena finished her tea, the liquid now cold against her tongue. She’d grown used to cold things. Cold stares from her grandmother. Cold silence from her brother. Cold judgment from everyone who thought she’d never measure up to the Lockwood name.
This, however, was different. This was her team. Her Pieces. And they couldn’t afford to be divided. Not today. Not when they’d face Old Prosperity for the first time in a matter of hours for the Welcome Feast.
“We will figure it out,” she said, loud enough for everyone to hear but soft enough that they had to listen. “I will figure it out.”
“How do you plan to do that exactly?” asked Viv, the Knight who would replace Jarom in today’s challenge. “We’re missing a Knight and a Pawn. The whole formation is compromised.”
Melena replied, meeting her gaze. “We’ve got options. That’s why the Sanctum requires backups for every position.”
“Options like what?” Ellis challenged. “Throwing the rest of us into the fire while you play it safe? That’s what Lockwoods do, isn’t it?”
He's not wrong. That is what Lockwoods had done - sent others to fight while staying safe in the palace. But she wasn't in the palace anymore. She was here, on this board, wearing the same uniform they were.
The spy would manipulate him. The Queen has to inspire him.
“You're right,” she said, surprising everyone including herself. “That is what Lockwoods have done. But I'm not sitting in the palace giving orders. I'm here. On this board. Same as you.” 
“Then what do you suggest, Your Highness?” The title was a mockery in Ellis’s mouth.
Before she could answer, Raven stepped forward, her boots clicking against the stone floor. She hadn’t touched food or drink, hadn’t sat down, hell, she hadn’t even looked at them for real. 
“Enough,” she said, the single word slicing through the tension. “Welcome Feast begins in three hours. If you’re not going to eat, go get dressed. You need to look your best tonight. You’re too busy fighting each other to realize Old Prosperity’s going to have their shit together.”
Melena didn’t bother arguing. 
Even if she was a cosmic ass, there was no denying that Raven was right. 
London Town was fucked. 
But Old Prosperity didn’t know that yet. 
## Chapter 15: Katherine 
“Chin up, Nicholas,” Nico hissed as he fussed with the shoulder of her gown  –  an actual gown. White with silver detailing that hugged her waist and flared at her hips, making her feel like she was playing dress-up in someone else’s body. “You’re representing Old Prosperity.”
Katherine resisted the urge to tell him exactly where he could shove Old Prosperity’s reputation. The last time she’d worn a dress before the Tournament had come back around was at her mother’s promotion ceremony five years ago. Before that? Never. Not even at University graduations, where cadets marched in lines wearing uniforms so starched they could probably deflect bullets.
But here she was, stuffed into silk and satin like some kind of porcelain doll, about to be paraded in front of cameras and officials and whatever poor souls had paid enough to watch sixteen people pretend they weren’t scared shitless.
“I still don’t understand why we can’t wear our Tournament uniforms,” Reign muttered beside her, tugging at the silver beading on her own gown. “We’re going to fight in them. Might as well eat in them. Stretch them out some.”
“Because,” Nico said with the exasperated tone of someone who’d explained this five times already, “Old Prosperity has an advantage London Town never will. Unity. Discipline. Order. But those royals have flash; tonight, we need to match it.”
Katherine caught Sebastian’s eye over Nico’s shoulder. He looked about as comfortable in his formal wear as she felt, which was to say not at all. The black tux was too tight across his shoulders, the collar too high against his neck. Only Atlas, standing near the entrance to the Great Hall, seemed unbothered by the transformation from soldier to doll.
“You getting all this, Captain?” Katherine called, just loud enough for Atlas to hear. “Unity and discipline. Flash and class. I’m sure you’re taking notes.”
Atlas didn’t smile  –  not quite  –  but his expression softened for a split second. “I’ve heard it before.”
Knowing him, he probably attended a hundred formal events just like this one. Had likely spent his childhood learning which fork to use and how to smile without showing his teeth, all while mastering combat maneuvers and frost techniques and managing to look damn good at it all. 
“Remember,” Nico said, addressing the group one final time, “London Town’s Pieces will be looking for weakness. Don’t give them any. Don’t stare. Don’t fidget. Don’t engage unless directly spoken to by a Sanctum official.”
“And if they engage us?” Houston asked from the back of the group.
Nico’s smile was sharp. “Then you show them why Old Prosperity has the highest Tournament win record in history.”
The doors to the Great Hall swung open with a dramatic flourish, revealing the space beyond. Katherine had expected something like the University’s mess hall  –  larger, maybe, with fancier decorations. What she got instead was pure theater.
Soaring ceilings disappeared into shadow above them. Crystal chandeliers hung like frozen rain, catching and scattering light in patterns too deliberate to be natural. Long tables draped in white and black linens faced each other across a middle aisle, set with gold and silver place settings that were probably hand-crafted every year for the competing provinces.
And at the far end of the hall, on raised marble thrones that looked like they’d been carved from a single block of stone, sat the Sanctum officials  –  sixteen figures in ceremonial robes, faces obscured behind those shimmery veils that moved like smoke.
Watching. Waiting. Smiling beneath masks no one was allowed to see through.
The whole setup smelled like smoke and boot polish and fresh lilies  –  like something meant to be admired right before it was broken.
Because that’s what this was, wasn’t it? A fancier version of the fights they used to stage in Year Nine, where they’d put two cadets in a ring and make bets on which one would tap out first.
Only this time, there was no tapping out.
“Places,” Nico murmured, and they moved forward as one, crossing the threshold into the hall with steps that had been rehearsed until Katherine could practically do them in her sleep.
On the far side of the room, London Town’s Pieces were already seated  –  black and crimson like a mirror universe of Old Prosperity’s white and silver. Katherine spotted Melena Lockwood immediately, sitting at the center of the table, back straight, chin lifted, looking every inch the royal she was born to be.
She had seen pictures of her before. Hard not to when the Imperial Princess’s face was plastered across nearly every news feed in the Empire. But pictures didn’t capture the reality of her  –  the way she seemed to fill space without trying, the way her eyes caught the light like they were storing it for later.
The way her fire felt.
That was the strangest part. 
Katherine shouldn’t have been able to feel it at all. Elements didn’t typically radiate unless actively used, and even then, only those with the same element usually noticed. A Frost sensing a Flamer’s element? Not normal. Not standard. Not in any of the textbooks Professor Aloysius had made them memorize.
But there it was  –  a hum under Katherine’s skin like a tuning fork that had found its resonant frequency. Not unpleasant. Just... there. It reminded her of a warm summer day, comfortable enough to still go about your regular duties but annoying if you hadn’t been properly dressed. Something about the heat lingered where she normally felt her own element forming. 
Except it wasn’t trying to burn her. 
It was almost … as if … it were saying hello. 
“You’re staring,” Atlas murmured from her left side. Minho had taken his seat to the right of her as their Pawns filled the eight seats in front of them. 
“I’m not,” Katherine lied, reaching for her water glass to give her hands something to do. “Just sizing up the competition.”
“Uh-huh.” Atlas didn’t sound convinced, but he didn’t press the issue. Instead, he followed her gaze across the table. “Think she’s naturally that scary or she’s been trained to be?”
“Both,” Katherine replied without thinking, then frowned at herself. How would she know that?
Melena chose that moment to look up, her amber eyes locking directly with Katherine’s. Katherine held the stare, refusing to be the first to look away. From this close, she could see the faint tracing of scars along Melena’s forearms  –  training marks, no doubt about it. Beneath all that royal pomp and circumstance was someone who had fought and bled and gotten back up again.
There was something else, too. Something in the way Melena held herself as if she contained something that wanted to be unleashed. Her fire, maybe. Katherine had heard the rumors about the Princess’s control issues, about training dummies turned to ash and practice rooms that had to be rebuilt.
Yet this didn’t feel volatile. It felt … purposeful. As if Melena had found the off switch everyone said she was missing.
The humming under Katherine’s skin intensified, and for a split second, the strangest thought crossed her mind: I know you.
