Which was ridiculous. She’d never met Melena Lockwood in her life. Had never been anywhere near the Imperial Palace. Had never gone to any of the fancy diplomatic functions where royals and military officers might cross paths.
And yet...
Surprise filled Melena’s eyes as if, just for a moment, the same impossible thought had crossed her mind.
Then one of the Sanctum officials tapped their glass, drawing everyone’s attention to the head of the room, and the moment was gone. Melena looked away. The humming faded. And Katherine was left with a nagging sensation that wouldn’t disappear. 
“You okay?” Minho asked from her right. “I’m fine,” Katherine replied, though she wasn’t sure that was true. “Just... getting weird vibes, I guess.”
She expected Minho to laugh it off, to make some joke about nerves or pre-battle jitters. Instead, he frowned, eyes flicking briefly to Melena before returning to Katherine.
“What kind of weird?”
Katherine forced herself to look away, unsettled by the entire exchange. 
Something wasn’t right.
She could feel it in her bones, in the frost that stirred restlessly beneath her fingertips, in the way her instincts screamed for caution even in this carefully controlled environment. But before she could examine the feeling further, the Sanctum Leader rose from his throne, arms spread wide in a gesture that commanded immediate silence.
“Pieces of London Town and Old Prosperity,” he began, voice resonating throughout the hall without needing a microphone. “Welcome to the Tournament Houses. For centuries, the provinces of our great Empire have settled their differences not through war, but through strategy. Not through chaos, but through chess.” His hands spread wider, with each word he spoke. “You sixteen represent the pride and power of your provinces. Your victories will be celebrated. Your defeats will be honored.”
Katherine resisted the urge to exhale deeply. They all knew what “honored” meant in Tournament speak. It meant closed-casket funerals. It meant names etched on memorial walls. It meant becoming a cautionary tale for next year’s Pieces.
“At the end of the Welcome Feast,” the Sanctum Leader said, “you will face your first challenge. Normally, I would announce the parameters myself. But this year, we are honored to have London Town’s handler present the First Challenge. Raven Morales, please step forward.”
From the London Town side of the room, a figure rose  –  tall, lean, dressed in a Tournament handler’s formal uniform. Katherine studied her carefully. She’d never met Raven before, but everyone in Old Prosperity knew who she was. One of the Sanctum’s most efficient operatives. The kind of handler who got results without excuses. Rumor had it she’d never lost a Piece in the first three challenges of any Tournament she oversaw.
Now she stood at the center of the Great Hall, her presence somehow more commanding than anyone would expect from a mere handler.
“Thank you, Master Durai,” she nodded toward the Leader. “Pieces of London Town and Old Prosperity, the Tournament is not about glory. It is about strategy. It is about facing impossible odds and finding a way through.”
She turned slowly, her gaze touching each table before continuing. “At the end of the Welcome Feast, you will face the First Challenge: War Simulation. The parameters of the challenge are simple. The challenge arena’s climate will change every hour. Your command centers will be hidden and your Pieces will be scattered at random.”
Katherine’s mind raced, already mapping out strategies, contingencies, and ways to use the shifting climate to Old Prosperity’s advantage, but the next words out of Raven’s mouth cut her train of thought off completely. 
 “No killing will be allowed.”
Katherine blinked. No killing? In a War Simulation? She glanced around the table, noting similar confusion on her teammates’ faces. Even Minho seemed caught off guard.
“Instead,” Raven explained, “victory will be determined by capture and hold. Each team must secure their opponents’ command center while protecting their own. Pieces taken off the field will be retrieved and returned to their House but will be ineligible to participate in the Second Challenge.”
The rules continued  –  time limits, scoring systems, prohibited techniques  –  but Katherine found her attention drifting. Something wasn’t adding up. The Tournament was designed to weed out weaker Pieces early, to force teams to adapt to losses. A no-kill first round meant all sixteen Pieces would survive at least one day, regardless of performance.
Katherine didn’t like those odds. 
Not at all. 
Movement from the London Town table caught her eye again. Melena was leaning slightly forward, her focus entirely on Raven, but Katherine noticed there was a tension, a wariness, that suggested she was having the same doubts. The humming under Katherine’s skin returned, softer this time but still there. 
“The challenge will begin at dawn,” Raven concluded. “I suggest you use tonight to prepare your minds as well as your bodies. You will never feel a moment this peaceful again.”
With that cryptic statement, she returned to her seat, and the Sanctum Leader rose once more.
“Now,” he said, his tone shifting to something almost jovial, “let us enjoy the Feast. After all, even soldiers must eat before battle.”
Servants appeared as if summoned by his words, bearing platters of food that steamed in the cool air of the hall. The formal portion of the evening was apparently over, giving way to the actual “feast” part of the Welcome Feast.
Katherine stared down at her plate as it was filled with delicacies she couldn’t even name  –  meats in sauces that smelled like spices she’d never encountered, vegetables arranged in patterns too perfect to disturb, bread that looked like it had been sculpted rather than baked.
“No killing,” Beckett, the Pawn directly in front of her murmured. “That’s new.”
“Maybe they’re finally growing a conscience,” Minho suggested, though his tone made it clear he didn’t believe that for a second. “Finally realizing death in the First Challenge doesn’t make for a good show.”
Katherine looked back toward the Sanctum thrones, at the officials who were now chatting and eating as if they hadn’t just announced a fundamental change to a centuries-old tradition.
That’s when she saw it.
At first, Katherine thought it was just a trick of the light  –  the way the shadows played against the marble wall behind the Sanctum thrones. But as she looked closer, focusing beyond the veiled officials to the stone itself, she realized what she was seeing.
Sigils.
Ancient symbols carved into the wall, so faint they were nearly invisible unless you knew exactly where to look. Curved lines, sharp angles, patterns that made her eyes want to slide away even as they drew her in. They seemed to shift slightly as she watched, though the wall itself remained perfectly still.
She’d seen sigils before, of course. The University was full of them  –  protection wards on training rooms, enhancement marks on combat equipment, privacy seals on instructors’ offices. Standard Remnian magic, basic stuff they taught in Year Seven Elemental Theory classes.
But these were different.
These were older. Raw. Almost alive in a way that made the hair on her arms stand up.
And one of them  –  a spiral pattern with jagged lines cutting through its center  –  was pulsing with a faint red glow. The pulse matched the cadence of the voices in the hall. growing brighter with each word spoken, then dimming as they all fell silent.
Katherine frowned. 
Sigils didn’t just... activate on their own. They needed to be triggered by an elemental match  –  Frost to frost-sigil, Fire to fire-sigil. They definitely didn’t pulse in rhythm with someone’s voice unless that person was actively channeling magic into them.
But no one wasn’t channeling anything. Everyone was just talking.
The part that scared Katherine more than the sigil was the fact that she could see it. 
Sight was an Elite ability – not a Frost one. She could create ice, manipulate cold, cause a thunderstorm if she was too angry; hell, she could even heal minor wounds. But sensing magic? Seeing sigils pulse with power?
That wasn’t in her skill set.
Katherine blinked hard, thinking maybe she was imagining things. Stress could do that  –  make you see patterns where none existed, find meaning in random shapes. But when she looked again, the sigil was still there. Still pulsing. Still wrong in a way she couldn’t quite define.
She knew that mark. Had seen it somewhere before. Not in a textbook or a lecture, but somewhere more intimate. More personal.
In her dreams.
Katherine almost gasped. She hadn’t dreamt of those symbols since she was a child. They always featured that exact sigil, though she’d never been able to remember the details clearly once morning came. Now, seeing it in front of her, the recognition was undeniable. This was the symbol from her dreams. The one that always appeared right before she woke up screaming, frost coating her sheets, the taste of ash in her mouth.
“What are you looking at?” Dax had leaned back and somehow slid closer to her. 
“Nothing,” she turned to face him. “Just zoned out.
“Huh,” was all he said, but how he said it quickened Katherine’s pulse.
He could see it too.
Before she could ask him about it, Zephyr stood up abruptly, his chair scraping against the marble floor loud enough to draw stares. His face had gone pale, his eyes fixed on the exact same spot Katherine had been examining.
On the sigil.
Their gazes met across the table, and in that instant, Katherine knew with absolute certainty that Zephyr could see it too. He took a step backward, knocking into a server carrying a tray of desserts. The crash of silver against marble broke the spell, drawing all eyes to the minor commotion instead of the wall with its pulsing sigil.
All eyes, except Katherine’s and Zephyr’s, which remained locked on each other.
He knows, she thought. He knows what it is.
Zephyr started moving toward her, weaving between chairs and servers with a determination that suggested he wasn’t concerned about what the cameras were catching in that moment. Sebastian was on his feet before Katherine could process what was happening, intercepting his younger brother with a casualness that didn’t match the tension in his shoulders.
“Whoa there, little brother,” he said, loud enough for those nearby to hear. “Not at the fancy dinner, okay? We’ve got strategy meetings for whatever this is.”
Zephyr said something in response – something urgent, from the look on his face – but his voice was too low for Katherine to catch whatever it was made Sebastian’s smile falter for a split second before he doubled down on the performance.
“Yeah, yeah, you can tell me all about it later,” Sebastian said, steering Zephyr back toward his seat with an arm around his shoulders. “Now’s not the time for family drama with our dear older brothers.”
As they moved away, Zephyr looked back over his shoulder at Katherine, his expression so intense it was almost physical. His lips moved  –  not to Sebastian, but to her.
Three words. 
Simple enough to read even without hearing them.
Don’t trust them.
## Chapter 16: Katherine – The First Challenge
One second she was in the transport chamber.
The next – 
Fuck.
Katherine’s boots sank past her ankles, mud sucking them down like it was trying to swallow her whole. The air was so thick she could barely pull it into her lungs. Sweat broke instantly across her skin, mixing with the water dripping from leaves overhead.
Jungle. 
They’d dropped them in a damn jungle.
She twisted, trying to get her bearings, but there was nothing but green in every direction. No teammates. No landmarks. Just vines and trees and the distant sound of something that definitely wasn’t human.
“Atlas?” she called out, then immediately regretted it. Stupid. Giving away her position like some first-year cadet who’d never seen combat. She clamped her mouth shut, listening hard for any response. Nothing but the buzz of insects and the steady drip of water.
She was alone.
Her com-band was dead  –  screen black, no signal. Probably jammed by the Sanctum to make this even more “challenging.” She tapped it anyway, like that would magically fix it. It didn’t.
“Tactical dispersal,” Nico had called it during briefing.
This was sabotage, plain and simple. Throwing them across the map like confetti, forcing them to waste time finding each other instead of locating the command center. It was a novice move – something Katherine hadn’t dealt with since Year Five battle composition games. 
She lifted her hand, trying to call frost to her fingertips. She needed a compass, something to at least give her north. The element responded, but it was wrong; sluggish and weak like it was fighting through maple syrup to reach her skin. A pathetic crystal formed, barely the size of her nail, then melted almost instantly.
“Come on,” she hissed, trying again. Focus harder. Push past the humidity. But it was no use. Her element was drowning in this place, smothered by heat and moisture.
Great. Just great. Alone, lost, and half-crippled in enemy territory.
The mud made a wet sucking sound with every step, like the ground itself was mocking her. She gritted her teeth and pushed forward. Standing still was suicide. She needed high ground. Needed teammates. Needed a plan that wasn’t just “try not to die.” Sweat ran down her back, between her breasts, into her eyes. Her uniform  –  pristine white when she’d entered the transport  –  was already stained dark green and brown. She didn’t wipe it away. Better camouflage.
Something skittered across a branch above her. She froze, tracking the movement. Not human. Not an immediate threat. But a reminder that she wasn’t just fighting London Town here  –  she was fighting the damn arena itself.
The first hour was nothing but sweat, silence, and the kind of curses even her mother would raise an eyebrow at her. She was careful not to make a sound beyond her breathing, which was loud enough in this thick air. Every step was mentally noted. Every move she made was because she absolutely had to. She stuck to the shadows where she could, using the dense foliage as cover while she searched for any sign of the rest of her Pieces. 
She didn’t think about Atlas again. Didn’t think about Sebastian or even Zephyr. Didn’t think about where they might be or what might’ve happened to them. She couldn’t afford that distraction. Not now. Not when her own survival was hanging by a thread in this green hell.
Something moved ahead  –  a flash of white against the endless green. She dropped instantly, pressing her body flat against the mud. Her hand went to her hip where her ice dagger would normally form, but all she got was that same pathetic response from her frost.
“Shit,” she breathed, barely a whisper.
If that was London Town, she was screwed. In this climate, with her element compromised, she couldn’t take on a Flamer or a Terrain. Not alone. Not and win.
The movement came again. Closer this time. 
She lay perfectly still, years of training kicking in. Control her breathing. Slow her heart rate. Become part of the forest floor. Wait for them to pass.
They didn’t pass.
Instead, the jungle floor itself began to shift, vines weaving together in complex patterns that spread toward her position with disturbing intelligence. It wasn’t random, nor was it natural. 
A Terrain was manipulating the environment.
The question was which sides?
Katherine had seconds to decide  –  stay hidden and risk being caught in whatever they were creating, or reveal herself and potentially face an enemy she couldn’t defeat.
Her muscles tensed, ready to spring into action. Ready to fight even if her frost was useless. Ready to run if she had to. The vines stopped just short of her position, forming a perimeter around her. Vines moved around until finally they spelled out a message. I see you.
“Identify yourself,” she called out.
The jungle shifted, and a figure rose from the undergrowth not ten feet from her. For a split second, she thought it was the forest itself taking human form  –  then she recognized the eyes staring back at her from beneath the mud and leaves.
Nova Chen.
One of her Pawns.
Relief hit her so hard it almost made her dizzy. Or maybe that was just the humidity.
“Nicholas,” Nova whispered, barely audible over the constant drip of the jungle. “You’re loud enough to draw half of London Town to our position.”
Katherine pushed herself up from the mud, not bothering to wipe off the mess caking her uniform. “You could have announced yourself instead of playing jungle ghost.”
“And risk alerting anyone else in the area?” Nova shook her head, eyes constantly scanning their surroundings as she spoke. “I’ve been tracking you for twenty minutes. Thought you were one of them at first.”
Katherine glanced down at her mud-covered self. “How could you mistake this stunning white uniform for theirs?”
“They’re out there. Three of them, maybe half a mile east. Moving together.” Her eyes flicked to Katherine’s hands. “Your frost isn’t working right, is it?”
Katherine tried again, focusing hard, but the crystal that formed was barely bigger than a raindrop before it melted. “The humidity’s too high. Too much ambient heat.”
“That’s the point.” Nova gestured to the dense foliage. “This hour is designed to handicap Frosts and favor Terrains. When it shifts, it’ll probably swing the other way.”
“You seem to be managing just fine.”
She didn’t respond directly, just nodded toward the ground where the vines continued their intricate dance, weaving a pattern that extended outward from where she stood. A personal alarm system, Katherine realized. Anyone steps on those, she’d know exactly where they were.
“I’m in my element. Literally,” Nova finally said. “But I can’t fight them alone.”
It wasn’t a complaint. It was a tactical assessment. The kind of thing they drilled into them at the University  –  know your strengths, acknowledge your weaknesses, adapt accordingly.
“Where are the others?” Katherine asked.
“Don’t know about most. Found Reign about an hour ago, but we got separated during a London Town patrol sweep.” Something in Nova’s expression tightened briefly. “She should be able to handle herself, though.”
Katherine nodded, knowing she was right. Reign was nothing if not resourceful. If any of them could survive this environment alone, it was her.
“Command center?”
Nova shook her head. “Nothing concrete. But there’s a clearing about two miles north where the vegetation patterns change. Too deliberate to be natural. Might be worth checking.”
Katherine weighed their options. Moving together meant easier detection but better odds if they encountered enemy Pieces. Splitting up meant covering more ground but higher risk for each of them. With her frost compromised and Nova’s element thriving, the choice was obvious.
“We move together,” she decided. “Find as many of our team as we can, then locate the command center.”
Nova didn’t argue, just nodded once and began dismantling her vine network with quick, efficient movements. Within seconds, the jungle floor looked undisturbed, as if she’d never been there at all.
“I’ll take point,” she said. “You cover our rear. And try to keep your frost under control. Last thing we need is a glowing trail every time you get jumpy.”
Katherine wanted to argue that she didn’t get “jumpy,” but bit her tongue. Nova was right. So, instead, she nodded before Nova started to lead the way.
The jungle swallowed them both, and Katherine followed Nova’s lead, stepping exactly where she stepped, moving exactly how she moved. It was almost like a dance  –  if dances involved mud up to your knees and the constant fear that something was about to kill you.
She didn’t let herself wonder who was already down. Wondering didn’t save anyone.
Focus. Breathe. Move.
They’d find them. They had to.
By midday  –  or what Katherine thought was midday, since the jungle canopy blocked any real view of the sun  –  they’d found Reign and three of the Pawns. Reign was exactly where Nova predicted, holding position against a massive fallen tree that she’d used as natural cover. She didn’t look surprised to see them  –  just nodded like they were right on schedule and fell into step without a word.
Two of the Pawns  –  Griffin and Tegan  –  had found each other early on and stuck together, which was smart given their complementary elements; Griffin being an Elite and Tegan a Terrain. The third, a quiet Elite named Blair, practically stumbled into their path while being pursued by what he described as “something big with too many legs.”
Six total. Almost half of their team. Still no command center. Still no Atlas. 
Katherine pushed that last thought away every time it surfaced. Couldn’t think about him right now. Couldn’t wonder if he was hurt or captured or worse. Focus on what was in front of her. On the mission. On survival.
“We need to move faster,” she said, surveying their small group as they paused to rehydrate. “At this rate, we won’t find the command center before dark.”
Reign wiped sweat from her forehead with the back of her hand. “If we move any faster, we’ll leave a trail even a blind Pawn could follow. Besides,” she nodded toward Tegan and Griffin, both slumped against a fallen tree trunk, “these two are about to drop from heat exhaustion.”
“We can’t afford to slow down,” Katherine insisted. “The longer we stay here, the more ground they’ll gain.”
Blair cleared his throat nervously. “But the briefing said terrain shifts every hour. Shouldn’t we have experienced at least one shift by now?”
Katherine frowned. He was right. By her estimate, they’d been in the Challenge for at least three hours. The terrain should have shifted multiple times according to the parameters Raven had announced.
Unless they’d been lied to. 
Again.
“Maybe they changed the rules,” Tegan suggested, voicing what they were all thinking.
“Or maybe they’re waiting for something specific to happen before triggering the shift,” Nova said, her hands still working methodically through the vines at her feet, extending her sensory network. “Some kind of threshold we need to cross.”
“Like finding the command center?” Griffin asked.
Katherine shook her head. “No, they wouldn’t make it that obvious. It would have to be something – “
She never finished the sentence.
The world tore apart beneath their feet.
No warning. No mercy. 
One second, jungle. The next, fire.
The transformation was so sudden and complete that Katherine’s brain couldn’t process it at first. The humid air evaporated instantly, replaced by searing heat that stole the breath from her lungs. The dense foliage disintegrated and burned away as if it had never existed. The ground beneath them hardened, cracked, and split open to reveal molten rock glowing red-hot in jagged fissures.
Volcanic wasteland ripped across the map like a fever dream.
“Move!” Katherine shouted, grabbing Tegan by the arm as the ground where they’d been standing moments before collapsed into a pool of bubbling lava. “High ground! Now!”
The six of them scattered, instinct overriding any strategic coordination. The air went sharp and dry, searing their throats with each breath. Katherine’s sweat turned to steam against her skin, the sudden change in humidity making her dizzy.
But something else happened too.
Her frost responded.
In the jungle, the humidity had smothered her element, made it weak and unreliable. Here, in the dry heat of the volcanic field, her frost crystallized instantly at her command, stronger and sharper than before. The contrast was so dramatic that she just stared at the ice forming along her arms for a moment, marveling at its return.
Then reality snapped back into focus.
Welcome to hell. 
Welcome to London Town’s advantage.
This terrain was designed for Flamers  –  giving them endless fuel, endless heat, and endless power. But it also stripped away the humidity that had been hampering her frost. A double-edged sword, perfectly calculated to force both sides to adapt.
“Regroup!” she called out. “Form up on that ridge!”
She pointed to a flat, elevated area about fifty yards away that seemed stable enough to support them all. Nova was already there, having used her element to propel herself to safety before the ground fully transformed. The others were making their way across the treacherous terrain, leaping from one solid rock to another as the ground between them bubbled with molten lava.
Katherine was halfway through cursing the Sanctum and all their sadistic terrain designers when she spotted him  –  Houston, one of their Rooks, trapped on a sinking rock platform, lava rising on both sides. Two London Town Flamers closed in fast from the opposite ridge, their black and crimson uniforms stark against the orange glow of the volcanic field.
Would you kill to save a life?
The question hammered in her head, sudden and unwelcome. The same one that had haunted her during practice, during the PAE, during every combat scenario they’d thrown at her. The same one that made her hesitate every time.
This wasn’t practice. This wasn’t a simulation. This was real, and Houston was going to die if she didn’t move.
Think. Breathe. Run.
Katherine pushed every ounce of power she had into her veins, forcing the ice through her fingertips, sculpting a bridge across the molten rock. The frost hissed and steamed as it made contact with the scorching air, but it held  –  barely, just strong enough to support her weight if she moved quickly.
Her hands shook like her body knew what her pride refused to admit – she was running out of power. Her knees damn near buckled from the strain. But she kept going, one step at a time, the ice bridge extending ahead of her with each movement.
“Jump!” she shouted to Houston as she neared his sinking platform. “Now!”
He hesitated – just for a second – then leapt across the gap toward her. Katherine grabbed his arm, yanking him onto the ice bridge just as his platform disappeared beneath the lava with a sickening hiss.
And then they were there. The London Town Flamers. Closer now. Close enough that she could see their faces  –  confusion, surprise, worry. Not enemies from Nico’s briefing. Real people. Teenagers trying to survive.
Would you kill to save a life?
The question twisted differently now because technically. she could. The rules said no killing, but accidents happened in the Tournament all the time. One unstable section of ice bridge. One wrong step. The lava below would do the rest. No one would question it  –  not with the terrain so volatile. Not with the risk she was taking to save her teammate.
The thought sickened her even as it crossed her mind.
She kept building the bridge, extending it not just toward safety but creating a parallel path  –  away from the lava pool, toward a stable ridge where the Flamers could retreat. She didn’t look at Houston’s face; she didn’t want thanks. Didn’t want judgment for saving people who’d been trying to trap them a few moments earlier. She just wanted to get off the damn bridge before the lava finished whatever the rules didn’t. 
Together, they raced back across her rapidly melting creation, the heat from below threatening to collapse it at any moment. Behind them, the London Town Flamers hesitated before taking the path she’d offered, scrambling away from the spreading lava.
The bridge was deteriorating faster now, the strain of maintaining multiple paths draining Katherine’s strength like nothing she’d experienced before. The bridge cracked under their feet, steam swallowing the space ahead in waves of heat and white.
Five more steps. 
Four. 
Three.
A fireball launched over their heads, close enough that Katherine felt her hair singe. She didn’t look back. Couldn’t afford to. Was it an attack? A warning? A distraction to cover their retreat? It didn’t matter. Whatever it was, she was just glad that it hadn’t collided with her face. 
Two steps. 
One.
They collapsed onto solid ground with the kind of exhaustion you can’t train for  –  the bone-deep fatigue that comes from pushing your element beyond its natural limits. Katherine’s breath came in ragged gasps, her chest burning with each inhale of the scorching air.
Houston was saying something  –  probably thanks, maybe a demand to know why she’d helped the enemy  –  but Katherine wasn’t listening.
Because she’d seen something on the far ridge. Something that made her blood run cold despite the overwhelming heat.
Crimson hair. A flicker of flame. 
Melena Lockwood.
Their eyes locked for one second across the volcanic wasteland  –  Queen to Queen, frost to fire, Old Prosperity to London Town. Katherine expected to see mockery in that gaze. Triumph. The smug satisfaction of a royal watching her subjects struggle.
She saw none of those things.
Melena simply nodded. 
And then she was gone – swallowed by the smoke like she was never there.
It wasn’t respect. Not exactly.
But it wasn’t hatred either.
And in the Tournament, that might be the closest thing to a thank you that Katherine was going to get.
## Chapter 17: Melena – The First Challenge
Something bit her.
Again.
“Fucking hell,” Melena hissed, slapping at her neck where another insect had decided she’d make a perfect breakfast. The thing smeared between her fingers, leaving a streak of blood – hers, not its – across her palm. She wiped it on her uniform, adding to the collection of stains that had transformed the pristine black and crimson into something unrecognizable in the three hours since she’d landed in this green nightmare.
Sweat ran down her back in streams, plastering her shirt to her skin. Her hair – normally perfectly styled by palace attendants – hung in damp curls around her face, frizzing in the oppressive humidity. She’d given up trying to fix it after the first hour.
This wasn’t like the curated gardens back at the palace, where exotic plants were trimmed and contained for aesthetic pleasure. This wasn’t like the nature preserves she’d visited on diplomatic trips to the Southern Provinces, viewing jungle life from the safety of climate-controlled transport vehicles.
This was wild. Untamed. Hostile.
And she was completely, utterly unprepared for it.
Nine months. Nine months away from court, away from her element, away from everything familiar. Maven had taught her to blend into crowds and extract secrets from drunk merchants, not survive hostile terrain. She'd spent more time learning to pick locks than learning to actually use her fire for anything beyond lighting candles and impressing diplomats.
What use is knowing how to forge documents when you're being hunted in a jungle?
Melena tried again to summon her fire, desperate for even the smallest flame to burn away the underbrush blocking her path. A pathetic spark flickered at her fingertips before dying out with a hiss.
“Come on,” she muttered, focusing harder. The fire responded, but weakly  –  more smoke than flame, barely enough to singe a leaf, let alone clear a path. The humidity was smothering her element, choking it before it could fully form.
The fire felt foreign in her hands, like trying to write with her non-dominant hand. Too long. Too long since she'd used it for anything real. In Shadowmere, flames drew attention - the kind of attention that got spies killed. So she'd learned to suppress it, to ignore it, to pretend it wasn't there.
Now I need it and it barely responds.
If her grandmother could see her now – the future of the imperial bloodline, stumbling through mud, swatting at insects, unable to even manage a proper flame – she’d probably accelerate the plans to replace Melena with a more suitable heir.
A crack echoed somewhere to her left. Something large, moving through the undergrowth with purpose. Melena froze, every muscle tensing. Friend or foe? Human or... whatever else lived in this place?
She didn’t know. Had no way of knowing. She’d been dropped here alone, com-band dead, element compromised, with only the vaguest instructions to “find the command center” and “secure the objective.” As if this were a routine drill, instead of thirty-two “young adults” scattered across hostile territory, half of them actively trying to take out the other half. Melena had rolled her eyes when Raven said they weren’t kids. Had she ever met anyone between the ages of 18 and 25 before?
“Who’s there?” she demanded, trying to make her voice sound authoritative despite the fear crawling up her spine. “Identify yourself!”
No response. Just more rustling, getting closer.
Melena backed up, her heel catching on an exposed root. She stumbled, arms pinwheeling, and fell hard onto her ass in the mud with a splash.
Perfect. Just fucking perfect.
Something erupted from the underbrush  –  a flash of black and crimson that her panicked brain registered as friendly a split second before she saw the face.
“Your Highness,” Tiran drawled, not even trying to hide his amusement at finding her sprawled in the mud. “Embracing nature, I see.”
Melena scrambled to her feet, dignity shredded beyond repair. “Where the hell have you been? I’ve been wandering this place for hours!”
“Surviving,” he replied simply, leaning against a tree trunk. “Unlike some people, I didn’t grow up with servants wiping my ass. I actually know how to navigate without GPS. My father made sure of it.”
“I don’t have servants wipe my –” she cut herself off. “That’s not the point. We need to find the others and locate the command center.”
Tiran raised an eyebrow. “Already found Ellis. He’s setting up a temporary base by a water source about half a mile back.” He glanced at her mud-caked uniform. “Figured I’d scout ahead, see if I could find our illustrious leader before she got herself eaten by whatever made that sound I heard ten minutes ago.”
“What sound?” Melena asked, immediately glancing around.
Tiran grinned. “Exactly.”
She wanted to punch him. Wanted to set his smug face on fire. But her element was useless in this humidity, and physical combat had never been her strong suit  –  especially against someone who clearly knew how to handle himself in this environment.
“Are you just going to stand there looking superior or are you going to take me to Ellis?” she snapped.
“Oh, I’m absolutely going to do both,” Tiran replied, pushing off from the tree. “Try to keep up, Your Highness. And watch your step. The mud gets deeper over here.”
As if to emphasize his point, Melena’s next step sank her boot ankle-deep in muck that made a disgusting squelching sound when she pulled it free.
“I hate this place,” she muttered, shoving a hanging vine out of her face only to have it swing back and slap her cheek. “I hate this Tournament. I hate this whole damn –”
“Save your energy for walking,” Tiran interrupted, already several paces ahead. “We’ve got bigger problems than your personal comfort.”
Melena bit back another retort and focused on following his path through the undergrowth, trying to mimic his steps to avoid the worst of the mud. He moved through the jungle with an impressive and infuriating ease, barely disturbing the foliage around him while she crashed through like a drunken elephant.
“How are you so good at this?” she finally asked, curiosity overcoming her pride.
“My family has holdings in the Southern Provinces,” he replied without turning. “Spent my summers there as a kid. This kind of landscape’s nothing new.”
“Must be nice,” she muttered.
“What, having useful skills? Yeah, it is.”
Before she could form a suitably scathing response, they emerged into a small clearing where a stream cut through the dense jungle. Ellis sat on a fallen log, looking almost as mud-covered and miserable as she felt. When he saw her, he stood quickly, Raven’s ingrained sense of protocol briefly overriding the situation.
“Your Highness,” he acknowledged with a nod that wasn’t quite a bow. Melena knew he’d likely never bow to her.
“Ellis,” she replied, immediately scanning the area. “Is this it? Just the three of us?”
“So far,” he confirmed, grim. “Com-bands are jammed. No sign of the others.”
“Or the command center,” Tiran added, kneeling by the stream to fill a crude container he’d fashioned from what looked like large leaves folded together. “Drink,” he said, offering it to her. “You’re probably dehydrated.”
Melena hesitated, looking dubiously at the murky water.
“It’s clean enough,” Tiran sighed. “Unless Her Highness would prefer to wait for the palace water sommelier?”
“Fuck you, Tiran,” she snapped, grabbing the leaf cup and drinking deeply despite her reservations. The water was warm and tasted of earth, but it was wet, and that was all that mattered right now.
“You two done?” Ellis asked, clearly not amused by their bickering. “Because in case you haven’t noticed, we’re in serious trouble here.”
Melena wiped her mouth with the back of her hand  –  an action that would have horrified her etiquette tutors. “We need to find the others. Safety in numbers.”
“We need to find the command center,” Ellis countered. “That’s the objective. Secure it first, then gather forces.”
“With what forces?” she asked. “There’s three of us. Old Prosperity has military training. They’re probably already organized and hunting us down as we speak. We need our full team before we can even think about taking the objective.”
“Protocol says – “
“Fuck protocol!” Melena’s temper flared, and for a brief, beautiful moment, so did her fire  –  actual flames dancing along her fingertips before the humidity smothered them again. “Protocol assumes we know what we’re doing. Protocol assumes we have functioning communications. Protocol assumes a lot of things that aren’t true right now.”
“She’s right,” Tiran said, surprising them both by agreeing with her. “We’re at a massive disadvantage. Old Prosperity trains their cadets for this exact scenario from Year One. They’re used to operating in hostile environments and adapting to changing conditions. We’re not.”
“So, what’s your brilliant plan?” Ellis demanded, frustration evident in his tone. “Wander around the jungle hoping we bump into the others? Hope Old Prosperity is feeling merciful when they find us?”
“No,” Melena said, the beginnings of an idea forming. “We use what we have. Tiran knows this terrain. You’re good at tactical assessment. I’m...” she hesitated.
“Relentlessly stubborn?” Tiran offered with a smirk.
“The Queen,” she finished, glaring at him. “And royal or not, I’m still a Lockwood. We didn’t build an empire by following other people’s rules.”
But we also didn't build it by getting ourselves killed in the first challenge. Princess training kicked in automatically – assess the situation, identify assets, maintain team cohesion. Even if she couldn't fight like Old Prosperity, she could still think like royalty.
Lead them. That's what they need. Someone to make the hard calls.
Ellis looked skeptical. “What are you suggesting?”
“We become the hunters instead of the hunted,” she replied. “Find high ground, locate our people, locate the command center, and hit it before Old Prosperity realizes what we’re doing.”
“That’s suicide,” Ellis said flatly.
“That’s strategy,” Melena countered. “They expect us to be disorganized. To panic. To flail around in the mud while they pick us off one by one. So we do the opposite.”
Tiran was watching her with an expression she couldn’t quite read – somewhere between amusement and... respect? No, that couldn’t be right.
“It’s still three of us against who knows how many of them,” Ellis pointed out. “And we’re not exactly combat specialists.”
“No,” Melena agreed. “But we are royals. We’re raised to think we’re better than everyone else.” She smiled thinly. “Time to prove it.”
Before either of them could respond, the jungle went silent.
One second, the cacophony of insects and distant animal calls filled the air. The next  –  nothing. As if every living thing suddenly held its breath.
Ellis froze mid-protest, eyes darting around the seemingly unchanged environment. “What – “
The ground heaved beneath them.
One second, jungle. The next, fire.
The transformation was so violent and immediate that Melena’s brain couldn’t process it. The humid air evaporated, replaced by searing heat that stole her breath. The dense foliage disintegrated like it had never existed. The ground beneath them hardened, cracked, and split open to reveal molten rock glowing red-hot in jagged fissures.
Volcanic wasteland ripped across the landscape, transforming everything in an instant.
“Move!” Tiran yelled, grabbing her arm and yanking her sideways as the ground where she’d been standing collapsed into bubbling lava.
Ellis wasn’t so lucky. The terrain shift caught him off-guard, the solid earth beneath his feet suddenly gone. He screamed  –  a raw, terrified sound  –  as he plummeted through the newly formed fissure.
Melena lunged for him instinctively, her hand grasping for his even as Tiran’s grip on her other arm tightened.
“Let go!” she shouted at Tiran. “He’s falling!”
“So will you if I let go!” Tiran shouted back, his fingers digging into her bicep hard enough to bruise.
It happened so fast. Ellis falling. Her reaching. Tiran pulling her back. The ground continuing to shift and crack beneath them.
Then she felt it  –  her fire. No longer smothered by humidity, it surged through her veins like it had been starved, responding to her panic with raw power. Finally. For the first time in months, her fire felt like itself – eager, hungry, alive. No need to suppress it, no need to hide. Just pure elemental power responding to her desperation.
This is what I've been missing.
Flame erupted from her palms, instinctively seeking something to burn, something to control.
For a split second, she thought: This is my element. This is my advantage.
Then the cliff edge crumbled beneath her feet.
She fell.
The world spun in a kaleidoscope of terror  –  black rock, orange lava, gray smoke blurring together as she plummeted. Her stomach lurched into her throat. Her arms flailed, desperate for anything to grab onto. Her mind couldn’t process how quickly everything had gone wrong.
Her hands scraped against the obsidian wall, fingers catching and tearing on the razor-sharp edges. Pain lanced through her palms, hot and immediate, but she barely registered it through the overwhelming panic of freefall.
Then impact.
Her body slammed into something hard and unyielding  –  a ledge jutting from the cliff face. The collision knocked the air from her lungs in a violent rush. Pain exploded through her left shoulder as it took the brunt of the landing. Something popped  –  a wet, sickening sound accompanied by an agony so intense her vision briefly went white.
She tumbled, momentum carrying her toward the edge of the narrow shelf that had interrupted her fall. Her right hand shot out on pure instinct, fingers closing around a jagged protrusion of rock. Her body jerked to a halt, her dislocated shoulder screaming in protest as she dangled half-off the ledge.
For several seconds, she couldn’t move. Couldn’t breathe. Couldn’t think. Her world had narrowed to the white-hot pain radiating from her shoulder and the desperate grip of her bloodied fingers on the rock.
With a strangled cry, she hauled herself fully onto the ledge, rolling away from the edge. She lay there, gasping, each breath sending fresh waves of agony through her ribs.
“Melena!” Tiran’s voice echoed from above, distant and distorted through the ringing in her ears. “Melena, can you hear me?”
She tried to respond. Managed only a groan that might have been a yes.
Slowly, she forced herself to assess the damage. Her left shoulder hung at a sickening angle – definitely dislocated. Blood coated her palms where the obsidian had shredded her skin. Her ribs screamed with each breath, though whether they were broken or just brutally bruised, she couldn’t tell.
She was alive. Injured, but alive.
And stuck on a ledge thirty feet down a cliff face with molten lava bubbling another thirty feet below. 
“Ellis?” she called, her voice hoarse from the smoke filling the air. “Ellis, can you hear me?”
A faint sound from her right drew her attention. Turning her head – even that small movement sending pain lancing through her shoulde  – she saw him. Ellis had landed on another outcropping about twenty feet away, his body pressed flat against the cliff face. Blood streamed from a gash on his forehead, staining half his face crimson, but he was conscious, his eyes wide with shock and fear.
“Don’t move!” she called to him, each word an effort through the pain. “The ledge might collapse!”
“Wasn’t planning on it,” he replied shakily, his usual composure shattered.
Above them, Tiran’s face appeared at the cliff edge. “You two not dead yet?” he called down, somehow managing to sound sardonic even in this situation.
“Not yet,” Melena gritted out. “Got any brilliant ideas for getting us out of here?”
“Working on it,” he replied, disappearing from view.
Melena leaned back against the cliff face, trying to steady her breathing through the pain. The irony wasn’t lost on her  –  finally in an environment where her fire would work properly, and she was trapped somewhere it was completely useless.
She stared up at the cliff edge, wondering if Tiran would actually come back. If he’d risk his own safety to rescue them. Or if he’d make the strategic choice  –  leave them, find the others, continue the mission without them.
It would be the smart call. The Old Prosperity call. The call she herself might have made, if their positions were reversed.
Didn’t mean it wouldn’t hurt like hell.
Minutes stretched into what felt like hours. The heat was oppressive, making it hard to breathe, hard to think. Sweat poured down Melena’s face, soaking into the collar of her already ruined uniform.
“I see someone,” Ellis called suddenly, his voice strained. “Old Prosperity. Four or five of them, moving along the ridge about a hundred yards north.”
Melena tensed, pressing herself closer to the cliff face. If Old Prosperity spotted them like this they’d be eliminated from the challenge without even putting up a fight.
Not that they’d kill them  –  the “no killing” rule was still in effect  –  but capture meant removal from the next challenge. Game over before they’d even started.
“Tiran?” she called upward, trying to keep her voice low enough not to carry across the lava field. “Tiran, you still there?”
No response.
A small shower of pebbles rained down from above. Melena looked up to see not Tiran, but Sine peering over the cliff edge. She’d barely spoken during their pre-Tournament briefings, but Melena remembered the way she’d knocked over training dummies twice her size during Raven’s mandatory combat drill the night of the Welcome Feast. 
“Your Highness,” Sine whispered down to her, glancing nervously toward where Ellis had spotted the Old Prosperity team. “Don’t move. They’re scanning the area with thermal imaging.”
Thermal imaging? Who the hell had thermal imaging in a Tournament challenge?
“Old Prosperity’s Terrain,” Sine said, as if reading her thoughts. “They have two that are highly advanced. Might be on the same power level as the Captains themselves.”
“Where’s Tiran?” Melena asked, ignoring the growing ache in her dislocated shoulder.
“Gone to find rope,” Sine replied. “And I got through to Maxim on comms. The signal’s back, but patchy.”
Melena’s heart leapt. Communications working again meant coordination. Organization. Maybe they weren’t completely screwed after all.
“Status report,” she demanded, falling back on the formal language drilled into her during the preparation sessions.
Sine’s expression confirmed what Melena already suspected  –  the news wasn’t good.
“The command center’s been located,” she said grimly. “Old Prosperity got there first. They’ve had at least a dozen Pieces gathered since the terrain shift, all defending a perimeter in the northern part of the arena.”
“And our team?”
“Scattered,” Sine replied. “Viv and two Pawns are trying to mount a counteroffensive, but...” she trailed off.
“But they’re outnumbered and outskilled,” Melena finished for her. “What are Maxim’s orders?”
Sine hesitated, glancing toward where Ellis lay injured on his own precarious ledge. “Full retreat,” she said finally. “Regroup at the western edge of the simulation arena. It’s the only defensible position with our limited numbers.”
“He’s right,” Melena said, surprised to find herself agreeing. 
“But what about you and Ellis?” Sine asked. “Tiran’s trying to find something to pull you up, but Old Prosperity is getting closer. If they spot us...”
Melena understood the unspoken question. What should they prioritize? 
The mission or their Queen?
She didn’t hesitate. “Tell Maxim he knows what to do.”
“We can’t just leave you here,” Sine protested.
“That’s exactly what you’re going to do,” Melena said, channeling every ounce of royal authority she could muster while hanging off a cliff with a dislocated shoulder. “If they catch all of us, we lose everything. If they just catch me and Ellis...” she paused, the words bitter but necessary, “we still have a fighting chance in the next climate change.”
Something above Sine caught her attention. The Pawn glanced back, then quickly returned her focus to Melena.
“It’s the order,” she confirmed, her expression apologetic. “Direct from Maxim. Full retreat. Sine, Tiran, and all remaining Pieces to fall back immediately.”
So Maxim had made the call Melena herself had just advocated for. The rational choice. The strategic choice.
It still felt like betrayal.
“Go,” Melena said, her voice steady despite the turmoil churning inside her. “Tell Tiran  – “
“Tell him yourself,” Sine interrupted, glancing behind her again. “He’s refusing the retreat order.”
Before Melena could process that, Sine was gone, replaced by Tiran’s face at the cliff edge. He looked different  –  the smug confidence replaced by pure anger, his usual perfect grooming ruined by ash and sweat.
“Found rope,” he said, already beginning to lower something down to her. “Not much, but it might hold. Old Prosperity’s closing in from the north. We’ve got maybe five minutes before they spot us.”
“You need to retreat,” Melena said, watching as what appeared to be strips of uniform fabric knotted together snaked down toward her. “Maxim’s orders.”
“Fuck Maxim and his orders,” Tiran replied. “I’m not leaving you hanging on a cliff while Old Prosperity takes the win. Now grab the rope.”
Melena stared up at him, genuinely baffled by his defiance. “Why?”
“Because you’re my Queen,” he said finally. “And I’m not losing this Tournament on the first day.”
It wasn’t the whole truth – she could tell from the way he wouldn’t quite meet her eyes – but it was all she was going to get right now.
And honestly, it was enough.
The makeshift rope dangled just above her outstretched hand. If she stretched, if she managed to stand despite the pain radiating from every part of her body, she might be able to reach it.
But there was Ellis, still trapped on his own ledge, injured and unable to help himself.
“Ellis first,” she said, nodding toward her teammate.
“Not enough time,” Tiran replied tensely. “Old Prosperity’s almost here. We get you out, then circle back for him if we can.”
“Ellis first,” Melena repeated, her tone leaving no room for argument. “Or I stay right here and we all get captured together.”
Tiran stared at her for a long moment, clearly weighing his options. Then, with a muttered curse that would have scandalized the palace etiquette tutors, he shifted, redirecting the rope toward Ellis’s position.
“Your Highness is as stubborn as she is filthy,” he called down to her. “And that’s saying something right now.”
Despite everything  –  the pain, the danger, the impending failure  –  Melena felt her lips twitch toward a smile.
“Just get him up there,” she replied. “Then we’ll see about me.”
If she was going to lose this challenge, she’d do it on her own terms. Not because some military brats from Old Prosperity had better training. Not because her team fractured under pressure. Not because they’d been born in London Town. 
And then, as if Melena’s worst nightmares had come to life, the rope slipped. 
Ellis was halfway up the cliff when the knots gave way, sending him crashing back onto the ledge with a cry of pain that echoed across the lava field. The impact dislodged a shower of obsidian fragments that plummeted past Melena into the bubbling molten rock below.
“Fuck!” Tiran’s voice drifted down. “Old Prosperity’s almost here. They’ve spotted our position. We need to move now!”
“Go!” Melena shouted back. “Get out of here! That’s an order!”
For once, Tiran didn’t argue. He disappeared from the cliff edge, leaving Melena and Ellis trapped below. Melena leaned back against the hot stone, exhaustion and pain threatening to overwhelm her. She’d been so close. So close to getting at least one of them out, to salvaging something from this disaster.
“I’m sorry,” Ellis called from his ledge, his voice strained. “I should have – “
“Save your breath,” she interrupted. “It’s not over yet.”
But it was. They both knew it. It was only a matter of time before Old Prosperity found them, captured them, eliminated them from the next challenge.
First day. First failure. Exactly what everyone expected from London Town’s pampered elite thrown into a military simulation. Her grandmother would be furious. The Council would be smug. The entire Empire would watch as London Town was humiliated by the province they considered beneath them.
All because she couldn’t navigate a damn jungle.
Movement from the north caught her attention. She pressed herself against the cliff face, trying to minimize her visibility, though she knew it was probably futile. If Old Prosperity had thermal imaging as Sine had claimed, they’d spot her body heat against the cooler rock easily.
But what she saw wasn’t a team of white-uniformed Pieces coming to capture them.
It was activity near the center of the lava field. Figures moving with coordination that could only come from years of drilling together. Old Prosperity, engaged in some kind of operation. From her position on the cliff, Melena had an unobstructed view across most of the northern quadrant of the simulation. She could see them clearly now  –  a group of at least four Pieces, all in the white and silver of Old Prosperity, working together to... what? Secure a position? Extract a teammate?
Then she saw her.
Katherine Nicholas. Queen of Old Prosperity. Moving with purpose toward a something on the far side of the lava field.
One of her Pieces  –  a large male, probably a Rook  –  was trapped on a rapidly sinking rock platform, lava rising on both sides. Two London Town Flamers were closing in from the opposite ridge.
Melena didn’t recognize her own teammates from this distance, but their intent was clear. They had the Old Prosperity Piece trapped. An easy elimination. All they had to do was touch them, and they’d be unable to participate. 
She expected Katherine to retreat. To make the strategic choice, just as Maxim had done. To sacrifice one for the good of the many.
Instead, Katherine ran.
Melena watched, transfixed, as frost erupted from Katherine’s fingertips, forming a bridge across the molten rock. It shouldn’t have been possible  –  not in this heat, not with the thermal currents working against her. Yet the ice held, steaming where it met the superheated air but maintaining its structure through what could only be sheer force of will.
Katherine moved faster than Melena could have ever expected given they were in the middle of a damn volcano. 
And what she did next left Melena genuinely stunned.
Instead of rescuing her teammate and retreating, Katherine extended the ice bridge creating a parallel path away from the lava pool, toward a stable ridge where the London Town Flamers could retreat from where they were now trapped. 
She was helping them. Helping her enemies.
It shouldn’t have mattered to Melena. Shouldn’t have registered as anything but a tactical error, a weakness to be exploited later.
Instead, Melena was grateful. 
Almost. 
The ice bridge began to deteriorate under the strain, steam rising in clouds that partially obscured Melena’s view. She could see the London Town Flamers hesitate, then take the offered escape route, scrambling away from the spreading lava.
A fireball arced over the scene  –  whether an attack or a warning, she couldn’t tell. Katherine and her rescued teammate made it to solid ground just as sections of the ice bridge collapsed into the lava below.
Then, as if sensing she was being watched, Katherine looked up.
Melena could only nod. 
She’d never tell Old Prosperity thank you but a nod would do. 
## Chapter 18: Katherine – The First Challenge
The cold hit hard.
Literally. 
The transition wasn’t seamless. She heard the ice forming  –  a sharp, crystalline sound that rippled outward as the terrain changed. Felt the temperature plummeting past freezing, past bitter, into something that had no business existing outside laboratory conditions and Old Prosperity in January. 
Her breath came out in a cloud so dense it momentarily blocked her vision. When it cleared, the true scale of their situation hit her. No shelter. No landmarks. Just snow and ice stretching to a horizon she couldn’t even see through. Heat evaporated. Sweat froze. The volcanic landscape disappeared in a heartbeat, replaced by endless white that burned her eyes worse than the lava had.
“Mother – “ The curse died as Nova collapsed beside her, the Terrain’s knees buckling as if someone had cut the strings holding her up. Her fingers scrabbled at her throat, panic flaring in her eyes. “Can’t – breathe – “
Katherine didn’t think about her next move, she just moved. Her hand shot out, ice flowing instinctively from her fingertips to form a thin shell around Nova’s face. She wasn’t trying to trap the cold. She was creating a microclimate where the girl could draw air without it freezing her lungs.
And that’s when all the pieces seemed to fall into place.
Her element was back, answering without resistance for the first time since they’d entered this hellscape. She’d spent hours in the jungle, barely able to form a single crystal. Spent hours in the volcano, pushing her limits just to maintain a simple bridge. Now, her element surged in response to the slightest thought, eager and potent, and hers again.
For anyone else, this might have felt like coming home. For Katherine, it felt like another layer of pressure crushing down on her chest. Because now? Now, she had no excuse if they died out here. No hostile environment to blame. Just perfect conditions for a Frost, and six people looking to her to save them.
“Fucking temp shifts,” Reign gasped through clenched teeth, already positioning herself between Griffin and the worst of the wind. “Almost rather have the lava back.”
Houston was huddled over Tegan, using his bulk to shield the smallest member of their group from the biting cold. The girl’s lips were already turning blue, her shivers growing weaker by the second. Blair stood with his back to the wind, scanning the endless white for any sign of threat or shelter.
“We need to move,” Katherine said, strengthening her shield to encompass all six of them. “We stay out in the open, we die.”
“Move where?” Nova asked, voice steadier now that she could breathe. “There’s nothing but snow in every direction.”
Katherine scanned the horizon, searching for anything that broke the monotony of white. There  –  to the north. A darker line against the snow. Either a ridge or a ravine, but either way, it meant potential shelter.
“That way,” she pointed. “Everyone stays close. No more than arm’s length apart.” She looked at Houston. “Can you carry her?”
Her Rook nodded, already scooping Tegan into his arms. The Pawn didn’t even protest, which told Katherine exactly how bad she was feeling.
“I’ll take point,” Katherine said. “Blair, you take rear guard. You’re the only Dawner on our team. We’re going to need your vision. Reign, you’re in front of him. Keep your eyes open for stragglers.”
“By stragglers, you mean the rest of our team?” Blair asked quietly.
Katherine didn’t answer. They all knew the odds of finding anyone else in this wasteland were slim at best. Still, she couldn’t help scanning the horizon for a glimpse of silver hair or those too-serious eyes that never seemed to miss anything.
Atlas was out there somewhere. So was Sebastian. So was Minho. So was Zephyr. 
But right now, her job was to keep the six with her alive long enough to find them.
The snow was already past their knees and still falling. Each step took effort, the kind that quickly drained whatever reserves they had left after hours in the jungle and volcano. Katherine pushed ahead, using her element to compact the snow beneath their feet, creating an easier path to navigate. It wasn’t much, but it was something.
“Keep moving,” she called behind. “Don’t stop. Don’t rest. Just keep moving.”
The ridge  –  because that’s what it was, she could see it more clearly now  –  seemed to grow no closer despite their efforts. But Katherine refused to believe it was a mirage. Refused to accept that they might be walking toward nothing but more snow and ice and eventually a forced surrender of the challenge.
“I’ve got movement,” Blair called from behind, his enhanced vision scanning through the white-out conditions. “Three o’clock, about fifty yards.”
Katherine’s hand went to her hip, already forming into a dagger jagged enough to slice through bone. “London Town?”
“Can’t tell,” Reign squinted. “Too much snow.”
“Form up,” Katherine ordered, pulling the group into a tighter formation. “Back to back. Reign, Houston, you’re with me facing outward. Nova, keep Tegan and Griffin protected.”
They moved into position quickly as Katherine expanded her shield, making it denser. It was less about temperature control now and more about potential defense. The wind shifted, momentarily clearing the air between them and whatever was approaching. Through the brief window of visibility, Katherine caught a glimpse of white  –  not London Town’s black and crimson that she’d been expecting, but Old Prosperity white.
“Hold position,” she said, not lowering her guard. “Could be ours, could be a trap.”
The figures drew closer, resolving into distinct shapes through the blizzard. Two moving rather well considering all things and one barely upright between them. All wearing the unmistakable uniforms of Old Prosperity, though they were so caked with ice and snow they almost blended into the landscape.
Katherine knew that stance, that walk, that –  
“Atlas,” she whispered. 
“And Sebastian,” Reign added, already moving forward. “He doesn’t look good.”
Katherine squinted through the snow. Reign was right. Sebastian was slumped between Atlas and someone else – was that Dax? His uniform was stained dark in places that looked too much like blood for comfort.
“Potential friendlies approaching,” she announced to her team. “Stay alert but hold your fire.”
They closed the distance, Katherine clearing a path through the worst of the snowdrifts. As they drew near, she could see the full extent of Sebastian’s condition. His face was gray beneath the snow collecting on his skin, his eyes barely open. A makeshift bandage wrapped his thigh, ice crystals forming along its edges where blood had seeped through and frozen.
“Houston, Griffin,” she called. “Help me get him stable.”
Houston gently set Tegan down and moved to take Sebastian’s weight from Atlas, who looked like he’d been holding Sebastian upright through sheer force of will.
“Katherine,” Atlas nodded, relief flooding his eyes. “Good to see you.”
“What happened?” she asked, already examining Sebastian’s wound while Griffin used what little magic he could generate to keep the injured man from going into shock.
“Rock slide during the transition,” Atlas replied. “Found him pinned under debris. It’s bad. Bad doesn’t even cover it, there’s probably some internal bleeding too. Been keeping him cold to slow it down.”
“Smart,” she murmured. “How long has he been like this?”
“Too long,” came the grim reply.
Sebastian’s eyes fluttered open at the sound of her voice. “Hey, Nicholas,” he managed, attempting a grin that looked more like a grimace. “Turns out... rock slides really suck.”
“You’re an idiot,” She shook her head. “A big one.”
“Not arguing,” he replied, letting his head drop forward like holding it up was too much work.
Dax stepped forward, his own uniform covered with some of Sebastian’s blood. “We need to keep moving. Command center’s not far, maybe two miles northeast. Minho’s got a perimeter established.”
Katherine felt a surge of hope. If Minho had secured the command center, they’d have shelter, supplies, maybe even medical equipment for Sebastian. 
Maybe they could win after all. 
“Two miles,” she repeated, looking at her team. Tegan half-frozen. Nova barely able to use her element. Sebastian bleeding through his bandages. “Can we make it?”
Dax nodded. “Should be able to.”
“Perfect,” Katherine nodded. “Houston, you’ve got Sebastian. Dax, take point with me. Atlas, can you handle rear guard?”
“Always,” he replied, frost already gathering at his fingertips as he scanned the horizon behind them.
“Then let’s move,” she said, strengthening her shield around them all. “Two miles to the command center. Two miles to safety. We can do this.”
What felt like forever and a day later, the command center emerged from the blizzard like something out of a fever dream  –  a perfect cube of black stone rising from the endless white, its surfaces unnaturally smooth against the chaotic landscape. No windows. No visible entrance. Just a monolith that shouldn’t exist but somehow did.
“That’s the command center?” Blair asked. “Looks more like a prison.”
“Standard Sanctum architecture,” Dax replied, not slowing his pace. “Function over form.”
Katherine studied the structure as they approached. No banners. No markings. Nothing to indicate which province had claimed it, though if Dax was right, Old Prosperity already had control. The only sign of life was a perimeter of ice spikes forming a defensive ring about twenty yards out from the base of the structure.
“How do we get in?” Nova asked, eyeing the seamless black walls.
“We don’t,” Dax said, reaching for his com-band which had apparently started working again. “They let us in.”
He tapped a sequence into the device, and the ice spikes nearest them began to recede into the snow, creating a narrow path just wide enough for them to pass single file.
“Move fast,” Dax ordered. “Perimeter’s only down for thirty seconds.”
They quickened their pace as much as their exhausted bodies would allow, Houston narrowly carrying Sebastian through the gap while Katherine supported Tegan. The moment they crossed the boundary, the ice spikes rose again, sealing them inside the defensive ring.
A vertical seam appeared in the black stone so thin Katherine almost missed it. It widened silently, revealing a doorway tall enough to enter without stooping. Warm air rushed out, carrying the scent of metal and something else, something chemical that made Katherine’s nose itch.
Inside was nothing like the exterior suggested. The command center was a single cavernous room, its ceiling lost in shadow far above. Screens covered the walls, displaying various camera feeds from around the arena. Workstations lined the perimeter, each occupied by a white-uniformed Piece monitoring different aspects of the defense system. And at the center, standing over a holographic map of the entire arena, was Minho.
He looked up as they entered, his face betraying nothing beyond a slight tightening around his eyes when he saw Sebastian’s condition.
“Help home,” he said to no one in particular, and two Pieces immediately broke from their stations to take Sebastian from Houston. “Status report.”
It wasn’t clear who he was addressing until Katherine realized everyone was looking at her, waiting. Right. She was Queen. Even if Minho was King, she was the only person on the board he was going to listen to. 
“Six recovered during the jungle phase, including myself,” she said, falling into the familiarity of a military briefing. “Almost lost Houston during the volcanic transition, but as you can see, we managed to meet back up. Found Atlas, Sebastian, and Dax in the snow. Sebastian’s injured with a leg wound and some possible internal bleeding. We are functional but exhausted.”
Minho nodded once, processing. “Noah and Kamal are here. Arrived about an hour ago with Justin and Beckett. We’ve secured the command center and established a defensive perimeter. All Pieces accounted for except Zephyr.”
“What?”
“He’s not here,” Minho turned back to the hologram. “We don’t know where he is.”
She glanced at Sebastian, but he was already being moved to what appeared to be a makeshift medical area in one corner of the room. Dax’s face had gone carefully blank, the way it did when he was suppressing something that might interfere with the mission.
“Any contact with London Town?” Atlas asked, moving to study the holographic map.
“Not really,” Minho replied. “They’re disorganized, mostly running from the shadow constructs.”
“The what?” Katherine raised an eyebrow.
Minho gestured to one of the screens where a dark figure moved through the snow with unnatural grace. “Sanctum’s little surprise. Non-human combatants designed to ‘elevate the challenge.’ They entered the arena during the last transition.”
“And you didn’t think to warn us?” Katherine couldn’t keep the edge from her voice.
“Part of the Challenge is holding down the command center,” Minho replied. “We’ve had our hands full doing exactly that.”
Katherine bit back her next words, knowing they wouldn’t help. Minho was technically right but the thought of Zephyr out there alone, possibly being hunted by those things...
“We need to find him,” she said instead.
“We will,” Minho assured her, though his tone suggested it was far from his top priority. He raised up a white piece of paper with some kind of text printed on it. “The challenge only ends when one province controls the command center for three consecutive hours or when time runs out.”
“How much time left before we win?” Reign asked, already moving to one of the empty workstations.
“One hour, fourteen minutes,” Minho replied. “London Town is still somewhat scattered. We think Atlas, Dax and Sebastian scared them making their way back over here. Based on their movements, they are attempting to regroup.” 
Katherine moved to the map, her gaze drawn to a flashing dot near the eastern boundary. “What’s that?”
“Unknown,” Minho admitted. “Could be Zephyr. Could be London Town. Could be another construct. We don’t have enough vines in that sector to be sure. We’ve been tracking movements out there based on the vines that Nova put down. Thank you by the way.”
Nova nodded tiredly from where she’d collapsed into a chair. “Are they still functional in this cold?”
“Barely,” Minho replied. “But they’re better than nothing.”
Katherine studied the dot’s movements. It was moving in a strange pattern; forward, then back, then zigzagging like it was searching for something. 
Or running from something.
“How fast can we get a team out there?” she asked.
“We can’t,” Minho replied immediately. “Not without compromising our defense. We need everyone here when London Town makes their move.”
“If that’s Zephyr – “
“If that’s Zephyr my ass. He’ll have to hold out until we’ve secured this challenge,” Minho cut her off. “I don’t like it either, but we can’t risk losing everything to save one Piece.”
“Even if that Piece is your brother?” The words came out sharper than she intended.
Minho’s face hardened. “I’m doing this because he’s my brother.”
“Bullshit,” Katherine said. “You’d go after him if it was the other way around.”
“No,” Minho replied coldly. “I wouldn’t. And Zephyr wouldn’t expect me to.”
The tension between them drew every eye in the command center. Katherine knew she was pushing it  –  challenging Minho in front of the others was something that would have her in more trouble than mess hall duty back home  –  but she couldn’t just stand there while Zephyr was out in that frozen hellscape alone.
“I’ll go,” she said finally. “Just me. The odds are better that way. Fourteen of you stay here, and I go out there.”
“No,” Minho replied without hesitation. 
“I’m a Frost in a winter wonderland,” Katherine countered. “I’ve got the best chance out there.”
“Which is exactly why you need to be here when London Town attacks.”
“You’ve got Atlas,” she nodded toward him. “And Houston. Two Frosts should be enough to hold this place.”
“The answer is no, Nicholas.” Minho’s voice had that final-decision edge that had intimidated her on a regular basis.
“Then make it an order, Captain,” she challenged. “Put it on record that you ordered your Queen to stand down while your brother might be dying.”
She could practically see him calculating the politics of it, how it would look to the Sanctum, to the viewers, to High Command back home. How everyone would see that Minho’s selfishness had gotten his own brother hurt. The outcast would be worse than anything his career would be able to handle. She’d backed him into a corner, and they both knew it.
“Go,” Atlas said suddenly, breaking the staredown between them. “I’ll hold the defensive line.”
They both turned to look at him, Minho with narrowed eyes, Katherine with surprise. Atlas rarely contradicted Minho in tactical situations out loud these days. 
“Not you too.” Minho demanded.
“She’s right,” Atlas shrugged. “Houston and I can manage the line if London Town attacks. Who knows what they have in store for the Second Challenge. Zephyr’s always been a bookworm, a walking encyclopedia. We’re going to need him in his right mind if we want to continue our winning streak.”
“We haven’t even won this one.”
“And we wont without your brother.”
It was smart – framing it as a tactical necessity rather than an emotional decision. It was exactly the kind of argument Minho would accept.
After a long moment, Minho nodded. “Take Beckett and Justin. They’ve been here the longest. Standard search and rescue, Nicholas. Just like back home. You’ve got forty-five minutes, then I need you back here regardless. Clear?”
“Crystal,” Katherine replied, already moving toward the exit.
“And Nicholas,” Minho called after her, his voice softer than before. “Bring him back.”
She nodded once, acknowledging what he couldn’t quite say directly. For all his talk about how he needed her there, Minho was worried about his brother. Katherine found Beckett and Justin already waiting by the exit, geared up and ready. 
“Standard search and rescue. Just like home boys,” she instructed as they stepped back into the howling storm. “Thirty yards between us, constant visual contact. If you lose sight of me or each other, use light signals. If you encounter constructs, do not engage alone. Fall back and regroup. Questions?”
“None.” Beckett answered. 
“If it’s Zephyr, he’ll be wearing white like us. If it’s London Town, they’re in black and crimson. If it’s a construct...” she trailed off.
“We’ll know,” Justin finished grimly.
They moved out in a triangular formation, Katherine taking point with Beckett and Justin flanking. The blizzard seemed to have intensified since they’d reached the command center, the wind whipping between formations that cut visibility to mere yards ahead. Katherine used her frost to create a tunnel of relatively calm air around them, making it easier to spot movement in the swirling white beyond. The command center disappeared behind them within minutes, swallowed by the storm.
“Stay sharp,” she called over her shoulder. “And keep an eye on those tracking devices.”
The small handheld units Minho had given them showed the mysterious dot’s location about half a mile northeast of their position. Still moving in that strange pattern  –  forward, back, zigzag. They pushed through the snow in silence, each step taking them further from safety and closer to whatever or whoever was out there. The tracking device beeped softly as they closed the distance, the sound nearly lost in the howling wind.
“Movement!” Justin called suddenly, pointing to their left. “Something dark, moving fast!”
A figure, taller than any human, seemed to flow across the snow rather than walk. No face visible beneath its hood, just darkness deeper than night. Its arms ended in elongated fingers like icicles that left no prints in the snow.
And where there was one – 
“Form up!” she ordered. “Back to back!”
They barely had time to move into position before three more shapes materialized from the storm, surrounding them in a loose circle. The constructs made no sound, their hooded forms seeming to absorb the very light around them.
“Remember what Minho said,” Katherine said quietly to her teammates. “They’re shadow-based. Compress your elements until they glow, then strike. And whatever you do, don’t let them separate us.”
The constructs attacked as one, moving so fast they were little more than blurs against the white. Katherine met the nearest one with a wall of compressed ice, forcing it to divert its charge. Beside her, Beckett unleashed a blast of elemental energy that momentarily drove his attacker back.
Justin wasn’t so lucky. His construct closed the distance before he could fully prepare, those razor-sharp fingers slashing across his chest. He stumbled backward with a cry of pain, blood instantly freezing on his uniform. Katherine reacted without thinking, sending a wave of coldness that encased the construct attacking Justin in a solid block of ice. It wouldn’t hold for long, but it bought them the seconds they desperately needed.
“Justin!” she called. “You good?”
“Still breathing,” he gritted out, hand pressed to his wound. “Hurts like hell, but I can move.”
Katherine nodded, already turning to face the next attack. The constructs were learning, adapting to their defenses. The one she’d initially repelled now moved more cautiously, circling rather than charging directly.
“We need to break through,” she said, eyes darting for any weakness, any opening. “We can’t win a fight against four of these things.”
Beckett pointed to a narrow gap between two of the constructs. “There! If you can hit them with enough force, we should buy ourselves some time to get around them.”
It wasn’t a great plan, but it was the only one they had. Katherine nodded, quickly outlining the attack pattern. On Justin’s mark, she unleashed her element in a coordinated assault, ending with a burst of ice that momentarily drove the constructs back.
“Now!” Katherine shouted, and they bolted through the gap, running full tilt into the storm.
They didn’t stop until the constructs were lost in the blizzard behind them, the tracking device showing their target still ahead. Katherine slowed only enough to check Justin’s wound  –  nasty but not immediately life-threatening  –  before pressing on.
“There,” Beckett pointed to a dark shape ahead, barely visible through the snow. “Some kind of structure.”
The ice shelter appeared through the blizzard like a mirage – a natural formation where two massive sheets of frozen stone had fallen against each other, creating a makeshift cave. A figure stood guard outside, swaying slightly despite the defensive posture.
“Stop right there!” The voice was raw, barely carrying over the wind.
Katherine froze, signaling for Beckett and Justin to do the same. “Zephyr?”
The figure trembled visibly, arms raised in what might have been a combat stance if not for how badly they were shaking. “Who’s there?”
“Katherine,” she called. “With Beckett and Justin. Your brother sent us.”
