As they drew closer, Katherine could see Zephyr’s condition more clearly. His uniform was torn in several places, blood frozen in dark patches across the white fabric. His face was alarmingly pale except for patches of wind-burn across his cheeks. But what caught her attention was the strange shimmer around his hands – not just flame like she’d expect from an Augustine, but something else, something that flickered between orange and blue.
“Identify yourself,” he demanded again, voice cracking. “Properly.”
“It’s Katherine,” she repeated. “Queen’s position. Old Prosperity.”
Zephyr shook his head violently. “Not good enough. Those things  –  they sound like people you know. Use voices you recognize.”
“What happened to you?” Katherine frowned, taking another careful step forward.
Zephyr’s laugh was borderline hysterical. “I’ve been running from them for hours.” His eyes darted to something behind her. “They mimicked Sebastian. Called for help. Almost fell for it before I realized...”
His hands shook harder, and that’s when Katherine saw it clearly – where flame should have been steady around a Flamer’s fingers, Zephyr’s right hand carried orange fire edged with blue. His left hand held something else entirely – delicate frost patterns swirling around his fingertips.
Impossible.
Fire and ice. 
Together.
In two different hands. 
Katherine probably would have fainted if they hadn’t been in the Tournament. 
“What’s your Year status?” Katherine asked suddenly, trying to ground him.
“Year Ten. Early Apprenticeship. Combat Protocol Division.” The words came automatically. Katherine nodded. She’d heard Sebastian boasting about that – his little brother getting selected for the elite division despite being barely twenty. 
“Zephyr, you’re using – “
“I know what I’m using,” he cut her off sharply, fear flashing in his eyes. “I can’t stop it. I can’t – “ He looked down at his hands in panic. “Minho’s going to kill me.”
Katherine moved closer, seeing the signs clearly now – the violent shivering despite the flames at his fingertips, the confusion in his eyes, the slurring at the edges of his words.
He had hypothermia. 
“You’re freezing,” she said softly. “Your body’s defenses are shutting down.”
“Can’t be that,” he argued weakly. “I’m a Flamer. We don’t freeze.”
“Everyone freezes in this,” Katherine replied. She glanced at Beckett. “We need to get him warm.”
Zephyr backed away. “Don’t touch me. I’m fine. Just need to – to...” His eyes rolled back briefly before he caught himself.
Katherine reached for him before he could collapse. “Zephyr, we need to get you to shelter.”
“No!” He jerked away. “Can’t leave. Have to protect – “ He broke off, glancing back at the ice cave.
“What are you protecting?” Katherine asked. “Who’s in there?”
The conflict played across his face. “A girl. Those things were hunting her.” He swallowed hard. “Couldn’t leave her out there. Not after what I saw them do to that other Piece.”
“What other Piece?”
“He…He…” He shuddered, and both elements flared around his hands in response. “He stopped moving and they left him alone but I couldn’t find him. He’s not dead. Not dead. Not dead.”
Katherine felt sick, but she took another step toward Zephyr, signaling Beckett and Justin to follow her lead. “Let me see who’s in there.”
“Minho’s going to lose his mind,” Zephyr muttered as he stepped aside, the dual elements still dancing around his trembling hands. “Bad enough I couldn’t find Sebastian. Now I’m harboring the enemy and...” He looked down at his hands. “Doesn’t matter. Nothing matters if those things find us.”
Katherine entered the shelter, eyes adjusting to the dim light. A figure lay huddled against the far wall, crimson hair against ice – unmistakable even in this state.
Melena looked nothing like the Imperial Princess from the Welcome Feast. Her uniform was torn, left shoulder at an awkward angle, blood frozen in trails down her face from a gash at her temple. Her breathing was shallow, labored – shock or hypothermia or both.
“Your Highness,” Katherine said, kneeling beside her.
Melena’s eyes fluttered open, minor recognition and confusion mixing in her gaze.
“You,” she managed, voice barely audible. “Come to finish what the volcano started?”
“If I wanted you dead, I wouldn’t have kneeled,” Katherine replied, examining the dislocated shoulder. “This needs to be reset.”
“Everything hurts already,” Melena said through clenched teeth. “What’s more pain?”
Katherine positioned her hands. “On three. One – “ She shoved the shoulder back into place without finishing the count.
Melena’s scream echoed through the small cave, quickly muffled as she bit down on her fist. Her face went even paler, sweat beading on her forehead despite the cold.
“You lied,” she gasped when she could speak again.
“Always count early for dislocations,” Katherine replied, fashioning a proper sling from her field kit. “Less time to tense up.”
Justin appeared at the entrance. “Those constructs are circling back. We need to move.”
Katherine looked at Melena, then at Zephyr who was now slumped against the wall, the dual elements flickering weakly around his hands. “Neither of them can make it to command on their own.”
“So we leave her,” Justin said. “She’s London Town.”
“She dies out here, it’s on us,” Katherine countered. “No killing rule, remember?”
“This wouldn’t be killing,” Justin argued. “It would be – “
“Strategic non-intervention. I heard you,” Katherine cut him off. “Not happening.” She turned to Beckett. “Take Zephyr. I’ve got her.”
Beckett hesitated only briefly before moving to support Zephyr, who barely seemed to register being moved. The dual elements had faded to almost nothing now as his body focused on survival over control.
Katherine turned to Melena. “Can you stand?”
The princess tried to push herself up, but her legs gave out immediately. Katherine caught her, lifting her into her arms with one fluid movement. Melena was lighter than she expected.
“I wouldn’t have done this for you,” Melena murmured against Katherine’s shoulder as they emerged into the storm.
“I know,” Katherine replied, remembering that moment across the lava field, the acknowledgment in Melena’s eyes that said otherwise. “That’s why I’m the one doing it.”
## Chapter 19: Melena
Waking up hurt like hell.
First, the smell  –  antiseptic mixed with something burnt, like someone had tried to sanitize a fire. Then the light, too harsh against her eyelids, making her head throb even before she opened her eyes. And finally, the pain  –  a detailed map of every injury she’d collected, from the dull ache in her shoulder to the sharp stab in her ribs every time she breathed too deeply.
Melena blinked against the infirmary lights, her brain still trying to piece together how she’d gone from dangling off a cliff to lying on a Tournament House med bay bed. Her memory skipped. Half-frozen images. Katherine. The snow. That weird flash around Zephyr’s hands.
She shifted, testing the limits of her body’s betrayal. Her left arm was secured in some kind of high-tech sling, way different than the one Katherine had slid her arm into in the blizzard. Her face felt raw, her skin had been stripped by wind and ice, and her lips were cracked beyond recognition. If her grandmother could see her now, she’d declare the dynasty dead and buried.
Outside the curtained-off bed, someone was yelling.
Melena stiffened, instinctively trying to make herself smaller against the pillows. That voice, cutting through the sterile quiet like a knife could only belong to one pers. 
Raven.
“ Complete fucking disaster…” The handler’s words bounced off the walls, fragments reaching Melena through the thin curtain.
“No kill round…” More words lost to distance and the pounding in Melena’s head.
“Entire roster…”  A pause, then louder, “And embarrassment to London Town…”
Melena winced. She didn’t need to hear every word to know they’d failed spectacularly. That somehow, in the first challenge where death wasn’t even an option, London Town had still found a way to lose.
“Carried by the enemy…” Raven’s voice rose again, sharp with disgust.
Heat bloomed in Melena’s chest, the first flicker of her fire since she’d been dragged in from the cold. Shame, anger, and embarrassment were all wrapped into one burning coal lodged somewhere between her lungs.
She pushed herself upright, ignoring the flash of agony that shot through her ribs. The room tilted briefly before settling back into focus. Without thinking, Melena swung her legs over the side of the cot, determined to see who was facing Raven’s wrath. She didn’t need their pity. If she had to limp in, fine. But they were going to see her stand beside them. The minute her feet touched the cold floor, however, the truth settled over her like a weighted blanket.
They’d lost the first challenge. The enemy had rescued her. And something told her Raven was just getting started.
Melena made it to the prep hall on legs that felt like they belonged to someone else. Her royal upbringing kept her spine straight despite the agony shooting through her ribs with every step. Lockwoods didn’t wince. At least not where anyone could see it. Her grandmother called it sovereignty. Melena called it bullshit with posture. Right now, it was the only part of ettiquette training that seemed worth a damn.
The room fell silent as she entered. Thirteen faces turned toward her, a mix of injuries and expressions that told their own stories of the disaster that was Challenge One. Some were still wrapped in bandages. Some wouldn’t meet her eyes. Tiran nodded slightly from where he leaned against the far wall, the only acknowledgment she got. At least he’d made it back. Small victories.
Raven stood at the center of the room, hands clasped behind her back, looking like she’d like to push each of them into the next active volcano they encountered. Her Tournament jacket was still pristine, not a wrinkle or stain to be found, making their mud-caked, blood-stained uniforms look even more pathetic by comparison.
“How kind of Her Highness to join us, because now that we’re all here,” Raven continued, pacing the length of the room, “let’s review exactly how badly you all fucked up.”
“We walked into Challenge One with fourteen out of sixteen Pieces. The only province with backups activated before the starting horn.” Her eyes narrowed. “And somehow, we still came out worse than Old Prosperity.”
She crossed to the status board on the wall, pulling up their roster with a sharp gesture. Names appeared in bright red  –  some flashing, some dimmed.
“Let’s do a headcount, shall we?” Raven’s finger tapped each name like she was counting corpses. “Jarom Fairchild: still on probation, ruling under review. No verdict from the Sanctum yet on whether he’ll be allowed to participate in future challenges.”
Jarom’s face remained carefully neutral, though his hands trembled slightly at his sides.
“Olivia Rembrandt: still under investigation for element augmentation. No elemental usage is permitted until the case is closed. She gets to sit and watch while the rest of you try not to embarrass yourselves further.”
Olivia stared at the floor, shame radiating from her in waves.
“Ellis Vale,” Raven’s voice softened suddenly. “Found after the challenge ended. Alive. But he won’t be standing for the next one. He’s out. Injuries are non-lethal but non-negotiable.”
Melena felt the weight of those words settle in her stomach. Ellis had been on that cliff with her. Had fallen just like she had. But while she’d been carried to safety by an enemy with a hero complex, Ellis had been left behind.
She’d told them to leave him. That was on her. Didn’t change how it felt. 
“So, when you all take the floor for the second challenge, you’ll be down to thirteen,” Raven concluded, her gaze sweeping over them like searchlights. “Thirteen. All sixteen of you should be eligible, and yet here we are. Still think I’m the problem?”
No one dared to answer her. And somehow, that silence stung more than any insult.
“Old Prosperity didn’t lose a single Piece,” Raven continued. “Not one. Despite the terrain shifts. Despite the constructs. Despite everything the arena threw at them. And do you know why?”
She didn’t wait for a response.
“Because they function as a unit. While London Town…” her eyes fixed on Melena “Gets carried across the finish line by their enemies.”
The fire that had been simmering in Melena’s chest flared hotter. “The climate changed,” she found herself whispering. “We thought it would allow us to have more Pieces in the Second –”
“And whose choice was it to split up?” Raven cut her off. “Whose brilliant strategy scattered our forces across the arena with no communication, no coordination, no plan beyond ‘try not to get caught?”
Melena felt the eyes of her team on her as if they were waiting for her to defend herself. So she did what she always did – squared her shoulders and acted like she wasn’t bleeding.
“Mine,” she said simply. “It was my call.”
A flicker of – surprise, maybe? – crossed Raven’s face before vanishing behind her professional mask.
“At least you own your failures,” she said after a moment. “That’s something most royals never learn. The Second Challenge begins tomorrow,” Raven said, addressing the room once more. “Old Prosperity is going to come in confident, united, and hungry for another win. Meanwhile, you’ll be hobbled, divided, and carrying the weight of this disaster on your backs.” She paused. “Unless you decide right now that you’re not going to let one failure define this Tournament.”
She tapped the status board again, and their names rearranged – forming new pairings, new check-in partners, and new nightly tasks. 
“Study these. Memorize them. Your partners have changed, but the objective hasn’t.” Her gaze swept over them one final time. “Dismissed. All except Her Highness.”
Melena stayed rooted to the spot as the others filed out, some limping, some helping each other waiting for whatever additional reminder of her failure Raven had saved for a private audience. When the door closed behind the last Piece, Raven just looked at her for a long moment, her expression unreadable.
“The Augustine boy who carried you out,” she said finally. “What did he say?”
Melena blinked, thrown by the unexpected question. “Zephyr? Nothing. He was barely conscious. Hypothermia and…” She hesitated before continuing. “And then there was something else.”
“Something else,” Raven repeated flatly. “Care to elaborate?”
Something in her tone made Melena wary. “He was confused,” Melena lied. “He wasn’t like himself.”
Raven’s expression didn’t change, but her posture shifted subtly with a tension that hadn’t been there before. “I see. And did he explain this... momentary lapse?”
“No,” Melena said carefully. “Like I said, he was barely conscious.”
Raven studied her for a long moment as if trying to decide if she believed her. Then she nodded once. “Get some rest. You’ll need it for tomorrow.”
As Melena turned to leave, Raven added, “And Your Highness? The next time you give an order to retreat, make sure it doesn’t leave one of your own behind.”
The words followed Melena out of the room, heavier than any crown she’d ever worn.
#
It took her twenty minutes to find him. Not in the training room, where half the team had gone to burn off their frustration. Not in the common area, where the others huddled in small groups, comparing injuries and excuses equally. Not even in his quarters, where any sane person would’ve retreated for at least an hour of solitude after the day they’d had.
No. Tiran Blackwood was sitting on the fucking floor just outside the med wing, boots half unlaced, hands clasped between his knees like he’d been waiting on something or someone. He didn’t look surprised to see her. He didn’t look surprised to see her. Then again, he never looked surprised by anything – which used to annoy the hell out of her. Now, it just made her tired.
“If you’re looking for Raven, she went to file the official loss paperwork,” he said, voice flat with exhaustion. “Apparently, there’s a special form just for first-round failures.”
“I’m not looking for Raven,” Melena replied, wondering if her legs would allow her to sit beside him without embarrassing herself. Her ribs screamed in protest as she slid down the wall, but she managed to make it look almost intentional.
They sat in silence for a moment, stealing glances at one another when they thought the other wasn’t looking. Tiran had that hollowed-out look that came from pushing an element too far for too long  –  one of those exhaustions that sleep wouldn’t touch.
“What happened to Ellis?” she finally asked.
Tiran’s jaw tightened, a muscle jumping beneath skin that looked too pale against his dark uniform. “We thought Old Prosperity got him.”
“But they didn’t.”
“No.” He looked away, fixing his gaze on the opposite wall. “He crawled toward the sound of the countdown. They found him collapsed near the boundary line. Took too long to stabilize him.”
“I gave the order to retreat,” she whispered. 
“You made the call you had to.”
“Did I?” 
Tiran didn’t answer right away. Just sat there, staring at his hands like they might hold some answer to a question he hadn’t asked. Finally, he said, “We couldn’t have carried both of you.”
“Then I should’ve made someone else stay,” she whispered, the words burning her throat. “I should’ve sent Sine back for him. Should’ve made sure –”
“Should’ve, would’ve, could’ve,” Tiran cut her off. “That’s not how it works and you know it. You gave an order. They followed it. Doesn’t make it the right one.”
She didn’t respond to that. He was right and somehow, that made it worse. The med wing doors opened, and a Sanctum doctor emerged, tablet in hand, not even glancing at the two of them huddled on the floor. Melena learned the first night that the Tournament staff were good at pretending Pieces didn’t exist outside the arena. It likely made the inevitable casualties easier to process.
“I’m so fucking tired of being a Lockwood,” she muttered. She didn’t mean to say it but she wasn’t going to take it back either.  
Tiran turned to look at her then, really look at her, his violet eyes searching to see if what she’d just said was even true. “I don’t think I’ve ever heard you admit that before.”
“Yeah, well.” She let her head fall back against the wall, wincing as it connected harder than intended. “Almost dying has a way of cutting through the bullshit.”
She felt rather than saw him shift beside her. One minute, they were sitting beside each other, and the next, his arm was around her shoulders, pulling her into him with a gentleness she hadn’t known he possessed.
She should have resisted. Should have maintained the careful distance they’d established over years of political maneuvering and palace intrigue. Should have remembered that Tiran Blackwood was the son of her grandmother’s most vocal critic, that his family had built a fortune on challenging the crown at every turn, that whatever temporary alliance they had in this Tournament was just that – temporary.
But his shoulder was warm, and she was cold in a way that had nothing to do with the arena’s frozen wasteland and everything to do with being her mother’s daughter. So instead, she melted into him, her head finding the hollow beneath his collarbone like it belonged there.
“Their Queen carried me out,” she murmured against his jacket. “She could’ve left me there. She should have left me there.”
“But she didn’t.” His voice was a rumble she could feel through her cheek.
“No. She didn’t.” Melena couldn’t make sense of it. Couldn’t stop replaying that moment over and over. “Why would she do that? We’re enemies.”
Tiran’s hand moved absently against her arm, tracing circles that left warmth in their wake. “You don’t have to like someone to save their life.”
The hallway lights dimmed briefly – the ten-minute warning before curfew.
“You should get some rest, Princess,” he said, his voice softer than she’d ever heard it. “Tomorrow’s a new disaster waiting to happen.”
She almost smiled. “Such a comfort, Blackwood. Truly.”
Neither of them moved. His arm stayed around her, her head stayed on his shoulder, and for just a moment, they weren’t a Lockwood and a Blackwood; they weren’t two political dynasties with generations of calculated animosity between them. They were just Melena and Tiran, bruised and exhausted and finding comfort in the only place it was offered.
“We’re going to lose this Tournament, aren’t we?” she whispered, the question barely audible.
Tiran didn’t lie to her. Didn’t offer empty reassurances. Instead, he pressed his lips gently to the top of her head – so briefly she might have imagined it – and said, “Not if I have anything to say about it.”
The hallway lights dimmed again, more insistently this time.
Reluctantly, they pulled apart. Tiran stood first, then offered his hand. She took it without hesitation, letting him pull her to her feet with more care than strictly necessary.
“Get some sleep, Your Highness,” he said, the formal title sliding back into place like armor.
“You too, Blackwood.”
They parted at the junction where the hallway split – her to the royal quarters, him to the Bishop’s chambers. As she walked away, Melena found herself running her fingers over the spot on her arm where his hand had been, wondering when Tiran Blackwood had stopped being the enemy and started being something much more dangerous.
## Chapter 20: Katherine – The Second Challenge
Katherine had been studying Empire History challenges since she was twelve.
Not because anyone told her to. Not because it was required curriculum at the University. But because those challenges were the only part of the Tournament that actually made sense to her. Combat was chaos. Strategy was guesswork. But history? History had patterns. Rules. Answers you could find if you knew where to look.
She’d memorized every format, predicted every outcome, analyzed every strategy from Old Prosperity’s common room while other cadets complained about boring theory classes. When other people saw dusty documents and ancient poetry, Katherine saw puzzles waiting to be solved.
So, when they announced Challenge Two would be Empire History, something in her chest settled for the first time since they’d arrived at Havenwood.
This was what she’d been training for her entire academic career.
The formal testing wing looked nothing like the cramped examination rooms back at the University. Ancient stone walls stretched up toward a vaulted ceiling painted with scenes Katherine didn’t recognize – that weren’t in any textbook she’d studied, figures in armor that predated the current Imperial design. The whole space felt heavy with age, like they’d been dragged into some forgotten corner of Imperial history.
“Look at all this setup,” Sebastian muttered from beside her.
Katherine followed his gaze toward the front of the room. Archive devices hummed behind protective barriers, their crystal cores pulsing with contained magic. Display screens showed readouts in scripts she couldn’t read. Technicians in white coats moved between the equipment like they were performing surgery instead of preparing documents.
“Why all the machinery?” she asked Atlas, who was scanning the room with the same tactical assessment he used for combat zones.
“Document materialization,” he said. “They can’t risk bringing the originals out of Imperial vaults. Too much chance of damage or theft. So they recreate perfect copies down to the ink composition and paper aging.”
“That seems like cheating.”
“Welcome to the Tournament.”
Katherine kept watching the setup process. On the broadcasts, they never showed you the fifteen minutes of preparation required to create “authentic historical documents” for the challenge. Never explained how archive specialists could pull texts from locked vaults and recreate them with enough precision to fool trained historians.
Made her wonder what else the broadcasts didn’t show.
Across the room, London Town was settling into their positions at a table that looked like it belonged in a palace instead of a testing facility. Melena sat with perfect posture, hands folded, looking like she’d been born for this kind of formal academic theater.
Which, to be fair, she probably had been.
The distance between the two teams was intentional – far enough apart that they couldn’t overhear strategy discussions, close enough that they could watch each other’s reactions. Katherine had seen this setup in tournament broadcasts before, but being inside it felt different. More intimate. Like they were performing for one another instead of the audiences across the empire.
“Final calibrations,” one of the technicians announced. “Document integrity at ninety-eight percent.”
Katherine frowned. “Ninety-eight percent of what?”
“Authenticity markers,” Atlas explained. “Magical signatures, material composition, historical accuracy. They have to match the originals exactly or the challenge gets thrown out.”
“And the other two percent?”
Atlas shrugged. “Margin of error. Or deliberate modification, depending on who you ask.”
Katherine raised an eyebrow but Atlas had already turned to Sebastian and started another conversation before she could say anything else.
“Pieces, take your positions,” the proctor called out. “Challenge Two will commence in sixty seconds.”
The proctor stepped forward once the room had fallen completely silent. She was older than Katherine had expected, with gray hair pulled back in a severe bun and the kind of posture that screamed decades of military service. Her voice carried easily across the auditorium without any amplification.
“Welcome to Challenge Two: Empire History.”
Katherine barely breathed as the challenge was announced. Her fingers hovered just above the table, ready to begin the minute they were told they could.
“Each team will receive two historical documents. Your task is to determine which document represents authentic Imperial record.”
Simple enough. Standard authentication challenge – analyze primary sources, identify historical markers, make the right choice. Katherine had solved practice versions of this in under ten minutes.
“You will have fifteen minutes to examine both texts. Teams may confer during analysis, but final decisions must be submitted by your designated academic Piece.”
That was her. Katherine had scored highest on Old Prosperity’s history assessments since Year Seven. No one on her team could argue with her about history.
“The team with correct authentication wins Challenge Two and advances with full points. The losing team will face scoring penalties in subsequent challenges.”
Katherine flexed her fingers, readying herself. Around the room, other academic Pieces were making their own preparations – adjusting positions, taking deep breaths, settling into analysis mode. The girl from London Town – not Melena, but one of her teammates – nervous. Good. Nervous people made mistakes.
“Document materialization will commence in ten seconds.”
A low hum filled the auditorium as the archive devices activated. Katherine felt the air shimmer around their table, magic pulling historical texts from Imperial vaults and recreating them with the way Atlas had described earlier. The smell hit her first – old parchment, ink, the particular mustiness that came with documents that had existed for centuries.
“Materialization complete. You have fifteen minutes. Begin.”
Two documents appeared on their desk.
Katherine leaned forward immediately, her academic training taking over. First step: quick scan for obvious identifiers. The document on the left bore a header she recognized instantly – The Breathbound Law, as recorded in the Imperial Archives, Year 847.
Relief flooded through her. She knew this text. She’d memorized this during her first year at the University – every Remnian citizen had. It was carved into courthouse walls, recited at state ceremonies, embedded in their national memory.
The opening verses were as familiar as her own name:
Before the stars were taught to burn,
Before the tide was told to turn,
A voice was born in blood and breath,
A crown was forged, a vow to Death.
The Order rose with chains of grace,
To bind the world in rightful place.
And power kept by blood alone,
Shall guard the heart of every throne.
This was going to be easier than she’d thought.
Then she looked at the second document – and all that confidence cracked.
No official header. No archival stamps or Imperial seals. Just text that looked… older. More complex in its formatting. The language structure seemed similar to the Breathbound Law, but the content was completely different.
Katherine read the opening lines once, twice, three times, but each time only made her frown deepen.
When the void first spoke in flame,
We mistook the silence for a name.
Memory coiled in ancient breath,
The script was written, not the death.
What sings in blood does not decide.
What echoes back is not the guide.
Katherine leaned closer, eyes scanning for a trick she couldn’t name.
“What the hell is this?” she whispered.
“What’s it say?” Atlas asked.
Katherine was already deep into analysis mode, her academic training taking over. “Give me a minute. I need to check the authentication markers.”
She started with the Breathbound Law, scanning for the details Professor Aloysius had drilled into them.
“This one checks out,” she said, pointing to specific sections. “See the way they reference ‘chains of grace’? That’s Imperial language from the early expansion period. And the meter matches documented Imperial verse patterns.”
She moved to the second document, applying the same analytical framework. No archive notation. Language structure was... different. Still verse, but the rhythm felt older somehow. Content was abstract, philosophical.
“This second one doesn’t have any of the markers,” Katherine continued, working through her mental checklist. “No official stamps, no dating, no reference to known Imperial events or figures. Plus the language is all wrong for Imperial documentation.”
Sebastian leaned over to look. “So obvious fake?”
“Has to be.” Katherine read through more of the unfamiliar text, cataloging inconsistencies. “Look at this line – ‘Time is a spiral, not a line.’ Imperial documentation doesn’t deal with abstract philosophy. It sticks closer to the historical record.”
But something nagged at her as she worked through the authentication process. The formatting of the second document looked older than the Breathbound Law, even though it didn’t have any of the same formatting as the first one. Even the paper composition seemed different.
Katherine glanced up for a second, letting her eyes rest. Across the room, Melena was reading the second document with the kind of focus that looked less like analysis and more like… recognition.
Weird.
Katherine shook her head and went back to work.
“One minute remaining,” the proctor announced.
Katherine looked back at their documents.
The second document failed every official test.
The choice was obvious.
“Teams, please submit your final responses,” the proctor announced.
Katherine handed over their analysis form, watching as technicians collected responses from around the room. The archive devices hummed as they dissolved the documents back into magical energy, leaving only empty tables and the lingering smell of old parchment.
“Results will be announced momentarily,” the proctor said, consulting a tablet that had appeared in her hands.
This was different than waiting for scores at Delegation. This time, she wasn’t afraid to look at the board to see who’d win because there was no way Old Prosperity was going to lose.
They’d done everything right and she knew it.
Atlas sat back in his chair, arms crossed. Sebastian was spinning his stylus between his fingers, but even he looked relaxed. This wasn’t like the physical challenges where anything could happen. This was academics and the only person on their team who remotely knew enough to challenge her call was Reign and she hadn’t spoken at all.
“Taking forever,” Sebastian muttered.
Katherine shot him a look. “They have to verify our justifications. Make sure we didn’t just guess.”
Seven minutes later, the proctor looked up from her tablet.
Katherine straightened automatically.
“Team with correct authentication: Old Prosperity.”
Sebastian grinned. “Told you it was obvious.”
“London Town loses points for incorrect authentication and will face scoring penalties in subsequent challenge.”
Katherine looked across the room, expecting to see the standard reaction to losing points in the Tournament. Frustration, maybe some arguing about the fairness of the challenge, definitely some worried looks about what scoring penalties meant for the next challenge.
Most of London Town’s team delivered exactly that. Shoulders slumped, heads shaking, the kind of defeated body language that came with knowing you’d just made your situation worse.
But Melena didn’t look surprised.
She didn’t look disappointed either.
Katherine stared at her for a long moment, recognition hitting like ice water in her veins. She knew that look. Had worn it herself against Daniels in the evaluation ring – that moment when your shoulders drop half an inch and your jaw goes slack, not from surprise but from resignation. 
When you stop fighting the outcome because you’ve already accepted it. The way your hands go still in your lap, fingers uncurling from fists they never really formed. How your breathing shifts from the sharp inhales of combat to something deeper, steadier, like you’re settling into defeat instead of raging against it.
It was the look of someone who’d seen the killing blow coming and decided not to dodge.
Katherine remembered the weight of Daniels’ boot on her sternum, the way her ribs had screamed as he pressed down. The moment when she could have grabbed his ankle, could have sent frost crawling up his leg until he was frozen solid. Her fingers had tingled with the ice blade forming in her palm – just enough to slice through skin, to stop breath, to end things permanently. She’d had the opening. His stance too wide, guard dropped, throat exposed. One strike, clean and simple.
And she’d chosen not to take it.
Just like Melena was choosing now.
In the Tournament, every point mattered. Every challenge could mean the difference between survival and elimination.
Nobody chose to lose.
Unless they thought the “wrong” answer… was actually right.
## Chapter 21: Melena – The Second Challenge
Melena shifted in her chair, trying to find a position that didn’t make her ribs scream in protest. Her left arm was still trapped in the high-tech sling from the infirmary, her face felt like she’d been dragged through broken glass, and every breath reminded her of exactly how spectacularly she’d failed the First Challenge. But Empire History? This was different. This was her chance to prove she wasn’t the complete disaster Raven had been screaming about for the past twenty-four hours and then some.
“An embarrassment to London Town.” The words still echoed in her head, sharp and unforgiving. Well, today she’d show them what an Imperial Princess could actually do when the challenge played to her strengths instead of her weaknesses.
She’d been studying Remnian history since she could read. Had memorized every major treaty, every succession law, every piece of Imperial propaganda disguised as academic curriculum. Her tutors used to joke that she could recite the Imperial Laws backward while performing the royal protocol. And they weren’t wrong.
This is where I prove I belong here, she thought, ignoring the way her team kept glancing at her arm sling. Not stumbling through some jungle like a fucking amateur.
Around the table, her teammates tried to look optimistic, but she could see the doubt lurking in their expressions. They’d watched her fall apart in the jungle. Watched her get saved by Katherine fucking Nicholas – Old Prosperity’s Queen, their supposed enemy. The humiliation still burned hotter than any physical injury.
Tiran sat to her right, close enough that she could feel the steady warmth radiating from his skin. After everything – after he’d helped her through the aftermath, after he’d stayed when she expected him to leave – something had shifted between them. Not trust, exactly, but maybe the beginning of her being able to tolerate him for more than thirty seconds.
Progress. Slow progress, but progress, nonetheless.
“You sure you’re feeling up to this?” Viv asked carefully, her eyes flicking to Melena’s sling. She’d been promoted to replace Ellis after his injuries from the First Challenge – another reminder of how badly things had gone. “We could have Maxim take point on the analysis…”
“No.” The word came out harsher than she intended. “I think I can handle a history lesson. I had the best education the empire could offer.”
Viv opened her mouth to speak but seemed to think against it when Melena sighed heavily. 
She needed this. Needed to prove that yesterday’s disaster was a fluke, not a preview of what London Town could expect from their Queen. Needed to show them that Princess Melena Lockwood was more than just royal blood and political connections.
And more than Caelum’s lapdog. 
They were down to thirteen Pieces now, just like Raven said they would be. 
Viv was starting to fill the Kings Knight position better than Melena wanted to admit,  Jarom’s investigation had stalled any hope of a replacement, and Olivia, while technically still on the board, wasn’t allowed to use her element. Three Pieces gone, one of them still sitting at the table like she wasn’t half a threat.
Maxim’s expression tightened. “We need the points after yesterday. London Town can’t afford another loss. I don’t think any of us want to continue to hear Raven’s screams all night.” 
“I won’t fail,” Melena said, and she meant it. “Have you ever known me to get Empire History wrong?”
“No,” Maxim replied. 
It was a rhetorical question, but she saw the moment of hesitation before he answered. Because yesterday, they’d watched their invincible Imperial Princess reduced to pathetic sparks by some humidity and trees. If she could fail that spectacularly at something as basic as summoning fire, what else might she be wrong about?
Everything, whispered a traitorous voice in her head. Caelum’s deal. Elias’s safety. Your ability to protect anyone, including yourself.
Across the auditorium, the buzz of preparation rose like static. Voices dropped to hushed tones. Chairs scraped stone. A few tables had already gone quiet—nerves tightening into focus, hands smoothing out documents and repositioning datapads like the order of objects could somehow sway the outcome.
Melena didn’t move. She just observed.
The testing chamber was circular, built like an old Remnian courtroom, with the proctors seated in raised alcoves and the Sanctum looming above them all like judgment made flesh. A cold draft rolled through the space, cutting against the warmth of the lamps and scanners being brought online. The floor was polished stone, etched with the emblem of the Empire—its gleaming silver surface now layered in dust, history, and something Melena was sure wouldn’t come out with cleaning products. 
Her gaze landed on Old Prosperity’s table.
Katherine sat exactly where Melena expected her to: front and center, posture rigid, hands folded like she wasn’t just ready—but already analyzing. A girl leaned slightly toward her, probably whispering last-minute calculations, while Atlas hovered behind them with that infuriating calm he always carried, like nothing could ever catch him off guard.
Melena’s stomach twisted.
She hated that she recognized the way Katherine held tension in her shoulders. Hated that she knew what that kind of stillness meant—what it took to sit in a room full of enemies and keep your expression unreadable. Katherine wasn’t just prepared. She was anticipating something. And she wasn’t the only one.
Tiran must’ve noticed the shift too, because he leaned closer and murmured, “They’re already planning their submission strategy.”
Melena didn’t respond. She couldn’t afford to look over-eager.
Instead, she cataloged the setup. There were five tables in total, each spaced evenly around the perimeter of the chamber, with high-definition holo-projectors suspended above them—waiting. Four scanners per table. Two digital styluses. One control pad for cross-referencing source material, locked until activation. Everything was standard procedure, but somehow it still felt…off.
“Two minutes to document materialization,” the proctor announced.
Melena inhaled slowly through her nose.
Maxim slid a tablet across the table toward her. “You want the format guides?”
She didn’t even look down. “No.”
She already knew them. Treaty formats. Succession scrolls. Military orders. Each category had its own set of markers—hidden royal seals, watermark layering, shifts in linguistic phrasing across different Empress reigns. She’d spent years learning to recognize the difference between a first-edition charter and a copy made three years later. One misplaced verb tense or incorrect date structure could unravel an entire forgery in under sixty seconds.
She flexed the fingers of her good hand.
The Empire might rewrite its own history every decade, but Melena had memorized the original drafts. She could read between the lies. She could decode the silences. She could dismantle whatever fake paper they were about to be handed and tell them exactly who wrote it, why they wrote it, and who they were trying to fool when they did.
Her eyes drifted to the Sanctum’s observation box above, where a figure leaned forward slightly—only just enough to be noticed. She didn’t recognize them. Didn’t need to. Whoever they were, they’d come to see whether the Imperial Province still remembered how to win a war without blood.
They would.
Because if she failed here, it wouldn’t just be another loss on the scoreboard. It would be proof that she didn’t deserve the title she carried. That she was just another pretty name in the Imperial court, too soft to lead, too arrogant to study.
Too weak to save her brother. 
Even as she tried to project confidence, Melena couldn’t shake the memory of Maven’s voice, half-whispered through shadowlit lessons back in that hidden corner of Shadowmere.
“The truth and the record aren’t always the same. And if you ever have to choose, follow the one that keeps your people breathing.”
Back then, that had felt revolutionary—liberating, even. A reminder that her role as Princess didn’t mean swallowing everything the Empire fed her. That maybe, just maybe, she could learn the system well enough to understand how a place that Shadowmere could exist. 
Wasn’t that the point? Wasn’t that why she studied late into the night with smuggled Somnorian texts, dissecting ancient myths like they were state secrets? Shadowmere didn’t just sharpen her knowledge. It changed the way she thought. How she saw history, power, even herself.
And yet, for the first time since she entered the auditorium, Melena admitted to herself how unsure she really was. 
The low hum of the archive interface filled the auditorium, vibrating just beneath the surface of Melena’s skin. A shift in the air followed—dense, metallic, laced with the kind of power that made your molars ache—and she knew the Elite magic had begun.
Above them, threads of gold and violet light spun from the projector’s core, interweaving in ancient patterns as the retrieval spell locked in. This was sacred protocol. Nothing could be tampered with. Nothing could be fabricated. Whatever appeared had already been logged, sanctioned, sealed.
Two pedestals rose from the floor. Both empty. For now.
Then the first began to fill.
A ripple of magic swept through the air. A flicker of seal, script, and then—parchment. Thick, heavy-stock, tinted with age and edged in the crimson dye used only during Empress Caelia’s reign. Wax still clung to the corners, cracked and gold-dusted.
Melena leaned forward before it even finished materializing.
The Breathbound Law.
She didn’t need a title. She didn’t need a date stamp. That format was burned into her brain. The flourished headers, the line spacing, the signature placements—it was Imperial 101.
“Let breath be bound to land and name—
And keep the flame, and keep the flame.”
Melena almost smiled. It was practically ceremonial at this point. The same poem they made schoolchildren chant like a prayer to the throne. A loyalty document disguised as literature. This one she could handle.
She’d already won this round.
She’d already known the answer. 
Then the second pedestal activated.
And everything in her stilled.
The summoning pattern was different. Older. Ancient. Its weaves moved in a spiral, not a line, and her breath caught in her throat before the text had even appeared. Something about the magic felt familiar…
Then the words arrived.
“When the void first spoke in flame,
We mistook the silence for a name.”
The shock hit her system so hard that her dislocated shoulder suddenly screamed, pain spiking white-hot through her arm. She pressed her good hand instinctively to her sling, trying to breathe through the wave of agony.
That wasn’t Remnian.
That wasn’t even supposed to be in Remnia. 
The Dreamwalker’s Writ.
She didn’t just recognize it. She knew it. Had traced those same lines under the flickering light of Maven’s underground archive. Had sat on a cold floor with her knees drawn to her chest while Maven whispered each stanza like it was gospel, like it could teach her how to save Elias.
“Memory coiled in ancient breath—
The script was written, not the death.”
Not some knockoff. Not a forgery. 
Which meant only one thing.
Caelum.
She could feel his fingerprints all over the magic—that same dream signature that had made her skin crawl in the marketplace, power that moved in spirals instead of straight lines. Of course, he’d leave breadcrumbs in front of a camera, knowing she’d recognize them. Knowing she’d be the only one in the room who could.
Her chest tightened.
“Your Highness?” Viv again.
Melena realized she’d been sitting in complete silence, staring at the documents. No analysis. No notes. No confident breakdown like she always did. Just frozen.
She opened her mouth. Closed it.
The Breathbound Law was authentic, yes. But next to the Writ? It looked like a polished echo of something harsher, crueler, fuller. Her gaze snapped back to the second document.
“The mind that wakes must still forget.
The dream recalled is not truth yet.”
Maven had made her recite that line until her throat was raw, until the words scraped coming out and she could barely whisper. Not just because of the cadence, but because of what Maven said after.
There will come a time when you will have to chose, Princess. 
Melena swallowed hard. The weight of that lesson pressed down on her harder than any speech from Raven ever had.
“Princess,” Tiran leaned closer to her. “Something’s wrong.”
Not a question. A statement. 
Melena could see Maxim checking his watch in her peripheral vision, could feel the team’s growing confusion. This wasn’t how these challenges went. She was supposed to analyze, explain, choose within minutes. Not sit there staring like her brain had short-circuited.
“Nothing,” she lied, still staring at the Writ like it might bite her.
Twelve minutes remaining.
The proctor’s voice sliced through the auditorium, and Melena felt her world fracture along invisible lines. Her teammates were staring at her, waiting for the confident analysis that always came so easily. But her brain was stuck between two realities—Imperial Princess who should know nothing about Somnorian texts, and information broker who’d spent months studying exactly what was sitting in front of her.
Both worlds. 
Colliding. 
Right here. Right fucking now.
I can’t. I can’t do this. How the hell am I supposed to navigate this without revealing where I learned any of it?
“The first document,” she started, then stopped. Her voice sounded disconnected from her body. “The Breathbound Law. It’s...”
Ten minutes remaining.
“It’s what?” Maxim pressed, and she could hear the edge creeping into his voice.
Melena’s chest tightened. She couldn’t breathe properly. Couldn’t think past the crushing weight of secrets she couldn’t share. Her shoulder throbbed where the sling pressed against her collarbone, and her hands were starting to shake.
I’m going to fuck this up. Just like everything else.
Across the auditorium, she could see Minho at Old Prosperity’s table. Their eyes met for a split second, and without thinking, she reached out with her mind.
Minho.
The mental connection sparked to life instantly—invisible, undetectable, the one safe communication she had in this nightmare.
Jesus, Mel. What now? His voice filled her head, not even bothering to hide his aggravation. You look like you’re about to have a panic attack. What’s wrong?
Eight minutes remaining.
“Standard Imperial formatting,” she managed to say aloud to her team, while her real conversation happened in the space between thoughts.
I think— She stopped herself before she could explain about Shadowmere, about Maven, about everything she couldn’t tell him. I think the second one is real.
There was a pause in Minho’s mental voice. What? That doesn’t make sense. The Dreamwalker’s Writ isn’t even—how would you know anything about—
I can’t explain. The desperation bled through their connection. I just—there’s something about it. It’s like I’ve seen it somewhere or read it before.
Melena…
Tiran’s actual voice cut through her mental conversation. “You’re not making sense. What about the second document?”
Six minutes remaining.
Mel, whatever you think you know about the Writ, you need to go with the Imperial record, Minho said sternly. Now is not the time to do that thing you do when you’re curious. There is no need for London Town to lose this Challenge too. Do not lose this over some feeling.
But it wasn’t just a feeling. It was months of forbidden education, weeks of studying the exact text that was somehow sitting in front of her. Knowledge she couldn’t explain without destroying everything.
Trust me, she sent back desperately. I know how this sounds, but—
“Your Highness!” Viv’s voice cracked with frustration. “We need a decision! Now!”
Three minutes remaining.
“Which document?” Maxim demanded, his voice rising.
Choose the Breathbound Law, Minho urged in her mind. Whatever you’re thinking, just choose the right answer.
Two minutes remaining.
“Choose!” Maxim shouted.
Raven would say she was choking. Weak. A disgrace to London Town. Maybe she was.
The chaos of voices, the ticking clock, Minho’s warnings in her head, the impossible weight of living two lives—it all crashed together until something inside her chest snapped. In that moment of complete overwhelm, with her teammates shouting and time running out and both her worlds demanding contradictory choices—
“The Dreamwalker’s Writ!”
The words exploded out of her mouth like a confession torn from her throat.
The auditorium fell dead silent.
Mel, what the fuck—
And Melena felt the blood drain from her face as she realized what she’d just done.
I didn’t mean to say that. I was supposed to say the Breathbound Law. I was supposed to—
But it was too late. The words were out there, broadcast to the entire Empire, recorded for posterity. Princess Melena Lockwood had just declared a forbidden Somnorian text to be authentic Imperial history.
Melena didn’t know what scared her more; the looks on her team’s faces or the fact that the Sanctum was probably going to kill her.
## Chapter 22: Katherine – The Third Challenge
Nico promised to never sugarcoat things to them.
Up until now, he never had, but this morning felt different. He stood in front of their group looking like he'd rather be anywhere else – hell, even back in the jungle getting his ass handed to him by mosquitoes the size of hummingbirds. His usual permanent scowl had graduated to something that might've been concern, if Nico was capable of human emotions.
“Alright, listen up,” he said, arms crossed. “Two challenges down, both wins. Don't let it go to your heads because the real Tournament starts today
Katherine felt her stomach drop straight through the floor. She'd been waiting for this – the moment when the “no killing” rules got thrown out the window and they stopped playing elaborate war games. Around her, the others shifted uncomfortably. Even Sebastian's usual smartass energy seemed muted, his fingers drumming against his thigh in a pattern that screamed anxiety.
“The Maze,” Nico continued, stepping to the side to show a hologram that had form over the common room table. “Also known as The Trap.”
Katherine watched as the hologram took the shape of a multi-level maze. The maze looked like something designed by a psychopath with architectural training. Twisting corridors that seemed to change angles as you watched, sections that appeared solid but would likely dissolve into empty space, levels that stacked and intersected in ways that defied basic physics. Katherine could already feel her brain trying to map potential routes and failing. 
“Each team enters simultaneously,” Nico said, his voice flat and clinical. “But you won’t all enter together. You'll be separated. Deliberately.”
Sebastian's drumming stopped. 
Atlas raised an eyebrow. 
Nova frowned. 
Separated meant vulnerable. Separated meant isolated. Separated meant exactly the kind of scenario designed to break team cohesion.
“The objective isn't elimination,” Nico continued. “It's retrieval. Each team has a specific artifact hidden somewhere in the maze. Find it. Secure it. First team to retrieve wins.”
Nova leaned forward. “Something tells me you’re about to come at us with something we aren’t going to like.”
Nico’s smile was not reassuring. “This will be the turning point for the Tournament.”
“How come?” Katherine found her way back into the conversation. 
“Statistics show that the third challenge is where most teams lose the most Pieces. Normally, you would have lost Pieces in the First Challenge but my guess is the no-kill rule was implemented to protect the Imperial and Royal families from embarrassment if their heirs died early.”
“So why put it back now?”
It was Minho who spoke up this time. “Because you can’t have a Tournament where no one dies.”
Even the way he said it was cold. Not angry, not disgusted, not even sarcastic — just a fact laid out like a piece of rotten fruit. In a way, it was more disturbing than if he’d sounded eager for it.
Nico just nodded, eyes glittering with that same dead-eyed clarity. “The Sanctum has to cull the field. Otherwise, there’s no point in the rest of the board. That means you need to be ready for casualties — and you need to be ready to do what’s necessary when it comes down to it.”
Katherine felt the room shift. Even the air seemed heavier, as if each oxygen molecule was being carefully counted and rationed. No one looked at anyone else. Not even Dax, who usually lived for this kind of shit. He just dug a thumbnail into the soft skin of his palm and stared.
Nico let the silence stretch. “You’ll go in two hours. I expect every one of you to read the Maze's layout and memorize possible strategies. No second-chance scouting. No ‘explore and adapt’ bullshit. If you waste time in there, the only thing you’ll find is a quick and unpleasant exit.” He shifted the hologram with a flick of his wrist, zooming in on a particularly convoluted section where the floors overlapped twice before dead-ending into a vertical drop. “There are kill corridors. Choke points. Blind turns. You will not have comms. The Maze suppresses nearly all elements. Occasionally, when the environmental cycle resets, you may get a window. But don’t rely on it. If you try to brute force your way through, you will die.” He let that final word settle across the room like dust after a bomb.
“Scary,” Sebastian scoffed. “And here I was thinking we’d have another easy Challenge.”
“I’m afraid nothing after this is easy,” Nico glanced around at all of them. “This will be especially hard for the Terrain. You will not be able to feel when the maze is changing. It will feel like everything is constantly in motion. You are the only elementalists whose elements will be completely suppressed.”
Katherine watched as the color drained from Reign’s face. 
She didn’t envy her. Frost was always a reliable fallback, even if it came at the expense of your own blood sugar. But for the Terrains, it was like being sent into a firefight with your arms zip-tied to your sides. Katherine tried to catch Reign’s eye, offer some silent comfort, but the other girl just stared at the hologram like it might reveal a secret if she glared hard enough.
“Are there constructs?” Nova asked.
“There are always constructs,” Nico replied. “But the Maze is the real enemy here. You keep your wits, you’ll make it through.”
When the briefing ended, the team scattered — some toward the kitchen for breakfast meal, some to the bunks, some to the tiny prayer alcove Nova found last night at the end of the hall. Katherine just stood in front of the Maze’s glowing projection, memorizing the patterns. She tried to think like the designer: where would you hide something if you wanted to make it look impossible, but still theoretically navigable? What would you do if your job was to whittle down two armies of overachievers into a handful of survivors?
She didn’t have answers. Just a low, persistent dread at the base of her skull, and the certainty that if she fucked up in there, she’d never hear the end of it when she made it home — if she made it home. She was still staring at the hologram when someone cleared their throat behind her. She turned to find Zephyr Augustine standing there, hands shoved in his pockets, looking like he’d rather be literally anywhere else.
Now that he wasn’t hypothermic and delirious, she could actually see what he looked like. Definitely an Augustine — same keen jawline as his brothers, but his face was softer somehow, like he hadn’t grown into all those angles yet. His hair was darker than Sebastian’s, if that was even possible, cropped shorter but not military-regulation like Minho’s. A few freckles were scattered across his nose, which was surprising. Neither of his brothers had freckles. His Tournament uniform was perfectly pressed, buttons aligned, but there was a small tear in the sleeve that he’d tried to mend himself. 
“Hey,” he said, which wasn’t exactly the most eloquent opening she’d ever heard from an Augustine. “You good?”
“Am I good?” Katherine almost laughed. “That’s a hell of a question right now.”
“Yeah, I know. Stupid question.” Zephyr shifted his weight, and she noticed he rolled his shoulders back when he was nervous — nothing like how his brothers carried themselves. “I meant... how are you feeling? After the whole hypothermia thing. You probably saved my life.”
Katherine frowned. She’d been expecting him to ask about strategy or formation planning, not checking on her health. “I’m fine. Question is, are you? You were half-dead when we dragged you out of that ice cave.”
“Better now.” He glanced down at his hands, and Katherine caught sight of something else — burn scars across his knuckles, small ones, like he’d been practicing how hot his element could actually get. “Look, I wanted to thank you. For what you did. For me and that London Town girl.”
“Melena,” Katherine said automatically.
“Right. Melena.” Zephyr looked up, and his eyes were lighter than his brothers’ — more gold than green or brown. “That was…weird, wasn’t it?”
Katherine shrugged. “Weird doesn’t begin to cover it.”
“Sebastian thinks it was a trap. Some kind of long game.” Zephyr’s fingers drummed against his thigh — must have come from Sebastian. “Minho thinks it was tactical stupidity on her part.”
“What do you think?”
“I think she was scared.” The words came out quietly, like he wasn’t sure he should be saying them. “When she looked at me in that shelter, she looked... I don’t know. Like she was seeing something that reminded her of someone else.”
“Transport’s loading.” Minho’s voice cut through whatever moment they’d been building. Katherine turned to find him standing a few feet away, expression unreadable. His eyes found Zephyr immediately. “We move in five.”
Zephyr straightened immediately, that nervous shoulder roll disappearing the second his older brother appeared. “Right. Coming.”
Minho stepped closer, and Katherine watched his focus narrow entirely to Zephyr. Everything else — the hologram, the mission briefing, even her presence — became secondary. “You sleeping?”
“Yeah.”
“Eating?”
“Yes, Minho.”
“Your hands steady?”
Zephyr held them up briefly, and Katherine caught the slight irritation in his voice when he said, “See for yourself.”
Minho’s jaw tightened almost imperceptibly. He was good at hiding concern, but not when it came to his brothers and at the rate he was going, the entire Empire was going to know soon enough. “Don’t get cute with me.”
“I’m fine,” Zephyr sighed. “I promise. There’s nothing to worry about.”
Katherine felt like she was witnessing something private but before she could speak, Minho’s gaze found hers.
“Let’s go,” Minho turned toward the exit. “Both of you.”
The transport was smaller than Katherine had expected, cramped enough that they couldn’t avoid sitting close together. She ended up wedged between Reign and Nova, with the Augustine brothers scattered across from them. Sebastian had claimed a window seat and was staring out at nothing, fingers tapping that same anxious rhythm against his knee. Atlas sat near the front, studying a tactical tablet like it might reveal some secret about surviving the next few hours.
“How long’s the flight?” Nova asked, voice tight.
“Twenty minutes,” Nico replied from the pilot’s area. “Enough time to make sure you don’t throw up your lunch.”
He pulled up another holographic display, this one showing sixteen different colored dots scattered around the Maze’s perimeter. “These dots represent you. I’ll be up here watching each of you.”
“What happens if we run into London Town Pieces?” Dax asked.
“Whatever you think keeps you alive,” Nico said flatly. “This isn’t a drill anymore.”
Katherine felt her stomach drop as the transport shuddered slightly, beginning its descent. Through the small windows, she could see the arena spread out below them — a massive black structure rising from barren ground like some kind of monument to suffering.
The Maze.
Even from this height, it looked wrong. The kind of wrong that made the hair at the back of her neck stand up. The walls seemed to shift slightly in her peripheral vision, though when she looked directly at them, they appeared solid. Shadows pooled in corners that shouldn’t exist, and the whole thing gave off an aura of malevolence that made her skin crawl.
“Jesus,” Reign whispered beside her. “It’s huge.”
It was. Katherine had studied the holographic projections, memorized the layout. But seeing it in real life made her realize how little any of that mattered. This wasn’t a puzzle to be solved or a course to be navigated. 
This was a predator and they were its prey. 
The transport touched down with a gentle bump that felt like a death sentence being signed.
Katherine took one look at the Maze entrance and knew they were fucked.
The transport had deposited them on a patch of yellowing grass maybe fifty yards from the structure, close enough that she could see every detail of the black stone walls stretching up into the sky. But what made her stomach drop wasn’t the Maze itself — it was what waited between them and the entrance.
Seventeen figures in Sanctum gray stood in formation, their ceremonial robes moving slightly in the breeze. Sixteen of them held what looked like strips of black fabric. 
Blindfolds.
“Oh hell no,” Beckett whispered beside her. “No fucking way.”
Katherine glanced at him and saw genuine terror in his face. Sweat beaded along his forehead despite the cool air, and his hands were shaking. “What’s wrong?”
“I can’t—” He swallowed hard, staring at the blindfolds like they were weapons. “Small spaces. Can’t do small spaces when I can’t see.” His breathing was getting faster, shallower. “I’ll lose my shit. I’ll fucking lose it.”
Katherine could see him spiraling, that telltale pattern of panic she’d watched other cadets go through during particularly brutal training exercises. His pupils were dilated, hands clenching and unclenching at his sides. Before Katherine could respond, another transport descended from the sky, sleek and black where theirs had been white. 
She watched as the opposing team filed out onto the grass, maybe thirty yards away, their black and crimson uniforms stark against the yellowing field. Even from this distance, she could pick out Melena’s crimson hair.
Then something shifted beside her.
Minho had gone completely rigid; if he’d been worried earlier, then Katherine was afraid to name the emotion that filled his face now. Sebastian stopped mid-sentence in whatever joke he’d been telling, and Zephyr took a step closer to them without seeming to realize he was doing it.
All three of them were staring at the same spot across the field.
“Shit,” Sebastian whispered, and his voice cracked slightly. “I forgot.”
“Forgot what?” Katherine asked, though she was starting to get a sick feeling in her stomach as she followed their gaze.
“Maxim’s a Terrain,” Zephyr said. “He’s a Terrain.”
In all of Nico’s warnings about element suppression, in all the briefings about how the Terrains would be crippled, Katherine realized then and there that none of them had been thinking about their brother. 
“Fuck,” Minho said, and Katherine had never heard him sound so helpless. “He’s going to be completely blind in there.”
“They didn’t tell London Town about the suppression, did they?” Sebastian’s hands were shaking now. “He doesn’t know what’s coming.”
Katherine watched as Maxim’s gaze found his brothers across the field. Even from this distance, she could see the moment he spotted them — the way his posture shifted, something that might have been relief flickering across his face before he remembered where he was. What side he was on.
The younger Augustine brothers stood frozen, and Katherine realized she was watching something break in real time. They were about to watch their older brother walk into a death trap, and there wasn’t a damn thing they could do about it.
The Sanctum Leader’s voice echoed across the field, amplified by magic until it seemed to come from the air itself.
“Pieces of Old Prosperity and London Town, welcome to the Maze. You stand at the threshold of the Third Challenge, where strategy meets survival, where preparation meets reality.”
Katherine watched as the sixteen Sanctum officials began moving toward them. The seventeenth figure — taller than the others, with robes that shimmered differently in the light — remained stationary at the center of their formation.
An Elite mage. 
Beckett made a sound that was definitely a whimper this time. “No, no, no, no. Can’t do this, can’t fucking do this.”
“Each Piece will be paired with a Sanctum guide for transportation into the Maze via Elite magic at this time.”
Elite magic transportation. Shadow travel. Katherine had heard about it but never experienced it — the kind of instantaneous movement that Elites used to slip between spaces, bending reality to step from one location to another in the blink of an eye. Supposedly, it felt like being folded in half and shoved through a space too small for your body.
“Prepare for pairing,” the voice commanded.
The Sanctum officials spread out, each approaching a different Piece. Katherine found herself face-to-face with a figure whose gender was completely obscured by their robes and ceremonial mask. They held out the blindfold without a word.
“This is bullshit,” she muttered, but took the fabric anyway. It was softer than she’d expected, silk maybe, but thick enough that she knew it would block out everything.
“Pieces, don blindfolds now,” the Sanctum voice commanded.
Katherine hesitated for exactly one second, then tied the silk around her eyes. The world disappeared, replaced by absolute darkness that felt heavier than it should have. Her other senses immediately sharpened — she could hear Beckett’s increasingly panicked breathing, smell the ozone that always preceded serious magic, feel the subtle vibrations in the ground as the Elite mage began to move.
“Shadow travel will commence in thirty seconds,” the voice announced. “Do not resist the magic. Do not attempt to remove your blindfolds. Do not—”
Beckett broke completely.
“I can’t do this!” His voice cracked, high and desperate, raw with pure terror. “I can’t see, I can’t breathe, I can’t— someone please, please get it off me, I can’t—”
Katherine heard running footsteps, probably Beckett trying to bolt, followed by the sound of someone being tackled to the ground. His breathing was coming in rapid gasps now — full panic attack territory, the kind that could make someone pass out.
“Get it off me, get it off!” He was sobbing now. “I can’t breathe, I’m gonna die, I can’t—”
“Piece will comply or be removed from competition,” a Sanctum official stated flatly, like they were discussing the weather instead of a human being having a breakdown.
“Beckett,” Katherine called out, trying to project calm she didn’t feel. “Just breathe. Count backwards from ten. We did this all the time back home.”
“I can’t— can’t count, can’t think, everything’s— help me please—”
“Ten,” she said firmly, loud enough to cut through his panic. “Nine. Come on, with me. Eight.”
She heard him trying to match her count, his voice shaking and broken but audible. “Seven— fuck, seven—”
“Six. Five. Four.”
“Three,” he managed, still crying but breathing slightly better.
“Two. One.”
The shadow travel hit like being shoved through a keyhole.
Katherine’s world collapsed inward, reality folding around her like origami made of pain. This wasn’t like the smooth transportation magic she’d imagined — this was violent, wrong, like being turned inside out and fed through a meat grinder. Every nerve in her body screamed as space bent around her, as the Maze reached out with invisible hands and yanked her through dimensions that shouldn’t exist.
She couldn’t tell which way was up. Couldn’t tell if she was moving or if the world was moving around her. Her stomach tried to climb out through her throat while her brain insisted that none of this was possible, that bodies weren’t meant to exist in whatever space she was currently occupying.
Then she was falling—
Katherine hit stone hard, her knees cracking against unforgiving marble as her body tried to remember how gravity worked. Her stomach heaved, bile burning the back of her throat. The blindfold was gone — when had that happened? — and she found herself retching onto black stone that reflected her face like dark water.
Everything hurt. Her bones felt wrong, like they’d been taken apart and put back together by someone who’d lost the instruction manual. Her vision doubled, then sharpened. Stone walls loomed on three sides, disappearing into shadows above.
She tried to stand and immediately fell sideways, her inner ear completely fucked from whatever had just happened to her. The world tilted at impossible angles, making her grab for the wall to keep from falling again.
“Fuck,” she gasped, tasting blood where she’d bitten her tongue during the landing. “Fuck fuck fuck.”
She was inside the Maze.
Alone.
And she felt like someone had scrambled her insides with a fork.
Stretching ahead of her was a corridor that seemed to go on forever, lit by some source of light she couldn’t identify. Everything was too bright and too dark at the same time, her eyes still trying to adjust to existing in normal space again. Somewhere in this labyrinth of stone and shadow, fifteen other Pieces were probably having the same realization she was, assuming they’d survived the shadow travel at all.
They were definitely fucked.
## Chapter 23: Melena – The Third Challenge
The shadow travel felt like drowning in reverse.
Melena hit the Maze floor face-first, tasting blood and marble dust while her brain tried to remember how bodies were supposed to work. Everything hurt in ways that didn’t have names — like someone had taken her apart at the molecular level and put her back together using instructions written in a language they couldn’t read.
“Fuck,” she gasped, pushing herself up on shaking arms. Her fire flickered weakly at her fingertips, barely more than sparks. Even her element seemed confused about existing in normal space again.
The corridor stretched ahead of her, all black stone and shadows that moved wrong. Too quiet. Too empty. She’d expected screaming, fighting, the sound of people dying in interesting ways. Instead, the Maze offered her nothing but silence and the steady drip of something that better be water somewhere in the distance.
She was alone.
Completely, utterly alone.
And somewhere in this labyrinth of pretty death traps, her scattered teammates were either already dead or working on it.
“Well,” Melena said to the empty air, wiping blood from her split lip. “This is going exactly as planned.”
She took inventory. One working arm, one mostly-dead arm, two legs, no visible broken bones. Her ribs screamed whenever she inhaled too hard, but she’d gotten used to that. Her uniform, miraculously, had held together through the transition, though the jacket’s left sleeve was stained with blood — hers, probably. The comm-band on her wrist still showed “NO SIGNAL,” which felt like the crowning detail of her life.
At least the Maze had the decency to be beautiful. Walls of black marble, veined with silver that glimmered when she turned her head just so. The corridor forked ahead, one path sloping gently up and the other down into darkness. Melena considered her options. Up meant maybe light, maybe people, maybe the Sanctum’s infamous trap platforms. Down was almost certainly a dead end, but it would at least put her closer to the rumored heart of the Maze.
She chose down. Always easier to take the fall than risk the exposure on high ground. Besides, gravity was her only real ally here.
The air thickened as she descended, each step echoing too loudly. After twenty meters, the corridor narrowed until her shoulders brushed the walls. Far ahead, something shifted — a scratch of movement, a ripple of air. Melena pressed herself flat, heart pounding. That’s when it hit her: this was exactly the kind of idiotic move that got people turned into Maze decorations.
Melena backed up carefully, pressing her palm to the wall as she went. She sent a pulse of heat along the stone, hoping to pick up on traps or hidden doors — nothing. Even the echo was weird, as if her fire couldn’t decide if it wanted to be a wave or a particle in here.
The floor changed ten meters back from where she’d been standing. Same black marble, but smoother, as if it had been polished by a thousand feet before her. She crouched, studying it, then tossed a chunk of broken stone from the entryway onto the suspicious patch. The instant it landed, the air shimmered blue and a grid of razorwire materialized above the floor, slicing the rock into a neat handful of pebbles. The whole thing vanished in less than a second.
“Nasty,” Melena said. She wasn’t sure if the Maze was listening, but if it was, she wanted it to know she saw right through its bullshit.
She tested every step after that. Sometimes the traps were obvious, sometimes not. Sometimes they were just the room itself, reshaping behind her so the way back was sealed off with a hum of magic and the stink of ozone. After five minutes, the only thing Melena could trust in here was her own paranoia.
When she reached the narrow section again, she moved slower, fire crackling softly in her palm for light. The stuttering figure was still there, but now she could see it better.
It wasn't moving anymore.
The corridor opened up ahead into what looked like a chamber, and she inched closer, flame guttering as she shielded her face and let her eyes adjust. The chamber beyond was beautiful, just like everything else in this nightmare. Curved walls that seemed to glow with their own inner light, delicate archways that looked like they’d been carved by artists instead of engineers. And in the center, a pool of water so perfect it looked like a mirror.
Except the water wasn’t clear anymore.
The figure was slumped against the wall just inside the entrance, head bent at an impossible angle, arms splayed like the wings of a broken bird. For a moment, Melena thought it was a construct, one of the Maze’s rumored puppets — until the glint of a London Town pin caught her eye, half-torn from a battered lapel.
Ivy.
Melena’s mind refused to process it for a full second. She blinked, but the image didn’t change: Ivy, the Pawn who’d made it through the jungle, who cried at the first kill order and then didn’t stop smiling for the rest of the night, now pinned to the marble by three gleaming spikes driven through bone and stone alike. Her face was frozen in a look of shock, mouth open as if she’d died mid-sentence.
Melena staggered, dropping to her knees. She reached out, half-expecting her hand to pass through a hallucination, but it was all horribly, physically real. The blood had pooled around Ivy’s shoes and dried in rivers across the floor, flowing toward the perfect pool that was now stained deep red. Her uniform was still mostly intact. Still warm.
For a long, blank minute, Melena just sat there, fire flickering useless in her palm. Ivy had never been more than a name on a roster, a face in the crowd — but now she was what the Maze turned people into. An object lesson.
She pressed the back of her hand to her mouth, trying to force down the bile rising in her throat.
The Maze had made it beautiful. Made it perfect. Made it look safe.
And then it had killed her teammate while Melena was twenty meters away, learning how to avoid fucking razorwire. The chamber felt smaller suddenly, the walls pressing in. Ivy’s face, frozen in shock, blurred and shifted until it wasn’t Ivy anymore.
The video call had been perfectly clear. Her mother’s face filling the screen, smiling as she talked about picking up pastries for Cordelia’s birthday the next day.
“I’ll be home in twenty minutes, darling. Tell your sister I found those chocolate ones she likes.”
Melena had been lying on her bed, com-pad propped against pillows, complaining about some stupid palace function she’d have to attend. Normal. Safe. Just another conversation with her mother.
Then the screech of tires. The sudden jolt of the vehicle. Lucia’s eyes going wide with terror.
“Mel—”
The impact. The spinning. The sickening crunch of metal and glass. Her mother’s scream cutting off mid-word as the call stayed connected, showing nothing but smoke and shattered windshield and blood spreading across expensive upholstery.
Melena had screamed her mother’s name at the screen until her voice gave out, but there was no answer. Just silence and smoke and the awful certainty that she’d watched her mother die and hadn’t been able to do a damn thing about it.
“No,” Melena whispered, but she couldn’t tell if she was talking to Ivy or her mother. “No, this isn’t— this isn’t how—”
Her fire exploded outward without warning, wild and uncontrolled, scorching the beautiful walls black. The flames reflected in the bloody water, making the whole chamber dance with orange light. She couldn’t breathe. Couldn’t think. The air felt too thick, too hot, like she was drowning.
Her mother had been coming home. Had been talking about pastries and birthday parties and normal things. And then she was just... gone. One second laughing about Cordelia’s sweet tooth, the next second nothing but twisted metal and silence.
