Ivy had been walking through a beautiful chamber. Probably thinking about finding her teammates, getting home safe. And then she was just... gone. One second breathing, the next second pinned to marble with her eyes wide and shocked.
“She was just trying to come home,” Melena sobbed. “She just wanted to go home.”
Her flames roared higher, licking at the ceiling. She could feel her control completely shattered, her element feeding on grief and terror and rage until she was nothing but pain and the absolute certainty that everyone she cared about was going to die while she watched, helpless and screaming.
The video call was still connected. Still showing the wreckage. Still waiting for her mother to move, to speak, to prove this was some kind of horrible mistake.
But she didn’t move. She didn’t speak. She was just gone.
Melena couldn’t get enough air. Her chest felt crushed, ribs tight around lungs that wouldn’t work properly. The fire was everywhere now, reflecting off the blood, off the spikes, off Ivy’s empty eyes that looked too much like her mother’s empty eyes in that twisted metal coffin.
“I can’t,” Melena gasped, pressing her palms against her temples as her fire raged around her. “I can’t, I can’t, I can’t—”
The flames were climbing the walls now, feeding on her panic, turning the perfect chamber into an inferno. She was going to burn the whole place down, burn herself alive, burn everything until there was nothing left but ash and—
“Melena.”
The voice cut through her spiral like ice water. Tiran stood in the chamber entrance, violet eyes reflecting the firelight, looking like he’d walked straight through hell to get there. Which, she realized dimly, he probably had.
“Princess,” he said again. “You need to breathe.”
But Melena couldn’t. Her fire was still raging and she could feel herself coming apart at the seams. Everything hurt. Everything burned. And Ivy was still dead, and her mother was still dead, and—
Tiran stepped through the flames like they were nothing, violet eyes never leaving her face. The fire should have driven him back — Melena had summoned the Imperial flames, flames hotter than any other fire in Remnia. It didn’t. He kept coming until he was close enough that she could see her own reflection in his eyes — wild, broken, desperate. His shirt was singed at the edges, ash streaking across his cheekbones, but he didn’t seem to notice. Didn’t seem to care. 
“Look at me.” His hands cupped her face, warm against her tear-streaked skin, thumbs brushing away her tears. “Just look at me, Melena.”
Not Princess. Not Your Highness. Just Melena. 
“There you are,” he whispered, resting his forehead against hers as she looked up at him. Their breath mingled in the heat. His violet eyes were so close she could see the flecks of gold around the pupils. To anyone watching, it would look like he was about to kiss her. Melena’s fists curled into his jacket, pulling him closer. She wanted to disappear into him, to let him take the weight of her grief and carry it somewhere she couldn't feel it anymore.
“I know,” he said softly, his lips barely an inch from hers. She could feel the shape of the words against her mouth, could taste the smoke and ash on his breath from walking through her flames. “I know it hurts. I know you want to fall apart.”
His voice dropped lower, so quiet she had to strain to hear it over the blood rushing in her ears.
“But you’ll have time to grieve later. Don’t let her death be for their entertainment.”
“How?” she gasped, voice breaking. “I can’t—I can’t control it.”
Tiran pulled back just enough to meet her eyes again, and what she saw there made her breath catch. Vulnerability. Fear. The same helplessness she felt, reflected back at her from someone who was always boasting about having the answers. 
“May I?” His voice softened, still holding her like she might shatter.
Then, his hands began to slide down her body as he lowered himself. Over her tear-stained cheeks, down the column of her throat, across her shoulders. His palms skimmed her arms, fingers trailing along the fabric of her uniform until he reached her hands, which were still fisted in his jacket. Gently, carefully, he untangled her grip and brought her hands to his, holding them between his palms like they were something precious.
Melena's knees buckled, her body instinctively moving to follow him down. She wanted to collapse beside him on the cold floor, to curl up in the space between the blood and the spikes and never have to stand again. But Tiran didn't move with her. Didn't pull her down with him. His hands stayed steady around hers, supporting her weight without forcing her to fall.
And that's when it hit her.
He wasn’t falling with her. He wasn’t collapsing.
He was kneeling to her.
Melena froze, breath catching as she stared down at him. Even on one knee, he was still tall enough that she didn’t have to look far to meet his gaze, but the position was unmistakable. 
He was bowing to the Imperial Princess while she was falling apart, choosing to serve even when she couldn't serve herself.
“May I take your flames, Your Imperial Highness?” he asked, her full title on his tongue with none of the mocking edge it usually carried. The words settled over her like a shroud, clamping down on the chaos in her head. Everyone bowed to her eventually — court, council, tutors, the endless parade of suck-ups back at the palace— but no one ever asked her permission first. No one ever asked if she wanted them to.
“I don’t—” Her voice cracked, the syllable torn in half. She tried to pull back, but Tiran’s hands stayed gentle on her cheeks,
“Let me,” he insisted, and she realized he wasn’t asking permission for himself. He was offering her a way out of her misery.
“Yes,” she whispered.
Tiran’s fire reached for hers, and the first touch made her entire body shudder. Not from pain, but from relief so intense it almost brought her to her knees again.
His flames were nothing like hers. Where her fire was jagged edges and burning rage, his was steady. It wrapped around her chaos like warm hands cupping something fragile, and suddenly the crushing weight in her chest shifted.
This wasn’t the fire that used to dance at his fingertips during those endless court dinners, teasing hers until she wanted to set his smug face ablaze. This wasn’t him showing off, wasn’t him trying to get under her skin or prove some point about how much better he was than her. 
No, this felt like Tiran Blackwood was trying to save her. 
She could feel his heartbeat through the connection between their flames, and it was so achingly familiar and so impossibly not her own that she wanted to scream. Instead, she clung to it with everything she had. The flames rushed between them, weaving in a pattern that was neither hers nor his but theirs. The Maze seemed to notice, the air tightening as if in disapproval — but all Melena cared about was the weight of Tiran’s fire drawing the poison out of her system, burning it clean.
The world tunneled down to a single point: her hands locked in his, her inferno curling around the blue-white core he offered like a lifeline. Her breathing slowed. The panic receded, not gone, but caged, held in place by the absolute, infuriating certainty of Tiran Blackwood’s focus.
For a long time, there was nothing else.
Then the fire guttered and died, leaving only the crackle of cooling stone and the slow, wet sound of her sobs and the rough scrape of his thumb across the back of her hand, back and forth, like a parent soothing a feverish child. Melena’s vision blurred to gray, then cleared, and she realized the walls weren’t closing in anymore. The air wasn’t so heavy. The light in the water had shifted from blood red to a dull, exhausted pink.
“I’m sorry,” she whispered. “I didn’t know she was here.”
Melena stared at Ivy’s face, at the way her teammate’s mouth was still open like she’d been about to say something. About to call for help, maybe. About to ask why. Maybe saying her last goodbyes before it all went to hell. A chunk of stone crashed down beside her, marble dust exploding across her vision. Melena jerked back, blinking, and that’s when she saw it — the wall behind Tiran sliding shut with grinding finality. More debris rained down as the ceiling shifted, the Maze rebuilding itself around them.
“Melena.” Tiran’s voice cut through the chaos. “We need to move. Right fucking now.”
She looked back toward where he’d entered. Nothing but solid stone, seamless as if there’d never been an opening at all. The only way out was forward, past Ivy, deeper into whatever hell the Maze had waiting.
“Shit,” she said, scrambling to her feet as another piece of ceiling crashed where she’d been kneeling.
“It’s closing in,” Tiran said, already moving toward the chamber’s far exit.
Melena looked at Ivy one more time, then knelt and gently closed her teammate’s eyes.
Then she stepped over the blood and followed him into the dark.
## Chapter 24: Katherine – The Third Challenge
Crack.
The sound wouldn’t stop. Every time Katherine closed her eyes — even for the split second it took to dodge falling debris — there it was. The sound of marble giving way under Beckett’s feet. The way his voice cut off mid-shout, surprised and sharp and then just… gone.
Crack.
She stumbled over a piece of shattered stone, caught herself against the wall, and kept running. The corridor stretched ahead of her, black marble slick with something that might’ve been water but probably wasn’t. Behind her, she could hear the Maze reshaping itself, swallowing the path she’d just taken.
“Katherine, watch the—”
That’s all Beckett had managed before the floor opened up like a mouth and ate him whole. One second he was there, pointing at some trap she’d missed, and the next there was just a hole and the sound of something heavy hitting stone far below.
She pressed her palm against her eyes, trying to stop the replay, and nearly ran straight into a wall that hadn’t been there a second ago. The Maze was herding her, just like it had herded everyone else. Funneling her toward whatever fresh hell it had waiting.
Crack.
Tears ran down her face, hot and stupid and completely useless. Old Prosperity didn’t cry during missions. Old Prosperity assessed, adapted, advanced. But Katherine couldn’t seem to stop the water leaking from her eyes any more than she could stop hearing Beckett die over and over again.
A chunk of ceiling crashed down where she’d been standing moments before. Katherine threw herself forward, boots skidding on the slick marble as she fought for balance. The corridor opened up ahead into what looked like a wider space — maybe stable ground, maybe another trap. She didn’t have a choice but to find out.
The chamber beyond was smaller than she’d hoped, just a circular room with three other exits. But the floor was solid black stone, no cracks, no give when she tested it with her weight. Katherine backed against the far wall, chest heaving, and finally let herself stop running.
Only then did she raise her hand to her forehead.
The salute was perfect — spine straight, shoulders back, hand positioned exactly as they’d drilled into her since Year One. Her face was streaked with tears and marble dust, her uniform torn at the shoulder where she’d scraped against a wall, but the salute was regulation perfect.
“Sergeant Beckett Rhodes,” she said, voice cracking on his name. “Terrain Division. Old Prosperity.”
She held the position for a full thirty seconds, the way her mother had taught her. Long enough to mean something. Long enough to hurt.
When she dropped her hand, the sound in her head finally quieted.
Crack.
Fuck. Not quiet enough.
Katherine started pacing. Three steps to the left wall, turn, three steps to the right. Her boots clicked against the stone in a rhythm that almost drowned out the sound of Beckett hitting bottom. Almost. She should be finding the others. That’s what a Queen Piece did — regrouped with her team, assessed the situation, made decisions. But which corridor led to them? The Maze had been shifting constantly, eating the paths behind her. For all she knew, she’d been running in circles while her teammates died one by one.
Crack.
“Stop,” she said out loud, voice echoing off the walls. “Just fucking stop.”
But it didn’t stop. And she didn’t know which way to go. And she couldn’t save Beckett because she’d hesitated, and she couldn’t save the others because she didn’t know where they were, and maybe that’s why she’d never tested for Sergeant. Maybe she’d always known she wasn’t leader material.
Katherine picked the middle corridor because it looked the most stable. Not because it might lead to the artifact — she wasn’t even thinking about the artifact right now. She needed to find Atlas. He’d know what to do. He always knew what to do.
Two steps in, something whistled past her ear. She threw up an ice shield on instinct, and whatever it was, shattered against the surface with a sound like breaking glass. Tiny metal fragments were scattered across the floor at her feet.
Darts. Poison-tipped, probably.
Katherine backed out of the corridor, heart hammering. The artifact could be down that passage. Could be anywhere. But she couldn’t check alone. Couldn’t risk it alone.
She tried the left corridor instead. The walls were smooth black marble, no obvious traps, but the passage curved so sharply she couldn’t see more than ten feet ahead. Each step echoed wrong, like the sound was bouncing off surfaces that shouldn’t exist.
A voice drifted from somewhere up ahead — human, male, calling out in pain.
“Help! Please, someone help me!”
Katherine froze. That could be Atlas. Could be Sebastian. Could be any of her teammates, hurt and trapped and needing her to—
Crack.
Beckett’s voice. Beckett calling for help before the floor swallowed him whole.
Katherine pressed her back against the wall, breathing hard. It wasn’t real. Couldn’t be real. The Maze was playing tricks, using voices to lure her into whatever fresh hell it had waiting.
But what if it wasn’t a trick? What if one of her teammates really was hurt, really needed help, and she was standing here doing nothing because she was too scared to trust her own judgment?
“I’m coming,” she called out, moving deeper into the corridor.
The voice came again, closer now. “Katherine? Katherine, is that you?”
Sebastian.
Katherine broke into a run, ice forming around her hands as she rounded the curve—
And slammed face-first into a wall.
The impact sent her reeling backward, stars exploding across her vision. Blood ran from her nose, warm and copper-tasting. The corridor behind her was gone. Solid wall, seamless marble, like it had never existed.
“Fuck,” she gasped, pressing her sleeve to her nose.
She’d been here before, in a way. The old study alcoves at the University, the ones everyone joked were haunted because your own footsteps echoed from behind when you walked past them at night. She’d never believed in ghosts — not until the night she pulled an all-nighter and heard her own voice whispering the answer to a test question she hadn’t studied for. The professor had told her later it was just stress. Sleep deprivation. The brain cannibalizing itself to keep going.
Katherine told herself the Maze was just another version of that.
Not that she believed it though.
She pressed forward anyway, half-crawling, half-staggering as the world spun and the blood soaked into her sleeve. There was no light source, but the marble glimmered faint blue as she passed, like it was illuminated from inside her own skull. She followed the voice — not because she believed it was real, but because if it wasn’t, it might still lead her to the next trap. Maybe it would even get her to Atlas.
The corridor sloped down, the air growing colder and more sour with every step, like a tomb slowly filling with ice. The voice ahead changed timbre — now it was her mother, calling for her from the porch after dark. Then her father, the time he’d scraped his hand open on barbed wire and tried not to let her see how much it hurt. Then Beckett again, but softer, almost gentle.
“You did your best,” the echo told her. “You did what you could.”
She almost laughed. The sound came out cracked, wrong.
Crack.
The voices kept calling and Katherine kept walking. One foot in front of the other. That’s what they drilled into you at the University when everything went to shit. When your squad was scattered and your comm was dead and you couldn’t think past the ringing in your ears. You moved forward until you found something that made sense.
Blood dripped from her nose onto the marble. The corridor blurred at the edges, walls breathing like they were alive. But her feet knew where to go. Left turn here. Straight for thirty paces. Right at the junction.
She’d memorized it. When? The Welcome Feast. Zephyr’s face going white, his chair scraping against marble as he shot to his feet. Everyone staring at him, but Katherine had been looking at the wall behind the Sanctum thrones. The sigils carved into stone, ancient and wrong, pulsing with that glow that matched the voices in the hall.
Don’t trust them.
That’s what Zephyr had mouthed to her. But Katherine’s brain was fracturing now, memory bleeding into reality, and the words twisted in her head. Don’t trust who? The Sanctum? The sigils? Or was he trying to tell her something else entirely?
Don’t trust them.
The spiral pattern with jagged lines. She’d seen it in her dreams since she was a child, always right before she woke up screaming. And now here it was, carved into the Maze’s heart, leading her deeper with every step.
Zephyr had looked terrified. His hands shaking as Sebastian pulled him away. Katherine stumbled, caught herself against the wall. The marble was warm under her palm, pulsing like a heartbeat.
Don’t trust them.
But she was already following.
Had she been following since she stepped into the Maze? Or long before that?
The pattern burned behind her eyes. The spiral. The jagged lines. The same ones that lived in her dreams long before the Tournament ever began.
Maybe it had always been leading her here.
But she kept walking.
Crack.
“Katherine.” Her mother’s voice now, floating from ahead. “You’re almost there, baby girl.”
Katherine’s vision wavered. The walls were silver now, not black. Or maybe they’d always been silver and she was just seeing them right for the first time. Her mother was back home, probably watching the broadcasts, probably sick with worry about her daughter bleeding in a death maze.
But her voice sounded so real.
“I know you’re not real,” Katherine said to the empty corridor. “But I’m gonna follow you anyway.”
Because the alternative was standing still. And standing still meant listening to Beckett hit the ground forever. Standing still meant thinking about how she’d hesitated, how she’d calculated instead of acted, how she was supposed to be their Queen but couldn’t save anyone.
Keep moving. That was the number one rule. When your brain stopped working, when the world stopped making sense, when everything you thought you knew turned to ash in your mouth — you kept moving until your training caught up with your panic.
The voices led her down. Always down. Toward whatever waited at the heart of this nightmare. Then there was a door.
Crack.
Katherine stared at it through the haze of blood loss and exhaustion. Smooth black stone, no handles, no hinges, just a perfect rectangle carved into the wall at the end of the corridor. The sigil pattern was etched around its frame — the same spiral with jagged lines she’d been following, but clearer now.
Don’t trust them.
Katherine was past caring about trust. Past caring about anything except completing the mission. She pressed her palm against the door.
It opened without resistance.
The chamber beyond was small, circular, lit by something she couldn’t identify. In the center sat a pedestal, and on the pedestal —
A crystal. About the size of her fist, dark blue shot through with silver veins that pulsed like a heartbeat. The moment Katherine stepped into the room, it flared brighter.
The artifact.
Katherine stumbled forward, legs giving out halfway across the room. She hit the ground hard, her already-damaged ribs screaming in protest. The taste of blood flooded her mouth once more as her teeth cut into her tongue, but she kept crawling. Blood and spit mixed on the floor as she hauled herself toward the pedestal.
Crack.
Still hearing Beckett fall. Still hearing the sound of her failures. She’d made it.
Katherine reached up, fingers closing around the crystal. The moment she touched its surface, the world exploded into light.
When her vision cleared, she was no longer alone.
An Elite mage stood before her, robes shimmering with contained magic. Their face was hidden behind a mask of silver mesh, but their voice carried clearly through the chamber.
“Congratulations, Queen Piece of Old Prosperity. You have successfully completed the Maze Challenge.”
Katherine pushed herself to her knees, the crystal clutched against her chest. The room spun around her, voices still whispering at the edges of her hearing. “Is... is it over?”
“The challenge is concluded. Old Prosperity claims victory.” The mage’s head tilted slightly. “The artifact you hold grants a single privilege for the next challenge. You may choose your partner for Burden and Bonds.”
Choose her partner. Katherine’s mind struggled to process the words through the fog of pain and madness. She could pick anyone. Anyone at all.
Crack.
Beckett’s dead. Beckett’s dead and I could have saved him but I didn’t move fast enough, didn’t think fast enough—
Reign. Reign who knew about the nightmares, who stayed up with her after the lake thing when she was seven and couldn’t stop shaking. But what if I get her killed too? What if I hesitate again and—
No. Focus. Choose someone. Anyone.
Sebastian? Sebastian who made stupid jokes even when people were dying, who tried to save Zephyr by failing on purpose. But he’s reckless, God he’s so fucking reckless, and I can’t — I can’t watch another person fall because I—
Crack.
Stop. Stop thinking about the sound. Think about — Minho. Minho was smart. Fast. Wouldn’t hesitate like she did. Wouldn’t freeze up when someone needed saving. But he’d throw me away if the math said to and maybe that’s what I deserve after Beckett—
Why can’t the sound just stop? Why won’t it stop?
Dax. Dax who used to follow Atlas around like a puppy when they were kids. But he looks at me like I stole something from his brother and maybe I did, maybe I took Atlas away and now Beckett’s dead and it’s all connected somehow, it’s all my fault and—
Nova. Sweet Nova who saved her life. Too young to die because of my choices, too young to pay for my failures —
Zephyr. He tried to warn her. Tried to tell her about the sigils, and she didn’t listen. I never listen, I just follow orders and people die and the sound won’t stop and I need it to stop, please just make it stop—
Crack. Crack. Crack.
Atlas.
Atlas always knows what to do. Atlas makes the sound go away when he’s there, and maybe if I choose him, the voices will stop and Beckett will stop falling and —
“Stop,” Katherine whispered, pressing her palms against her ears. “Please just stop.”
Her hands clenched her hair. She could barely hear the mage speaking anymore. The world narrowed down to blood, spinning walls, and the artifact in her hand.
Maybe if I choose him, he will fix it.
Fix everything.
Fix me.
“Your choice, Queen Piece?” the mage prompted.
Crack.
“At…..las…” Katherine lifted her head. “Atlas…Tolliver.”
## Chapter 25: Melena – The Third Challenge
Movement. Arms around her. The smell of smoke and ash.
She was being carried. 
“—need to keep moving. The walls are shifting again.”
Her body felt wrong. Empty. Like someone had reached inside her chest and scooped out everything that made her fire burn. The Imperial flames. She’d used them. All of them. Every drop of power her bloodline had given her, poured out in that chamber with Ivy’s blood.
Ivy.
“—pulse is barely there—”
“—losing her—”
“—stay with me, Princess, don’t you fucking dare—”
Tiran’s voice, cracking with something that might have been terror. Her chest wasn’t moving right. Each breath felt like drowning in reverse.
#
Pain. Sharp, sudden, cutting through the darkness.
Melena could feel it happening. Her internal fire guttering like a candle in the wind. Her heart, which should have been burning steady and strong, was skipping beats. Missing one. Missing two.
Thump.
Nothing.
Thump-thump.
Nothing. Nothing. Nothing.
The cold was spreading from her chest outward, creeping down her arms, into her fingers. Her body temperature was dropping degree by degree. She could feel it all, every failing system, every shutting down process.
“There you are.” Tiran’s face above her, streaked with ash and something wet. Tears? “Christ, Melena, your heart stopped. Your fucking heart stopped.”
It had. She’d felt it. Felt the moment her heart forgot how to beat, felt her blood stop moving, felt the fire that made her who she was just…stop.
She tried to speak. Nothing came out but a wheeze.
“Don’t.” His hands pressed against her chest, right over her heart. She could feel the warmth of his palms against skin that was going cold. “Don’t try to talk. Just breathe. Just keep breathing.”
But breathing hurt. Each inhale was like drowning, each exhale like dying. Her lungs felt heavy, waterlogged. 
Thump.
Long pause.
Thump-thump.
Wrong. All wrong.
She couldn’t feel her legs. Couldn’t feel her arms. The cold was spreading faster now, her body giving up on heating itself. She was freezing from the inside out, and there was nothing she could do to stop it.
#
Voices, distant and distorted.
“—told you splitting up after we found you was—”
“—lost three more in the eastern corridor—”
“—Princess isn’t going to make it—”
The last words cut through everything else. Isn’t going to make it.
Melena forced her eyes open. Her body was trying to restart itself, fire flickering weakly in her chest like a dying ember someone was trying to blow back to life. But everything was wrong. Her shoulder was screaming — dislocated again, probably from when Tiran had to grab her. Her left arm hung useless, sending sharp jolts of pain through her every time they moved.
And they were moving. Fast.
Blonde hair, green eyes — Seraphina, running beside them. Two other faces she didn’t recognize, keeping pace. Everyone’s breathing hard, looking over their shoulders like something was chasing them.
“What—” Melena started, but speaking sent agony through her shoulder.
“The walls are collapsing,” Seraphina panted. “Whole sections coming down. We have to keep moving.”
Melena could feel her heartbeat trying to stabilize, her internal temperature slowly climbing back toward normal. But her body felt like it belonged to someone else. She couldn’t move her fingers, couldn’t turn her head without everything spinning.
“How many?” she whispered.
“Melena, don’t—” Tiran started, his breathing ragged from carrying her while running.
“How many of our people are gone?”
The silence stretched too long. Seraphina stumbled, caught herself. Two other faces looked away.
Too many. Too fucking many.
Her vision grayed at the edges as her shoulder sent another wave of agony through her system.
#
Falling.
Always falling.
The world spinning away from her, voices becoming echoes, Tiran’s arms the only thing keeping her tethered to anything real.
“No, no, no.” His voice, desperate now. “Stay here. Stay with me.”
She could feel herself slipping. Not just consciousness — everything. Like her soul was trying to leave her body and she didn’t have the strength to hold on.
“I can’t,” she breathed.
“Yes, you can.”
“It hurts.”
“I know. I know it hurts. But you have to fight.”
Fighting. She’d been fighting her whole life. Fighting for her grandmother’s approval, fighting for her place in succession, fighting to save Elias. She was so tired of fighting.
“Let me go,” she whispered.
“Never.”
#
The sound of running water. Or was that blood?
Her heartbeat was wrong. Too slow. Skipping beats like it couldn’t remember the rhythm.
“—extraction point, but she needs medical—”
“—won’t make it that far—”
“—bleeding internally—”
Bleeding. That explained why everything tasted like poison.
Tiran’s arms were around her, carrying her like she weighed nothing. 
“Tiran.” Her voice was barely a whisper.
“I’m here.”
“If I don’t—”
“Don’t.”
“If I don’t make it out, tell Cordelia—”
“You’re going to tell her yourself.”
“Tell her I’m sorry.”
His grip tightened. “Melena, you’re scaring me.”
Good. Someone should be scared. Someone should understand that this was it, that the Imperial Princess was about to die in a fucking maze because she couldn’t control her own power.
#
Light. Harsh and artificial.
Medics. Voices shouting orders. Hands reaching for her, trying to take her away from Tiran.
“No,” she gasped, clinging to his shirt with fingers that barely worked. “Don’t leave me.”
“I won’t,” he said, but they were pulling him away, pulling her onto some kind of stretcher.
“Please.” The word broke coming out. “Please don’t leave me alone.”
The last thing she saw before the drugs took her under was Tiran fighting past the medics, his hand finding hers.
“I’ve got you,” he whispered. “I’m not going anywhere.”
But his voice sounded so far away.
Somewhere in the space between consciousness and nothing, Melena finally understood why Elias had come home from his Tournament with dead eyes and shaking hands. Why he’d disappeared for days at a time, why he’d flinched when she touched him, why he’d looked at her like he was seeing a ghost.
This. This was what broke him.
The knowledge that you could watch people die and keep breathing. That you could fail everyone who mattered and still have to wake up the next morning. That surviving meant carrying the weight of everyone who didn’t.
She was never going to forget Ivy’s face. Never going to stop hearing the sound of her teammates falling. Never going to forgive herself for not being fast enough, strong enough, good enough to save them.
Just like Elias never had.
The darkness pulled her under, and Melena let it take her, knowing she’d never really come back from this place.
Not all of her.
Not the parts that mattered.
## Chapter 26: Katherine
The floor was cracking under Beckett’s feet.
Katherine could see it happening in slow motion — the black marble spider-webbing outward, chunks breaking away, the hole opening like a hungry mouth. Beckett was pointing at something, his mouth moving, but she couldn’t hear him over the sound of stone giving way.
“Katherine, watch the—”
She reached for him, but her hands moved like they were underwater. Too slow.
Always too slow.
Beckett’s eyes went wide with surprise, then fear, then nothing as he plummeted into darkness.
The sound when he hit bottom — crack — echoed up from the depths.
“You could have saved him,” a voice whispered. “You should have saved him.”
She spun around, looking for the speaker, but there was only the hole and the sound of stone hitting stone and—
Katherine jolted awake with a scream that tore from her throat like broken glass.
Her room was dark, shadows moving wrong in the dim light filtering through the windows. Sweat soaked through her sleep clothes, hair sticking to her face, heart hammering against her ribs like it was trying to escape.
Crack.
Something moved in the corner of her vision — a figure by the window. Dark clothes, pale hair.
Intruder.
Katherine lunged for the nightstand where she’d left her dagger, frost already forming around her fingers. She was halfway across the bed when the figure spoke.
“Easy. It’s just me.”
Katherine froze, crystals still dancing around her hands. “Atlas?”
He sat in the window seat like he’d been there for hours, fully dressed in his Tournament uniform despite what had to be past midnight. Positioned where he could see both the door and the windows.
“What the fuck are you doing in my room?”
“Nico sent me.”
“Nico?” Katherine didn’t lower her hands. “Why?”
Atlas was quiet for a moment, silver eyes reflecting the dim light. “You’ve been screaming my name in your sleep. Loud enough to wake half the House. He asked me to... handle it. No one can sleep.”
A slow, sick heat crept up Katherine’s neck. She’d been trained to control shame — deny it, repress it, weaponize it — but right now, with Atlas quietly watching her from across the room, she couldn’t stop herself from curling in on instinct, like a field mouse waiting for the hawk’s shadow to pass. She tried to steady her breath, but each inhale was a little too sharp, each exhale catching on the jagged memory of Beckett’s body tumbling away.
“So you decided to break in,” she said, voice struggling for strength and failing. A trickle of frost slid down her palm before she willed it away.
“I knocked. You didn’t answer.”
Katherine raised an eyebrow. “How’d you get in? You don’t know the code.”
“Nico gave it to me. Said, ‘If she blows up, you’ll be the only one she doesn’t kill on accident.’” Atlas’s lips twitched, almost a smile, but the humor didn’t reach his eyes. “He was concerned—”
“Concerned about me having nightmares? Join the fucking club,” Katherine muttered as she sat on the edge of the bed. “Half the people in here have nightmares. Mine just happen to be on the louder end. Sorry for waking you. You can go back to bed now.”
“Katherine,” Atlas took a step toward her, but she shook her head. 
“No,” she whispered. “You don’t get to do that here.”
“Do what?”
“Show up like everything is fine,” Katherine chuckled sadly. “You always show up once something has happened. Never during. Never before. Always after.”
She pressed her fists into her eyes, pressing until fireworks spattered behind her lids, until she could pretend there was nothing but the sparks and not the image of Beckett’s face right before it went slack. “You always—” she started, but the rest stuck in her throat, a lump accusation she hadn't trained herself to swallow. “You can tell Nico I’m fine. Next time I’ll punch a pillow instead of screaming.”
She expected Atlas to leave. Instead, he crossed the room and sat on the floor at the foot of her bed, legs drawn up, arms folded over his knees. For a while, neither of them spoke. The silence was thick and uneven, the kind that pressed on your eardrums until you wanted to scream just to shatter it. In the dark, she could almost see the snow white of his hair falling into his face. Now that the world was silent again, Katherine couldn’t remember the last time she’d seen him wear his hair down — military regulations usually prevented her from seeing much of him anyway, but still.
She almost reached out to touch it — just to make sure he was really here and not another hallucination conjured by her idiot trauma brain — but stopped herself at the last second. She was pretty sure if she touched Atlas, she’d fall apart in a way that wasn’t recoverable.
“I didn’t want to come here,” Atlas said, after a while. “Your nightmares are your business. But you scared Minho. And he knows you don’t scare easy. So.” He shrugged, like that explained everything.
Katherine snorted, but it came out shaky. “Didn’t realize Minho cared.”
“He doesn’t,” Atlas said, and the faintest trace of a grin flickered at the corner of his mouth. Then, quieter: “But I do.”
That hurt more than she expected. She wanted to be angry at him—at the way he always managed to make her feel like a twelve-year-old with skinned knees, desperate for someone to say she was going to be okay. And his lips were on hers.

## Chapter 28: Melena
“Fuck this place. Fuck this Tournament. Fuck everything.”
Melena sat on the floor of some random hallway in Havenwood at 4 AM, still covered in dirt and dried blood, the medics hadn’t bothered to clean off properly. They’d patched her up, reset her shoulder, pumped her full of healing potions, and sent her on her way with a cheerful — “Your flames will take care of the rest!”
Right. Because fire could fix everything.
She picked at a piece of debris under her fingernail — probably from when the ceiling came down on Jin and Alexei. Her fire was doing its job — she could feel the warmth moving through her bruises, stitching up the internal damage, getting her body back to fighting condition. But her head? Her head was fucked.
Every time she closed her eyes, she saw Ivy’s face. Heard Jin’s screaming. Felt her heart stop beating in her chest while Tiran carried her through those collapsing corridors like she was already dead.
Maybe she should have stayed dead. Would’ve saved everyone a lot of trouble.
Her flames flickered around her hands, restless and angry. She couldn’t control them properly — they kept sparking higher when she got pissed, which was constantly now. The doctors said it was normal, that trauma could make your element unstable for a while but Melena didn’t want to admit that she was traumatized. 
Seraphina and Sine had filled in the gaps when she woke up in medical – told her how half their Pieces were also injured. Like that was supposed to make her feel better. Like knowing other people failed too would somehow erase the fact that she'd stood there panicking while her Pieces were struggling.
And Tiran? The man who'd carried her bleeding body through collapsing corridors, who'd held her hand while she was dying, who'd promised he wouldn't leave her alone?
He hadn't come to see her. Not once.
Three hours since discharge, and nothing. No visit, no message, no “hey, glad you're not dead.” Just silence. Which was probably for the best because what the fuck was she supposed to say to him anyway? Thanks for watching me have a complete mental breakdown? Sorry, you had to carry my corpse around?
But, God, she wanted him to come anyway.

Not after weeks of Palace etiquette lessons, not after that month-long stint in Shadowmere with a fever that almost killed her. This was a different kind of tired, the kind that gnawed at the inside of your skull and left everything else hollow.

Caelum.
Dark hair, dark eyes, that same unsettling smile that never quite reached his eyes. He looked exactly the same as he had in Shadowmere, except now she could see his pointed canine teeth when he spoke.

She wanted to ask what he meant, but the words twisted and stuck. Instead, she said, “You talk like you know everything.”
“I don’t know everything. But I know more than you.”
“Does it help? Knowing more?” She let her head rest against the wall. “Because it sure as hell hasn’t helped me.”
He considered. “It helps with the nightmares, sometimes. And when it doesn’t, there’s liquor. Or other things.”
“Great, so I can look forward to alcoholism or death. Just what I always wanted.”
“It’s better than the alternative. You could still be dead.”
“For all you know, I am dead,” Melena said. “Maybe this is the afterlife, and I get to spend it with your smug face for eternity.”
“Wouldn’t that be a fitting punishment for both of us.”
“You’re a real asshole, you know that?” 
Caelum nodded. “They say it runs in the family. But you should sleep. You’ll need strength for tomorrow.”
She snorted. “What’s tomorrow? Another fucking challenge? Can’t wait.”
“Not a challenge,” Caelum said. “A reckoning. The Maze took more from you than any of them realize. Now you have to decide what to do with what’s left.”
Melena let her eyelids drift shut, if only for a second. “Already decided, didn’t I?”
“Did you?” Caelum asked, and for once, his voice was gentle.
She didn’t answer. She didn’t have to.
He stood, brushing invisible dust from his knees. “Try not to burn the place down before sunrise,” he said.
“No promises.”
She heard his footsteps fade. The hallway felt emptier than before, but at least she walked away from the conversation knowing one thing for sure. 
Melena Lockwood was not ready to die. 
## Chapter 29: Katherine – The Fourth Challenge 
Katherine had chosen Atlas as her partner while half out of her mind with grief. Smart? Probably not. But when the energy tether wrapped around her wrist in the arena, connecting her to him two feet away, she realized just how fucked they both were.
The air hummed with magic so thick it made her teeth ache. Tiered seating rose around them like an amphitheater, packed with spectators who'd come to watch them die. In the middle of the arena, two pedestals rose from white sand. One held a crystal that pulsed with white light—theirs. The other gleamed crimson.
“Your objective,” Nico's voice echoed from speakers embedded in the arena walls, “retrieve your team's artifact from the center platform while preventing the opposing team from reaching theirs. A winner will be named when a team has a pair return the artifact back to their teams starting position.”
The band of crackling blue energy around Katherine's wrist went hot as something slammed into her head like a freight train.
Atlas.
Not his voice. His entire brain, apparently. The way he catalogued threats—London Town pairs sorted by element and skill level in the time it took her to blink. Combat drill muscle memory that wasn't hers was layering over her own movements until she couldn't tell if the impulse to shift her stance was coming from her training or his. His ribs aching from some old injury she'd never taken.
Katherine.
Her name, but loaded with about fifteen different meanings, she didn't have time to decode. Relief, terror, responsibility—all of it dropping on her like dead weight. His heartbeat echoing in her chest. Adrenaline that tasted like his, not hers.
Breathe, something said in her head, and she realized she'd stopped. When she pulled air in, his lungs moved too. Automatic. No thought required.
“The bonds will link not just your bodies, but your minds and elements. What one feels, the other experiences. What one thinks, the other knows. There will be nowhere to hide.”
Atlas's frost grabbed at hers before she could stop it. Ice started crawling up both their arms in spirals she wasn't making. Her element had apparently decided it belonged to him now.
“Partners who cannot synchronize will find their bonds becoming a burden rather than a strength.”
Atlas's memories crashed into her—last night, his hands, the way she'd fallen apart. Her face went hot as she realized he was getting her embarrassment in real time, tasting every mortifying second of it.
“The challenge begins—”
A horn went off. 
“—now.”
She was standing still one second and the next she was stumbling forward as the tether yanked her along. Around them, twenty-two other Pieces had the same brilliant idea—run for the center, grab the crystal, win. 
Easy.
Nothing about this was going to be easy.
Katherine slammed into Atlas as they both tried to go different directions. His elbow caught her ribs. Pain shot through her chest and immediately bounced back at her through their connection. They weren't the only ones struggling. All around them, pairs stumbled and fell as they tried to coordinate movements they'd never practiced. A London Town pair went down hard to their left, the girl screaming as her partner's sudden direction change sent them both sprawling into the sand. One of the Old Prosperity pairs—Katherine thought it might be Nova and Tegan—had stopped moving entirely, just standing there trying to figure out how to take a single step together.
Left foot, Atlas thought at her. Move on three. One, two—
Katherine moved on two accidentally. They collided again, and this time they both went down, knees hitting the sand hard enough to send shock waves through the mental connection.
The arena was enormous—Katherine could see that now, sprawled on her back with Atlas's weight pressing down on her chest. Three football fields at least, with massive stone walls rising up around them and tiered seating packed with spectators. The white crystal that marked their target pulsed from a platform in the center, maybe a hundred yards away. 
Get up, Atlas thought, but he wasn't moving either. Katherine could feel his frustration bleeding through the tether, mixing with her own until she couldn't tell which of them wanted to scream more.
You get up, she thought back. You're the one on top of me.
Your fault. You moved early.
Your fault for giving a bad countdown.
Around them, other pairs were having better luck. Melena and Tiran had found their rhythm somehow, moving together as they advanced across the sand. Behind them, another London Town pair—Katherine didn't recognize either of them—had figured out how to at least stand up together. 
How are they so good at this? Katherine wondered.
They don’t have two left feet, Atlas replied grimly. We need to move.
Maybe we should figure out how first.
On three, Atlas tried again. Real three this time. We stand up together. Ready?
Ready.
One.
Katherine tensed.
Two.
She could feel Atlas doing the same.
Three.
They moved together this time, the tether swinging between them as they rose to their feet. For a moment, Katherine felt a surge of triumph.
Then she saw the artifacts.
Both crystals—the white one that marked Old Prosperity's target and the crimson one meant for London Town—flickered like dying light bulbs.
And vanished.
The white crystal materialized on a platform fifty yards to their right. The crimson one appeared somewhere behind them, near the starting line where London Town had begun, based on the way some of the Pieces turned their bodies. Sebastian stood twenty yards ahead with Zephyr, flames guttering around their joined hands as they processed the fact that they'd been sprinting toward nothing. 
The targets move, Atlas realized. How the—
That's when the blonde girl from London Town hit the invisible wall.
Crack.
“Katherine, watch the—” Beckett's voice in her head rang out. 
No no no—
But the panic was already climbing her throat, choking her, because around the arena, everyone was falling. Another pair slammed into a barrier she couldn't see —crack— and all she could think about was Beckett pointing at something, trying to warn her, and she'd been standing there like an idiot.
Kamal and Justin went down hard. 
Crack. 
Another London Town girl stumbled, crying out as her knee twisted. 
Crack. 
Just a joint popping, but Katherine's brain kept translating it wrong.
Sebastian was moving his hands too fast, gesturing at Zephyr the same way Beckett had been trying to explain the trap she'd been too stupid to understand. Everyone was trying to warn each other about shit Katherine couldn't see, couldn't predict, couldn't stop.
Just like the Maze.
Katherine.
Atlas's voice was in her head, but it sounded underwater. 
I can't make it stop, she gasped, pressing her hands to her ears.
Calm down, Atlas tried, but she could feel his own control starting to fray as her emotions tore through the connection between them.
I can't, she thought back. It keeps happening, they keep falling, and I can't save any of them—
Her frost surged without permission, ice shooting up her arms in jagged bursts that hurt like hell. Through the tether, Atlas's element responded to hers, feeding off her panic, spiraling beyond either of their control.
Katherine, you need to calm down—
But she couldn't calm down because she could feel his fear now, too. Not just for her—of her. And that just proved what she'd always known about herself. 
She was broken. Dangerous. Couldn't be trusted with anyone's life.
The tether around her wrist started burning.
And Atlas’s walls came down.
She wouldn’t have believed it, but then memories that could only be his began to play out with every breath she took. 
Beckett reporting for duty on his first day, nervous as hell but trying to hide it behind a cocky grin.
Atlas promoting him to Sergeant because Minho pressured him into giving the kid a chance.
Dax teaching him how to read terrain maps in the field.
Atlas and Minho writing the letter to his parents after the Maze.
Katherine, that's not— Atlas tried, but she could taste the truth underneath his words
She doubled over, clutching her head. She was going to collapse. She was going to puke or scream or both and it would be on camera, for the whole world to dissect, just like every other time she lost her shit in front of an audience. She clawed at her own arm, nails digging into the skin above the tether, but the pain only fed the feedback loop. Atlas’s panic was spiking, ricocheting around in her skull with her own. They were both going to lose it, and the challenge had barely started.
Katherine didn't know who hit the ground first, but suddenly she was face-down in the sand, nose filling with the iron taste of blood and cold. Her fingers wouldn’t respond. Atlas was next to her, but not moving, not even shaking. Nothing about him ever looked out of control. Not until now. Black veins pulsed through the frost, every capillary tracing up his throat and over his jaw in a pattern she’d never seen. The look on his face—slack-jawed, eyes wide—wasn’t even pain. It was more like surprise. Like he’d never considered that his own element could betray him.
“Atlas?” It came out as a raw, scraped gasp.
Nicholas, he rasped in her head, the words slurred by static. It’s just a feedback loop. I can handle it.
She wasn’t sure he could. Ice started to form at the corners of his mouth and eyelids, crunching audibly when he blinked. He tried to push himself to his knees, but his limbs shook so badly Katherine could feel them vibrating through the tether. Around them, the chaos of the arena was ramping up. Pairs sprinted for their targets, tripping and colliding, curses floating over the sand. Melena and Tiran were closing in on the white crystal, just a few dozen yards ahead. They moved like a single body—awkward, but together, Tiran pulling Melena by the tether. 
London Town was going to win the challenge. 
The crack came again—close, inside her skull this time, close enough to make her retch. She tasted bile and ice. For a second, Katherine thought she might pass out. Maybe that was better. Maybe, if she played dead, the crack would finally disappear. 
I’ve been trying to avoid your thoughts in my head, but damn it, you are insufferable. 
The voice sounded so much like Atlas. She needed it to be Atlas. 
If you kill my brother, I will haunt you for the rest of your life, Little Frost. Do you understand me?
Not Atlas.
Dax. 
But that didn't make sense. Dax couldn't access their connection. Unless—
Unless being bonded to Atlas meant she was bonded to everything Atlas was bonded to.
I’m not —
Killing him? Find my voice, Little Frost. 
Katherine turned her head as much as she could from her position on the sand, scanning the chaos of the arena until she found him. Dax was maybe fifty yards away, tethered to Houston, both of them frozen mid-sprint toward what looked like Old Prosperity’s target. But while Houston was tugging at the tether, clearly trying to keep moving, Dax had stopped completely. His body was rigid, his free hand pressed to his chest like he was having a heart attack.
If this is what heartbreak is like, I don’t think I need to ever experience it, Dax's voice cut through with pain. 
Katherine watched as Dax's body jerked, his back arching the exact same way Atlas's had moments before. Houston was shouting something at him, probably asking what the hell was wrong, but Dax wasn't responding. His eyes were locked on Katherine. 
This is not how I planned on dying. 
She flexed her hand, tried to send a pulse of thought down the tether. It backfired instantly, the sensation doubling, tripling, as it bounced off of Atlas and back to her. 
What part of you’re going to kill him did I not make clear?
Katherine dug her fingers into her palm, searching for something solid, anything that wasn’t made of ice. Her whole body vibrated with Atlas’s pain, but Dax’s panic blared in her skull, overlaying the arena’s chaos like a siren. There wasn’t enough room in her head for both Tolliver brothers and her own existential spiral.
She tried to shut the connection down, to lock herself behind layers of ice, but the bond had no off switch. Every attempt to distance herself just made the pain worse. Her teeth ground together; she could taste blood now, sharp as a bitten tongue. The sand where her cheek pressed was freezing under her, and she was pretty sure that was her own fault.
Atlas was struggling to get his breathing back under control. She could feel the lines of his thoughts—orderly, relentless, like a man fixing a machine while it exploded in his hands. He counted heartbeats. He recited lists of survival priorities. He even muttered the opening lines of the Remnian code of conduct, over and over, and it was so pathetic and so him she started to laugh, which set off another spike of ice down her sternum.
She forced herself upright. Atlas mimicked her movements before she could even protest that he needed to stay down. They were shaking, both of them. On the far end of the arena, a Sanctum official shouted a warning, but it was just background noise to the tidal wave inside her. She locked eyes with Atlas. Just for a second, the world narrowed to the twin mirrors of his gaze. The pain, the humiliation, the absolute chaos. Neither of them had their shit together. 
Not anymore. 
Katherine wasn’t sure they ever did. 
She could hear Dax screaming in the distance. Not literally, but the emotional echo through Atlas’s memory. Dax as a kid, his voice reverberating down the hallways of the base, always too loud, always too much, until Atlas would drag him into a supply closet and threaten to ice over his entire collection of contraband energy drinks if he didn’t shut the fuck up. Training missions, the two of them side by-side, Dax’s voice always in the background, a goddamn metronome of chaos. Now, it was a siren.
She needed to get a grip. 
Atlas was still on his knees, cold sweat running down his face. In her own head, Katherine started to count. She needed to break the loop. The first thing you did in a panic attack was ground yourself—she’d learned that much from Reign after the lake incident. Find five things you could see. Four things you could touch. Three things you could hear. Two things you could smell. One thing you could taste. 
Sight: Atlas, doubled over and gripping the sand with white-knuckled fists. The blue tether sparking between them, arcing with static. Zephyr and Sebastian locked in a footrace with Melena and Tiran for the crystal. The silver flash of the Sanctum's staff uniforms on the sidelines, braced for disaster. The empty stands above, the crowd a blur of color and intention.
Touch: The scalding burn of the tether on her wrist. The raw sting of her own blood under her fingernails. The grit of sand digging into her kneecaps. The cool pressure of frost blooming beneath her skin.
Sound: The white noise rush of the crowd, the ragged pull of Atlas’s breath, the distant whump of bodies slamming into sand, the electronic buzz of the announcement system, stubby shouts from the sidelines.
Smell: ozone, sharp and metallic, riding above the salt sweat of her skin; the faint, bitter tang of fried circuits, like the air right after lightning; a whiff of blood, probably hers.
Taste: copper and bile in her mouth, slick and sour. Also the memory of black coffee, Atlas’s, lingering like a ghost between her teeth.
She focused on that last bit. Pure spite, the way he always loaded his mug until it could qualify as an acid weapon. It was something real, something not made of panic or frost. She clung to it. Dug in. Forced herself upright, even as her legs threatened to betray her all over again.
Atlas was still on his knees, but his breathing was less ragged now. He looked up at her, white hair plastered to his forehead with sweat and sand. His mouth twitched in a way that might've been a laugh if he wasn't busy trying to survive her breakdown.
“I’m fine,” she said, out loud, her voice shaking. “We’re fine. We’re fucking fine.”
“If you say so,” he stammered, trying to catch his breath. 
You’re a goddamn mess, Little Frost, Dax muttered in the back of her head. 
Thank you. She looked over at him again. 
It was the first time in her life anyone had called her that and made it sound like a compliment.
The arena was chaos, but a plan started to assemble itself in the weird static void between her and Atlas’s skulls. The white crystal was still somewhere to their right, but it was cycling through platforms at random now—every time a pair got within ten meters, the artifact would disappear and reappear at another location across the field. Whoever designed this had a sick sense of humor.
Katherine shoved herself to her feet and found that Atlas was already mirroring her, the two of them swaying like drunks at a funeral. She tried to take a step and the tether jerked him forward, nearly sending them both into the sand again. It was like walking on ice with your ankles zip-tied together. But if she focused—if she projected her movement a split second before she made it—Atlas’s body would respond. She could feel the microsecond echo of intent, the strange double vision of two brains piloting one muscle group. If she moved on three, but thought about it on two, he’d move with her.
She tried it. Head fake left, real move right. Atlas caught the drift before she made it and adjusted his weight. They lurched, but they didn’t fall. Atlas shifted his weight, and Katherine felt it as an electric tingle up her leg. She let her intent float just ahead of her footfalls—like a trickle of Frost before the full freeze. He picked up the signal. The second step was… almost smooth. By the third, she didn’t even have to think about it. Their strides synced, the tether’s pull fading to the background. They moved. Not perfectly, not like the London Town pair burning toward the next spawn point, but together.
The white artifact appeared fifty yards ahead of them, pulsing brighter. The sand glittered blue-white, and Katherine saw the catch: each time the artifact relocated, the ground beneath it shimmered with a threadwork of ice that wasn’t hers. She narrowed her eyes, squinting against the glare.
“That’s a trap,” she muttered. “Has to be.”
Atlas grunted agreement, and she caught a flash of satisfaction through the tether. He was already scanning for the next tell, double-counting the steps to the target, mapping out the likely kill zone. She tried to do the same, but her head throbbed with the effort. Maybe it was the feedback loop. Maybe it was just her own goddamn paranoia. 
She was so focused on mapping the trap that she almost missed the change in the crowd’s pitch. The noise, a dull roar moments before, spiked into a shrill, collective intake of breath. Katherine’s head snapped up just in time to see Melena and Tiran—still joined at the wrist—vault over another pair of fallen Pieces and slam into the platform where the crimson crystal was pulsing. They didn’t hesitate or slow, just went full tilt, Melena’s hair a streak of red, Tiran’s face set like he was running at the gates of hell.
How the hell had they gotten there so fast? Katherine’s brain struggled to process the timeline. She and Atlas had barely made it five steps; the London Town team had crossed half the arena. Impossible, unless—
They’d never stopped. 
## Chapter 30: Melena – The Fourth Challenge
The red light on the camera had been blinking for six hours.
Six hours since Challenge Four ended. Six hours since they'd dragged her out of the Maze more dead than alive. Six hours since the Sanctum started loading bodies onto transports while the remaining Pieces watched from the hangar windows like they were supposed to learn something from it. Melena was still watching. Counting the seconds between blinks on that camera like a heartbeat she couldn't escape. 
One-two-three-four. 
One-two-three-four. 
She could see London Town's remaining Pieces scattered throughout the hangar — Maxim talking to Raven in low voices near the far wall, Seraphina sitting on the floor with her head in her hands, others she couldn't name because her brain was still too scrambled to process faces. Eight Pieces left. Half of them looked like they'd throw up if anyone asked them to move.
Melena included.
The Naming Ceremony was in less than an hour, and she hadn't even finished writing the letters yet. Eight families. Eight condolences she was supposed to compose while watching their children's bodies get loaded into cargo holds like freight. The Sanctum had been very clear — it would be broadcast live, every tear, every stumble, every moment she proved she wasn't good enough to keep her people alive.
Imperial Princess or not, she was theirs now.
At least when she was dead two days ago, she didn't have to think about this. Didn't have to find words to explain how Jin died or Alexei or Ivy or any of the other five Pieces whose names she kept forgetting because her brain wouldn't hold onto anything that wasn't survival.
She'd been trying to write the letters for the past hour. Had a bottle of whiskey she'd stolen from the medical bay and three different pens because she couldn't stand the way her handwriting changed when her hand was shaking. But every time she put pen to paper, all she could see was their faces. All she could hear was Caelum's voice in her head telling her to gather intelligence, to focus on the mission, to remember why she was really here.
Save Elias. That was the mission. Get information on what the Sanctum was planning and survive long enough to get that intel back to Caelum. Nowhere in that plan had she actually considered she might have to watch people die. That she might have to write condolence letters. That surviving might mean understanding her brother better than she ever thought she could. 
She'd been so fucking blind.
Maxim had offered to help her write the letters since he was their King Piece, but Melena had waved him off. It felt like her responsibility. She was the Imperial Princess. The families would want to hear from her.
Did Elias—
The thought caught in her throat. Had her brother done this too? Sat in some hangar with bodies being loaded onto transports and tried to find words that would make death mean something? Her grandmother hadn't let her watch. Hadn't let her see what the Tournament did to him. The only parts she knew, came from Elias’s breakdowns. And now Melena was here, writing letters to families she didn't know, and she finally—
She couldn't finish the thought.
The Sanctum would read these letters before they went out. Would analyze every word, looking for weakness, probably trying to see if she would be able to handle it the same way her brother did. So she wrote what they expected instead — direct, no nonsense, factual but never cold. “Your son was brave.” “She gave her life for her province.” “We will not forget him.
What she actually wanted to write was: I'm sorry.
That was it. Just I'm sorry. Over and over again until the words stopped meaning anything.
Melena pressed the heels of her palms against her eyes, trying to stop the headache building behind her skull. The hangar was too bright. Too cold. The sand from the arena was still in her hair, gritty against her scalp every time she moved. She could still feel the phantom pull of the tether on her wrist even though the Sanctum had cut it hours ago.
No one had changed out of their Challenge gear yet. Hadn't been given the chance. The Sanctum wanted them to watch the bodies being loaded first. Luke was leaning against the far wall, still picking sand out of his sleeve. Ellis sat near the transport bay doors, head tilted back, staring at nothing. Astor — Gods, Astor looked worse than any of them. She was still trembling. Melena could see it from across the hangar, the way her hands shook every time she tried to wipe his face. She wanted to talk to them, to check on each of them, but she didn’t have the words. She moved to the floor-to-ceiling window instead and pressed her forehead against the glass. From there, she could see Old Prosperity's transport in the distance. Eight Pieces left. Somehow, they still looked like they had their shit together, even though Melena knew they'd gone through the same hell.
A gust of wind hit the glass as the hangar doors opened for the last body.
Melena watched them carry it out. One of the Pawns — Leo, she thought, but her brain was too scrambled to be sure. They'd wrapped him in London Town colors, crimson and black, like that would somehow make his death more dignified. Like fabric could erase the way he'd screamed when the tether snapped and took half his element with it.
Four Sanctum officials carried the stretcher between them, moving in perfect sync as if they'd done this a thousand times before. They probably had. The Tournament had been running for centuries. How many bodies had passed through these doors? How many times had remaining Pieces stood exactly where Melena was standing now, watching their teammates knowing there had been nothing they could do? The stretcher disappeared through the doors, and Melena caught a glimpse of the transport beyond — its cargo hold already full, seven bodies laid out in neat rows. Leo would make eight. The last one. The doors sealed shut with a mechanical hiss that echoed through the empty hangar.
That was it.
All eight of them gone.
Melena let the chill from the window bleed through her uniform, hoping it would bite through the numbness that had been spreading through her chest for the past hour. She couldn't feel her hands anymore. Couldn't feel much of anything, really. Which was probably a blessing. If she could feel, she might actually break, and that wasn’t the plan, with the Sanctum still watching. That red light on the camera hadn't stopped blinking once.
One by one, London Town's remaining Pieces started filing out of the hangar. Luke first, shoulders hunched, sand still crusted on his uniform. Then Ellis, moving like every step hurt. Astor wouldn't even look at the doors where the bodies had gone, just kept her eyes on the floor as she stumbled toward the exit. Maxim and Raven went together, their quiet conversation dying as they passed Melena's position at the window.
No one said anything to her.
What was there to say?
The hangar emptied slowly, Pieces trailing out to their quarters to change into formal uniforms, to wash the arena off their skin, to pretend they weren't all about to break in front of cameras.
Until only she and Tiran remained.
He hadn't said a word since they'd gotten back. Not when the hoverjet landed. Not when the Sanctum officials started loading bodies. Not when the other Pieces joined them in the hangar. He'd just stood there near the doors, arms crossed, watching her watch the transports like he had nothing better to do.
Melena could still feel him in her head sometimes. Little flashes of thought that weren't hers, phantom sensations from the tether that had connected them in the arena. The Sanctum said the effects would fade, but Melena wasn't so sure. She could feel the shape of his presence even now, a weight in the back of her skull that hadn't been there before Challenge Four.
She hated it. Hated that he'd seen inside her head, seen every panicked thought, every moment of weakness. Hated that she'd seen inside his too — his calculations, his strategy, the way he'd mapped out their path to the crystal while she was still trying to figure out how to breathe.
Hated that they'd won because of it.
The sand on her uniform had dried into a stiff crust that cracked every time she moved. Her hair felt like it weighed ten pounds, matted with sweat and grit and Gods knew what else. She needed a shower. Needed to change. Needed to do about a hundred things before the Naming Ceremony, but she couldn't make herself move away from the window. If she moved, she'd have to start writing again. And if she started writing, she'd have to think, and thinking required an energy Melena didn't have. 
“You know you don't have to do this,” Tiran said finally.
His voice made her flinch. She'd almost forgotten he was still there.
“The Naming,” he continued when she didn't answer. “You could let Maxim handle it.”
Melena didn’t turn around. “The King’s supposed to protect his Pieces.”
“And you’re not the King.”
“No.” She pressed her forehead harder against the glass. “But I’m the one who had to be saved in the Maze. I’m the one you all were so concerned with getting out while—” She stopped. Swallowed. “While they were dying.”
The silence stretched between them. Through the window, she watched the transport’s engines fire up, blue flames licking at the undercarriage. In a few minutes, it would lift off. Would carry those eight bodies back to wherever the Sanctum sent them. Back to families who’d sent their kids to the Tournament thinking maybe, just maybe, they’d come home.
“It wasn’t like Maxim knew them either,” Melena said, her voice quieter now. “But he probably carried their bodies out of the arena himself. Put them on the stretchers even though the officials probably told him no. Made sure they were wrapped properly.” Her hand curled into a fist against the glass. “The least I could do was write the letters.”
She heard Tiran move closer. Not touching her, but close enough that she could feel the heat coming off him even through her uniform. Close enough that if she turned her head even slightly, she’d see him in her peripheral vision.
She didn’t turn.
“Jin wanted to be a teacher,” she said instead. “Did you know that? After the Tournament, he was going to go back home and teach kids how to use their elements safely. He told me about it the night before Challenge Three. Said he wanted to make sure no one else grew up afraid of their own fire the way he had.”
And Alexei?” The words kept coming even though she wanted them to stop. “He was just accepted into Medical Flight school in Easton. Wanted to study how to merge his element with the Flight’s technology. He showed me the acceptance letter. Kept it folded in his pocket like a good luck charm.”
Her breath fogged the glass. She watched it spread, then fade.
“Allison’s mother just had another baby. Six months old. Allison showed me the pictures the night before the Maze.” Her eyes were burning now, but she refused to blink. Refused to let the tears fall where the cameras could see them. “Did you know she called home every night? I caught her once after Challenge Two, and she made me promise I wouldn’t laugh at her for it. Every night during shift change, she’d call just to watch the baby sleep. Said no one would catch her breaking communication rules if she timed it right.”
“Melena—” Tiran started.
“And Leo.” Her voice cracked on his name. “He was only seventeen. His parents didn’t even want him to enter, but he needed the money for his grandfather’s medical treatments. Elite magic is expensive to treat when it goes wrong, apparently. So he sat for the exam anyway. Passed with high enough scores that they couldn’t refuse him even with the medical waiver. It would make one hell of a story if he won.”
The transport’s engines were fully powered now. Any second, it would lift off.
“Eight families are going to get my letters,” Melena whispered. “And none of them are going to care that I feel guilty. They’re just going to see Princess Melena Lockwood, who has everything, while they have nothing.”
“All Gods, Melena.” Tiran’s voice made her shoulders tense. She could hear him moving closer, could feel the shift in the air between them.
“You think I don’t know what you’re doing?” He was right behind her now, close enough that his reflection appeared in the glass next to hers. “You think I can’t see you drowning yourself in every single detail because if you know enough about them, if you care enough, somehow that makes up for the fact that they’re dead and you’re not?”
The transport lifted off. Melena watched it rise, engines blazing blue-white against the pre-dawn sky. Eight bodies inside. Eight families waiting.
“Stop.” Tiran’s voice was hard now. Cutting. “Just stop with the fucking martyr act.”
That made her turn. Finally. She whipped around to face him, and the sudden movement sent sand cascading off her uniform onto the floor between them.
“Martyr act?” Her voice came out sharper than she intended. “I’m trying to—”
“You’re trying to make yourself feel better.” Tiran cut her off. His jaw was tight, eyes darker than she’d ever seen them. “You want to know what those families are going to see when they watch your little ceremony? They’re going to see a girl who lived her entire life in palace walls trying to make herself feel better by reciting their children’s life stories like some kind of fucked-up bedtime story.”
Melena’s breath caught. The transport was disappearing into the distance now, just a speck of light against the gray sky.
“You think knowing Leo was seventeen makes his death more tragic?” Tiran stepped closer. “You think Allison’s baby sister makes her death more meaningful? They’re all equally dead, Princess. Guilt doesn’t bring people back from the dead, and martyring yourself won’t make their families forgive you.”
“Fuck you.”
The words left her mouth before she realized she’d spoken them. 
Tiran didn’t flinch. Didn’t even blink. Instead, his mouth curved. Not quite a smile. Something that made her stomach drop.
“There she is.”
Melena’s heart kicked against her ribs. “Excuse me?”
“The real Melena Lockwood.” Tiran stepped closer. And closer. Until there was hardly any space between them. She could feel the heat rolling off him, could see the arena sand still caught in his hair, the exhaustion carved into his face. “I thought she’d never show up.”
“Don’t—” Melena started.
“Don’t what?” His voice dropped lower. “Don’t tell you that you’re performing grief instead of actually feeling it?” He tilted his head. “Or don’t remind you that I spent six hours in your head today, Princess, so I know exactly what you’re doing?”
Her hands were shaking. No — her entire body was shaking. She wanted to hit him. Wanted to scream. Wanted to shove him away, but her hands were already fisting in the front of his uniform, and she couldn’t tell if she was trying to push or pull.
“Fuck you,” she said again, because apparently those were the only words left in her vocabulary.
Tiran’s hand came up fast enough that she flinched. But he didn’t touch her. His palm hovered near her cheek—close enough that if she breathed too deep, they’d make contact.
“You want to know what I think?” His voice dropped as if he were trying to cut through whatever fog she’d wrapped herself in. Before she could answer, his other hand slammed against the window beside her head, caging her in. “I think you’re not crying for them. You’re crying for yourself. Because you’re alive and they’re not, and you don’t know how to live with that.”
The red light on the camera was still blinking. She could see it reflected in the window behind Tiran’s head. They were watching. The Sanctum was always watching.
“Fuck you,” she spat.
His thumb pressed against her jaw then. Not soft. Firm. Enough pressure that she couldn’t ignore it. “You don’t get to do this. Not on camera. Not in front of the Empire.” His eyes bore into hers. “You don’t get to make their deaths about you.”
“I’m not—”
“You are.” His hand finally settled against her face, tilting her head up so she had nowhere to look but at him. “You’re turning them into stories because if they’re stories, they’re not real. If they’re not real, you don’t have to feel it. And if you don’t have to feel it, you don’t have to survive it.”
Melena’s breath was coming too fast now. She could feel his chest against hers every time she inhaled, could feel the heat of him bleeding through both their uniforms. The sand on his skin. The way his jaw was clenched so tight she thought his teeth might crack.
“I have to feel something,” she whispered, and her voice sounded wrecked even to her own ears.
“Then feel this.” Tiran’s grip on her face tightened. Not gentle. Not asking. His fingers pressed into her jaw hard enough to leave marks, and when Melena tried to pull away, he didn’t let her.
“What are you—”
The words died in her throat.
There was something in her chest. A flicker of heat that didn’t feel like hers. At first, it was just wrong —foreign, invasive, like someone had reached inside her ribcage and touched something they had no right to touch. Then it was a wave. Rolling up from her stomach to her ribs, catching in her lungs like a cough she couldn’t clear.
Melena’s breath snagged. Her hands flew to Tiran’s wrists, nails digging into his skin, but he didn’t move. Didn’t let go. Just held her face between his palms while whatever this was spread through her body like wildfire.
For a second, she thought she might actually throw up. Or burst into flame. Whichever came faster.
It wasn’t her fire.
She knew her fire. Had lived with it her entire life. The familiar, gnawing, always-hungry furnace that lived in her chest and demanded to be fed. This was something else entirely. Hotter. Heavier. Like the air itself had been poured into her ribcage and ignited from the inside out.
It burned, but not in the way she was used to Imperial flames burning. This felt like—
Like—
Melena tried to pull her face away again, tried to get space, tried to breathe, but Tiran’s grip only tightened. His thumbs pressed against her cheekbones, holding her in place, and she realized with a shock that traveled down her spine that he was doing this. Whatever was happening to her right now, he was the one making it happen.
“What—” she gasped, but the question wouldn’t form properly. Her thoughts were scattering, fracturing under the weight of heat that kept building in her chest. “What are you—”
“Do you feel that?”
His voice dropped. Low enough that the Sanctum couldn’t possibly hear over the wind still rattling the windows. His thumb traced her bottom lip, and the fire in her chest flared so hot she gasped.
The sound that came out of her was embarrassing. Desperate.
Tiran’s eyes darkened. “That’s mine.”
The words didn’t make sense. None of this made sense. The heat in her chest was still building, still spreading, and Tiran’s hand was still on her face, and she couldn’t—
“What?” It came out breathless. Broken.
“The fire you’re feeling right now. In your chest. The one that’s burning so hot you think you might combust.” His thumb traced her bottom lip again, slower this time, and the flames responded. Flared. Like they were his to command. “That’s mine, Princess.”
Melena’s knees went weak. She would’ve fallen if Tiran’s other hand hadn’t moved from the window to her waist, fingers digging into her hip hard enough to bruise as he held her upright.
“The vial,” his voice was rough now. Strained. Like whatever he was doing to her was costing him something too. “The one I gave you before Delegation. You thought it was temporary, didn’t you?”
“It was supposed to—” 
“It was never temporary.” His forehead dropped to hers, and the contact sent another shock through her system. The fire in her chest roared in response, reaching for him, like —
Like it knew where it came from.
Like it knew who it belonged to.
“You—” The accusation died on her tongue. “Why didn’t you tell me?”
“Would you have believed me?” His hand on her waist tightened, pulled her closer, until there was no space left between them. Until she could feel every hard line of his body pressed against hers. “Would you have let me keep it there? Or would you have tried to rip it out of yourself the second you found out?”
She would have. They both knew it.
“That’s how you always know.” The pieces were clicking into place now, one by one, building a picture that made her want to scream. “When I’m spiraling. When I’m drowning. When I’m about to—”
