“Lose your shit?” Tiran’s mouth was so close to hers now she could feel the words. “Yeah. I know. Because you’re carrying me with you, Melena. Right here.” His hand moved from her waist to press flat against her chest, right over her heart — right where his fire lived inside her.
The sound that tore out of her throat was embarrassing. Raw. Desperate. Tiran made a noise in the back of his throat — something between a growl and a laugh that got stuck on the way out. His hand pressed harder against her chest, and the fire underneath his palm burned so hot she thought she might actually die.
“There’s a pulse to it,” he said, voice gone hoarse. Rough in a way that made heat pool low in her stomach. “Every time you think about dying, it spikes. Every time you’re about to do something reckless, it flares. Every time you’re drowning in guilt you have no right to carry, I feel it.”
She was shaking. Couldn’t stop. Her hands fisted in his uniform, pulling him closer even though they couldn’t get any closer, even though every point where their bodies touched felt like it was on fire.
“Why?” Was all she could manage. 
She expected a smart-ass answer. Expected him to say something about strategy or survival or the Tournament. Instead, his eyes went dark, and when he spoke, his voice was almost gentle.
“Because if I didn’t, you’d get yourself killed.”
No lecture. No smirk. No shot at her status or her family or her reckless need to save everyone. Just the truth, stated like a fact — like her survival was the only thing that mattered. Melena’s pulse was breaking rhythm against the heat of his palm. The burning in her chest was still spreading — down her spine, into her lungs, her stomach, the insides of her wrists. She tried to step back, needed air, needed space, needed to think, but his hand on her chest held her steady.
“I’m not going to let you fall apart. Not here. Not on camera. Not in front of the whole fucking Empire.” His thumb brushed across the spot where her heart was hammering. “I don’t go down without a fight, Princess. And neither does anything that belongs to me.”
Belongs to me.
The fire in her chest blazed. Hot enough that her vision blurred. Hot enough that she couldn’t tell where her flames ended and his began. Couldn’t tell if the desperate need clawing through her body was from the fire or from the way Tiran was looking at her — and she was fine with either option. 
“So here’s what’s going to happen. You’re going to walk out of this hangar. You’re going to stand in front of those cameras and name our dead. And you’re going to do it without breaking.”
“I can’t—”
“You can.” His forehead pressed harder against hers. His lips were so close now they should have just kissed. “Because you’ve got me. Right here. And I didn’t give you a piece of myself just so you could waste it on guilt and pretty speeches that don’t mean shit.”
“I hate you,” she breathed.
“Good.” His hand finally left her chest, and the loss of contact made her want to grab him and put it back. His fingers trailed down her sternum, her ribs, before he stepped back and put distance between them.
His absence felt brutal. Wrong. Like cold water on burns.
He was watching her with that look — the one that said he knew exactly what he’d done to her. His eyes dropped to her chest where she could still feel the phantom heat of his hand, where his fire was still burning steady and refusing to let her fall.
“Not when you’ve got me burning inside you.”
## Chapter 31: Melena
The cameras were already rolling when Melena stepped into frame.
She knew because the red lights had switched from blinking to solid. Because the hangar had gone silent except for the hum of equipment and the blood rushing in her ears. Because she could feel every lens trained on her like targets, pressing down on her shoulders, her chest, the space between her ribs where Tiran’s fire still burned.
They’d cleaned her up first.
Dragged her into some back room an hour ago and scrubbed the arena sand from her skin until it hurt. Washed the blood from her hair — hers or someone else’s, she couldn’t remember anymore. Put her in formal black. A dress this time instead of a uniform. London Town colors: crimson trim at the collar and cuffs that felt too bright, too red, too much like the blood they’d just washed off.
And the tiara.
Gods. The tiara.
Heavy on her head. Gold and rubies catching the harsh lights. The Imperial tiara she hadn’t been allowed to wear since Delegation. Now they wanted her to wear it. Wanted the Empire to see royalty grieving. Wanted to make sure everyone knew she was the Empress’s granddaughter, second in line to the throne, before they watched her name the dead.
A spectacle. That’s what this was.
Behind her, the other Pieces stood in formation. Maxim in formal blacks with medals pinned to his chest she’d never seen him wear. Raven in a dress that made her look severe. Cold. Seraphina’s blonde hair pulled back so tight Melena wondered if it hurt. Luke. Ellis. Astor. All of them scrubbed clean, dressed up, positioned like dolls.
Even Tiran looked different. House colors — deep violet against black, the Blackwood crest on his collar. His white hair pulled back. Standing slightly apart from the others, exactly where the Sanctum had put him.
They’d built a stage. Made performers out of all of them.
The Sanctum announcer’s voice crackled through the speakers: “Citizens of the Empire. Today we honor those who have fallen in service to their provinces. Princess Melena Elizabeth Beatrix Lockwood will speak the names of London Town’s dead.”
Melena’s hands didn’t shake.
She’d made sure of that.
The tiara felt like a threat. Like a reminder. Like her grandmother’s hand on the back of her neck, forcing her to look straight ahead.
She did.
Chin up. Shoulders back. Every inch the Imperial Princess.
And then she spoke.
#
Empress Aracelia Lockwood didn’t flinch when her granddaughter appeared on screen.
She sat in her private study with Helena standing beside her chair, both of them silent as the broadcast started. Melena stood center frame. Tiara on. Spine straight. Looking like—
“She looks like Lucia,” Helena said quietly.
Aracelia’s jaw tightened. “Don’t.”
“I’m just saying—”
“Don’t.”
Helena went quiet. Knew better than to push. They’d been friends for thirty years, and she knew what that tone meant. What that single word was holding back.
On screen, the Sanctum announcer’s voice filled the study: “Princess Melena Elizabeth Beatrix Lockwood will speak the names of London Town’s dead.”
Melena looked straight at the camera. “Allison St. James.”
Good. Her voice was steady. Not shaking like it had been after the last challenge when she’d called Aracelia from the medical wing, half high on whatever Elite magic was keeping her alive and couldn’t stop apologizing for something that wasn’t her fault.
But then Melena kept talking.
“Allison was an Elite from London Town. Twenty years old. Daughter of Parker and Alli St. James. Sister to Clara St. James who was six months old. She was a pretty baby. Same hair and eyes as Allison. She loved being a big sister more than she loved being an Elite and that said something.”
Helena’s breath caught.
Aracelia’s fingers dug into the armrest.
“She’s...” Helena’s voice was tight. “She’s—”
“Going off script.” Aracelia’s throat felt raw.
Helena turned to look at her. “That’s not good.”
“It’s dangerous.” Aracelia forced herself to take a breath. “She’s doing exactly what Elias did. Caring too much. Feeling too much. And look what happened to him.”
The silence stretched.
They both knew. Had both watched Elias come home from his Tournament and not speak for three weeks. Had watched him flinch every time someone touched him, watched him wake up screaming, watched him disappear into himself until there was nothing left but a shell that looked like her grandson but wasn’t. Aracelia had lost him. Lost him the same way she’d lost Lucia — not to death but to something worse. To breaking. 
“Melena is not Elias,” Helena said.
“No.” Aracelia’s eyes stayed on the screen. On her granddaughter’s face. “She’s not. Because I didn’t let her be.”
After Elias broke, Aracelia had made a choice.
Melena would be different.
So, she’d been harder on her. Stricter. Made her write condolence letters until her handwriting was perfect, even when her hands shook. Made her stand dry-eyed at state funerals. Made her learn how to perform strength when she was falling apart inside. Because if Melena was going to survive this world — survive being an Imperial Princess, survive the Tournament if she ever entered, survive the weight of the crown that would eventually be hers — she needed to be harder than Elias ever was.
Needed to be harder than Lucia had been.
Lucia, who’d cried at every state funeral. Who’d written personal letters to every family who lost someone in service to the Empire. Who’d been soft and kind and everything a Crown Princess shouldn’t be. And it had killed her. Maybe not literally — the car accident had done that — but her softness had made her weak. Had made her vulnerable. Made the crown she’d worked so hard for vulnerable and as Empress, her job was to ensure the heir didn’t make the same decision. 
On screen, Melena continued speaking about Allison, this time talking about her accomplishments. Her voice still steady. Her spine still straight. But her jaw was tight. The same way Lucia’s had been when she was trying not to cry.
Aracelia’s chest hurt.
Elias had shattered. Quick. Complete. Like glass hitting concrete. But Melena? Melena would burn. Would keep burning and burning until there was nothing left. And Aracelia didn’t know if she’d built her granddaughter strong enough to survive that kind of heat.
“She’s not alone,” Helena said, nodding at the screen. “Tiran Blackwood has been with her since Challenge Four. Whatever happened in that hangar, he pulled her through it.”
Aracelia’s eyes found Tiran. Standing behind Melena in his House colors. Watching her like—
Like she was his responsibility.
“I want a full report on that.”
“Already on your desk.”
The camera stayed on Melena’s face as Aracelia watched her granddaughter prove to the entire Empire that the Lockwood line was still strong.
That London Town still had a Princess worth following.
That Aracelia’s training hadn’t been wasted.
Even if it cost Melena everything.
Even if it cost her the same thing it had cost Lucia and Elias.
Her humanity.
#
Caelum had stopped breathing somewhere around the second name.
“Volt Aran.”
Maven was standing near the window, arms crossed, watching the broadcast in that way she watched everything — cataloging, analyzing, already three steps ahead. She hadn’t said a word since Melena appeared on screen.
Smart woman.
On screen, Melena kept talking.
“Volt was a Flamer from a low noble house. He was good at his element — better than most of us, honestly. We didn’t talk much. Only crossed paths a few times. But the first night, he told me a joke.”
She paused. Just for a second. Just long enough that Caelum’s chest tightened.
“It wasn’t even funny. Something about a Flamer walking into a bar and setting the place on fire. I laughed anyway because he looked so nervous and I wanted him to know someone was listening. Now, I can’t stop thinking about that stupid joke.”
Maven’s shoulders tensed.
Caelum’s hand curled into a fist on the armrest.
Two days ago, she’d been falling apart. He’d felt it through the dream magic — that raw, jagged panic that came from watching people die and not knowing how to live with it. Had shown up in her head in that hallway, told her to stop drowning in guilt that would get her killed. To get her shit together.
And she had.
Sort of.
This — whatever this was — wasn’t what he’d meant.
On screen, Melena’s jaw was tight. Her voice steady. Her hands weren’t shaking the way they had been when he’d found her crying in that corridor, barely holding her fire back, talking about death as if she were ready to meet the reaper himself. She looked strong. Looked exactly like what the Empire needed to see.
She also looked like she was three names away from breaking.
Caelum knew that look. Had seen it on other assets, on people he’d sent into situations they weren’t ready for because the mission required it, and he’d needed them alive long enough to get what he needed.
Most of them hadn’t made it out.
“She’s compromised,” Maven said finally.
“No.” The word came out harder than he’d intended. “She’s nineteen.”
“She’s a spy.”
“She’s a kid I sent into a death game to save her brother. There’s a difference.”
Maven turned to look at him. Really look at him. The way she did when she was trying to figure out if he was about to do something stupid.
“You’re getting attached.”
“I’m being realistic.” He forced his eyes back to the screen. “She went in there to gather intelligence on the Sanctum. To find leverage. Instead, she’s—” He gestured at the broadcast. “—doing this.”
“This is what you told her to do. Perform. Show them strength.”
“I told her to survive.”
“Same thing.”
“It’s not.”
Because surviving meant keeping your head down. Meant not drawing attention. Meant remembering that eight dead Pieces were statistics, not people, not friends, not responsibilities you couldn’t afford to carry. Surviving meant not standing in front of cameras and bleeding for an Empire that would eat you alive the second you showed weakness.
That is what he’d sent Melena in there to do. 
“Pull her out,” Maven said.
“No.”
“Caelum—”
“She’s not done.” He cut her off.
“And if she breaks before then?”
“She’s Lucia’s daughter,” was all he could offer her in response. 
“I know.” That was the problem. “That’s what scares me.”
Because Melena wasn’t breaking like glass. 
She was burning. Slow. Hot. The kind of burn that looked like strength until there was nothing left but ash.
And Caelum had been the one to light the match.
#
The whiskey burned going down.
Good.
Michelle needed it to burn. Needed something to focus on that wasn't the screen. Wasn't the girl standing there in that ridiculous tiara looking so much like Tobias it made her want to throw the liquor at the wall.
The broadcast had moved to the third name.
"Ivy Lennox."
Fuck.
Behind her, someone inhaled sharply. Probably Dalia. She'd known the girl's father. They all had. Elite Elder Lennox was a bastard on his best days, but his daughter? Ivy had been good. Smart. Didn't deserve to die before she could pick her division. 
Michelle took another drink. Didn't taste it.
On screen, Melena kept talking. "Ivy was an Elite. Twenty years old. She had a younger brother back home who she called every night."
Michelle's hand tightened on the glass.
Katherine called every week too. When she could. When she wasn't too exhausted or injured or trying not to let Michelle hear how close she'd come to dying.
"She showed me pictures once — he couldn't have been more than ten. She said she was going to buy him new textbooks when she won. The good ones. The kind that don't fall apart after a semester. You’re probably wondering why the daughter of the Elite Elder had textbooks as a problem when money wasn’t."
Melena's voice was steady. Lucia had sounded like that sometimes, too, when she was trying not to cry. Tobias had hated it. He told Michelle once, late at night after too much whiskey, that he wished Lucia would just break. Just once. Just let herself feel something without worrying about how it looked.
Now Michelle was watching his other daughter do the exact same thing.
"She's holding up well," Commander Tolliver said from somewhere behind her. "The Princess. Better than I expected after Challenge Four."
Michelle didn't respond. Didn't trust her voice.
"Katherine looked good last challenge," Tolliver continued. Trying to sound confident. Trying to convince himself that his sons were going to make it out. "She and Atlas figured out that tether. They'll be fine."
His wife made a noise. Disagreement maybe. Or just fear.
Michelle took another drink.
Katherine was still out there. Still fighting. Still one challenge away from ending up in a body bag with the others.
And Michelle couldn't do a damn thing about it. Couldn't pull her out. Couldn't protect her. Couldn't even tell the people in this room that she wasn't just some bastard captain's daughter. That she was—
"She looks like him," Dalia said quietly, sitting down next to her. Michelle hadn’t even heard her get closer. "Melena. She has his eyes."
Michelle's throat went raw.
Dalia was one of three people who knew about Michelle and Tobias. Who'd watched them try to make it work for years before it all fell apart. Who'd held Michelle when Lucia died, and Tobias followed six months later, when Michelle realized she'd lost him twice. Dalia had been the only one to come over night after night to make sure Michelle wouldn’t die too. And when the responsibility of being the Queen of London Town meant that she couldn’t do it, she’d send her sons, Maxim and Minho, to do it. 
"She has his spine too," Michelle said. Forced the words out. "She’s strong, I’ll give her that. Unreadable facial expressions. Tobias learned that from Lucia."
"Katherine does the same thing," Dalia observed.
"I know."
Melena had gotten his eyes. His jawline. His tenacity. It was written all in the way she couldn’t control her flames. 
Katherine had gotten his stubbornness. His way of going quiet when he was hurting. That thing where he'd lock everything down until he was alone and then fall apart all at once.
"You seeing what I see?" Dalia said, nodding at the screen.
Michelle did.
Tiran Blackwood stood behind Melena, slightly apart from the other Pieces. Watching her. Not the cameras. Not the crowd. Just her. Like he was tracking every breath, every micro-expression, every sign that she might be about to break.
Michelle's chest went tight. She knew that look. Had seen it before. 
On Tobias's face. 
When he'd watch Michelle after bad missions. After days when she'd had to make calls that cost lives. After nights when she couldn't sleep because she kept seeing the bodies. He'd stand there. Just like that. Watching her. Making sure she was still breathing. 
Michelle finished her whiskey. Poured another. Handed one to Dalia.
Commander Tolliver started talking about Old Prosperity's strategy for the next challenge. His wife joined in. Dalia added something about Minho's leadership.
Michelle didn't hear any of it.
Just watched Tobias’s daughter walk off screen.
And prayed Katherine wouldn't be next.
#
The letters arrived before the names finished.
Every courier wore black. Every envelope sealed with the Imperial crest in red wax and gold trim. No one knew who decided they should arrive mid-broadcast — whether it was planned or if someone in the Sanctum thought it would make the grief look organized.
It didn't.
It just made it cruel.
"Cael Navarro."
In the lower districts, Maria Navarro opened her door to find a soldier on her porch. The envelope in his hand already crumpled at the edges from the cold.
She took it. Read the first line standing in the doorway.
By the time she looked up, the soldier was gone.
"Sine Cauldiste."
In the merchant district, Arya Cauldiste was watching with Sine's twin sisters when the knock came. She opened the letter in the hallway while the broadcast continued in the other room.
"—she called them her 'little disasters'—"
Elara folded it without finishing. Put it in the drawer with the bills.
The twins didn't ask what it said.
"Leo Keene."
On the outskirts of the medical district, Leo's father Jacob was at the clinic when the courier found him. His wife Anne was home. Hadn't left the house since Leo left home.
He held the letter in one hand. Didn't open it.
The broadcast played on the screen behind the reception desk.
"—he was only seventeen. He needed the money for his grandfather's treatments—"
Jacob asked if they could turn off the screen.
"Jin Wang."
In the harbor district, Jin's mother Keiko was alone when it came. Her husband at work. Her daughter at school. Just her and the courier and an envelope that felt heavier than it should.
She didn't open it right away.
Just sat with it in her lap, listening to the Princess say her son wanted to be a teacher.
"—he asked me if he looked crazy in his tournament uniform once. I laughed because he looked so nervous—"
Keiko finally broke the seal. Read it once. Then again before putting it in the drawer with Jin's teaching certification.
"Alexei Rhae."
At the Flight Academy in Easton, the Director opened the letter before Alexei's parents arrived. Read it. Handed it to Alexei's advisor without speaking.
"—he'd just been accepted into Medical Flight school. He was brilliant. He was going to change everything—"
When Alexei's mother Elena finally got there two hours later, the letter was waiting on the Director's desk.
She read the first line.
I'm sorry I couldn't bring him home.
Folded it. Put it in her purse. Didn't say a word.
Somewhere in Shadowmere, Maven shut off the feed. Caelum turned it back on.
He listened to every word. Every pause. Every breath.
Melena's voice filled the study, too full of feeling for a girl who'd been taught to survive without it.
"They were more than names. They were people. Our people. They had families who loved them. They had futures. And as your Imperial Highness, I'm sorry—"
Her voice cracked. Just slightly. Just enough.
"—I'm sorry I couldn't bring them home."
Across London Town, families read the same words at the bottom of each letter.
We will not forget them.
Melena didn't say that part.
She just stood there. Spine straight. Tiara glinting.
And when she turned to walk away, the entire Empire went still.
## Chapter 32: Katherine
The screen blinked on at 1400 hours sharp.
Katherine was still on her bed when the broadcast started. She hadn't moved much in the past six hours except to shower and change into clean clothes. The Sanctum sent a notification through her room's panel thirty minutes ago. London Town would have their Naming Ceremony first because they'd won Challenge Four. Katherine exhaled as she watched Melena walk into the frame. 
Standard protocol was simple. Handler introduces the speaker. Speaker reads the names. Three sentences per Piece — rank, province, one line about service or whatever the province was known for. Keep it brief. Keep it clean. Don't make the families watching feel too much because if you did that, people started asking uncomfortable questions and the last thing the Sanctum wanted was questions. 
Katherine had sat through enough mandatory viewings to know exactly how this was supposed to go. Melena would stand at that podium for maybe ten minutes total. Eight names. Twenty-four sentences. Then it would be over, and everyone could move on to preparing for Challenge Five.
Except Melena didn't have notes at her podium.
No notes meant one of two things. Either Melena had memorized the approved statement — unlikely given the state London Town had been in after Challenge Four — or she was planning to wing it. Katherine's stomach dropped. You didn't wing a Naming Ceremony. The Sanctum wrote the scripts. The King Pieces “approved” them. The Queen Pieces read them word for word. That was how it worked. That was how you kept the ceremony under tight control. 
Katherine's eyes moved to the seven Pieces standing behind Melena in formation. She counted them twice, to be sure she hadn’t overlooked anyone. Maxim front and center, spine rigid. Tiran off to the side, isolated. The others were faces she didn't recognize — a blonde girl staring at the floor, a boy whose hands wouldn't stop shaking.
Eight total. London Town started with sixteen and now had half. Old Prosperity had eight too — would have eight after Minho read Beckett's name in two hours along with seven others.
Katherine's wrist still ached where the tether had burned during Challenge Four. She rubbed at it absently, even though the mark had faded hours ago. The phantom sensation of Atlas's thoughts in her head had faded too, mostly, but every so often she'd feel something that wasn't hers — a flicker of concern, maybe annoyance, she couldn't tell. Dax had said the effects would wear off completely in a day or two, but right now Katherine could still feel the echo of the connection humming under her skin. She wondered if that's what was making her chest feel tight as she watched Melena stand at the podium. 
She should be analyzing what Melena was about to do. Should be running scenarios. Should be thinking about what this meant for Old Prosperity. But all she could manage to do was sit on her bed and watch a girl she didn't know stand at a podium, and hope whatever came out of her mouth wouldn't somehow make everything worse.
Maybe that was the problem. Katherine had spent so long trying to think three moves ahead that she'd forgotten how to just exist in the present moment. How to watch something happen without immediately calculating its tactical value.
But then she’d just be stupid. This was the Tournament. Every moment was of tactical value. 
Katherine's vision blurred. Just for a second, but enough that she had to blink hard to bring Melena back into focus on the screen. Her hands were shaking against her knees — small tremors she couldn't control, the kind that came from pushing your body past its limits and expecting it to keep functioning anyway.
She should probably eat something. Drink water. Do any of the basic maintenance tasks that kept soldiers alive between combat drops. But moving felt impossible right now, and Melena was about to speak, and Katherine needed to see what happened next even if her body was trying to shut down.
Tactical value. Right. She'd worry about staying conscious after the ceremony.
Her vision went gray at the edges. Not black — that would've been easier. Just this slow fade where the screen stayed visible but everything else started disappearing. Katherine knew what was happening. Her body was shutting down non-essential systems to conserve energy. Peripheral vision first. Fine motor control next. Then consciousness. She dug her nails into her palms hard enough to draw blood. The pain helped. Brought her back from the edge just enough to keep watching.
Stay awake. Just ten more minutes. 
Melena would say eight names, Katherine would see what happened, and then she could pass out for however long her body needed. Katherine thought she’d gotten her body under control, but then the humming under her skin exploded. Katherine gasped, doubling over as sensation flooded through her— not pain exactly, but overwhelming. Atlas's thoughts crashed into hers like they had in the arena. His alarm. His assessment running parallel to hers. His feelings that felt too present to be leftover echo. Then came Dax, the heat of his internal flame washing over her, causing her element to flare in response. 
And beneath that, something else. Something that had nothing to do with Atlas at all. A pull toward the screen. Toward Melena. Like Katherine's body recognized something her brain couldn't process. She tried to pull it back, but Dax's heat was still flooding through the connection, and her element was responding to it like they were still in Challenge Four. Fire and ice, action and reaction, completely out of her control.
Katherine pressed her forehead against her knees and counted. One. Two. Three. Separated the sensations like she'd learned to do in the arena. Atlas's alarm went in one mental box. Dax's heat went in another. Her own panic got shoved down where it couldn't interfere. And that fourth thing — the pull toward Melena — she didn't have a box for that one yet.
By the count of ten, she could breathe again. By twenty, she could lift her head and look at the screen.
Melena still stood at the podium. Still hadn't spoken. The Sanctum announcer's voice filled the silence: “Princess Melena Elizabeth Beatrix Lockwood will speak the names of London Town's dead.”
Katherine's hands were still shaking, but she could see clearly now. Whatever was happening with the tether, she'd deal with it after the ceremony. 
Melena looked straight at the camera. “Allison St. James.”
Good start, strong pronunciation. Protocol said she'd add rank and province next, maybe one sentence about—
“Allison was an Elite from London Town. Twenty years old. Daughter of Parker and Alli St. James. Sister to Clara St. James who was six months old.”
Katherine's chest tightened.
Wait.
No. That wasn't right. Her chest was fine. She was fine. She was just watching a ceremony, same as she'd done a dozen times before. Standard protocol. Eight names. Move on.
But her hands were cold.
Katherine looked down. Frost was spreading across her knuckles. Thin lines of ice webbing between her fingers like her element had decided to activate without asking. She flexed her hand. The frost cracked but didn't disappear.
“She was a pretty baby. Same hair and eyes as Allison. She loved being a big sister more than she loved being an Elite and that said something.”
The cold shot up Katherine's arms. Fast. Too fast. She tried to pull her element back, but it wouldn't listen, right as Melena’s voice cracked on the word “something.”
Then Katherine's lungs forgot how to work.
She gasped. Dragged in air that felt too thin. Her ribs were tight, constricting, and that didn't make sense because she was sitting. She wasn't running. Wasn't fighting. She was just—
Watching.
Just watching.
But her body didn't care. Her body was reacting as if Melena's grief was hers. Like that crack in Melena's voice had split something open in Katherine's chest too.
This wasn't the tether. The tether was with Atlas. The tether was fading. This was—
“Volt Aran.”
The temperature in the room dropped.
Katherine felt it in her bones. In the space between her heartbeat. One second the air was normal, the next it was so cold she could see her breath. She looked around. Nothing had changed. The panel still glowed on the wall. The screen still showed Melena standing at that podium. But the air was wrong. Too cold. Too intense.
“Volt was a Flamer from a low noble house. He was good at his element — better than most of us, honestly. We didn’t talk much. Only crossed paths a few times. But the first night, he told me a joke.”
Katherine's hands were shaking. She pressed them flat against her thighs, trying to stop it, but the shaking spread. Up her arms. Into her shoulders. Her whole body was vibrating like something was trying to tear its way out from the inside.
“It wasn’t even funny. Something about a Flamer walking into a bar and setting the place on fire. I laughed anyway because he looked so nervous and I wanted him to know someone was listening. Now, I can’t stop thinking about that stupid joke.”
No.
No, that wasn't—
Katherine's throat closed. Just locked up. She couldn't swallow. Couldn't breathe. There was something in her chest, forceful and lingering and wrong, and it was spreading. Burning. Except she didn't burn. She was ice. She was a Frost. She was—
Melena's guilt crashed into her.
Not her guilt. Katherine didn't know Volt. Didn't know any of them. But she could feel it. Feel the weight of knowing he'd died scared. Feel Melena's rage at the Sanctum for putting them in that arena. Feel the helplessness of standing at a podium and trying to make death mean something when it didn't. When it couldn't.
Katherine's ice surged.
Frost exploded across the walls. Up. Out. Spreading so fast she didn't have time to stop it. It crawled across the floor, up the bed frame, coating the window in patterns that looked like screaming. She tried to get some semblance of control but she couldn’t. It was if her ice was reacting to something deeper than logic. Deeper than control. Something that felt like grief but couldn't be because Katherine wasn’t supposed to be grieving right now. She was supposed to — 
"Ivy Lennox."
Katherine doubled over.
Her stomach twisted, violent and sudden, and she had to press her forehead against her knees to keep from throwing up. Her hands found the edge of the bed, gripping so hard her knuckles went white. Ice spread from her palms, freezing the sheets solid.
"You’re probably wondering why the daughter of the Elite Elder had textbooks as a problem when money wasn’t."
Katherine could feel it. Feel Ivy’s hope. Feel Melena's rage. 
It wasn't hers.
None of this was hers.
But her body was reacting like it was. Like every name Melena spoke was carving something out of Katherine's chest. As if every detail was another cut. Another wound. Another reminder that people died in this Katherine's vision blurred. She blinked hard, trying to clear it, but the room was tilting. 
The tether was supposed to be gone.
This wasn't supposed to be happening.
“Cael Navarro.”
Katherine's world whited out.
Not black. White. Like her vision had frozen over. Like ice had crept into her eyes and decided she didn't need to see anymore. She could still hear. Could still hear Melena's voice breaking. Could hear the way it cracked on Cael’s name like saying it out loud was tearing her apart.
“Sine Cauldiste.”
Katherine's chest split open.
That's what it felt like. Like something had reached inside her ribs and yanked. Like grief had hands and teeth and it was ripping. She gasped, and the sound was raw. Primal. Not hers.
But it came from her throat anyway. She could feel Melena breaking. Feel the exact moment control slipped. Feel the guilt and rage and helplessness pouring out like blood from a wound that wouldn't close.
And Katherine's ice responded. It wasn't spreading anymore. It was exploding. Coating the walls so thick the stone underneath disappeared. Sealing the door shut. Crawling across the ceiling in fractals that looked like grief given form. Katherine's teeth were chattering. Her whole body was shaking. She couldn't feel her fingers. Couldn't feel her toes. Couldn't feel anything except the cold and Melena's grief and the certainty that if she didn't stop this, she was going to freeze herself to death. The frost on the window cracked. Just split. Right down the middle. Like the glass couldn't hold the weight of what Katherine's element was doing.
Melena kept talking. Leo Keene. Seventeen. Needed money for medical treatments. Jin Wang. Twenty-two. Only boy in his family. Alexei Rhae. Nineteen. Had just been accepted into Medical Flight school.
Each name hit Katherine hard. Each detail was like a blade. And with every word, her ice spread further. Colder. Deeper. Until the room was sub-zero and Katherine couldn't remember what warm felt like.
She tried to stand. Her legs gave out. She hit the floor hard, ice cracking under her knees, and the cold surged.
This was bad.
This was really bad.
She was going to die. In her own room. Frozen solid because she couldn't figure out how to stop feeling what some girl two provinces away was feeling on a broadcast that had nothing to do with her.
Before Katherine could close her eyes, the door exploded inward. Not opened. Exploded. Fire melting through the ice seal in seconds, heat blasting into the room like a furnace.
Dax stood in the doorway, flames dancing along his arms, eyes locked on her.
“Well, if it isn’t Little Frost.”
Katherine tried to respond but she couldn’t. Her jaw was locked from the cold.
Dax didn't wait for her permission. Just walked in, and the temperature shifted with him. Not warm enough to be comfortable. Just enough to remind her body that hypothermia was a choice she didn't have to make. He looked around the room. At the ice coating every surface. At the frost patterns on the ceiling. At Katherine on the floor, shaking, unable to stand.
“Damn,” he said, almost impressed. “You really went all out.”
“Get out,” Katherine managed through chattering teeth.
“Nah.” Dax crouched in front of her, flames still burning along his forearms. “I’m good here.”
“I didn’t ask—”
“You didn’t have to.” He grabbed her wrists. Not gentle at all. Almost too damn firm. She couldn’t pull away even if she wanted to. “You were broadcasting loud enough that I felt it three floors down. So, either you learn how to keep your shit contained, or I’m gonna start assuming you wanted me to show up.”
Heat met cold. His palms pressed against hers, and the ice coating her hands melted. Not painfully. Not with steam or shock. Just… balanced.
Katherine tried to pull back. Dax’s grip tightened.
“Breathe,” he said.
“I am breathing.”
“No, you’re panicking. There’s a difference.” His fire spread up her arms, melting the frost coating her skin, and for a second, Katherine wasn’t sure how he was doing that without burning her. “Now breathe. Or I’m gonna make you.”
“You can’t make someone—”
Dax leaned in closer. Close enough that she could see the gold flecks in his eyes. “Watch me.”
She dragged in air. It hurt less than before. The ice on the walls started to recede, responding to Dax’s heat like it needed the reminder that her element wasn’t the only one in the room.
“There you go,” Dax chuckled. “See? Not that hard.”
“I had it under control.”
“Liar.” He smiled, and it was the kind of smile that made her want to punch him. “You were about to freeze yourself into a coma, and we both know it.”
Katherine glared at him. Dax’s smile widened.
“You’re doing it again,” he said.
“Doing what?”
“That thing where you pretend you don’t need help.” His thumbs brushed across her wrists and her frost began to spread again before Dax pressed down, melting it away. “It’s cute. Ineffective, but cute.”
“I don’t need your help.”
“Then why’s your element still responding to mine?” Dax tilted his head, studying her like she was a puzzle he hadn’t quite figured out yet. “Why’s your ice pulling back every time my fire gets close?”
Katherine didn’t have an answer for that.
“The tether’s supposed to be gone,” she said instead.
“And yet it’s not.” Dax’s fire dimmed slightly, just enough that the room stopped feeling like a furnace. “But this didn’t come from me or my brother.”
“Then what is it?”
“You tell me.” His eyes locked on hers. “Because from where I’m sitting, it looks like you just lost your shit over a ceremony for a province that’s not even yours.”
Katherine’s stomach dropped. “I was just watching—”
“Bullshit.” Dax cut her off. “Look around you, Ice Princess.”
“This doesn’t make sense.”
“No, it doesn’t.” Dax let go of her wrists, but he didn’t move back. Stayed crouched in front of her, close enough that the heat coming off him was the only thing keeping her from shaking again. “But it happened anyway. So either you figure out what the hell that was, or you’re gonna walk into Challenge Five and get eaten alive.”
Katherine’s hands curled into fists. “I can handle Challenge Five.”
“Can you?” Dax raised an eyebrow. “Because Heart’s Mirror doesn’t care how tough you think you are. It finds the cracks and widens them. And right now, Little Frost, you’re all cracks.”
“I’m fine.”
“You’re a goddamn mess.” Dax reached out, fingers brushing frost from her cheek. The touch was so light she almost didn’t feel it. Almost. “And you know what the worst part is? You’re not even trying to hide it anymore.”
Katherine’s breath caught. “What’s that supposed to mean?”
“It means,” Dax said, his thumb tracing her jawline, “that six months ago, you would’ve fought me harder. Would’ve pushed back. Would’ve told me to go to hell and meant it.” His hand dropped. “Now? You’re just tired. And that’s dangerous.”
“I’m not—”
“Yes, you are.” Dax stood, offering her a hand. “You’re exhausted. You’re running on fumes and as a Queen Piece, that’s dangerous for us.”
Katherine stared at his hand. Didn’t take it.
“I can get up on my own.”
“I know you can.” Dax didn’t move. Didn’t lower his hand. “But you’re not going to. Because if you do it yourself, you have to admit you needed the help in the first place. And we both know you’d rather freeze to death than admit that.”
Katherine wanted to argue. Wanted to prove him wrong. But her legs were shaking, and the room was still half-frozen, and Dax was right. She hated that he was right.
She took his hand.
Dax pulled her up. Too fast. Too close. She stumbled forward, and his other hand caught her waist, steadying her. They were inches apart. Close enough that Katherine could feel his heartbeat. Close enough that his heat was seeping into her, warming places that had been cold for hours.
“You good?” Dax asked, and his voice had that edge to it. The one that said he knew exactly what he was doing.
“Fine,” Katherine said, but she didn’t step back.
Neither did he.
“You know,” Dax said, his hand still on her waist, “if you wanted my attention, there are easier ways to get it.”
“I didn’t—”
“Sure you didn’t.” His smile was back. Lazy. Confident. Infuriating. “Just like you didn’t call me in here.”
Katherine’s pulse kicked up. “That’s the connection from the tether.”
“Is it?” Dax’s thumb brushed her hip, and Katherine’s element flared in response. “Because I’m pretty sure the tether didn’t make you react like that.”
“Like what?”
“Like you’re trying real hard not to think about what it would feel like if I wasn’t just warming you up.”
Katherine’s face burned. “You’re an asshole.”
“Yeah.” Dax leaned in, his mouth so close to her ear that she could feel his breath. “But I’m the asshole who just saved your life. So maybe think about that before you freeze yourself to death next time.”
He stepped back. The loss of his heat felt brutal. Katherine hated that she noticed.
“Challenge Five’s in forty-eight hours,” Dax said, like the last two minutes hadn’t just happened. “You need to figure out what’s going on with you and the Princess before then. Because whatever that was?” He gestured at the ice still coating the walls. “That’s gonna get you killed.”
“I don’t have anything going on with Melena.”
“Keep telling yourself that.” Dax headed for the door, then stopped. Looked back at her. “Oh, and Little Frost?”
“What?”
“Next time you need someone to pull you out of your own head?” He winked at her. “Just ask. I’m better at it than Atlas anyway.”
Katherine’s stomach dropped. “What’s that supposed to mean?”
“It means,” Dax said, flames dancing along his fingers, “that I know exactly how you feel about my brother. And I know you’re trying real hard to pretend that connection from Challenge Four didn’t change anything between you two.” He tilted his head. “But it did. And now you’re stuck trying to figure out if what you’re feeling is real or just leftover tether feedback.”
Katherine’s mouth went dry. “You don’t know what you’re talking about.”
“Don’t I?” Dax’s eyes locked on hers. “Because from where I’m standing, you’re terrified that you might actually want him. And you’re even more terrified that he might want you back.” He paused. “And now I’m here, making it worse. Because you can’t figure out if you’re drawn to me because of the connection or because you actually are.”
“I’m not—”
“Yeah, you are.” Dax’s voice went soft. “And that’s the problem, Little Frost. You’re so busy trying to control everything that you forgot how to just feel.” He stepped closer again, flames dimming until they were barely there. “So here’s what’s gonna happen. You’re gonna eat something. Drink water. And stop lying to yourself about what’s going on in that pretty head of yours. Because if you walk into Heart’s Mirror like this — confused, exhausted, and too damn stubborn to admit you need help — you’re not coming back out.”
Katherine couldn’t breathe. Not from the ice this time. From the fact that Dax had just laid out every fear she’d been trying to bury and made it sound casual.
“Why do you care?” she asked.
Dax’s smile faded. For a second, he just looked at her. Really looked at her.
“Because,” he said finally, “someone other than my darling brother has to.”
He left.
The door hung open, melted ice dripping from the frame.
Katherine stood in the middle of her frozen room and tried to figure out which was more dangerous: the fact that Dax knew exactly how she felt about Atlas, or the fact that he was right about her being drawn to him too.
Both, probably.
Both was definitely more dangerous.
## Chapter 33: Melena – The Fifth Challenge
The chamber was white.
Not the kind of white that felt clean or safe. The kind that hurt to look at. Like staring at snow under full sun. Like being inside a star that hadn’t decided whether to burn or freeze. Melena’s boots hit marble that was too smooth. Raven had prepared them for this part — for walking into the Challenge’s clinical room. Melena’s footsteps echoed all wrong in here, like the sound had nowhere to go but straight into her skull. Everything was white — ceiling, walls, the seamless shelves, the razor-edged table at the center, even the elegant block letters stenciled across the far wall: SUUM CUIQUE. Her mother had said it meant “To Each Her Own,” but Melena always thought it was more of a threat than a motto.
They’d been separated from the boys on the team for this part. Seraphina stood beside her, then Astor, and then Viv.
Melena’s hands wouldn’t stop shaking. Small tremors she tried to hide by crossing her arms. Didn’t work. Seraphina noticed anyway — shot her a look that said get it together — but Seraphina’s own breathing was too fast. Too shallow. Astor was staring at the wall like if she looked hard enough, she could see through it. And Viv — Viv looked like she was about to throw up.
The Sanctum had given them exactly thirty seconds of instruction before transporting them: enter, face what the Mirror shows, exit when released. No weapons. No early exits. Failure meant disqualification — if you made it back from whatever you were shown.
That was it. Nothing more. Nothing less.
The room was empty except for the table. And on that table, a single mask. Silver mesh. The kind that covered your whole face.
“Princess Melena Elizabeth Beatrix Lockwood.”
The voice came from everywhere and nowhere. Calm and clinical. Like a doctor about to explain why the surgery would hurt but assuring you it was necessary. An Elite mage materialized in front of her. Their robes were the color of absence — shifting between black and nothing depending on how the light hit. Their face was already hidden behind a mask identical to the one on the table, but Melena could feel their eyes. Studying her. Cataloging her. Finding every weak point she’d spent twenty years trying to hide.
“Please approach the table.”
Melena walked forward. She could feel the other girls watching her, but she didn’t look back. If she hesitated, she’d never make it. The mask was heavier than it looked — cold in her hands, the mesh biting into her fingers. She’d worn things like this before — ceremonial fencing masks, royal pageant gear, shit her grandmother thought built character. This one felt different. Like it wanted to fuse with her bones.
“Put it on,” the Elite said, as if Melena was a child who might not follow instructions.
She did. The mesh slid over her face, pressed too close to her eyes. The world went white, then, as the mask activated. Vision stripped away, replaced by blank nothing.
Sound vanished too.
She tried to move, but couldn’t. Her arms and legs were clamped by some magic — or muscle. Nothing so crude as physical restraint, just an absolute certainty that this was how it would go, and any desire to scream or run or incinerate the mask off her face was distant.
From the outside, Melena probably didn’t move. Maybe she even looked like everything was going to be fine. But inside the mask, the world was a sensory-deprivation tank. No up, no down, nothing but the pulse of her own heart thudding through her chest. She’d lost time before — blackouts in the practice tunnels playing around with Minho, the time she’d fainted in the lily garden at age twelve — but never like this. Never while her mind was still up and screaming.
Her fire tried to respond — the element surging toward her fingertips. It hit a wall. Not literally, but it definitely hit something. Magic that tasted wrong. Like metal and ash right after a fire had been put out.
“Your element will not help you here,” the Elite’s voice filtered through the white. “This is not a challenge where you will be able to use it.”
The white started to shift. Melena could feel it more than see it — the sensation of walls forming around her, floor solidifying under her boots, air pressure changing.
Then the mask released her.
Melena gasped. Stumbled forward. Her hands went to her face — but the mask was gone. Just her skin. Just air. Just —
She looked up.
And the breath left her lungs in a rush.
#
The chamber from the Maze.
Melena recognized it instantly. Would have recognized it blind, deaf — hell, even dead. The curved walls that looked carved instead of built. The delicate archways that framed nothing. The pool in the center that had been clear once, before it filled with blood.
And Ivy.
Slumped against the far wall. Three spikes driven through her chest — pinning her to marble like a butterfly in a display case. Her head tilted at an angle that made Melena’s neck hurt just looking at it. Her arms splayed out. Her legs bent wrong. Blood everywhere. On the floor. On the walls. Pooling in the perfect water until it looked like liquid rubies.
Melena had seen this before. Had lived this moment. Had burned the entire chamber down trying to make it not real.
But this time was different.
This time, Ivy’s chest was moving.
“No,” Melena whispered.
Ivy’s eyes opened slowly before she looked down at the spikes through her body like she couldn’t understand how they got there.
“Melena?”
Melena’s legs moved before her brain caught up. She ran — boots slipping in blood as her hands reached out. She dropped to her knees hard enough that pain shot up her thighs, but she didn’t care. She was here. She was with Ivy. She could fix this.
“Hold on,” Melena said. Her hands hovered over Ivy’s body — not touching, not knowing where to touch. Where to press. How to stop it. “Just hold on, I’m going to —”
“It hurts,” Ivy whispered. Blood bubbled at the corner of her mouth and spilled down her chin. “Why does it hurt so much?”
“Don’t —” Melena’s throat closed. “Don’t touch them. Don’t move. I’ll get help, I’ll —”
But there was no help. No medics. No teammates. Just Melena and Ivy and three spikes that shouldn’t be there.
Ivy’s hands moved toward the spike in her chest — fingers trembling as she tried to pull it out.
“No, no, no, don’t touch it!” Melena grabbed Ivy’s wrists, pulling them away from her body. “You’ll make it worse, you’ll —”
“I was just walking,” Ivy said. Her voice was getting quieter, as if she were speaking from very far away. “Just looking for the team. Trying to find you. Did I — did I do something wrong?”
“No.” Melena’s chest felt like it was caving in. “No, you didn’t do anything wrong. You were perfect. You were —”
“Then why am I dying?”
The question hung in the air between them. Ivy’s eyes were so wide. So young. So fucking confused.
Melena couldn’t answer. Couldn’t find words that would make this make sense. Because it didn’t make sense. Ivy was eighteen — had dreams about taking her father’s position on the council one day. Had shown Melena pictures of her family. Had cried after Challenge One and then smiled for the rest of the week.
And now she was dying in a beautiful chamber while Melena knelt in her blood and couldn’t do a goddamn thing to stop it.
Again.
“I was going to open a bakery,” Ivy said. Blood sprayed from her lips when she coughed — red mist spreading through the air. “Make the best pastries in the Empire like my dad taught me to. Chocolate ones. The kind my baby sister’s always reaching for.”
“You will,” Melena lied. The words tasted like ash. “You’re going to make it out of here and open that bakery and your sister will eat chocolate pastries every day and —”
“Melena.” Ivy’s hand found hers — slick with blood and already going cold. “I don’t think I am.”
“Yes, you are.” Melena’s fire surged as her flames erupted from her palms without permission — trying to burn away the spikes, the blood, the certainty that she was watching someone die. “I’m not letting you die, you hear me? I’m not —”
The fire hit the spikes and stopped. The Imperial flames that could burn through steel couldn’t touch whatever was holding Ivy to the wall. Ivy’s breathing was getting shallower — each inhale sounding like drowning, like her lungs were filling with something that wasn’t air.
“I’m scared,” she whispered.
Melena’s vision blurred as tears began to fall down her face. “I know. I know, but I’m — I’m right here, okay? You’re not alone. I’m not leaving you.”
“Promise?”
“I promise.”
Ivy smiled — just a little. Just enough for Melena to notice the blood on her teeth. “That’s good. I don’t — I don’t want to be alone.”
Her grip on Melena’s hand tightened — then loosened.
Her chest stopped moving.
The silence that followed was absolute. No breathing. No heartbeat. No sound except Melena’s ragged gasps and the drip-drip-drip of blood hitting marble. Melena sat there — kneeling in blood that was already going tacky — holding a hand that would never hold hers back, staring at a face that would never smile again.
Four minutes and thirty-seven seconds.
The thought appeared in her head unbidden. Four minutes and thirty-seven seconds. That’s how long it had taken. How long Ivy had spent dying while Melena knelt there and couldn’t stop it.
The sound in her ears shifted — like wind screaming through a keyhole — and then stopped.
“Congratulations.”
Melena’s head snapped up. The Elite mage stood in the chamber entrance — hands folded, watching, like this was a performance they’d seen a thousand times before.
“You have witnessed truth,” they said, voice showing no sign of emotion. “Ivy Lennox did not die instantly in the Maze. She spent four minutes and thirty-seven seconds dying alone in this chamber while you stood in the corridor, unaware.”
Melena couldn’t speak. Her throat was full of smoke and grief and rage and words that wouldn’t form.
“Do you understand what this means?” the mage continued.
“Shut up.”
“It means you failed her.” The mage tilted their head. “You were her Queen. Her Princess. The one who was supposed to protect her. And you let her die afraid and alone because you were not fast enough. Not smart enough. Not good enough.”
“I said shut up.”
“Truth does not silence itself, Princess.” The mage’s voice never changed. “Tell me — did Ivy’s death serve a purpose? Or was it simply waste? Another body added to the pile so you could continue competing, continue proving yourself, continue chasing a crown that might not even be worth the blood it costs.”
Melena’s fire exploded. She wasn’t thinking. Wasn’t trying. Just burning. Burning everything — the walls, the floor, the spikes, Ivy’s body, the mage. All of it.
The chamber turned into an inferno — white stone blackening, air superheating, everything Melena had burning outward until there was nothing left but ash and heat and the certainty that if she burned hot enough, none of this would be real.
But when the flames died —
Ivy was still there.
Still pinned to the wall. Still dead. Still staring at nothing with those wide, confused eyes.
“You cannot burn away truth,” the mage said. They hadn’t moved. Hadn’t even flinched. “You can only endure it.”
The chamber shifted as the walls dissolved. Ivy’s body faded away like smoke as the white of the mask returned. The mage walked closer.
“That’s one,” they said. “Shall we continue?”
Melena wanted to say no. Wanted to rip the mask off her face and run. But her body wouldn’t respond. 
“Yes,” she heard herself say.
The mage nodded. “Very well.”
#
This time, Melena knew where she was before the walls finished taking shape.
The study. Her mother’s private study in the palace.
The smell hit her first — jasmine and old books and that specific kind of quiet that only existed in rooms where important decisions were made, often before Cordelia or Louis had gotten up for breakfast. Then came the light — soft and golden — filtering through the tall windows that overlooked the lily garden. The same garden where Melena used to practice her forms while her mother worked. The same garden where she’d burned half the roses when she was eleven and couldn’t control her fire yet.
The same garden her father used to watch from that same window.
The books were exactly where they’d always been — spines arranged by color instead of subject because her mother thought it looked better. The crystal decanter on the sideboard, half-full of whiskey her father pretended not to drink. The portrait above the fireplace — Lucia with all four of her children. Elias standing tall. Melena trying not to squirm. Cordelia grinning. Louis barely old enough to sit still.
A family that didn’t exist anymore.
Melena’s boots hit the carpet and she immediately wanted to leave. This room held too many memories — too many moments of watching her father pretend everything was fine when it wasn’t. Too many nights of hearing him and her mother argue behind closed doors about things Melena was too young to understand but old enough to know mattered.
“Welcome home, Melena.”
Her father’s voice.
She turned — and her breath caught.
Tobias Lockwood stood by the window in his funeral blacks. Not the clothes he was buried in — the clothes he’d worn to her mother’s funeral. The formal Imperial mourning attire with the Lockwood crest embroidered in silver thread at the collar. The shirt that was too crisp. The jacket that hung wrong on his shoulders like he’d lost weight but nobody had noticed yet. His crimson hair was longer than he usually kept it, falling across his forehead — unwashed, uncombed, like he’d stopped caring.
His green eyes — the same shade as hers, the same eyes that used to light up when she walked into a room — were hollow and empty. The kind of empty that made Melena’s chest hurt just looking at him.
This wasn’t her father from before her mother died.
This was her father from after.
Melena took a step back, watching him like he might disappear if she blinked. “You’re not real,” she said. “You’re just another trick.”
“Maybe,” Tobias murmured. His gaze stayed on the window. “But the Mirror only shows what’s already inside you.”
“That’s not an answer.” Her hands were already sparking. “Why this memory? Why you?”
He finally turned. “Because you never let me explain.”
Melena laughed — sharp, humorless. “Explain what? How you left us? How you let Elias carry everything while you hid in here?”
“I didn’t hide.”
“You vanished.” She took another step closer. “You let us think you stopped loving us.”
Tobias flinched — just barely. But it was the first real reaction she’d seen.
“I never stopped loving you,” he said quietly. “That was never the problem.”
“No,” Melena said. Her voice came out rougher than she intended. “I’m not doing this.”
“You don’t have a choice.” Tobias didn’t turn around. Just kept staring out that window like if he looked hard enough, he’d see something worth staying for. “The Mirror doesn’t care what you want.”
“Fuck the Mirror.”
“Language.” The correction was automatic — the same way he used to say it whenever she and Elias argued too loud at dinner.
“Don’t. Don’t you dare correct me like you have any right to—”
“To what?” Now he turned — finally — and the look on his face made Melena want to scream. “To be your father? I was your father, Melena. For seventeen years, I was your father.”
“Was.” The word tasted like poison on her tongue. “Past tense. Because the second Mom died, you stopped.”
Tobias’s jaw tightened — that tell he had when he was trying not to say something he’d regret. Melena had the same tell. Same clench. 
“I didn’t stop being your father,” he said quietly. “I’ve always been your father.”
“You stopped looking at us.” Melena’s voice cracked. “You stopped talking to us. Elias became the Crown Prince early. All because you couldn’t be bothered to do your fucking job and help our grandmother run this fucking empire so we could grieve.”
“I didn’t make him do anything.”
“You didn’t stop him either!” The fire erupted from her palms before she could control it — flames licking up her arms, turning her skin hot enough that the air around her shimmered. “The Privy Council said Elias should take over and you just — you just nodded. Didn’t fight. Didn’t argue. Didn’t even pretend to care that they were putting the weight of the Empire on your son who’d just lost his mother.”
Tobias watched the flames with the kind of detached interest someone might give a mildly interesting painting.
“You want to know why I didn’t fight?” he asked.
“Yes!” Melena shouted. “Yes, I want to know why you gave up on us.”
“Because every time I looked at you —” Tobias’s voice finally broke. Just a little. “All I saw was her.”
The flames died.
Melena stood there, smoke curling off her skin, and felt something in her chest...
Crack.
“You have her eyes,” Tobias continued. His own eyes were wet now. “Not the color. The shape. The way you look at people like you’re trying to figure out if they’re worth your time. That’s Lucia. All Lucia.”
“I have your eyes,” Melena whispered. “Everyone says I have your eyes.”
“You have my eye color. But you look at the world the way she did.” Tobias took a step toward her. “Elias has her patience. Cordelia has her laugh. Louis has her hands — did you notice that? Same fingers.”
Melena’s throat was too tight to respond.
“And you,” Tobias said, stopping directly in front of her, “you have her intensity. Her fire. Even though you’re a Flamer and she was a Flamer and I’m a Frost — somehow you still move through the world exactly the way I do. Aggressive. Uncompromising. That thing where you’d rather burn everything down than admit you’re wrong.”
“That’s not—”
Crack.
“It is.” A ghost of a smile touched his face. “You’re more like me than any of them. Same temper. Same stubbornness. Same inability to forgive yourself for anything. But when I looked at you —” His voice… 
Crack.
“When I looked at any of you after she died, all I could see was everything I’d lost.”
The silence stretched between them. Outside, wind moved through the lily garden. Inside, Melena could hear her own heartbeat — too loud in her ears.
“You had four children who needed you.”
“I know.”
“Elias was barely old enough to take care of himself, let alone raise Cordelia and Louis. Do you know what that did to him? Having to be a parent at twenty? Wasn’t like our grandmother could just stop being the Empress.”
“Yes.” Tobias’s hands curled into fists at his sides. “I know exactly what it did to him.”
“Why?” Melena’s voice rose. “What could have 
“Because I couldn’t look at them without wanting to die!” Tobias’s voice splintered as the words tore their way out. “Because every time I tried to do the right thing, I just made it worse. I couldn’t help you. I couldn’t help myself. So I let the Privy Council take control. Let them take the Empire, let them take Elias, let them take you —because if I tried to hold on, I’d drag you all down with me. Your mother wouldn’t forgive me for that.”
Melena was shivering now, even though the air was still sun-warm and jazzed with the static of her element’s aftermath. She wanted to scream. Break something. But everything here belonged to her mother, and the only things to break were the ghosts.
“I just wanted to stop existing. Just — stop. Stop breathing. Stop pretending. Stop feeling everything and nothing at the same time. I didn’t know how to be your father without being her husband first. Everything I was — everything I knew about being a parent — came from her. She was the one who knew what to say when you had nightmares. She was the one who could make Cordelia stop crying. She was the one who taught me how to talk to Elias about the weight of being heir because she thought he’d take it better from his father and not the voice of the crown. Without her —” He spread his hands. “I didn’t know how to be what you needed.”
Melena felt tears on her face. When had she started crying? She wiped them away — angry, frustrated, grief pouring out of her in ways she’d spent three years trying to contain.
“You left us a letter,” she said. “That’s it. Just a letter saying you were sorry and you loved us. And then you died six months later.”
“I know.”
“Do you?” Her fire erupted again — hotter this time, angrier. “Do you know what it’s like to find out your father killed himself and the only explanation you get is a piece of paper? And you’re only seventeen?”
Tobias didn’t flinch from the flames. “I didn’t kill myself, Melena.”
“Then how’d you die?” Melena cried out. “The reports said it was an accident. They said you slipped on the wet stairs one night after the maids cleaned and broke your neck. But I know how much you drank after she died. I know because I watched you do it. You disappeared like once a week, and you’d come back reeking of whiskey and sadness and not even look at us—”
Tobias’s hands trembled. He tried to lace them behind his back — the way he used to during council briefings — but they wouldn’t stay still. “I didn’t eat,” he said quietly. “Didn’t sleep. I’d sit in that chair —” he nodded toward the desk, “— and stare at the door until sunrise. Hoping she’d walk through it like she used to.”
He chuckled at the memory. “I’d tell the servants to leave the lights on at night. Said it was for the children when really I couldn’t stand the darkness of the bedroom without her.”
Melena’s throat burned. “Dad —”
He was crying now, not bothering to hide it anymore. Not the way men were supposed to cry in the Empire — with dignity, neatly contained, and always in private. The tears streaked his ruined face, caught in the lines around his mouth, and made him look younger than Melena remembered. So much younger. Like a boy who’d lost the only thing that mattered and didn’t know how to stand up after.
“Dad.” Melena hated how small her voice sounded. “I miss you.”
Tobias dropped to one knee the way he had when she was little, back when her mother was alive and the world made sense. “You don’t have to forgive me, but you do have to let yourself live, Melena. She would want that. She’d haunt me for eternity if I let all I was become a memory that weighed you down.”
Melena wanted to say something, something that would set the room on fire or crack the windows, but all she could do was run into her father’s open arms. Her father’s arms closed around her, shaking as he caught her. Melena could smell the ghost of his cologne, the ever-present tang of liquor, the sweat-soaked grief he’d worn like a second skin for years. Her cheek pressed to his shoulder, and she hated how good it felt, how safe, how wrong. Because it wasn’t real. This was a trick of the Mirror, not a homecoming. But her body remembered the shape of him, the comfort, how sometimes when nothing else worked he’d just hold her until she stopped shaking.
He held her for a long time. She couldn’t say how long. Time in the Mirror bent in ways that it didn’t on the outside. At last, he pulled back, hands cupping her face. His thumbs wiped away the tears that wouldn’t stop, the way he used to when she was small and embarrassed to be crying in front of anyone
“I loved you. Past tense,” Tobias said. “I love you. Present tense. Even now. Even here. But that love wasn’t enough to save me. And I’m sorry it wasn’t enough to make me stay.”
The chamber started to dissolve — Tobias fading with it. But before he disappeared completely, he leaned forward and pressed a kiss to her forehead. The way he used to when she was little. When things were simple. When her mother was alive and her father was whole and the world made sense.
“You’re going to survive this,” he whispered against her skin. “Because you are Lucia’s daughter. And that means you don’t break — you burn. And what did Mama say about burning?”
“Burning is surviving.”
“That’s right,” he gave her one final smile. “Burning is surviving.”
Then he was gone.
And Melena was alone in the white again.
She couldn’t cry. Couldn’t scream. Couldn’t do anything but breathe too fast and try not to choke on the air.
This was what she’d done the first time, too — the night they told her he was gone. She hadn’t broken then, either. She’d folded her grief into duty, into posture, into the sound of her grandmother’s voice telling her to stand up straight, Princess.
“You didn’t grieve him,” the mage’s voice said softly, materializing in the white. “You replaced him. You stepped into his place. You made yourself too busy to feel.”
“I burned.”
“You buried him in your duty.”
Melena’s hands curled into fists. “That’s what survival looks like.”
“Is it?” the mage asked. “Or is it surrender? That’s two. Would you like to stop?”
Melena wiped her face. “No.”
“Your father abandoned you,” the mage said. “He chose his grief over his children. Does knowing that his love wasn’t enough change anything? Does it make you less of a princess? Less of an heir? Less of a person?”
“Shut up.”
“Or does it confirm what you’ve always suspected?” The mage tilted their head. “That you’re only valuable when you’re useful. That love is conditional. That if you’re not perfect, people will leave.”
“I said shut up.”
“Interesting.” The mage’s voice never changed. “Your father couldn’t control his grief. You can’t control your fire. Both of you burn when you don’t know what else to do. Both of you destroy when you’re afraid. Tell me, Princess — what makes you different from him?”
“Nothing,” Melena whispered — the answer she didn’t want to give, the truth she’d been running from. “Nothing makes me different.”
“Then perhaps,” the mage said, “you should ask yourself — if you can’t control your fire any better than your father could control his grief, what makes you think you can survive what broke him?”
The white chamber started to shift again.
Melena felt her stomach drop. “How many more?”
“As many as you need,” the mage said. “Until you understand.”
“Understand what?”
“What you’re willing to sacrifice to win.”
## Chapter 34: Katherine – The Fifth Challenge
Katherine counted six exits before the door sealed behind her.
Six doors. No windows. Marble floors that her boots barely made sound on. The room was too bright — that sterile kind of bright that reminded her of medical bays after bad training accidents. The kind of bright that meant someone was about to do something to you that you wouldn't like.
She was alone.
Atlas had been pulled left at the transport bay. Dax right. The rest of the boys scattered to different corridors before Katherine could even process what was happening. Reign had grabbed her hand for half a second — squeezed once — then got dragged through a different door entirely.
Now it was just Katherine and whatever the hell this challenge was.
Her wrist felt a little better but not much. Forty-eight hours later and she could still feel Atlas's panic in the back of her head. Still feel Dax's heat under her skin from when he'd walked into her frozen room. Still feel that pull toward Melena that made absolutely no goddamn sense. She was exhausted. She’d been running on adrenaline for so long that she wasn’t sure there was anything let in her to give. Katherine's hands curled into fists at her sides.
Focus.
Six exits meant six potential routes. The room was circular — maybe twenty feet across. No furniture except for a single table in the center. And on that table—
A mask.
Black mesh. The kind that would cover her whole face.
"Corporal Katherine Medina Celeste Nicholas."
The voice came from everywhere at once. Katherine spun, frost already gathering at her fingertips, but there was no target. Just a voice that seemed to fille every corner of the room and then some. An Elite mage materialized in front of the table. 
Katherine moved to defend herself when the mage raised a hand. "Your element will not help you here."
"That's what everyone keeps saying." Katherine didn't lower her hands. "Right before they try to kill me."
"Heart's Mirror does not kill." The mage's head tilted. "It reveals. Whether you survive what it shows you is another matter entirely."
Katherine's jaw clenched. "And if I refuse?"
"Then you forfeit." The mage's voice held no emotion. "Old Prosperity loses a Queen Piece. Your province loses the Tournament. Atlas Tolliver loses his another one of his soldiers and spends the rest of his career wondering if you were simply too weak to face yourself."
Katherine's frost spread across her palms.
"I'd suggest you put the mask on," the mage said. "Unless cowardice is hereditary."
Katherine's hands shook — from rage or fear, she couldn't tell anymore. She walked forward, grabbed the mask and put it on. 
The world went white.
Then it went black.
Not the comfortable black of closing your eyes. The black of drowning. Of suffocating. Of being buried alive in your own head with no way out.
Katherine tried to scream. Her lungs wouldn't work. Tried to rip the mask off. Her arms wouldn't move. Tried to call her element.
Nothing.
Just black. Just silence. Just the sound of her own heartbeat getting faster and faster and—
Crack. 
She knew this place.
The parade lane was chalked in blue on packed snow. Vendor tents were breathing steam. The air smelled like pine, fried dough, and hot metal from the portable heaters. Somewhere close she could smell the sugared almonds. Somewhere closer she could smell the acrid tang of burned sugar, which meant someone had torched another snack cart. Somewhere in the distance a crowd was singing the Empire’s anthem, their voices bouncing off the ice like chimes.
Katherine turned in a slow, juddering circle. She knew every inch of this corridor, even if her hands were three sizes smaller and her head barely reached the vendor carts. The world was crowded at this height. Older kids darted past, snatching up cups of mulled cider and armfuls of blue-and-silver pennants. A
“Don’t run,” a voice behind her said. 
Katherine turned and was face to face with her mother. 
Michelle Nicholas stood twenty steps back, fixating a scarf, talking to a vendor with a smile that didn’t quite reach her eyes. The pink hat with the stupid pom was tucked under her arm — Katherine’s hat — because Katherine always said it made her look like a baby. A thermos cap sat in her mother’s free hand, steam ribboning up and vanishing in the night.
Katherine’s pulse slipped. Ten years old. Mittens damp where she’d scooped snow. A wooden token pressed into her palm for the carousel she pretended she was too old to ride. The edge dug into her skin — star-shaped, with a tiny Old Prosperity crest burned into the middle.
Across the lane, soldiers in dress blacks did crowd control with soft voices. One laughed. One scanned. One smelled like oil and cold steel when the wind shifted. Music crackled, skipped, caught again. A banner snapped — OLD PROSPERITY’S MIDWINTER FEAST — and the rope thrummed through its knots.
Her wrist ached. Not the now-ache. The then-ache. The ghost of falling on the ice by the pond and saying she didn’t cry because Frosts don’t cry, and her mother saying, “Frosts feel, Katherine — we just don’t shatter.”
A little girl barreled into her. Katherine looked down at herself and met wide brown eyes in a face she knew too well — chapped lip, gap tooth, stubborn mouth already set for a fight.
“Sorry,” the girl blurted, already twisting away, already aiming for the line of glass ornaments with snow caught in the wire hooks.
Katherine didn’t move.
"Bet I can beat you to the other side!"
Katherine's head snapped to the direction of the other kids voice. Mara Kingston ran past her as well, blonde braids whipping in the wind. 
"No fair!" She watched as the words fell out of her younger self’s mouth. "You got a head start!"
"Then catch up!" Mara called over her shoulder.
Katherine chased after her, boots slipping on packed snow, the cold biting through her socks. She pumped her arms, trying to close the gap. Mara was fast — always had been, with those long legs and that competitive streak that made everything a challenge. Her younger self ran slightly ahead of her, and Katherine couldn't remember when she'd been such a fan of running. Her breath burned at the top of her throat as she charged through a cluster of teenagers standing around a pie cart, nearly toppling a stack of glazed berry tarts. She heard a vendor shout after her but the words dissolved into the general noise of the crowd.
Mara ducked between two ice sculptures — a pair of dueling rams, their horns locked together — and skidded to a stop at the edge of the Grand Boulevard, where the crowd thinned and the open expanse of the frozen lake gleamed under the spotlights. Katherine caught up, hands on her knees, chest heaving. She could taste iron and sugar, and her lungs ached from the run.
"Told you I'd win!" Mara laughed, that loud, unfiltered cackle that used to make Katherine's mother shake her head at dinner. 
Katherine watched as her younger self jumped up to high five Mara as they began to play at the furthest edge of the lake, their breath making twin clouds in the freezing air. Mara's scarf — red and fraying at the edges, the one she'd stolen from her older brother — whipped behind her as she spun.
"It's solid! See?" Mara stomped her boot down twice, grinning. "Thick as stone!"
Katherine remembered this. The confidence. The cold. The way Mara's cheeks flushed pink against her brown skin.
Then the world snapped.
Not tilted. Snapped. Like a bone breaking, like a rope pulled too tight finally giving way. The sound of the crowd cut out mid-breath. The snow that should've dusted her coat was gone. The mittens she'd been holding —gone. Katherine looked down and her stomach dropped into nothing.
Small hands. Her hands. Not the scarred ones she'd trained with for years, the ones marked with healed bruises and blade work. These were rounder, softer, pink from cold and trembling around the silver token she'd never get to spend. Her coat sleeves swallowed her wrists. She could feel her heart — thud thud thud — too loud, too fast, a child's rabbit-quick pulse.
No. No no no—
"Come on!" Mara shouted. She was already half a dozen yards ahead, running backward, arms spread wide like she owned the whole frozen lake. "You know, for a Frost, you sure are afraid of getting wet."
"I'm not scared," young-Katherine snapped.
But the words came out twice. Once from her ten-year-old mouth, high and defensive — and once from deep in her adult throat, almost as unsure as she’d been ten years before. The voices overlapped, wrong, so wrong her skin crawled. She tried to step back but her feet didn't listen. This body wasn't hers to control. Not anymore.
Mara skidded to a stop, throwing her arms out for balance. "Race you to the marker!"
The marker. That red buoy half-frozen into the center of the lake. Katherine remembered that part. 
"Wait—" she started, but Mara was already running, her laugh carrying across the ice.
And Katherine's body—her small body—moved before she could stop it.
The ice cracked under her first step.
The sound was like thunder breaking, sharp and final. A spiderweb of hairline fractures rippled outward, spreading faster than she could run. She reached for Mara, fingers brushing the edge of her coat—
Then the lake gave way.
Cold.
Cold.
It swallowed her whole, a mouth of teeth and darkness. The shock hit first — her lungs seizing, her body forgetting how to work. She tried to scream but water rushed in, choking her, burning her throat. Light shattered into pieces above her, warping and bending, so close but impossibly far. She kicked hard but her boots were tied to tight. The coat pulled her down like hands dragging her deeper. Up. Swim up. But which way was up? Everything was blue and black and spinning.
She could see Mara's shape — warped, rippling, mouth open in a scream Katherine couldn't hear.
Her lungs were screaming. Chest collapsing. Arms too heavy. The cold bit into her bones, made her thoughts slow and thick. This is it. This is how—
No.
No.
