She reached for the surface, fingers clawing through water like it was solid. Her reflection flickered above her— ten years old, then older, then both, splitting and bending like broken glass. Her vision blurred at the edges, darkness creeping in.
And then something moved inside her.
Not air. Not water.
Heat.
It started in her ribs — small and confused, like an ember she'd swallowed by accident. Then it pulsed. Piercing. Angry. A living thing that didn't belong. The warmth surged outward, crawling through her chest, her arms, her fingertips, wrapping around her like a second heartbeat made of fire and light.
The water around her hissed.
Steam ghosted from her fingertips. Her right palm started to glow — orange veins threading beneath her skin like lightning trapped under glass, branching up her wrist, her forearm. It didn't hurt. It should've hurt. But it felt like something breaking open, like a door she didn't know existed slamming wide.
The lake brightened. Every crystal, every fracture in the ice flared gold, turning the water into molten light. Katherine's eyes went wide, her mouth opening in a soundless gasp.
Frosts didn't—
The thought shattered.
White.
Not the soft white of snow or morning light. This was the white of erasure—blinding, absolute, the kind that burned behind your eyelids even when you squeezed them shut.
Then silence.
When Katherine's eyes snapped open, the lake was gone. No ice. No Mara. No ten-year-old body that didn't belong to her anymore. Just cold marble under her palms and the taste of smoke in the back of her throat. The air still smelled faintly of steam — sulfur and something burned, like metal left too long in a forge. Her gloves were damp, the leather dark and smoking at the fingertips like she'd grabbed a hot brand and held on too long.
She was back in the chamber.
When did I fall?
She was on her knees, breath coming in ragged gasps that sawed through her chest. Her heartbeat hammered in her teeth, her jaw, the base of her skull. Every nerve in her body felt scraped raw. Her right hand — the one that had glowed, the one that had burned — was still tingling, phantom heat crawling under her skin like insects made of fire.
The Elite mage stood where the table had been, hands folded behind their back. Waiting. They hadn't moved. Maybe they'd been standing there the whole time. Katherine tried to stand. Her legs didn't listen. Her knees buckled, hands slapping against the marble to keep herself from collapsing completely. The chamber spun once, twice, before settling back into focus.
"What was that?" she managed, the words scraping out of her throat. "What did you make me see?"
"We made nothing," the mage said simply, like they were discussing the weather. "The Mirror shows only what you've already chosen to forget."
Katherine's throat tightened. Chosen? "That wasn't—I didn't choose to—"
"Didn't you?" The mage's eyes were dark and unreadable. "Trauma is a clever thing, Frost child. It knows what you can't carry. What you'll break yourself trying to hold."
She remembered the heat. The way it had felt waking up inside her — foreign and terrifying and still somehow hers. The way the water had hissed when her palm glowed. Frosts didn't do that. Frosts were ice and winter and old bloodlines that bent the cold to their will.
Fire was everything she wasn’t.
The mage tilted their head, studying her like a specimen under glass. Their expression didn't change, but something shifted in their posture — a focus, like a predator catching a scent. "Tell me, Frost child—was that fear you felt when the ice broke?"
Katherine's mouth went dry.
"Or was it fire?"
## Chapter 35: Melena – The Fifth Tournament 
Melena didn't know which scenario this was anymore. Tenth? Fifteenth? Her sense of time had melted somewhere between the first and fifth scenario. Every reset scraped something off her until she wasn’t sure which memories were hers and which the Mirror had invented just to watch her flinch. Even her breath felt thinner, like the Mirror was using her oxygen to keep itself alive.
She'd killed Cordelia. Watched Louis burn. Stood in the throne room while her grandmother said Elias was always the better choice. Killed teammates. Burned rivals. Chosen herself until the pattern was so obvious even she couldn’t pretend not to see it.
Each time the mage appeared afterward with that same criticizing voice: "You prioritize power over people. Do you see the pattern?"
Melena was on the floor now, knees to chest, hands shaking. How long had she been in here? Time didn't work right in the Mirror. She could feel her body outside—still standing, probably. Still breathing. But here, she was losing pieces of herself.
"I'm done." Her voice sounded wrecked. "I'm fucking done. Let me out."
The mage materialized. "You cannot leave until you face it."
"I've faced it." Melena's laugh came out like a scream. "What else do you fucking want from me?"
"The truth."
“I TOLD YOU THE TRUTH!” She tried to stand anyway, legs shaking, vision blurring. “I know I’m selfish. I know I’d choose the crown. I know I’d kill for it. Now let me OUT.”
The mage watched her like they'd seen this dance before.
"No."
"Why not?"
"Because you haven't asked yourself the real question."
Melena wanted to cry but she was too tired for that. "What question?"
The mage tilted their head. "If you would kill for the crown — if you would sacrifice anyone, burn anything, destroy everything standing between you and what you want — then what does that make you?" The air thickened, the white starting to pulse faintly gold beneath her palms, like the Mirror was already building the next scene out of her heartbeat. She hated how familiar that rhythm sounded — like a foreign pulse instead of her own.
"A monster."
"Perhaps." The mage paused, waving their hand around. She tried to protest but her body wouldn't cooperate. She just slumped there on the floor and let the Mirror take her wherever it wanted because she didn't have the strength to fight anymore.
The throne room reformed around her.
Again.
Always the throne room. The air wasn’t ceremonial this time — it smelled like dust and burnt wax, like no one had sat the throne in years. Melena looked around, half expecting to have to fight her way out of the room again until her eyes landed on the man in front of the dais. 
Elias. 
Standing in the one place she never wanted to see him again. 
He looked tired. That was Melena's first thought. Not like the prince from the portraits. Not like the brother she’d built her fury around. Just a man, sagging under a crown he couldn’t take off. Her perfect brother, the Crown Prince, the heir to the fucking Empire, looked like he hadn't slept in days. His jacket was rumpled. His hair wasn't styled the way he usually kept it. His eyes — those eyes that could convince anyone of anything — were red-rimmed and hollow.
He looked like Melena felt. For one impossible second, she thought maybe he’d forgive her. Maybe this was the version where he didn’t hate her yet.
"Hey, Mel," he said softly.
Melena didn't move from where she'd appeared — halfway across the throne room, too far away to touch him but close enough to see every detail. "You're not real."
"Does it matter?"
"Yes." Her voice came out harsh. "It matters because I'm losing my fucking mind in here and I can't — I can't do this again. Not with you. Not after—"
She stopped. Couldn't finish. Because she'd killed him already. Twice. Once in a scenario where he tried to take the throne from her. Once in a scenario where the Mirror made her choose between saving him or saving herself.
Both times she'd chosen herself.
Both times she'd watched him die and felt—
Relief.
That was the part that made her sick. Not the killing. The relief.
"I know what the Mirror's been showing you," Elias said. He took a step closer. Melena's hands sparked but nothing happened. "I know what you've been choosing. Every single time."
"Then you know I'm a terrible person."
"I know you're my sister." He stopped about ten feet away. Close enough that she could see the resemblance between them — same nose, same jawline, same way they both held tension in their shoulders. "And that you’re more realistic than me, at least."
"What's that supposed to mean?"
Elias smiled but it didn't reach his eyes. "It means I've been lying to myself for years. Pretending I wanted this. Pretending I could be what Grandmother needs. Pretending I could handle the weight of an entire Empire on my shoulders." He gestured at the throne behind him. "I can't, Mel. I never could."
"That's not—" Melena's throat closed. "You're the Crown Prince. You were born for this."
"No." Elias shook his head. "Mama was born for this. Grandmother was born for this. You—" He pointed at her. "You were born for this. I was just the one who happened to be a boy when Grandmother needed an heir to replace Mama."
The words hit harder than Melena expected. She'd spent twenty years resenting him for being first. For being chosen. For being the one everyone looked at when they thought about the future of the Empire.
And here he was saying he never wanted it in the first place.
"You ran after the Tournament," Melena said. It came out like an accusation. "You were the Empire’s pride. The Crown Prince who’d won his Tournament and then you just left. Mama died and you left. Do you know what that did to me? Having to step up because you couldn't handle it?"
"I know exactly what it did." Elias's voice was quiet. "It gave you what you always wanted."
Melena froze.
"Don't," she whispered. "Don't say that."
"Why not?" Elias spread his hands. "It's true. You've been waiting for me to fail your entire life. Waiting for Grandmother to realize I'm not cut out for this. Waiting for your chance to prove you're better, stronger, more worthy of the crown." He took another step forward. "And I gave it to you. The Tournament. The Queen Piece. The chance to show everyone what you can do without me standing in your way."
"I didn't—I never wanted you to—"
"Yes you did." Elias's voice wasn't angry. Just tired. So fucking tired. "You wanted me gone. Maybe not dead. But gone. Out of your way. And now here we are."
The throne room felt too small suddenly. Like the walls were pressing in. Like the air was too thick to breathe.
"This isn't real," Melena said again. "You're just the Mirror showing me what I'm afraid of."
"Am I?" Elias tilted his head. "Or am I showing you what you want?"
The floor started to shift. Not dramatically. Just—slightly. Melena looked down. Saw cracks forming beneath her boots. Spreading outward in thin, spiderweb patterns that made the whole room look like it could break if either one of them stepped too hard.
"What do you want, Mel?" Elias asked.
"I want—" Her voice caught. "I want to go home."
The air went brittle. Even breathing started to sound like breaking glass.
"Liar."
"I want—" Melena tried again. "I want my team to survive. I want to finish the Tournament. I want—"
"You want the crown." Elias said it with the same tone as a weather man describing sunny weather. "You want Grandmother to look at you the way she looked at Mama. You want the Empire to kneel." He paused. "And you want me out of the way so you can have it."
Melena's knees gave out.
She didn't fall—just sort of folded onto the cracked marble, hands pressed flat against the floor, breathing too fast. Her vision was going weird at the edges. Dark spots blooming and fading. Her chest felt like someone had replaced her ribs with broken glass.
"I don't—" she gasped. "I don't want you dead."
"Then what do you want?" Elias crouched down in front of her. Close enough now that she could see he'd been crying, tear tracks still damp on his face.
"It's okay, Mel," he said softly. "You can say it. Nobody's listening except us. Nobody's gonna judge you. Just— just say what you really want. For once in your life, be honest."
Melena looked up at him. Her brother. Her rival. The person she'd spent her entire life being compared to.
The person standing between her and everything she'd ever wanted.
"I want you to abdicate," she whispered.
Elias smiled. It was sad and understanding and somehow that made it worse. "I know."
"I want Grandmother to name me heir. I want the Empire to know I'm not the backup. I'm not second. I'm—"
"First," Elias finished. "You want to be first."
"Yes." The word came out broken. "I want to be first."
The throne room shuddered.
The cracks in the floor widened. Hairline fractures rippled across the marble, gold light seeping through like veins of molten guilt. It looked like fire pretending to be light. Even her shame burned. Elias stood up. Brushed off his pants like this was a normal conversation. Like his sister hadn't just admitted she wanted him erased from the line of succession.
"Then take it," he said.
"What?"
He gestured at the throne. "Take it. It's right there. Everything you've ever wanted. Everything you've been fighting for. Just—take it."
"I can't just—that's not how succession works—"
"This is the Mirror, Mel." Elias's voice was gentle. "Rules don't work the same way here. If you want the throne, if you want the crown, if you want to be first—" He stepped aside, clearing the path to the dais. "Then take it."
Melena stared at the throne.
It was smaller than she remembered, but somehow the shadow it cast still reached her feet even if it was just a chair made of black marble and red velvet. Just a thing her grandmother sat in when she wanted people to remember who was in charge.
Just the thing Melena had been reaching for her entire life.
"What happens if I sit in it?" she asked.
"You tell me."
Melena's hands were shaking again. She pushed herself to standing—legs unsteady, head spinning, everything in her body screaming to just lie down and let the Mirror keep her here forever because at least then she wouldn't have to make this choice.
But she'd been making this choice her whole life, hadn't she?
Every time she pushed herself harder in training. Every time she bit back a comment about Elias's diplomatic approach being too soft. Every time she looked at the throne and imagined herself sitting there instead of him.
Every single time, she'd been choosing this.
She walked toward the dais.
The cracks in the floor followed her. Spreading wider with each step. The darkness beneath getting closer. Hungrier.
Elias watched her go. Didn't try to stop her. Didn't argue. Just—watched. Like he'd been expecting this all along.
Melena reached the throne.
Put her hand on the armrest.
Felt the cold marble under her palm.
"If I sit here," she said without turning around, "what happens to you?"
Silence.
Then: "Does it matter?"
Melena's hand tightened on the armrest. "Yes. It matters. You're my brother. You're—"
"In your way."
She turned. Elias was closer now. Standing right at the edge of the dais. And his expression—
He looked relieved.
"Mel," he said softly, "I've been drowning since Mama died. Every time someone calls me Crown Prince, I feel like I'm suffocating. I don't want this." He gestured at the throne. "I never wanted this. But I couldn't say it because—because what kind of heir walks away? What kind of son looks at his grandmother and says 'I can't'?"
"Elias—"
"You can, though." His voice was certain now. "You can carry this weight. You want to carry it. So why shouldn't you?"
"Because it's not mine."
"It could be."
The throne room started collapsing — marble splitting, ceiling caving, floor giving way beneath Elias’s feet. He didn't move. Just stood there as the darkness swallowed him inch by inch.
"Elias!" Melena lunged for him but the space between them stretched impossibly wide. "ELIAS!"
"Take the throne, Mel," he called over the sound of everything breaking. 
"I DON'T WANT IT LIKE THIS!"
"Yes you do."
And he was right.
God help her, he was right.
Because even as Melena watched her brother disappear into the darkness—even as she screamed his name and reached for him and felt her fire finally ignite in pure, desperate panic—part of her was already calculating. Her tears steamed on the marble, hissing out one by one.
How long until Grandmother announced the change in succession.
How long until the Empire knew.
How long until she finally got what she'd been fighting for.
The throne room finished collapsing.
Melena was left standing on the dais, hand still on the throne, surrounded by nothing but darkness and the terrible certainty that she'd just killed her brother.
Not with fire.
Not with violence.
With want.
She didn’t scream again. There was nothing left that sounded human in her. The white returned slowly this time. Creeping in at the edges until it swallowed the darkness, the ruins, the memory of Elias's face as he fell. Melena was on her knees again. When had she fallen? She couldn't remember. Couldn't think past the roaring in her ears.
"Congratulations," The mage appeared in front of her. "You've faced the truth."
"I—" Melena's voice wouldn't work right. "I killed him."
"Did you?"
"I let him die. I let him fall. I—"
"You chose yourself," the mage corrected. "Every choice. Every moment where the Mirror asked you what matters more—your ambition or other people—you chose ambition."
"I'm a monster."
"Perhaps." The mage tilted their head. "Or perhaps you simply understand what most people refuse to admit: that power requires sacrifice. That crowns are built on bodies. That if you want to lead, you must be willing to let others fall."
Melena looked up. "I don't want to be like this," she whispered.
"Then what do you want?"
Melena opened her mouth. Closed it. Opened it again.
"I want—" Her voice cracked. "I want to be seen. I want people to know I'm powerful. I want Grandmother to stop looking past me. I want the Empire to know I'm not second. I'm not the spare. I'm—"
"Worthy," the mage finished. "You want to be worthy."
"Yes."
"And if being worthy means letting Elias fall? Means sacrificing teammates? Means burning everyone who stands between you and the crown?"
Melena's hands curled into fists.
"Then I guess—" She took a shaking breath. "I guess that's who I am."
The white exploded. Not with sound. Just—sensation. Like her entire body was being pulled apart and put back together at the same time. She felt the mask on her face suddenly—really felt it, the weight of it, the cold mesh against her skin.
Then it was gone.
She was back in the room again. The white room with the table and the other girls and—
Melena collapsed.
Hands caught her—Seraphina, maybe, or Viv—but she couldn't tell. Everything was too bright, too loud, too real after being in the Mirror for—
How long?
She looked up. Saw Astor leaning against the wall, eyes wide and hollow. Saw Viv sitting on the floor, rocking back and forth. Saw Seraphina staring at her as if she didn't recognize Melena anymore.
They all looked broken.
And Melena felt it rising in her throat—panic or pride, she couldn't tell. She'd just spent however long admitting the ugliest parts of herself to a magical torture device, and now she was sitting here in front of cameras and teammates and she couldn't take any of it back.
They knew. Or they would soon. What she wanted. What she'd choose.
Who she really was under all the training and the smiles and the act.
Her hands were still shaking. She pressed them flat against the floor, felt the cold tile, tried to ground herself in something real. But all she could think about was Elias's face as he fell. All she could feel was that horrible relief.
The Elite mage stood by the table. Watching. Waiting.
"Congratulations, ladies of London Town," they said. "You have completed the Fifth Challenge."
## Chapter 36: Katherine – The Fifth Challenge
Katherine's braids were stuck to her neck.
Her fingers found the base of her skull where the braids started — damp, cold, like she'd just pulled herself out of the ice. But the chamber was dry. The marble under her knees was dry. Her coat was dry.
Everything was dry except her.
Her throat burned as if she'd been screaming underwater for hours. Maybe she had been. She couldn't remember anymore. Couldn't remember where the Memory ended and the Now began. The mage stood in front of her — had they always been standing there? — watching with eyes that Katherine found herself wanting to claw out somewhere around the fifth time she'd drowned. Her palms were pressed flat against the marble. She could see the water stains spreading from her hands. Dark patches bleeding outward. Except — she looked again —they weren't water stains.
They were scorch marks.
Small. Faint. But there. Little burn marks shaped like her fingerprints smoking against the white stone.
"No." The word scraped out of her throat. "No no no—"
"Denial doesn't look good on you, Frost child." The mage shook their head. "When will you learn?"
"Learn what?" Katherine sneered.
"Acceptance."
Katherine's hands were shaking. Her skin felt too tight. Too dry. Like the lake had sucked all the moisture out of her and left her cracked and brittle as old leather. She dragged a hand across her face. Her cheek felt papery. Her lips were chapped so bad she could taste copper where they'd split. She tried to stand but her legs weren't listening. The world tilted sideways and she caught herself with one hand, the other going to her throat because she still couldn't breathe right. Still felt like water was in her lungs even though she knew — she KNEW — it wasn't real.
"Again," the mage said.
"No." Katherine's voice cracked. "I'm not doing that again. Absolutely not."
"You haven't finished."
"I drowned." Katherine's breath was coming too fast. Her vision blurred at the edges. "I felt—I saw—"
The mage tilted their head. "Again."
Katherine tried to scramble back but there was nowhere to go. The world peeled away like skin, like the Mirror was shedding itself, and she was falling — not down, but forward, into something that felt like memory but moved like a nightmare.
The training yard materialized.
Packed dirt under her boots. The smell of steel and sweat and old leather. Weapon racks lined the walls. Wooden practice dummies stood in formation, some of them sporting fresh gouges from morning drills. The sky overhead was gray—that flat, dead gray that meant snow was coming. She hated how well she knew this place. Atlas stood twenty feet away. Arms crossed. Face unreadable. Wearing his Captain's uniform—the one with the Old Prosperity crest at the collar, the one he would have worn if her mother had never sent him to the Legion. Katherine's frost spiked, ice forming bracelets on her wrists before she could stop it.
"No," she shook her head. "No, we're not doing this."
Atlas didn't move. Just watched her with those eyes that saw through every lie she'd ever told herself. "You don't have a choice, Corporal."
"I'm not fighting you."
"Then you'll die."
Katherine's jaw clenched. Her hands curled into fists and the frost spread—over her knuckles, down her fingers, turning her skin pale and cold. "This isn't real. You're not real. The Mirror's just—"
Atlas moved.
Fast. Too fast for someone his size. His fist connected with her ribs and the impact drove the air from her lungs in one violent rush. Pain exploded across her side — she was sure her ribs had cracked. She doubled over but Atlas was already on her, grabbing her by the arm and hurling her across the yard. The world spun. She hit the ground hard enough to rattle her teeth.
"Get up," Atlas said.
She spat blood into the dirt. Pushed to her knees. "You're not him. You're just another shitty trick."
Atlas knelt in front of her. "You think that matters? The pain is real. The lesson's real. You're still you."
He grabbed her by the collar and hauled her upright. She tried to summon her frost — to freeze his hands, create distance, anything — but her power sputtered. The ice on her arms fizzled and died. She was so tired. Her whole body felt empty, like the place where her soul was supposed to be had been flash-burned by something crueler than fire.
"Stop," she rasped.
Atlas's hand was still on her coat. He was so close she could see the threadbare patches on his sleeve. She did the only thing she could think of.
She headbutted him.
Missed. Her forehead bounced off his shoulder instead.
"Try again," he said, almost bored.
She hated him. God, she hated him so much she could taste bile. She twisted, tried to sweep his legs, but he caught her wrist, yanked her arm behind her back, and shoved her face-first into the dirt.
"Is that all?" His boot pressed between her shoulder blades. "You're not even trying."
She tried to scream but her mouth was full of dirt. Then he was hauling her up again, throwing her against the stone wall. She hit it hard, slid down.
"When are you going to defend yourself?" Atlas taunted. "I won't always be there to save you."
She forced herself to stand, blood hot in her mouth. "Screw you."
The yard stuttered — white at the edges, the mage a thin silhouette — then slammed back into dirt and breath and him. 
"You could end this," their voice echoed. "Just accept it."
Katherine spat. "Go to hell."
The world snapped back. Atlas advanced. "You're going to die here. You know that?"
She did. She'd known it from the second she failed the first scenario. Before she could think, he was on her again — jab to her temple, elbow to her jaw. Stars exploded across her vision. She tried to freeze the air between them, but her frost just puffed out, useless.
"Pathetic."
He grabbed her by the throat, lifted her clean off the ground. She kicked, clawed at his wrist, but his grip was iron. Her pulse pounded in her ears. Her feet swung uselessly as he raised her higher. The world tunneled into a black ring.
"You know what you have to do, Little Frost."
"I won't—" Katherine coughed, clawing at his hand. "—kill you."
He squeezed harder. Her lungs seized. Her head swelled. She thrashed, but she couldn't get leverage.
In that last smudge of oxygen, she remembered the scorch marks.
Heat bubbled under her skin, desperate and wild — nothing like the element she'd trained with her entire life. It was hot, so hot she expected her palms to melt through his sleeve.
Atlas's grip faltered. Not much. But enough.
The skin at his wrist sizzled under her palm. He made a sound — half surprise, half fury — and shoved her against the wall. But she didn't let go. She pressed her burning hand tighter and watched the fabric blacken, curl away, watched his flesh char and split beneath her thumb. He snarled and tried to jerk away, but she clung to him, both hands now, fire racing up her arms in a wave so intense she thought it might burn her alive from the inside out.
"Let. Me. Go."
Katherine dropped, legs folding under her, half-blind with pain. The stink of charred wool and seared skin hit her nose. She coughed, spat blood, looked up. Atlas was clutching his wrist, eyes wide. The burn ran all the way to his elbow, angry and raw. She saw it flicker across his face—just for a second—fear. Of her. Of what she'd done.
Then it was gone, replaced by an emotion she’d never thought she’d see from him.
Disgust.
"There she is," Atlas said, voice cold. "The real Katherine Nicholas."
He threw the words at her like stones. "You think all those years of training were about control? About restraint?" He spat blood into the dirt. "I always knew you weren't like them. Like your precious mother, or those sanctimonious Frost girls in Alleia. You never wanted to play defense. You wanted to destroy."
She shook her head but he kept coming, crowding her space. "I tried to protect you from it. But you love the violence. You love the anger. You love the way it feels to watch something break because of you."
"Shut up," she said, voice shredded. "You don't know—"
"I know you better than anyone." His burned hand closed around her shoulder. "You can lie to the world, but you can't lie to me."
He shoved her back. She caught herself on her elbows, cold marble warring with the heat crackling along her veins. She tasted ash. Blood.
The mage's silhouette flickered at the edge of the yard.
"Admit it." Atlas's smoking hand snapped out, caught her by the nape. "You want to burn."
Before she could spit in his face, he was dragging her to the center of the yard. The sky darkened, clouds bruising to violent purple. She dug her heels in but his grip was absolute. He forced her to her knees. She tried to summon heat again, but her hands fizzled. Atlas knelt behind her, pinning her arms to her sides.
"Watch," he said, and clapped a palm to her chest, right above her heart.
The cold hit immediately. Not hers, but his. A cold so deep it wasn't temperature at all—just the idea of absence, the memory of winter. It blossomed outward, veins of blue-white spreading through her torso, lacing up her neck, her jaw, her skull. The emblems on her forearms ignited with pain so excruciating she almost passed out. She looked down. The skin bubbled, blistered. The blue lines split and unraveled, shredding under the ice sliding beneath her skin.
She tried to claw at her arm but her fingers wouldn't move. Atlas was taking it from her. Not just the power —her birthright, her shield, her one fucking inheritance — but the only part of herself she'd ever known.
The frost in her veins shrieked as it died. She could feel it, taste it. The air around them grew thick, charged, like the moment before a lightning strike. Her skin crawled, blistered, and the blue of her family's legacy bled out, replaced by raw, gold heat. She gagged. Her body rejected it — the heat, the newness, the wrongness of it.
He'd taken her Frost, leaving her with nothing but Flame.
"Accept it, Katherine. Accept who you really are."
Tears fell down her face. She screamed — didn't know she could scream like that, didn't know her lungs held that much air. The sound tore out of her mouth as the clouds split open, dumping rain so cold it steamed when it hit her skin, but the fire inside her just kept building.
Atlas held her tighter. "Let it out or I'll do it for you."
"I can wield fire," she whispered through the tears.
"I can't hear you." His nails dug into her chest.
"I can wield fire," she gasped. 
His nails went deeper, past her ribs, past her lungs, into that place where her soul was supposed to be.
"You're still lying." He slammed the back of her head into the dirt. 
"I am—"
"You're NOTHING." He yanked her head up by her hair. "Just some bastard mistake your mother couldn't fix. A weapon that doesn't know which way to point."
He threw her again. This time, she landed against the wall before sliding down it.
"Say it again," he demanded.
"I can wield fire." Her voice was breaking apart.
"LOUDER."
"I CAN WIELD FIRE."
"What else?"
"I—I don't—"
His boot connected with her ribs. She felt something give. Heard the crack echo like thunder. "What. Else."
"I'm not a Frost!" The words tore out of her, bloody and raw. "I'm not—I never was—I'm broken, I'm wrong, I'm—"
"Keep going."
"I'm both — and it hurts!" She was screaming now, voice shredding itself. "I'm ice and I'm fire and I'm NOTHING because I can't be both! Frosts don't—we don't—"
Atlas grabbed her face, fingers digging into her jaw. 
"I loved you anyway," he said, voice suddenly gentle, which was somehow worse. "Even knowing what you were. Even knowing you'd hurt everyone you touched."
Katherine was crying. Ugly, broken sobs that made her ribs scream.
"You'll burn Reign first," Atlas continued. "Sweet, loyal Reign who thinks she can fix you. Then Sebastian. Then everyone in that gods-damned squad. And you'll tell yourself it was an accident."
"I won't—"
"You will." He let go of her face and stood. "We both know the truth."
His voice was fading. "You are a mistake. A beautiful, terrible mistake."
The training yard exploded into a thousand fragments and she was falling through white and noise and pain and—
The mask ripped off.
Katherine's back arched. Her mouth opened in a scream but no sound came out. Something was dragging her out of the Mirror by her bones, by her soul, by every piece of her that still knew how to be human. Her body slammed into marble. She couldn't move. Couldn't see. Couldn't breathe. Her hands were glowing — she didn't know what they were doing anymore.
Voices. Distant. Echoing.
Someone touched her shoulder.
Katherine screamed.
She scrambled backward, hands out, and frost—no, fire—no, both—erupted from her palms in a chaotic burst. The person jumped back. She couldn't see their face. Everything was white and bright and too loud and—
"Corporal Nicholas."
That voice.
The mage.
Katherine's vision cleared slowly. She was in the chamber. The real chamber. The mask lay on the floor, cracked and smoking.
And Reign.
Braids. Brown eyes. Reaching for her.
"Kat—"
"No no no you're not—this isn't—" Katherine's voice didn't sound like her own.
"Katherine, it's me." Reign's voice was shaking. "I'm here. We both—"
"Reign?" Katherine couldn't stop shaking. Her hands were still glowing—blue, orange, blue, orange—flickering like a broken light.
Reign's eyes dropped to Katherine's hands. To two elements that shouldn't exist together. Katherine saw it flash across her face—shock but then Reign forced her hands to stop trembling and grabbed her. 
"Ladies of Old Prosperity." The Elite mage's voice cut through the chamber as they stepped between them. "Congratulations. You have completed the Fifth Challenge."
Katherine stared at the mage. Then at Reign. Then at her hands.
Blue. Orange. Ice. Fire.
She started laughing.
It came out wrong. Broken. Edged with something that might've been hysteria. She couldn't stop. Couldn't breathe. Just laughed and laughed until the sound turned into sobs and She sat down against the wall while Reign let her fall apart.
Chapter 37: Katherine/Melena
KATHERINE
The lights in medical were too bright.
Katherine knew that because she'd been staring at them for six hours and they still hadn't dimmed. Still burned into her retinas. Still made her head pound like someone had taken a hammer to her skull and decided to keep going just for fun.
Her hands wouldn't stop.
Blue. Orange. Blue. Orange.
Frost crept across her left palm like ice on a windshield. Fire crackled on her right like she'd dipped it in gasoline and lit a match. Neither one listened when she told them to stop. Neither one cared that she was so fucking tired she wanted to cry.
It felt wrong. Not just the flickering—though that was bad enough—but the sensation underneath. Like her blood was boiling and freezing at the same time. Hot-cold-hot-cold until her stomach lurched and she had to close her eyes to keep from throwing up. Her skin felt too tight. Dry and cracked on her left side like she'd been standing in a desert. Damp and feverish on her right like she was burning from the inside out.
She was both. She was neither. She didn't know what the fuck was happening anymore.
Reign sat in the chair beside the bed. Had been there since they dragged Katherine out of Heart's Mirror. Hadn't left. Hadn't said much either, which was probably smart because Katherine didn't have words for what had just happened to her.
What the Mirror had shown her.
What Mirror Atlas had done to her.
"You need to eat," Reign said quietly.
Blue. Orange. Blue. Orange.
The door opened and a med-tech came in with a tray. Soup. Bread. Water. All of it smelling like absolutely nothing. The med-tech angled the tray to the ceiling cam before setting it down. Proof of “nutritional compliance.” Katherine looked at it and her stomach turned. Katherine wondered if they'd replay this moment during the pre-fight broadcast — Old Prosperity’s Queen too broken to eat soup. She could already hear the commentators' voices: Look at the toll Heart's Mirror takes. Look at what it costs to be powerful. 
"Just try," Reign said.
Katherine picked up the spoon because Reign asked. Brought it to her mouth because Reign was watching. Swallowed because she had to. A tally light glowed above the corner lens. “Off,” the med-tech had said hours ago. It still blinked every time she moved.
It tasted like ash. Like burnt plastic. Like her mouth was full of smoke and ice shards and nothing else existed.
She set the spoon down.
"Kat—"
"I tried." Katherine sniffled as tears ran down her face. "I can't."
Reign's jaw tightened but she didn't push. Just moved the tray aside and went back to her chair. She wiped her cheeks with the back of her hand, smearing water and sweat and probably a little snot. It didn't matter. Nothing mattered except the ache in her chest and the way her vision clouded at the edges, like the world was a snowglobe someone kept shaking. Sometimes the cold won, sometimes the heat, but always the shaking.
Katherine flexed her fingers over the blanket. The sparks danced and hissed, blue on one side, orange on the other, and she remembered what it felt like to be in that place, in the Mirror. To see Atlas, Mirror-Atlas, with his eyes bright, the way he smiled at her when he thought she couldn't see. She remembered how he looked at her when he realized she wasn't going to break, not even when he pushed her past the point of reason. She wanted to ask Reign if she remembered what happened in the Mirror. If Reign had a moment when everything broke and how the hell she was so calm. But Katherine couldn't get the words out, couldn't make herself say anything about what she'd seen—what she'd done.
Instead, she pulled the blanket tighter around her shoulders and tried to breathe.
The door opened again, and this time it was Atlas. Actual Atlas, not the one from her mind. A small red light blinked on the ceiling monitor. Atlas clocked it immediately — his eyes flicked up, then away, positioning himself so the camera couldn't catch his face full-on. The wall screen woke as Atlas crossed the threshold: NAME: TOLLIVER, A | STATUS: VISITOR. The font was polite. The meaning wasn’t.
Reign stood. "She needs to eat."
Atlas didn't argue. He waited until the door clicked shut behind Reign before he turned to Katherine.
“You’re here,” she whispered, afraid for him to hear her voice. 
“I’m here,” Atlas walked over to the stand and picked up the soup. “You need to eat. You can’t go into the next challenge this weak.”
The next Challenge. 
Katherine almost threw up. Reign told her that Old Prosperity still had eight Pieces alive and so did London Town but somewhere between then and now, Katherine forgot that mean they’d have to compete in the Sixth Challenge. 
“Three days,” Atlas stirred the soup as if he were preparing to feed her himself. “It’s an equal line up this year. Queen’s Bishop versus Queen’s Bishop. King versus King. Queen—”
“Versus Queen,” Katherine coughed. “I know.”
Blue. Orange. Blue. Orange. 
He took the spoon and filled it with broth, holding it out to her. His hand was steady, like he could borrow calm from the air itself. Like the Mirror hadn’t touched him at all, except she remembered how he’d looked inside, full of terror and longing and something that felt too much like hope.
Katherine stared at the spoon. “You’re not going to feed me.”
Atlas shrugged. “I’ll hold the bowl. You can do the rest.”
She reached for the spoon, and it shook so badly that a few drops splashed onto the blanket. Atlas didn’t flinch. He didn’t reach to wipe it away or make some offhand comment about how she was always a mess. He just waited, patient and silent, while she forced a few spoonfuls down.
The air between them was full of static, buzzing like a hive. She saw his hands, the bandages still fresh, the raw skin underneath. She wondered what he’d done to himself in there. If the pain was equal, if he hated himself for it as much as she hated him for it.
“Are you okay?” Atlas asked finally.
“No.”
Blue. Orange. Blue. Orange.
She just wanted it to stop.
MELENA
She just wanted it to stop.
The fire. The memories. The voice in her head that kept repeating what the mage had said: You prioritize power over people. Do you see the pattern?
Melena stared at the ceiling of her medical room and tried to count the tiles. Lost track somewhere around thirty-seven. Started over. Lost track again.
Every time she closed her eyes she saw it. The Mirror. The choices.
Over and over and over.
Pattern. Pattern. Pattern.
The word tasted like blood in her mouth.
Tiran hadn't left.
He'd pulled a chair up beside her bed six hours ago and hadn't moved since. Hadn't said much either, which was good because Melena didn't want to talk. Didn't want to hear whatever pep talk he was building in his head. Didn't want to be reminded that she'd spent however long in Heart's Mirror watching herself choose wrong over and over and over again until the pattern was so obvious even she couldn't pretend not to see it.
Power over people.
Every time.
Every fucking time.
Her fire sparked without permission. Little bursts along her fingertips that made Tiran shift in his chair but he didn't say anything. Didn't tell her to control it. Probably knew she couldn't right now even if she wanted to.
The door opened.
Different handler than the one who'd checked on her earlier. This one was older. Looked tired. Looked like he'd rather be anywhere else.
"Your Highness," he said, bowing to Melena before turning to Tiran. He positioned himself under the ceiling mic and spoke just loud enough for capture. The camera over his shoulder recording it all. "Your Grace. I've come with an announcement."
“Thank you,” Tiran nodded, slipping into a regal tone that Melena realized she hadn’t heard in weeks. “Continue please.”
“Challenge Six will commence in seventy-two hours," the handler read from his tablet. "Per Tournament tradition, mirrored pieces will engage in single combat. Victory is determined by yield, incapacitation, or death."
Melena didn't sit up. Didn't move. Just kept staring at the ceiling while her fire crackled and popped under her skin. She listened while the handler droned through the rest of the announcement. The words skittered across the surface of her brain but wouldn’t stick. A roster of who had survived, a warning about security restrictions for the next phase, something about mandatory nutritional intake. All of it pointless. Less than nothing.
"You are scheduled for physical rehabilitation at 0800 tomorrow," the handler said, as he clicked his tablet off. "We will monitor your vitals until then. If you require sedation—" He glanced between Melena and Tiran, searching for the break in her composure that would let him proceed as usual.
"She will be fine," Tiran replied for her. "Thank you."
The man bowed and left again. Melena went back to counting ceiling tiles. Thirty-eight. Thirty-nine. Forty.
She could feel Tiran staring at her. Could feel him building up to something. Could feel the words forming in his mouth before he said them.
Melena rolled onto her side, away from him. The IV in her arm tugged and burned, but that was fine. She preferred it to the other sensation. The one she couldn’t describe.
She wanted to sleep, but her brain wouldn’t let her. It kept replaying the same three or four moments from the Mirror, like some asshole had found the most humiliating parts of her life and decided that’s what she needed to see, over and over. She heard the mage’s voice again: You will always choose yourself.
She didn’t want to believe that. But she couldn’t shake the feeling that it was true.
“Melena,” Tiran said softly, “do you want me to leave?”
She hated that her immediate answer was yes. But she shook her head. “No. I just… can you not look at me?”
He gave a little laugh, too quiet to be mocking. “I can do that.” He turned his chair so he was facing the wall, like a child put in time-out. “Better?”
She didn’t answer. Didn’t thank him. Didn’t even feel relief, if she was honest. It was just less bad than before, and that was as close as she was going to get to comfort tonight. She closed her eyes and let the fire run wild under her skin, until the IV beeped and the pain in her arm grounded her enough to open them again. The tally light over the door went from red to amber: standby. Off was a fairy tale.
Forty-three. Forty-four. Forty-five.
KATHERINE
Katherine didn’t remember falling asleep, but she woke up with her hand in a bowl of ice water and Atlas sitting at her bedside, reading something on a datapad. Her wrist band vibrated once—TELEMETRY STABLE—then quieted, satisfied the show could go on. She tried to yank her hand back. Atlas didn’t let her.
“Leave it,” he said, not looking up.
She glared at him but she knew she lost the argument the minute she opened her mouth. 
“They want you in physical rehab but I told them you’d be fine.”
"Katherine raised an eyebrow. “You tell the Sanctum what to do now?”
Atlas shrugged. “This is the final Challenge. I don’t really think they care what we do at this point as long as they get their winner.”
“Where is everyone?”
“Likely leaning against the door listening to my every word,” Atlas snickered. 
And sure enough he was right. 
Sebastian grinned, crossing the room in three long strides. He held a lumpy paper bag in both hands. “We brought contraband.” He plunked it down on the rolling table. Katherine caught the scent of cinnamon and sugar. Her stomach lurched but didn’t rebel, so that was progress.
“You made it,” Zephyr said, like he was surprised. He took the empty spot at the foot of the bed, his arms crossed in a way that meant he was uncomfortable but hiding it. Houston tucked the paper bag beneath the bedframe as the door sensor clicked. Contraband was fine; broadcasting contraband wasn’t.
Dax said nothing. He hovered by the door, arms folded, face set in a hard line. His gaze kept darting to Atlas — never to her. Katherine wondered what happened in the Mirror for him, what his reflection had shown him. Minho was the last to enter. His hair was still wet from a shower and his hoodie looked out of place when the rest of them were dressed in their pajamas. He gave her a little salute and flopped into the nearest chair, looping one ankle over the other. “Nice to see you vertical, Nicholas.”
She tried not to wince at the nickname. Failed.
“Everyone give her some space,” Reign said pushing her way through the door. “You’re grown ass men. You take up space.”
“What’s wrong?” Katherine looked at Dax, ignoring the rest of them. 
“Katherine…” Sebastian trailed off as Zephyr exhaled hard. 
“What’s wrong, Dax?”
“They are separating us,” he said finally locking eyes with her. “The Sanctum wants all Pieces in isolation until the morning of the Challenge. Apparently we all have interviews and schedules and solo things to do as the Tournament wraps up. We’ve got four hours together before they come for us.”
Katherine flexed her hand in the ice water. The ice numbed her wrist all the way to her elbow, a relief so sharp it almost counted as pain. Katherine left it there, hand submerged, until she couldn’t feel her own fingers. When she pulled it out, frost filmed her nails and the tips of her knuckles. The orange was gone for a moment, only blue left. She flexed her palm and watched the chill slough away into the air, dissipating into nothing. She wondered, not for the first time, if it would ever stop.
She met Dax’s gaze. “So they’re just locking us up until game day?”
“Pretty much.” Dax’s voice was flat, as if he’d already resigned himself to it. “No group meals, no meetings, no contact. Not even with your own team.”
“Classic,” Sebastian muttered, rifling through the contraband bag. “Here. Eat something that doesn’t taste like chemicals.” He offered her a pastry, half-smashed and bleeding cinnamon from the middle.
Katherine took it, even though her stomach was still somewhere between queasy and dead. She broke off a small piece, chewed, swallowed. It sat in her gut like a cold stone, but at least it stayed down. 
"Smile for the cameras," Sebastian said, waving at the lens embedded in the ceiling corner. "They're eating this shit up. Ragtag team visits their dying Queen. Very heartwarming."
Houston flipped off the camera without looking. "Let them watch."
"They will," Minho shook his head at his brother. "That's literally their job."
They were quiet for a while, listening to the hum of the overhead lights and the distant echo of doors slamming down the hall. Someone started a card game—Houston, probably, because he always needed something to do with his hands. Minho made a show of shuffling the deck one-handed, letting the cards flicker between his fingers in a way that was unnecessarily loud. They played for nothing, just passing time, but every so often Zephyr glanced at the clock and Katherine knew he was counting down.
The camera's light never stopped blinking. Katherine caught Zephyrs glancing at it once, twice, his expression unreadable. She wondered what the broadcasts would say about this moment. Would they call it defiance? A final act of unity before the Pieces were scattered? Or would they cut it entirely because it didn’t match whatever story they’d been weaving about Old Prosperity?
No one talked about the Challenge. No one talked about what came after, or what they’d seen in the Mirror. They passed the time with stories about home, pretending like this was a normal night, like the Tournament didn’t exist. Katherine let herself be pulled along, leaning into the warm press of bodies and the familiar cadence of their voices. She wanted to remember this: the way Dax smiled when someone lost spectacularly, the way Sebastian always tried to cheat and always got caught, the way Reign sat with her arms crossed and her jaw set, but smiled anyway when Houston dealt her a ridiculous hand.
A ding caught their attention in the middle of Atlas and Dax’s arm wrestle match and suddenly they couldn’t deny where they were anymore. It wasn’t a clock; it was a door unlock schedule broadcast building-wide. One minute to collect themselves. Thirty seconds. Fifteen.
“It’s time,” Minho exhaled.
The room emptied in slow motion, each of them drawing out goodbyes as if time might bend and let them stay a few minutes more. Minho clapped her on the shoulder and ruffled her hair, pretending not to notice when frost stuck to his fingers. Sebastian left last, pausing at the door to give her a look that said, Don’t fuck this up, but also, I believe in you, even if you don’t.
Then it was just Katherine, Reign, and Atlas. The silence felt like a ledge. Katherine could jump or just stand here forever, looking down.
Reign squeezed her shoulder, gentle for once. “I’ll see you on the other side.”
Katherine nodded, because she didn’t trust herself not to break if she said anything. She stared at the door after Reign left, wishing she’d said something better, or at least something true. Atlas stood at the foot of the bed with his hands in his pockets. No datapad, no clipboard, no shield of authority. Just Atlas, skin pale and eyes hollow and voice soft when he spoke.
“Is there something you want to say?” she found herself asking him. 
Atlas opened his mouth to speak but shook his head instead. He looked at her like he meant to say something, like the words were on the tip of his tongue, but then he just shook his head and pressed his lips together. The skin around his mouth was raw, chapped, like he’d been biting it all night. She could see the struggle in his face, the wanting to be honest and the absolute terror of it. 
Katherine wanted to laugh but it would have come out wrong. “You’re allowed to say whatever you want, you know. I’m not going to combust.”
Atlas’s lips twitched. “Might freeze instead.”
She almost smiled. “That’s your department, Captain.”
The camera lens above the door frame caught everything. The way he lingered in her room. The way she looked at him. Atlas knew it. She could see it in the tension of his shoulders, the careful way he kept his expression neutral even though his eyes said everything his mouth wouldn't.
"They're watching," Katherine whispered.
"Let them," He took his hands out of his pockets, wrapped his fingers around the footboard of her bed, and leaned in. “If we make it out of this, I’ll tell you.”
Katherine blinked. “If we make it out?”
Atlas nodded. “If we make it out.”
MELENA
The night before the Challenge, Melena stood in her room alone. 
She hadn’t opted into spending time with the team before the isolation period. She’d specifically requested that no one come to visit her. Seraphina and Luke were in the courtyard with Astor. Tiran and Maxim were somewhere downstairs drinking. Ellis and Viv brought dinner to her door before heading off to do who know what.
Normalcy. 
They’d all opted for normalcy. 
As if any of this was normal. 
Melena sighed as she walked over to the vanity. The mirror was ringed with cheap lights, like something from a theater, and she stared at her own reflection for a while. The lights warmed two notches on their own. Broadcast setting. The fire in her hair had faded to copper, too much sick and not enough sleep. Her hands shook when she picked up the brush. She’d watched her mother do this a thousand times, back when Lucia had the time for her children in the mornings. Back before Melena knew how cruel the world was. 
The camera behind her reflected in the mirror, a small black dot above the door frame. She wondered if they were broadcasting this live or saving it for a highlight reel. The Imperial Princess, alone the night before her greatest test, haunted by her own reflection. They'd eat it up. The drama. The tragedy. She picked up the brush and started again. If they were going to watch her break, she'd at least look good doing it. 
The tangles fought back, but she forced them flat. She remembered the way her mother’s voice would soften at bedtime, the way her father would ruffle her hair before leaving for the night. She set the brush down and looked herself in the eyes. She tried to see what the Mirror saw. Was it obvious? Was it stamped across her forehead: Melena Lockwood, traitor to her own blood, power over people every time. She blinked and her vision doubled, a ghostly overlay of the Mirror version of herself staring back, lips curled, eyes burning. She balled her fists and pressed them into the table until the tremors stopped. She didn’t want to be that person. She didn’t want to go into the Challenge and prove the Mirror right.
You prioritize power over people. Do you see the pattern?
She saw it.
God, she saw it so clearly it hurt.
And now she had to do it again.
“One last time,” she whispered, picking up the brush. “One last time.”
Chapter 38: Katherine/Melena
KATHERINE
Katherine stared at her hands. At the ice spreading from the left. At the fire sparking from the right. At the proof of what she'd become.
She remembered waking up that morning.
Forty-eight hours after Heart's Mirror. The medical bay was dark except for the monitors tracking her vitals. Her hands had been tingling. Not painful. Just — wrong. Like her skin was too tight. Like something underneath was trying to get out.
She'd lifted her arm to look at the IV.
And screamed.
The Flamer emblems were there. On her wrists. Both of them. Crimson ink that looked like it had been there her whole life. Stylized flames wound around her Frost symbols — not replacing them, not covering them, but with them.
Proof.
Physical, undeniable, recorded proof that Katherine Nicholas was a dualist.
The medical tech had burst through the door. “Corporal Nicholas, what—”
Katherine had shoved her hands under the blanket. Heart hammering. “Nothing. Nightmare. I'm fine.”
The tech had stared at her. At the monitors showing her heart rate spiking. At the way her breath was coming too fast. “I need to check your—”
“I said I'm fine.” Katherine's voice had gone cold. “Leave.”
The tech nodded and left. Katherine pulled her hands back out and stared at the emblems in the dim light, tracing the flames with a shaky pointer finger. They were real. Not a hallucination. Not a trick of the light.
The Flamer Faction had claimed her. 
She'd sat there for twenty minutes trying to figure out how to hide it. How to explain it. How to survive what came next.
Because dualists were illegal. Had been illegal since the Faction Wars. Since the Empire decided that people who could wield two elements were too dangerous. Too powerful. Too much of a threat to the carefully maintained balance between the Factions themselves.
The punishment for being a dualist was death.
Not prison. Not exile. Death.
She wrapped the wrists, not because bandages fix illegal, but because cameras don’t catch what they can’t see. Blind spot at the north ceiling vent, eight seconds of static every twelve minutes — thank you, Houston. She set a timer on her band to breathe during each window. Told the handlers she'd burned herself during Mirror recovery. They'd believed her because why wouldn't they? Frost users got cold injuries. Sometimes their element backfired. It happened.
She'd made it twelve hours before Reign found her in the training room, trying to freeze the emblems off her skin.
"What are you doing?" 
Katherine had spun. Hands behind her back. "Nothing."
"Katherine." Reign stuck her hand out. "Show me your hands."
"No."
"Kat—"
"I said no!"
Reign crossed the room, grabbed Katherine's wrist, and yanked it forward before she could react. The bandages had fallen away. Reign didn’t breathe at first. Not when she saw the red threaded through the blue of her frost, not when the truth rearranged itself on Katherine’s skin. She’d seen the flames flare at Heart’s Mirror; this was worse. A heartbeat of pure, ugly fear. Then she locked it down the way soldiers do. 
"Fuck," Reign whispered. "Fuck. Katherine—"
"Don't." Katherine had tried to pull away. "Don't say it."
"You're a dualist."
"I know."
"You can't—" Reign's grip had tightened. "Katherine, you can't let anyone see this. The Sanctum. The cameras. Anyone. If they find out—"
"They'll kill me. I know."
Reign had pulled her into the blind spot under the camera. Reign palmed the panel twice, short–short, long — their shut-up code — and nudged her under the vent shadow. “You’ve got thirty seconds before the lens resets. Who else knows?”
“Just you.”
“We need to tell Minho and Atlas. Follow chain of command just like we would back home.”
“No.”
“No?” Reign whisper yelled. “No? We have to tell them and hope they choose you over the law. You can’t hide this without them.”
Reign hauled her down the corridor, cut into the common room, killed the lens with Houston’s override — short, short, long. 
“Ping Minho. Atlas too,” she commanded into the intercom, her fingers flying over the console. “No one else.”
Minho didn’t look at her first—he looked at the room. Vents, corners, the lens’s red dot like it might blink back to life. His fingers tapped a quiet four-count against his thigh and when his gaze finally hit her wrists, something in his face hardened. King Piece gone. Captain of the En Guard engaged.
“Reign.” He glanced over at her. “Blind the lens. Now.”
Reign nodded, picking up on Minho’s change. “Already done, Sir.”
“Atlas,” Minho said, still scanning. “Seal and dampen.”
Atlas slid to the door fram, checked the sweep strip, shoved the wedge under the door, then crossed to the wall panel and killed the room mic with a practiced thumb. Katherine felt the shift in the air—protocol replacing panic. She’d felt it on tarmacs and decks and during live fire. It sat heavy and steady on her skin, told her where to stand and how to breathe and who to follow — and right now that was Minho. 
He finally faced her wrists again, swallowed once, and didn’t flinch. “Understood.”
Then the order came.
“Houston, Dax, Sebastian, Zephyr. No comms. Eyes only.”Atlas was already palming the silent ping to the team channel, two taps and a hold that meant Captain’s call—mute—move.
Katherine stared at the emblems and at Minho in the same breath, and all she could think was: they weren’t just her Pieces; they were the En Guard and the Legion, and the room had just turned into a war room.
Houston shouldered in first like a door was something to be guarded, not walked through — no questions, no noise, just a sweep of the room, a sweep of her wrists, and a clipped “Copy” as he took the left wall and became furniture.
Dax came next, his grin slipping off his face the second he clocked the twin emblems. “Shit,” barely breath, then he shut up and posted opposite Houston, jaw working like he was chewing down the hundred jokes he’d normally make. 
Sebastian arrived thirty seconds later, eyes knifing to the ink, then to Minho, then back to Katherine. Sebastian didn’t look at her long. His eyes cut to Zephyr, and Zephyr…blinked wrong. A half-flinch he buried too late. Zephyr stood with his arms folded, gaze fixed above Katherine’s head, like if he didn’t look directly at her wrists, none of this would be real. He was shaking, just a little. Enough that Katherine saw it and hated herself for noticing.
They all looked to Minho. Katherine hated that. Hated the way the room waited for someone else to decide if she got to live or not. Like if Minho gave the go, she stayed breathing. If not, well. 
She locked her jaw and waited. 
Minho drew a slow, deliberate breath. “Here’s the situation. Nicholas is a Dyad.”
No one so much as blinked at the word. Can’t say the same for Katherine’s stomach. Minho let it hang there, then: “We don’t discuss it, not with each other, not with anyone. We don’t draw attention. We blend. We win the next round and we buy time for a solution. Clear?”
“Crystal,” Dax said. 
Houston just nodded. 
Sebastian looked at Katherine, not her wrists, and gave a low, “You got it.”
“If anyone outside this room finds out, we all hang. Don’t kid yourselves. The Empire wants this Tournament clean, and if they sniff a Dyad on the board, they’ll kill her and everyone who shielded her. No appeals. No mercy.”
Katherine tried to swallow, but her throat was a desert. She wanted to ask for water. She wanted to ask for her mother. She wanted to ask the universe why her. Instead she nodded, because that was what you did when Minho was in Captain mode.
“Can you control it?” Minho finally looked at her. voice a low mumble.
“Not… really.” She flexed her hands, felt the burn at her right wrist, then the needle sting of cold at her left. She curled her hands to fists, nails digging into her palm. “It’s only when I’m—” 
She couldn’t finish. She didn’t know the word. Scared, maybe. Or desperate. Or angry, which was worse, because she could already feel the anger rising: at the Factions, at the room, at herself. “I can try.”
Minho’s gaze flicked to Atlas, a silent question. Atlas didn’t answer right away. He just studied the blue and red writhing under Katherine’s skin, and his mouth went flat and thin. 
"So we hide it," Reign cut in before Atlas could respond. "Long sleeves. Gloves. Whatever it takes."
"No one can hide their element," Dax rolled his eyes. "How the hell do you expect her to hide two?”
Heat licked her right palm in agreement; she shoved it down and frost spidered across her left anyway.
“I’m dead. That’s the math,” she said, and the room didn’t argue. 
Sebastian frowned as he glanced at Zephyr. “Tell him.”
“Tell him,” Sebastian repeated, louder this time. “Or I will.”
Minho raised an eyebrow. "What is Sebastian talking about?"
Sebastian laughed. It came out bitter. "You want to know why I underperformed on the PAE? Why I deliberately tanked my scores to stay off the board?"
No one answered.
"Because if he made it, then so would I." Sebastian gestured at Zephyr. "And I wasn't about to let my baby brother walk into a Tournament where one slip — one fucking mistake — would get him executed."
Atlas went very still. "Executed for what?"
"Show them or I swear to the Gods—"
"Fine!" Zephyr yanked his hands out of his pockets and his sleeves up.
Katherine's breath caught.
Zephyr's wrists were covered in emblems. Frost snow on one arm. Flamer fire on the other. 
"Jesus Christ," Dax whispered.
Minho's face had gone gray. "How long?"
She couldn't stop looking. The Flamer marks on Zephyr’s wrist were still pink and raw, like the ink had only just set. The frost emblem, though, that was old—maybe as old as Zephyr himself. It was the Flamer that didn’t belong, and it pulsed red in the overhead light, a warning flare for anyone with eyes
No one said anything. Not even Dax. Not even Reign. The room just waited, like a bomb was about to go off and everyone wanted a front-row seat.
Katherine’s brain went static. She remembered Zephyr in the bunk room, refusing to meet her eyes, always adjusting his sleeves. She remembered the day of the PAE, how he’d looked sick. How Sebastian had hovered, always between Zephyr and the world, like maybe if he stood close enough, no one would notice the way Zephyr’s hands shook. She remembered thinking he was just scared. Just human.
He’s a Dyad, too. All this time. She wasn’t alone, not even remotely. The relief and the terror ran parallel, a double helix that made her want to laugh or cry or rip the emblems off her skin and his.
Zephyr’s face was a blank. He stood braced and silent, like he’d already been shot and was just waiting for the second bullet. 
“I’ve been like this since I was thirteen,” Zephyr rolled his sleeves down. “They appeared after a training accident.”
“Seven?” Minho turned so fast, Katherine almost missed it. “And you knew?”
"Of course I knew." Sebastian's jaw clenched. "You think I wouldn't notice when he started wearing long sleeves year-round? When he stopped swimming? When he had a panic attack every time someone mentioned anything related to water?"
"Why didn't you tell me?" Minho's voice had gone dangerous. "I'm your King. I'm your brother. You should have—"
"What?" Sebastian shot back. "Told you so you could report him? Send him back home to London Town?”
“Maybe,” Minho sneered. “And then we wouldn’t have to deal with hiding two of them.”
Katherine’s mouth went dry. Them. Like she and Zephyr were a species. Not people. Not even soldiers, just a problem to be managed. She let the word stick in her throat, didn’t cough it out. If Minho wanted to talk about Dyads like they were cargo, fine. At least cargo could be hidden in a bulkhead.
She looked at Zephyr. He didn’t look back. His face had gone slack, mouth working around nothing. He flexed his fingers, then balled them tight, and Katherine could almost feel the pulse of cold and heat fighting under his skin. Had his parents known? Had he told anyone, ever? Or had Sebastian been hauling that secret all by himself, waiting for exactly this kind of room, this kind of reckoning?
She almost respected it. The guts it took to haul your own brother through three years of training, risking everything, just so he could feel normal a little longer. Sebastian had taken three steps to Zephyr’s side. Shoulders squared, chin up, like he could stop a bullet with posture alone.Minho looked at them both — at Zephyr, then Katherine, then Zephyr again. He ran a hand through his hair and sighed. 
“There are two of you,” he repeated. 
“What now?” Houston asked, finally breaking his silence. Minho didn’t answer right away. His gaze floated up, out, past everyone, like he was seeing the Tournament Board in its totality and mapping every possible outcome in real time. 
Minho was quiet a full three seconds, a lifetime in captain time. His gaze found the floor, the wall, the door—anywhere but Zephyr or Katherine, like the answer was hiding in drywall. Then: “We continue as planned. We keep them covered, and we control the story.” 
He turned to Atlas, “You’ll run point on comms, nothing in print, nothing on open channels. Everything verbal, everything coded.”
“Never thought I’d be taking command from you again,” Atlas scoffed but nodded nonetheless. 
“And you,” he over looked at his brother again. “Teach her something. We’re up in two hours.”
Dax exhaled hard, clapping his hands as he moved from his position. “You heard the man. Dismissed.”
MELENA
The worst part wasn’t the fighting.
It was the costume.
They’d squeezed her into London Town crimson and black like she was an advertisement, not a person. High collar cutting into her neck, gold thread screaming QUEEN PIECE from her shoulder, cape pinned just so for when the cameras caught her “hero shot.”
“Imperial,” the dresser had said, smoothing the fabric over her hips like that made it less suffocating.
Now she was alone on a metal bench in the staging room, knees bouncing, palms pressed flat against her thighs to keep them from shaking. The uniform still didn’t feel like hers. It felt like something Lucia would’ve worn in one of those old videos from before she had kids. The fabric was expensive and stiff, not meant for real fighting. This was for the cameras. For the story they were going to tell about her when this was over.
They’d even redone her hair.
Her coils were parted and braided straight back, clean lines running from her forehead to the nape of her neck. The braids were gathered into a low knot, secured with gold pins shaped like tiny crowns. Her edges were slicked down, every flyaway bullied into submission. Her grandmother would call it “proper.” Lucia would’ve kissed her forehead and said she looked like her grandmother.
Melena just felt…contained. Like they’d taken everything about her and tucked it away under gel and gold pins.
Somewhere beyond the stone, sixty thousand people were losing their minds. She couldn’t hear words, just a wall of sound pressed against the arena roof, rattling its way down into her bones.
She pressed her hands harder into her thighs. Heat bled through the fabric.
A dark mark bloomed under her fingers.
“Shit,” she muttered.
She lifted her palms. The scorch was faint but visible. Great. First blood of the night and it was her uniform.
The handler outside the door didn’t react. He stood at his post with the checked-out look of someone who’d watched too many people walk through too many doors. Every now and then his gaze flicked to the corner cam, making sure he was doing the job the way the Sanctum liked: present, quiet, non-human.
They’d all placed their bets by now. She’d heard the way people talked about it in earlier Challenges—Regnants in their boxes arguing odds, normal citizens trading credits in the stands like this was a game and not a slow-motion execution.
The roar surged again, a chant punching through the walls, almost words.
Blood. Board. Blood. Board.
Melena shut her eyes and counted her breaths. In. Out. Maven’s voice in the back of her mind: You don’t have to like the game, girl. You just have to know how to play it better than they do.
The door hissed.
Melena opened her eyes, ready to tell whatever Sanctum official walked in that if they touched her collar one more time she was going to set it on fire on principle—
—and then froze.
“Cordelia?”
Her sister filled the doorway in full Imperial regalia. Deep Lockwood green dress, structured and high-necked, sleeves embroidered with the family crest, tiara sitting in the middle of her braids like it had grown there.
The holopanel above the door flicked on: VISITOR: HIH LOCKWOOD, C. 
“I had to argue with three handlers and one Regnant to get in here,” Cordelia said by way of hello. “I have eight minutes, not ten. Don’t waste them.”
She stepped inside before Melena could answer. The door sealed behind her with a soft hiss. It sounded too much like a closing mouth.
“You look like Grandmother,” Melena said, because it was easier than saying I’m glad you’re here. “A shorter, meaner version.”
Cordelia’s mouth twitched. “I’ll take that as a compliment.”
It wasn’t, and they both knew it.
Up close, the only sign she’d been crying was the faint swelling at the inner corners of her eyes. Whoever did her makeup had done overtime. Cordelia held herself like her spine was steel. Tiara straight. Chin up. Just the way their grandmother liked it. 
“You shouldn’t be back here,” Melena said, suddenly aware of the scorch mark on her lap and the way her own hands were still shaking.
“And no Imperial Princess should have to walk out on that atrocious board, but here we are.” Cordelia looked her up and down like she was assessing damage. “Turn around.”
Melena did. Cordelia adjusted her cape, smoothed a wrinkle at her waist, and flicked a bit of lint off her shoulder.
“It’s chaos out there,” Cordelia said quietly, still fussing with the fabric. “Rovencia showed up at dawn with flags and drums and speeches about ‘respecting tradition.’ The Regnant stood in front of the arena and called the Sanctum ‘soft’ for issuing the no-kill order.”
Melena’s jaw clenched. “Of course she did.”
“The other Regnants are worse,” Cordelia went on. “They’re pretending to be offended in public while placing bets in the back row. Old Prosperity’s Council is wagering on which of their Pieces goes down first—Atlas or Minho—and London Town’s nobles are trying to decide whether it’s better optics if you win clean or win bloody.”
Melena turned back around. “And what does the smartest Lockwood in the palace think?”
Cordelia’s gaze snapped to hers. “I think if the crowd keeps calling for blood, the Sanctum is going to give it to them. One way or another.”
Melena’s stomach dipped. “They gave their word. Grandmother gave her word.”
“I know what they said.” Cordelia’s voice was careful. As if she knew Melena was three seconds away from another breakdown. “I also know what happens when sixty thousand people push in one direction and the Empire is terrified of looking weak.”
Melena shook her head. “They won’t. Not in the Sixth Challenge. It’s too late in the Tournament for that. It’d be a PR nightmare.”
“That’s exactly why they might,” Cordelia shot back. “If this ends with both provinces walking away with all their high Pieces intact, Rovencia gets to go home and tell the rest of the world that Remnia’s Tournament is a performance now. That we don’t have the stomach for real sacrifice anymore. You know how the Council thinks. They’d rather apologize for the body count than apologize for ‘softness.’”
Melena didn’t want to admit that Cordelia was right. Not about this. Not when her hands already felt wrong.
“I haven’t killed anyone,” she said, before she realized she was going to say it out loud.
Cordelia blinked. “I know.”
“No, I mean—” Melena swallowed. “Other Pieces have. Tiran has. Ellis has. Some of Old Prosperity’s soldiers definitely have. I haven’t. I’ve burned, I’ve hit, I’ve knocked people off platforms or out of bounds, but—”
“But you’ve never aimed to end a life and followed through,” Cordelia finished softly.
Melena nodded.
Outside, the crowd’s chant braided into one word. She could finally hear it now.
“Blood. Blood. Blood.”
Cordelia drew in a breath. When she spoke again, her voice had shifted into that locked-in tone Melena had only ever heard in Council chambers.
“If you don’t walk back out of that arena,” she said, “or if you walk out and you’re not… you anymore, someone has to do the job.”
Melena’s chest clenched. “Cordelia—”
“Elias never had this conversation with you,” Cordelia pressed on. “And look how well that turned out. He disappeared and left you with a crown you didn’t ask for and no map. I’m not letting that happen to me.”
There it was. The thing they didn’t say. The thing that sat between Elias’s empty chair and the sealed file on his “illness.”
“You think I’m going to end up like him,” Melena said. It wasn’t a question.
“I think there are more ways to be taken off the board than dying,” Cordelia replied. “You could fall. You could be ‘stabilized’ somewhere quiet. You could come back so shaken they decide you’re too volatile to rule. You could win and still lose everything that makes you you.”
Melena stared at her. Cordelia stared back, tiara catching the harsh overhead light.
“If any of that happens,” Cordelia said, dropping her voice to barely a whisper, “you need to know I will take it. The crown. The Council. Watching after Louis. The mess you and Elias and the old guard made. I’ll take it. I’ll hate you a little bit for leaving it to me, but I’ll do it.”
“That’s not fair,” she said.
“Nothing about this is fair.” Cordelia’s mouth twisted. “But I’d rather walk in knowing than get blindsided standing where you are.”
Melena thought of Elias. Of how there’d been no warning. No “if I break, here’s what you do.” Just a brother who vanished into a foreign country and a storm of silence left behind.
She reached out before she could talk herself out of it and took Cordelia’s hand.
“If it comes to you,” Melena said, voice light enough for the cameras, “don’t be stupid with it.”
Cordelia’s mouth twitched. “That your official political advice?”
Melena’s thumb found the inside of Cordelia’s wrist.
— • — •
C.
Out loud, Melena glanced at the tiara. “It’s crooked. Grandma will combust.”
Cordelia didn’t move. “Let her.”
Melena kept tapping as Cordelia reached up and adjusted the piece. Maven had taught her one night in Shadowmere when Melena couldn’t sleep.
“Old code,” Maven had said, drumming her knuckles on the table. “From when people still sent light and sound instead of data. Useless, which makes it useful. Nobody important bothers to learn it anymore.”
Melena had thought it was just another one of Maven’s weird hobbies until the night Cordelia finished one of Melena’s half-tapped patterns at a state dinner without even looking at her.
“Naval dispatches,” Cordelia had said later, when she demanded to know how. “Pre-war. They used it to flash orders between ships. I got bored in History and memorized it.”
So it became theirs.
Not palace code. Not Council cipher.
Sister language.

A. E. L. U. M.
“I’ll check on Louis,” Cordelia said, perfectly bored for the camera. 
“His…calendar.” “Do that,” Melena murmured, smoothing a nonexistent wrinkle at Cordelia’s shoulder. “Keep him busy.” 
Cordelia’s lashes flickered once—the only tell. “Don’t make me do your job and mine.” 
Melena huffed a laugh that sounded like nerves and nothing more. “Wouldn’t dream of it.” Cordelia squeezed back—one quick press on Mel’s knuckles that translated clean as a bell: Got it.
“Your Highness,” the handler called from the hall without opening the door. “Time.”
Cordelia nodded before leaving the room, the door closing behind her with a soft hiss. The room suddenly felt bigger and smaller at the same time. Too much space for her thoughts, not enough air for her lungs.
She sat back down. Pressed her palms into her thighs again. Counted breaths she wasn’t sure were doing anything.
One. Two. Three.
The air shivered.
It wasn’t temperature. It was… texture. Like the world glitched for half a second and came back slightly misaligned.
Melena’s eyes flew open.
Caelum was leaning against the far wall like he’d been there the whole time.
No door hiss. No footsteps. No VISITOR line blinking to life on the holopanel. The handler outside hadn’t moved.
“You have got to be—” flame whispered across her knuckles, hers—thin, stubborn “—kidding.”
“Relax,” Caelum chuckled. “I’m only trespassing in your mind.”
“Cute.” She kept her gaze on the vanity, fixing a collar that didn’t need it. “Say what you came to say and get out before I start answering you out loud and give the cameras a show.”
His mouth curved. “Heart’s Mirror didn’t sand you all the way down. Good.”
“Keep my Mirror out your mouth.” She hissed. “What do you want?”
“The same thing I asked for the first time you cornered me in Shadowmere,” he said. “Communication schema. Guard rotations. Emergency evacuation lines. Sanctum broadcast relays if you can get them.”
“So the homework isn’t due after the Tournament,” Melena raised an eyebrow. “You want it now.”
“I wanted it yesterday.” He pushed off the wall. “And last night, when the feed from your little therapy maze hit our analysts, I assumed you weren’t bringing me anything ever again.”
She scoffed. “You watched me break and wrote me off. Sweet.”
“I watched you survive and revised my odds.” His eyes cut over her, dark enough to drink the light. “Barely.”
“Still here,” she said, holding his stare. “Try again.”
