He didn’t blink. “Fine. You look pretty enough for the broadcasts. Like an Imperial Princess should.”
“Watch the tone. I didn’t crawl out of Shadowmere to get lectured by a man who can’t bother to show up with a body.”
“Bodies complicate exits.” The sliver of a smile again—there and gone. “And before you pretend you’re not listening—don’t be righteous with me, Princess. You made a deal. I collect before your board starts eating its own.”
“That board is my people.” She stood because sitting felt like losing, and Melena had already lost enough. “London Town is not a bargaining chip.”
“Everything’s a bargaining chip,” Caelum said. “That’s why you’re good at this.”
“Fuck you, Caelum.”
“Maybe later. For now, bring me something that proves the Sanctum bleeds. I don’t need the whole artery. A vein will do.”
A soft knock on the outer door. “Your Highness?” the handler called. “Escort to the London Town gallery in two minutes.”
Melena didn’t look away from Caelum. “You picked a bad moment.”
“I picked the last one you get alone,” he said. “You want your brother to breathe long enough for me to make the right enemies shut up? Then make yourself useful. Give me that proves the Empire is the circus you keep pretending it isn’t.”
“You ever bring me news that isn’t a threat?”
“If I do, you’ll know I’m lying.”
The holopanel over the real door chimed to life: ESCORT ARRIVED.
Caelum’s outline thinned, voice trailing like smoke. “Gallery, then the floor. Your lower Pieces will go first. You know how this goes.”
“And if I don’t make it?”
He didn’t flinch. “Then I’m right about Heart’s Mirror.”
She swallowed the hit, swallowed the heat, swallowed everything.
“Get out of my head,” she said.
“Bring me my proof,” he answered, and vanished like he’d never been there.
The wrongness let go as the reset. The handler opened the door tight as the two minute mark passed. “Your Highness?”
Melena smoothed her cape, checked a braid that didn’t need checking, and gave the camera her best fake smile.
“One more time,” she exhaled a breath she didn’t know she was holding. “One more time.”
Chapter 39: Katherine – The Sixth Challenge 
The gallery was freezing.
Not actually freezing — the temperature was regulated, somewhere between comfortable and clinical — but Katherine's body couldn't tell the difference. She flattened both palms against the stone bench. Ice spiraled out from the left in fractals. Scorch marks bloomed black under the right. Where they met, the stone hissed—a tiny, furious sound that made her want to scream.
"Stop," she whispered. To her hands. To her elements. To whatever the fuck was happening inside her that she couldn't control. "Just stop."
They didn't listen.
Blue. Orange. Blue. Orange.
At least the viewing box was private. Reserved for non-competing Pieces and their handlers. Tinted glass. No cameras allowed inside. If she shattered completely, only Old Prosperity would witness it. They sat in order by Piece; Atlas on her left and Minho on her right. Atlas didn't look at her. His gaze cut through the glass to the arena floor, jaw set so tight she could see the pop of muscle above his collar. Minho, on the other hand, kept glancing over — not at her, but at the scorch marks she kept making. Like he was tracking her element by the evidence it left behind. Or maybe like he was waiting for her to lose control, and ready to move when it happened.
She curled her hands into fists, nails biting skin. The heat and cold war inside her wrists, each trying to outlast the other. She imagined the whole bench splitting in half, each side claiming territory until there was nothing left in the middle. 
A tap on the glass drew her back. Houston's reflection hovered on the other side, two fingers pressed to the window in their signal to get her attention. He raised his eyebrows, aiming a question she could answer or ignore. Katherine just stared back. Houston's mouth twitched — not quite smile, not quite frown — and he drifted away, returning to his solemn post with the other Blockers.
Atlas said, “They’re starting.”
Below, the arena spread like something ripped from a nightmare: a vast stone bowl, ancient and circular, with sixty thousand bodies stacked in tiers so high the top row dissolved into shadow. It looked smaller from up here. But the floor was a killing field, and the crowd knew it. Katherine saw the way the inner ring leaned forward at the rail, how the rows behind them pressed in, a human landslide. Red and blue and gold banners draped the upper tiers, all so heavy with color that the smoke haze caught and held their light, a sunrise made of blood and violence and whatever else the provinces needed to feel in the moment. 
Katherine's stomach turned over.
She hadn't been around people — real people, not her team — in six weeks. Six weeks of isolation in training facilities and sterile hallways and rooms that only held the same sixteen faces over and over until she'd memorized every scar and tell and microexpression. Six weeks of silence broken only by drills and debriefings and the mechanical voice of Nico counting down to the next challenge. She could feel the vibration of their voices rattling through the stone bench, through her bones, through the base of her skull where a headache was already building.
Sixteen platforms rose from the arena floor, each one isolated by gaps that dropped into nothing. She could see Melena's platform from here —directly across, too far for details but close enough to catch the crimson and black of London Town colors bleeding into the stone. Close enough to imagine Melena standing there in full Queen regalia, fire answering her pulse, probably thinking the same damn thing Katherine was thinking:
How the hell did we get here?
“Breathe,” Atlas said, eyes fixed on the arena below like he could map every exit by staring hard enough.
“I can’t—” She cut herself off, pressed harder into the stone. “It won’t stop.”
“One thing at a time. Just the breath.”
One thing. Right. Except her elements were tearing her apart, Heart's Mirror had cracked something she couldn't name, she was about to watch her people fight and maybe die, and somewhere in the crowd sixty thousand throats were building the same word over and over.
BLOOD. BLOOD. BLOOD.
“Stay with me,” he said, still watching the floor. “Katherine. With me.”
She nodded even though he couldn’t see.
“How long until—”
“Four minutes,” Minho cut in. “Houston’s got this.”
Katherine watched Houston’s platform rise. He stood at parade rest, helmet under one arm, posture so perfect it made her want to slouch on principle. And then the opposing platform, London Town’s answer, appearing like a sunspot. Ellis, she was pretty sure. She didn't know him. Didn't know anything except he was smaller, lighter, fast in the way that lived in footwork and reaction time. And in four minutes he'd be standing across from Houston under rules that meant someone might not walk away. He was already bouncing on his heels, grinning into the crowd and lapping up the noise.
“Corporal Katherine Nicholas.”
She looked up. Minho had turned his body towards her. And Katherine knew where this was going from the way he’d said her name. 
Katherine's stomach dropped. “Minho…”
“In the event of my incapacitation or death during the Sixth Challenge," Minho continued like she hadn't spoken, "command of the En Guard transfers to you effective immediately."
The room tilted.
Katherine grabbed the bench to stay upright. Both elements exploded at once, fighting each other, fighting her. "No. Absolutely not. I'm not—"
"Protocol allows a commanding officer to name their successor regardless of rank," Atlas cut in. "Which is why in the event of my death in the Sixth Challenge, command of the Legion will also transfer to you immediately."
"I'm a fucking Corporal," Katherine snapped louder than she expected it to be. "I don't have Sergeant stripes. I can't command the En Guard. That's your division—" she looked at Minho "—you can't just hand it off like—"
"I can," Minho said quietly. "And I am."
"What about Dax? What about—" Her throat closed. "There are people with actual rank. Actual years. People who've been doing this since before I was born—"
“Corporal,” Minho answered, same tone he’d used the first time he made her run the hill twice. “This is not a vote.”
Katherine laughed. "Have you looked at me?" She held up her hands—blue on the left, orange on the right, both wrong. "I can't control anything. You both have brothers here. Ask them!"
"Brothers are liabilities in this scenario," Atlas said. "They are also not on the command path."
Minho's mouth quirked, not a smile. "Dax isn’t going to give up the Vanguard. Sebastian would rather eat rocks and And Zephyr—"
"Zephyr is a child." Atlas didn't blink. "He can barely keep himself upright."
Katherine braced for the heat, but it was the cold that won. The bench frosted under her palm, a slick film that made her hand ache. "You don't get it. If I lose control—"
"You won’t," Minho replied. "You walked out of Heart's Mirror and you're still here. That's control."
"That's survival," she shot back. "Not the same thing."
"In the military," Minho said, "it's exactly the same thing."
BLOOD. BLOOD. BLOOD.
"I can't take both," she heard herself say. "En Guard and Legion. That's two divisions. I can't—"
“You can’t carry two COs,” Atlas confirmed. “If both of us go down, you choose. En Guard or Legion. The other rolls to the next in line.”
If. 
If they both died. If she had to choose which legacy to carry. If Old Prosperity made it home with a Corporal leading divisions she had no business commanding.
"You're both walking out.” She shook her head. “You’re both walking out,” she said, voice gone flat. “You hear me? You’re both walking out, and I don’t have to choose a damn thing.”
Minho and Atlas exchanged a look. Something passed between them that Katherine couldn't read. Didn't want to read.
"You might," Minho exhaled. "That's the Tournament. That's what we signed for."
"I signed to be Queen," Katherine spat. "To lead Old Prosperity into this arena and walk us back out. Not to—" She couldn't finish. Couldn't say inherit your corpse.
She didn’t have a word for the feeling that followed. It was too big for anger and too small for grief. It pressed on her from the inside, made her ribs ache and her teeth hurt, like her body was trying to shake itself apart rather than hold the words in.
The intercom crackled allowing her the chance to never have to respond. “Citizens of The Remnian Empire, welcome to the Sixth Challenge.”
#
The horn blared.
Houston moved first.
Frost erupted from his boots, spreading fast, turning black stone white in geometric patterns that raced toward Ellis's platform. He was creating a field of ice that would force Ellis to either stay put or risk slipping the second he moved.
But Ellis didn't move.
Just stood there watching, as if he knew something Houston didn't.
Katherine's stomach dropped. "He's going to shift."
"What?" Sebastian leaned forward.
"Blockers don't fight straightforward," Minho breathed. "They—"
Ellis's form rippled.
One second he was there — London Town crimson, human-shaped, standing on his platform. The next his edges blurred like someone had smudged wet paint. His outline softened. His body became something fluid, something that didn't obey normal physics.
The crowd gasped.
Ellis dropped to all fours. His limbs elongated, twisted, reformed into something with too many joints. His spine curved wrong. His face pushed forward into a muzzle — not quite wolf, not quite dog, something in between that made Katherine's brain scream wrong. Fur the color of smoke rippled across his body. His eyes went yellow.
Houston's frost faltered.
Katherine saw it — that split second of hesitation, of what the fuck, because training simulations were one thing. Watching someone's body unmake itself and become something else entirely? That was different. There weren’t too many Blockers in Old Prosperity outside of Minho and a few other cadets in the lower years. 
Ellis — the creature that had been Ellis — moved.
Fast. Faster than anything that size should move. He launched himself off the platform in a bound that covered thirty feet, landed on Houston's ice field without slipping. Claws—actual claws, black and curved—digging into frozen stone for purchase.
Houston recovered. Barely.
His frost exploded outward in a wave as he tried his hardest to create some distance. The ice formed walls between him and Ellis, thick barriers that rose from the platform in seconds.
Ellis went through them.
Not around. Not over. Through.
His body rippled again and Katherine watched his form compress, flatten, reform, and condense until it was the size of a large dog, then a small one, then some nightmarish blend of predator and liquid smoke. It slammed into Houston’s right side, claws raking the frost over his uniform. Houston staggered, boots skidding for purchase. Too late; Ellis was already behind him, climbing his back with that impossible, jointless movement.
Houston rolled, shaking the thing off. Where blood should have been, ice crystals fanned out from the wound, knitting the fabric closed. Houston had always been the toughest bastard in rotation, and it showed. He planted both feet, squared his shoulders, and shoved his shoulder back into place with a noise that made even Katherine flinch. 
Ellis lunged again, this time aiming low. But Houston anticipated it. He slammed his palms down and conjured a spike from the platform's side, catching Ellis in the chest and flipping him midair. The creature howled but landed on all fours, raked Houston’s calf with a swipe of its claws, and darted back.
Blood this time. Houston’s left pant leg bloomed red. But he didn’t slow. He chased Ellis, not even trying for distance, just brute-forcing the thing into a corner of the platform where the ice was thickest. The crowd roared. The sound burrowed into Katherine's teeth, made the world feel off-balance.
"He's not going to win this," Reign said from the far end of the line. 
"He just needs to last," Atlas replied.
Katherine wanted to believe them. But Ellis circled back, pounced, and Houston wasn’t quick enough. The thing’s jaws clamped around Houston’s arm. Katherine saw the teeth puncture flesh, saw the blood and the bone and the flurry of frost as Houston tried to freeze the wound and the monster at the same time. 
Ellis didn't go down.
His body absorbed Houston’s blow. Katherine watched the point of impact ripple, watched Ellis's form shift to distribute the force. The bruise formed and then disappeared as his cells rearranged themselves to mask the damage.
"Minho stood, moving towards the glass. "He's redistributing."
"What does that mean?" Reign leaned forward.
"Blockers can shift injuries around their body," Minho replied. "Hide broken bones. Mask pain. But it doesn't heal them. It just moves it. Eventually the body can't compensate anymore and—"
Ellis shifted again.
His wolf-form blurred. Reformed. Human again but wrong. Taller than before. Broader. His face was Ellis's face but the proportions were off—eyes too wide, jaw too sharp. And his hands—
His hands were claws.
Human arms. Human body. But his fingers had elongated into curved black talons that looked like they could punch through steel.
Houston went for his throat and Katherine knew, just knew that Houston had him. Until Ellis caught his wrist. Ice spread from Houston's fist up Ellis's arm but Ellis didn't flinch. He just held Houston's wrist in a grip that made Houston's face twist in pain.
Then Ellis squeezed.
Katherine heard the crack from the gallery.
Houston's wrist was shattered. Houston's whole body jerked, and for a half second, Katherine thought he'd go down. But he didn't. He pivoted, drove his forehead into the bridge of Ellis's nose with a sick whump. The Blocker reeled, nose streaming blood, and Houston ripped his arm free, sleeve darkening as blood and ice mingled.
On the far rail, the crowd screamed for more.
Ellis circled, nostrils flaring, blood painting his face in wild streaks. He was grinning, teeth bared. Houston flexed his broken hand, tried to close his fist, failed, and went right back on the offensive. He tackled Ellis by the waist, drove him down onto the ice, and used his full weight to pin the other Blocker’s limbs. The sound of their struggle echoed up to the gallery: boots scraping, breath wheezing, the animal panic in Ellis’s voice.
Ellis shifted again the muscles under his skin warping and twisting, his arms ballooning with muscle and the lines of his jaw distending. He threw Houston off like a child, then rolled to all fours, panting hard. Houston was already up. He braced his ruined hand against his chest, blood freezing on the knuckles, and charged before Ellis could shift again. 
Ellis let him come and they went down together, hitting the platform hard. Houston on top, used his weight to pin Ellis down, his good hand wrapped around Ellis's throat, squeezing. Frost spreading from his palm into Ellis's windpipe.
Ellis's form blurred.
His neck changed—thickened, became corded muscle too dense to strangle. His chest expanded. His arms lengthened. He was growing. His form rippled violently as Houston’s ice coated Ellis’s throat, chest and arms. Katherine could see the way Ellis's edges were becoming less defined as if he were out of things to turn into.
Ellis’s body kept ballooning, cords of muscle bunching under skin that no longer fit. But Houston’s grip never slackened. He had his good hand locked under Ellis’s jaw, thumb digging deep into the hinge of the throat, and with the other, he drove the splintered bones into Ellis’s ribs, using the pain to keep the Blocker’s writhing focused. Frost poured from Houston’s palm, spidering across Ellis’s face and down his chest in sheets. The air around them snapped with cold, steam rising from both bodies as they fought for every inch.
Katherine couldn’t breathe. Her own lungs wouldn’t expand. She watched Houston’s face, the way his jaw set, the way sweat froze on his brow. He wasn’t thinking about the crowd or the odds or even the pain. He was staring down at Ellis, at the monster under his hands, and choking the life out of it because that was the only way to win.
The monster thrashed, claws scraping deep furrows in the ice, but Houston’s hold was absolute. Ellis’s hands scrabbled at Houston’s wrist, fingers elongating, nails blackening, trying to pry him off. Houston didn’t let go. If anything, he squeezed harder.
“Yield,” Houston said through gritted teeth. “Do you yield?”
Ellis’s mouth moved, but no sound came out. His throat was too constricted from Houston’s ice. Ellis's hand came up, and Katherine thought he was trying one last strike. Instead, his hand opened, palm facing up.
The universal signal.
I yield.
The horn should have blared.
It didn't.
Katherine looked at Minho. "Why isn't—the horn—he yielded—"
Houston looked up at the Sanctum viewing box, waiting. His hand was still locked around Ellis's throat, the ice spreading slow, buying time. Waiting for confirmation. Waiting for permission to stop.
The officials didn't move.
Then a voice boomed through the arena, so loud it rattled Katherine's teeth: "Yield is not recognized. London Town King's Rook Ellis Vale's signal was non-verbal. Per Tournament regulation, verbal confirmation is required."
Verbal confirmation.
But Ellis's throat was frozen shut. Houston's ice had made it impossible—
The crowd understood before Katherine did. The chant started low, from the inner rings, then spread up and out like wildfire until all sixty thousand voices became one:
"FINISH. FINISH. FINISH."
Katherine couldn't breathe. Her elements went haywire — ice coating her left side, fire racing up her right arm. "Let him up," she whispered, but the words got lost in the noise. "He yielded. Let him—"
Houston's face twisted. He looked down at Ellis, then back at the Sanctum box, then at his own hand still wrapped around Ellis's throat. His whole body was shaking.
Ellis's eyes met his.
Katherine saw it from the gallery. Saw Ellis's lips move, trying so fucking hard to force the words past a windpipe that wouldn't open. His mouth forming shapes that should have been sounds: I yield. Please. I yield.
But no sound came.
No sound could come.
Houston closed his eyes.
When he opened them, Katherine knew the look. She'd seen it on her mother's face once, years ago, after a mission she never talked about. The look of someone going to that place soldiers go when they have to do things that will haunt them forever. 
The ice spread.
Up Ellis's throat, across his jaw, down into his chest. Freezing everything it touched. Cells crystallizing. Blood turning to slush. Organs stopping mid-function.
Ellis's body tried to shift one last time. His form flickered like his survival instinct was making one final attempt to save him. But there was nothing left to shift into. Nowhere left to hide the damage.
He stopped fighting.
Just... stopped.
The color drained from his face and Houston kept his hand there for two more seconds. Maybe to make sure. Maybe because he couldn't make himself let go. Then he wrenched it free like he'd touched live wire.
Ellis hit the ice.
Parts of him were already frosting over—skin pale and patchy, blood pooling out black and freezing where it touched stone.
Houston staggered back, cradling his broken wrist against his chest. The other arm hung limp. Blood soaked through his pants from the leg wound, dripping onto white ice and turning it pink. He looked up at the Sanctum box again. Waiting. Waiting for someone to tell him he'd done the right thing.
Finally, the horn blared.
"Winner: Old Prosperity King's Rook, Houston Maddison."
The crowd erupted.
Not shock. Not horror.
Approval.
They were cheering. Sixty thousand people cheering for a man who'd just been forced to kill someone who'd surrendered. Cheering like this was entertainment. Like Ellis Vale hadn't just died begging for mercy he couldn't voice.
Katherine's hands wouldn't stop shaking. Ice spread down her left leg. Fire licked up her right arm, so hot it hurt. She pressed both palms flat against her thighs but it didn't help. Nothing helped. Her elements were tearing her apart and all she could see was Ellis's mouth moving. 
I yield. Please. I yield.
## Chapter 40: Melena – The Sixth Challenge
Five bodies.
Melena had counted them. Had to count them. Because if she didn't keep track — if she didn't catalog every single piece that walked out of this arena wrapped in London Town colors — her mind would splinter and she'd start screaming and never stop.
Ellis. Astor. Seraphina. Viv. Luke.
Five.
The viewing box had gone silent. Down on the arena floor, sixty thousand people were losing their minds—chanting, screaming, shaking the very foundations of the stone bowl. But up here, behind tinted glass that muted sound to a dull roar, it was just Melena and Maxim and Raven and the two handlers who stood by the door like sentries.
And the bodies kept coming.
She'd watched Sebastian kill Luke three minutes ago. Watched Luke try to yield—verbally this time, learning from Ellis's mistake—and watched Sebastian's fire consume him anyway because the crowd wanted blood and the Sanctum had decided that once you stepped on that platform, yield meant nothing if the other fighter was already committed to the kill.
So Luke burned.
Melena had smelled it from the box. Burnt flesh and hair and the sick-sweet stench of fat rendering. The handlers had opened the door to escort his body out and the smell had rolled in like smoke, thick and choking.
She'd thrown up in the corner.
Maxim hadn't said anything. Just handed her a cloth and turned back to watch the arena like nothing had happened. Like he wasn't dying inside too. Like his brother wasn't about to walk onto that floor in two rounds and maybe never walk off.
Melena understood now.
Gods, she understood everything now.
Why Elias had run to Somnoria after his Tournament. Why he'd looked at their grandmother during family dinners and flinched like she'd struck him. Why he couldn't sleep without the lights on. Why he'd wake up screaming about names Melena had never heard—teammates, probably. Pieces. People he'd watched die.
Why their grandmother had never let Melena watch Tournaments growing up. "You're too young," Aracelia had said. "You'll have nightmares."
Melena thought she meant nightmares about the fighting. About the violence.
She'd meant nightmares about the choosing. About watching your people die and having to calculate whether their death brought your province closer to winning or not. About feeling relieved when the other province's Piece went down instead of yours. About understanding—really understanding—that every name announced was someone's child, someone's sibling, someone's person.
And you had to watch them die for a game.
Her hands were shaking against the railing. Small tremors she couldn't hide anymore. The metal was warm under her palms. Getting warmer. She could already smell it starting to heat—that metallic tang that came right before something melted.
You must survive, Caelum's voice whispered in her memory. Whatever you do, you must survive long enough to complete the task. If you fail, Elias disappears forever.
Five bodies.
And the Sanctum was already calling the next fight.
Tiran had come to her before Luke went down. Melena had been staring at the arena, trying to count her breaths and failing. Trying not to think about Ellis's face. About Astor's scream. About Seraphina's body hitting the platform and not getting back up.
“Melena.”
She didn't answer. She couldn’t. Because if she opened her mouth, everything would come out—the fear, the rage, the certainty that she was about to watch him die too. Tiran had stepped closer. Close enough that she could feel his heat. Close enough that his fire and hers started reaching for each other like they always did.
"I'm not saying goodbye," Melena whispered. "So if that's what you’re about to—"
"I know." His hand found her shoulder and squeezed once as he stood. "I know."
Tiran had leaned down and pressed his lips to her forehead. It was only for a second. Just long enough for Melena to feel the warmth of him, to smell smoke and leather and that specific scent that was just him.
Then he was gone.
And Melena hadn't said a word.
Hadn't told him to be careful. Hadn't told him to come back. Hadn't told him any of the things she should have said because she didn't know how to say them without breaking completely.
Now she was standing in the viewing box, watching him walk onto the platform, and all she could think was:
I should have said something. I should have—
"King's Bishop versus King's Bishop," the announcer's voice boomed through the arena. 
The crowd was on its feet before the names could even come out. The upper tiers stomped, shook banners, screamed themselves raw.
Across the arena, Atlas stepped into the light.
Melena knew him.
Not in the way she knew Tiran—fire pressed to fire, every argument, every late-night training session etched into her bones—but she knew Atlas Tolliver the way any Imperial Princess was required to know Old Prosperity’s favored sons.
She’d watched him stand three steps behind Minho at diplomatic briefings, silver eyes flat as mirrors while generals argued over troop rotations. She’d seen him bored out of his mind at state dinners, turning his water glass to frost with a flick of his fingers under the table. She’d heard her grandmother say, once, that if Old Prosperity ever decided to move against London Town, Atlas Tolliver was the one they’d send through the front door.
Now he was walking toward her Bishop.
Tiran’s platform rose first, lifting him above the arena floor. Dark hair pulled back at the nape of his neck. Violet eyes already burning. House Blackwood’s phoenix sat over his heart in gold thread, wings mid-flare, stitched in a way that made it look like it might take off.
Atlas’s platform stopped opposite him a breath later. White hair pulled into a high ponytail that shouldn’t have looked as masculine as it did on him. Silver eyes, as unreadable as ever, catching the floodlights and throwing them back. Old Prosperity blue cut in sharp, efficient lines. No family crest on his chest; just the simple, brutal sigil of his province at his sleeve.
Fire and Frost.
Her Bishop and Katherine’s.
“King’s Bishop versus King’s Bishop,” the announcer crooned, dragging every word out like he was savoring it. “London Town’s Tiran Blackwood against Old Prosperity’s Atlas Tolliver. Some of you have been waiting years for this one.”
The crowd roared. The sound rolled over the arena like thunder, even through the glass. Someone started a TIR-AN, TIR-AN, TIR-AN chant that caught quick and spread through the lower tiers. Old Prosperity’s section answered back, beating rhythm against the railings.
Melena couldn’t move.
Five bodies.
They were still wheeling Luke’s stretcher off the far side of the arena. The healers had stopped pretending it was anything but a recovery of remains. London Town’s colors dragged behind the cart, trailing in the soot. Raven stepped closer to the glass on Melena’s left. Her reflection flickered over the surface—dark eyes, tight jaw, hands clasped behind her back so no one would see them shake.
“Remember the rules for this one,” the announcer went on. “If both fighters are still standing when the clock runs out…”
The crowd finished it for him.
“DRAW!”
Melena’s stomach flipped.
The draw.
It was the only mercy built into this Challenge. The only loophole. Ten minutes of sanctioned brutality and then, if by some miracle both Pieces were still alive, the Sanctum called it a draw and the board didn’t change.
Except that wasn’t true, and everyone who’d made it this far knew it.
Bodies still broke. Bones still shattered. Magic still burned through reserves that didn’t come back overnight. You could walk off the platform technically alive and still be useless for whatever followed.
Tiran rolled his shoulders, fire licking over his knuckles before vanishing again. Atlas lifted his hands, palms open, Frost already tracing faint, spiderweb lines along the platform beneath his boots.
“Look at them,” one of the commentators said over the arena speakers, voice buzzing faintly in the viewing box. “Top two in their class. Blackwood versus Tolliver. There are generals who have nightmares about this pairing.”
“And pay attention to the bond signatures,” the second commentator added, almost gleeful. “You can feel it from here. Blackwood’s phoenix and Tolliver’s drake are livid they’re not on the field. If this goes the full ten…”
“We may finally see a bonded intervention force a draw,” the first finished. “Wouldn’t that be something for the history reels?”
History.
Melena swallowed hard. Her hands pressed harder into the railing. The metal was almost hot now, her fire humming just under the surface, looking for something—someone—to lash out at. Elias had warned her about this too. Not in words, but in the way he’d gone quiet when Tournaments came up. In the way his eyes had gone distant when she’d asked if he’d ever wished one of his matches had gone to a draw.
He’d always change the subject.
“Pieces,” the arena marshal’s voice boomed. “Ready yourselves.”
Tiran tilted his head, just enough that Melena knew he was looking toward London Town’s section, toward the viewing boxes. The cameras wouldn’t catch it; the angle was wrong. But she felt it like a fist closing around her ribs—this small, private acknowledgment that he knew she was watching.
Say something, her mind screamed. Bang on the glass. Make a scene. Do anything.
Her throat stayed locked.
Across from him, Atlas didn’t look anywhere but straight ahead.
Melena couldn’t tell if that was discipline or self-defense.
The timer glyphs flared to life along the edge of the arena.
Ten minutes.
A sharp chime cut through the air.
“Begin.”
## Chapter 41: Melena – The Sixth Challenge
Fire hit the platform first.
Tiran moved like the floor was already burning, a blur of heat and motion. A column of flame roared up in front of him, then split, arcing toward Atlas from two sides. It was an opening meant to overwhelm, to force a choice—shield left or shield right—and punish whatever he didn’t cover.
Atlas didn’t shield.
He dropped the temperature.
One second, the air around him was clear. The next, it turned to a white, snapping halo as ice burst outward from his boots in a perfect ring. Frost slammed into Tiran’s fire, steam exploding where they met. The roar drowned out half the crowd. The glass in front of Melena vibrated.
Heat washed against her skin. Her own magic flared, reaching for Tiran’s through habit, through terror.
Come on, she begged silently. Take the draw. Take it, take it, take it—
Tiran cut through the steam, already adjusting, fire spiraling tighter around his arms instead of the full-platform burn he’d opened with. Atlas met him halfway, ice condensing around his forearms in jagged, armor-like plates. They collided in the center of the arena—fists, elbows, knees, fire and ice crashing so close together that for a heartbeat, Melena couldn’t tell where one ended and the other began.
The commentators yelled something about form and stance and “unbelievable control under pressure,” but it blurred into noise.
All Melena could see was the clock.
9:12.
If they could both stay standing, if they could both keep breathing, the Sanctum would have to call it.
Nine minutes.
Nine minutes of watching Tiran gamble what was left of London Town on a boy from Old Prosperity who had never once looked afraid.
Her fingers dug into the railing hard enough that the metal finally gave, denting under her grip.
Five bodies.
If this went wrong, Tiran would be six.
And then there would only be two pieces left on her side of the board.
She should have been out there. Melena knew it in her sweat, in the way her teeth ground together. Bishops fought before Queens, but the order didn’t matter. It should have been her. She would have fought Atlas until her last breath. She would have bled him. She would have cracked the ice and the perfect posture and the Old Prosperity myth of invincibility and made the entire gallery remember that the Lockwood line did not break, not for anyone, not even the Tournament.
8:57.
Melena’s breath stuttered.
Minho.
She didn’t say it out loud. Didn’t have to. The thought went out like a flare aimed across the arena toward Old Prosperity’s box.
For a second, there was nothing.
Then a familiar presence snapped into focus at the edge of her mind—irritated and scared enough that it was almost painful.
I’m here, Minho answered, his mental voice rougher than usual. Don’t start. I know.
She didn’t ask how he was. She could feel it through the link. He was tired. It was so much worse than she’d ever heard him, so much static at the edge that she almost wanted to pull back. It felt like shouting across a battlefield. She tried to steady her breathing, ground herself in the moment.
He’s your Bishop too, Melena pushed, because she couldn’t help it. Because guilt sat like a stone in her chest and needed somewhere to go. Atlas—
Is an idiot, Minho cut in. But a trained idiot nonetheless. 
On the arena floor, Atlas ducked under a blast of flame, ice skidding him sideways. Tiran pivoted with him, refusing to overextend, refusing to give Atlas his back. Fire spilled across the platform in a low, creeping wave meant to box him in.
Atlas didn’t flinch.
He slammed both palms down. Frost shot out in a jagged line, split Tiran’s fire down the middle, and turned it into another cloud of steam.
8:02.
He shouldn’t be out there before me. Maxim and I should have gone before anyone else.
He chose this, Minho rolled his eyes. Same as Atlas. Don’t you dare take that away from him because it hurts to watch.
Her vision blurred. She blinked hard and focused on the arena.
Shapes moved in and out of the steam. A flash of violet. A streak of white. Then Tiran burst through the haze, close enough to Atlas that their shoulders slammed together. He grabbed a fistful of Old Prosperity whites and dragged Atlas into a brutal knee to the gut, fire detonating off the impact. Atlas’s breath left him in a harsh sound that the mics picked up, turning it into something ugly over the speakers. He retaliated on instinct—ice exploding from his skin in a sudden outward spike. Shards sliced across Tiran’s side, opening three parallel lines through cloth and flesh.
Blood hit the platform in drops, then in a thin, steady stream.
The crowd roared.
“That’s what we came for!” one commentator shouted. “No hesitation, no wasted movement—do you see that control on both sides?”
Control.
There was nothing controlled about the way Melena’s chest clenched.
“Tiran,” she whispered, useless, because he couldn’t hear her.
7:21.
Tiran backed away a step, shoulder heaving, blood soaking into his House colors. He didn’t look at the wound. He didn’t look at the clock. He looked at Atlas.
Then he smiled.
Not the reckless grin he used to piss her off. This one was smaller. Meaner. A little desperate around the edges.
Come on, it said. Make this count.
Atlas’s expression didn’t change much, but Melena had watched enough of him over enough time to catch the tiny things. The way his jbreathing slowed. The inch of space he took back on the platform, just enough to reset his footing. The flash of something like apology in his eyes before it froze over.
“He knows,” Maxim said quietly beside her, voice low so Raven didn’t stand a chance of hearing him. “Atlas knows one of them isn’t walking off that platform whole.”
Melena swallowed. “He’s your friend too.”
Maxim’s laugh was empty. “He’s Minho’s best friend. He just doesn’t admit it because it ruins his reputation.”
On the floor, Atlas lifted his hands, palms open again.
The temperature dropped. 
Cold slammed into the arena so hard Melena’s breath fogged against the glass. Frost crawled up the barrier wards, up the stone walls, turning the whole space into a glittering cage. The crowd shrieked in delight. The commentators sputtered through half a sentence about “pushing magical output” before one of them outright cursed.
Tiran set himself against it.
Fire roared off him in a full-body flare, a reflexive rejection of the cold that made the closest sections of the stands shield their eyes. For a moment, he looked like the crest on his chest brought to life—phoenix mid-burn, wings made of flame, refusing to be swallowed.
6:03.
“Look at the bond resonance,” the second commentator gasped. “You can almost feel Blackwood’s phoenix and Tolliver’s drake pressing against the ward lines—”
Melena could.
It started as a prickle along her spine. Then a pressure, like something enormous was circling the arena just out of sight, testing the edges of the Sanctum’s cage. Omi was ice and sky and old Alleian mountains whereas Astrea was heat and hunger and loyalty so fierce it bordered on feral obsession. 
Neither of them liked what they were seeing.
Neither of them were on the board.
Tiran moved first this time too, but it wasn’t reckless. He didn’t try to overpower the cold head-on. He used it.
He slid across a sheet of thin ice Atlas had left on the platform, turning what should have been a stumble into momentum. In two heartbeats he was at Atlas’s flank, flames condensed around one hand like a blade. He slashed low, aiming for the knee.
Atlas dropped his weight.
The flaming strike missed the joint by inches, searing across his thigh instead. The smell of burnt fabric and skin hit Melena a second later, layered over the ghost of Luke’s body still in her nose. Atlas hissed, but instead of retreating, he caught Tiran’s wrist and pulled him in.
Chest to chest. Fire and ice pressed so close the air between them screamed.
Then Atlas drove his forehead into Tiran’s face.
The crack echoed.
Melena flinched like she’d been the one hit. Blood burst from Tiran’s nose. His grip faltered, fire shuddering.
5:11.
He’s still on his feet, Minho sent and Melena knew he wasn’t being specific about which one. He’s not out yet.
Yet, Melena echoed, the word sour.
Atlas didn’t let go of Tiran’s wrist. He yanked him sideways, slammed him into an ice pillar that hadn’t been there a second ago. The impact was hard enough that even through the roar of the crowd, Melena heard something in Tiran’s side give away. He gasped and his fire stuttered. 
“Ribs,” Maxim said, teyes locked on the scene below. “At least two.”
Melena’s own ribs hurt in sympathy. Her fingers slipped on the railing; she didn’t realize she’d broken skin until she smelled her own blood, hot and coppery, underneath the phantom of burning flesh.
“Call it,” she rasped. At the Sanctum. At the commentators. At anyone. “Call the draw.”
No one did.
The clock ticked down.
4:32.
Tiran sagged against the ice for half a second. Then he shoved off it, using the pillar as leverage to kick both feet up and into Atlas’s chest. It wasn’t pretty but it was enough to tear himself free. They went down in opposite directions—Atlas skidding on his back across the frost, Tiran hitting the platform hard and staying there a breath too long.
Minho? Melena sent, panic spiking so sharp she tasted it.
Don’t— His mind-voice frayed, then steadied. He’s moving. Look.
Tiran rolled onto his side. One hand clutched at his ribs. The other dug into the platform. Fire trickled out between his fingers in a thin, shaking line.
He pushed himself up.
3:58.
There was blood on his teeth when he smiled again. 
“This is incredible stamina from both Bishops,” one commentator babbled. “They’re running hot! Look at the glyph readouts on the ward lines. One wrong push and—”
The rest of the sentence vanished under a fresh wave of the crowd screaming.
Atlas was up too.
He was breathing hard, pale from blood loss, white hair darkened with sweat at the temples. Frost still crawled up his arms, but it looked different now—ragged, spiking over bruises and burns instead of clean plates. One side of his uniform was charred black where Tiran’s flame had landed.
They looked like they’d already fought ten matches, not one.
3:02.
Tiran lifted his hand.
Fire answered slower this time.
He didn’t go for the full flare. He couldn’t. Melena could see it in the tremor of his fingers, in the tight set of his jaw. He threw a narrower blast, aimed high, forcing Atlas to shield his face.
Atlas did.
But it cost him.
The ice wall that rose between them was thinner than the others had been. Tiran’s fire punched through in two places, streaks of heat that licked over Atlas’s shoulder and side. He turned with it, taking the damage on muscle instead of throat, but his legs buckled for a heartbeat.
Draw. You’ve proved your point. Call the gods-damned draw.
The Sanctum didn’t move.
2:17.
The bond pressure spiked.
Omi coiled above Atlas like a stormfront, all jagged wings and carved-ice scales, blue-white lines of power tracing the length of his body. Astrea threaded through the ward lines around Tiran, feathers trailing sparks every time she brushed the invisible cage holding her out.
They weren’t supposed to manifest here. Not like this. NNot without half a dozen signatures from the Sanctum.
They didn’t care.
They’re going to break something, Minho whispered. 
He didn’t mean the glass.
1:46.
On the platform, the boys stopped fighting smart.
They just fought.
Tiran lunged, leading with his bad side because the good one couldn’t take another hit. Atlas was ready. He took the blow across his burned shoulder, swallowed the pain, and used it to close. His arm locked around Tiran’s throat, elbow grinding in. Tiran clawed at his forearm, fingers slick with his own blood. Melena saw the way they fit together—locked, shaking, one bad decision away from permanent.
The commentators had stopped talking.
The crowd hadn’t.
0:52.
Tiran dropped to one knee.
He didn’t get back up.
Minho— Melena’s thoughts frayed.
He’s alive, Minho shot back, too fast. The lie burned around the edges. He’s— just watch.
Tiran braced his hand on the platform. Steam rose where his blood hit the ice. He spat something red onto the floor—probably a tooth—and tried to push himself up. Atlas didn’t give him the chance. He seized the moment, pulled Tiran upright by the collar, and slammed him flat against the ice. Face first, hard enough that the platform rang. 
0:40.
Atlas stared at him, chest heaving, eyes narrowed against sweat and blood and whatever else was in them Melena couldn’t read from this distance. For a half-second—just one—she saw it:
The option to stop.
He could hold, stall, wait out the clock, let the draw come.
0:39.
The world buckled.
For a split second, nobody in the arena—not the crowd, not the Sanctum, not Melena herself—could tell what happened. One instant, Atlas had Tiran pinned, a forearm grinding into the back of his neck, the other hand braced to break his shoulder. The next, a sheet of white slammed through the platform, a shockwave so cold it froze the air solid and burst the glass on the front row.
Then fire.
Not fire like Tiran had been throwing. Not fire like Melena had ever seen, even in the worst palace accidents when Elias lost his temper. 
The force of it threw Atlas and Tiran to opposing sides.
“Is that?” Maxim began.
Omi. Minho finished. 
And Astrea.
The words didn't come from Minho. They came from everywhere—from the air itself, thrumming through Melena's chest like a second heartbeat she didn't ask for. The crowd heard it too. Half of them went silent. The other half started screaming.
Because now they could see them.
Omi uncoiled fully above the platform, scales glinting like fractured starlight, eyes twin points of cold that made the arena feel small. Every breath he took pulled frost across the wards in spiraling patterns. He didn't look at the crowd. He looked at Astrea.
And she looked back.
Astrea manifested in a blaze that scorched the ward lines black, wings spread wide enough to cast shadows across three sections of seating. Feathers of pure flame dripped embers onto the platform, and where they landed, the ice hissed and cracked and turned to steam. She was a phoenix, but not the kind from children's stories. This one looked ready to kill. 
0:32.
The boys were still on the ground—Atlas on his back, ice spreading from where Omi's power had thrown him, Tiran on his side, burns smoking across his armor where Astrea's fire had caught him. They were both breathing hard. Both bleeding. Both staring at their bonds like they'd just committed treason.
Maybe they had.
The commentators found their voices first.
"Ladies and gentlemen—" The man's voice cracked. He cleared his throat, tried again. "I—we are witnessing an unprecedented display of—of bond manifestation, full corporeal breach of tournament wards without Sanctum clearance—"
"This is INCREDIBLE!" The woman cut in, and she sounded gleeful, like she'd just watched someone pull off the impossible. "I've covered seventeen tournaments and I have never—not ONCE—seen mystic intervention at this level!"
The crowd was losing it. Chanting, screaming, on their feet—half of them didn't even know what they were watching, but they knew it was history.
Melena wasn't watching the crowd.
She was searching for Minho.
His face had gone carefully blank in the way it only did when he was working very, very hard not to react to something very, very bad. 
In. Minho’s voice held no room for argument. Both of you. Now. 
It wasn’t a request. It was a mental drag, a hard snap of gravity that yanked Melena and Maxim out of their bodies and into Minho’s mindspace. She hit the ground—always a ground, always some kind of surface under her feet in these constructs—hard enough to jar her knees. Maxim was already there, a hand braced on a black marble table that hadn’t existed before this second. 
The world here was cold. Melena hated it. Minho’s mindspace was a bunker, a place for triage, not comfort. There were no walls, but the sense of enclosure was absolute. She could feel his focus pressing in from every angle, like being inside a locked vault. 
“What the hell are we looking at?” Maxim spoke first. 
Cold slammed into her. Omi’s attention washed over them as he counted the fractures in Atlas’s ribs. Tracked his heart rate. Marked every place that Atlas has been injured and every place he hadn’t. Then came the heat, though Melena knew it never left. She could feel Astrea like a second pulse now. Fire threaded around Tiran’s heart, pulses radiating from her—here, here, here—as if she was keeping his heart from stopping. 
Melena hated that she knew that Astrea was. 
“They’re loud on the command channel,” Minho said finally. “Old Prosperity’s not thrilled the phoenix just put her talons on Sanctum wards.”
“‘Not thrilled,’” Maxim repeated. “That what they’re calling it?”
“Blackwoods are worse,” Minho went on, ignoring him. “Their line of credit with Astrea is…thin.” A pause. “She did not ask permission.”
Melena’s stomach turned. “So this wasn’t coordinated.”
“Oh, it’s coordinated,” Minho said. “Just not with the Sanctum.”
The ground under them shivered; the mindspace flickered, like something had banged on the outside of a closed door. Melena caught a flash of sound that wasn’t sound from Omi and Astrea.
“House channels?” Maxim asked.
“No” Minho confirmed. “The Tollivers are screaming at Alleia, Alleia screaming back. Blackwoods asking whether the Empress plans to step in. ”
“So who called them here?” Maxim frowned, crossing his arms over his chest. 
The mindspace buckled again, harder this time 
“They did,” Melena whispered as the pieces finally clicked together. “Atlas and Tiran.”
The mindspace went silent.
Not quiet—silent. The kind of silence that happened when something impossible had just been said out loud and nobody knew how to respond.
"That's not—" Maxim started.
"Possible?" Minho's voice was flat. "No. It's not."
But Melena could feel it now. The echo of it. Two calls, issued almost simultaneously, threaded through the bond channels with the kind of desperation that only came from someone who'd run out of options. Atlas hadn't asked Omi to intervene. He'd begged. And Tiran—
Tiran had done the same.
"Bonded don't answer calls during a match," Maxim said, but there was no conviction in it. "The wards block—"
"The wards block unauthorized manifestations," Minho corrected. "This wasn't unauthorized. Not by the bonds." He paused, and Melena felt the ripple of his attention shift, like he was listening to five conversations at once and trying not to drown in them. "They claimed right of preservation."
Melena's breath caught. "For each other?"
"For each other."
The ground buckled again. Harder. Something was trying to break into the mindspace, and Minho was holding it back.
"Who's that?" Maxim asked.
"Your grandmother," Minho said through his teeth. "She wants to know if I can confirm—" He stopped. Shook his head. "I'm not answering that."
Smart.
Because if Minho confirmed what they'd just realized—that two Tournament competitors had invoked preservation rights for their opponent—then the Empress would have to respond. And the Sanctum would have to respond. And every family watching would know that two boys had just looked at each other across a blood-soaked platform and decided they'd rather break the rules than become murderers.
Melena could still feel Omi focusing on keeping Atlas's cracked ribs from puncturing a lung. Could still feel Astrea's fire wrapped around Tiran's heart keeping the rhythm from faltering.
They were healing them.
In front of everyone.
"The Tollivers are furious," Minho said quietly. "Astrea acted without house clearance. The Blackwoods are—" He stopped. "They're asking Omi why he didn't consult the family council before manifesting."
"What's he saying?" Maxim asked.
"Nothing." Minho's jaw tightened. "He's not on their channel anymore. He's—" A pause. "He's only talking to Astrea now."
The mindspace shivered.
Melena felt something shift. A weight redistributing.
"They're shutting them out," she said.
"All of them," Minho confirmed. "The houses. The Sanctum. Everyone." His voice dropped. "They're saying this isn't a family decision."
Maxim went very still. "Then what is it?"
Melena didn't answer right away. She was watching the feedback loop between Omi and Astrea, the way their attention circled each other—not aggressive, not territorial, but something else. Something that looked almost like—
Recognition.
"They're not enemies," she said slowly. "They never were."
"Of course they're not enemies," Maxim said. "They're mystic creatures, they don't have—"
"No." Melena shook her head. "I mean they're not—they don't see each other as opponents. They see each other as—" She struggled for the word. "As the same."
Minho made a sound low in his throat. "Bound to children being forced to kill each other for sport."
Melena's stomach dropped, because that was it. That was the thing she'd been circling around since the Tournament started, since she nearly died in the Maze, since they stood in this arena night after night and watched their Pieces bleed. 
This was entertainment.
This was their entertainment.
And Omi and Astrea had just said no.
## Chapter 42: Katherine – The Sixth Challenge
She'd watched him break.
Watched Tiran grab him by the throat and squeeze. Watched ice crack under the weight of his body when he went down. Watched blood—his blood—spread across white fabric that was supposed to come back clean, supposed to look untouchable, supposed to mean something about Old Prosperity's perfect fucking legacy.
It didn't mean shit when you were bleeding.
Atlas had been breathing wrong for the last three minutes of that fight. Katherine knew because she'd counted. Counted his breaths the way she used to count her own when the panic got bad, when the walls closed in and she needed something to anchor to. His ribs were cracked, maybe worse—she'd seen the way he'd flinched when Tiran's knee connected, the way his whole body had locked up trying not to scream.
And he hadn't yielded.
Stupid. So fucking stupid.
Katherine's throat was raw. She didn't remember screaming but her voice was gone, scraped down to nothing. Minho was beside her somewhere—she could feel him more than see him, presence pressed against the edge of her awareness like he was holding himself together by force of will alone. Old Prosperity's entire section had gone silent when the bonds manifested. Before that it had been noise and people screaming for their favorite Pieces, like their Bishop wasn't bleeding out on the platform.
Now nobody was saying anything.
Because Omi and Astrea had just told the Sanctum to go fuck itself.
And Katherine—
Katherine felt something else.
It started as pressure. Built behind her sternum like someone was pressing on her chest from the inside. Then it sharpened, turned into something that felt almost like—
Melena.
Not her voice but her feeling. Terror and rage and relief crashing together so hard Katherine couldn't tell where one ended and the other began. She could taste it. Copper and smoke and something that felt like screaming even though no sound came out.
Melena had watched Tiran almost die.
Katherine had watched Atlas almost die.
And now they were supposed to walk out there and finish what their Bishops started.
The arena announcer's voice cut through the noise, smooth and practiced like he did this every day. Like six bodies hadn't been wheeled off this floor already. Like two more weren't about to follow.
"Ladies and gentlemen—" A pause, letting the crowd settle just enough to hear him. "The Sanctum has reviewed the events of the previous match."
Katherine's pulse kicked.
Reviewed.
That was a word that meant consequences. Meant someone was about to pay for what Omi and Astrea just did.
"After deliberation," the announcer continued, "the match between King's Bishops Atlas Tolliver and Tiran Blackwood has been ruled a DRAW by intervention. Both Pieces remain on the board. Both provinces retain their Bishops."
The crowd erupted. Half of them cheering, half of them booing, all of them loud enough that the glass in front of Katherine vibrated.
A draw.
Atlas was still alive. Still breathing. Still hers—
—for now.
The thought made her flinch. The arena lights blurred for a second, haloing out around the edges. Katherine blinked hard until the glass steadied, until Atlas’s crumpled form on the platform resolved into pieces she could understand: medics, stretcher, blood.
Not gone. Not yet.
Minho’s hand closed around her forearm. She hadn’t even realized she was halfway out of her seat.
“He’s breathing,” Minho said. “Katherine. Look at me. He’s breathing.”
She dragged her gaze off the arena and forced it to Minho. His pupils were blown wide, jaw clenched, the muscles in his cheek ticking in a rhythm she knew better than any anthem. His other hand was a fist on his knee. There were crescents on his palm where his nails had broken skin.
He was shaking.
“I know,” she rasped. It hurt. Everything hurt. “I know.”
It didn’t make it better.
From the other side of the gallery, she saw Melena’s silhouette. Even at this distance, the crown of braids and the tilt of her head looked pissed. The glass between them was too thick for sound, but Katherine imagined she could hear Melena’s voice anyway—something mean, something clever, something that might pull Katherine out of her own head for once.
“Queen Pieces, report to your handlers immediately,” the announcer said. “You have ten minutes until entry.”
Intermission. The Queens match always came with a reset since the last two battles of the night were usually the bloodiest. Katherine wasn’t sure how much bloody it could get after Atlas and Tiran. She barely remembered the walk. One second she was in the gallery with Minho’s hand bruising her arm, the next she was in a holding corridor, the air thick with the scent of blood they’d tried to scrub off the walls. The floor vibrated under her boots—crowd noise, cables, the hum of a thousand feeds beaming her death sentence live to the Empire. She’d never felt less like a Queen in her life.
A medic came at her with a scanner wand, his hands trembling as he checked her for injuries. She tried to glare, but her face wouldn’t cooperate.
“Your handler is here,” he said never once looking at her. “Vitals look good. You want something for the shakes?”
She shook her head. “No, thank you.”
“You have eight minutes.”
Nico didn’t bother clearing his throat or knocking. He stepped in like he owned the place, tablet in one hand, Sanctum ID clipped neat to his collar. His hair was pulled back into a tie that hadn’t moved all day. Katherine wanted to rip it out just to see something about him come undone the way everyone else on their team had. 
“How’s Atlas?” was the first thing she could muster. 
“Alive. Stable. That’s all I can say right now.” Nico looked at her pleadingly. “Anything you need to tell me before you go out there?” 
I can’t control my element, and I have a second one that’s likely going to be the reason I die tonight. 
“No,” Katherine whispered. 
“After everything that has happened tonight,” Nico continued, “your province still has all of your back line Pieces and your King. Remember that when you’re out there.”
The words were supposed to make her feel better. They didn’t. She just nodded and stretched her left hand, tried to get the frost to recede, then curled the right, feeling the heat pulse in her palm. If she squeezed hard enough, maybe the fire would burn itself out before the match. She didn’t believe that either.
Nico watched her for a second too long, like he was waiting for her to break down and confess everything. When she didn’t, he sighed and checked his tablet, stylus tapping out a staccato that set her teeth on edge.
“Protocol,” he said, flipping to the next page. “London Town and Old Prosperity will enter from opposite sides. You will circle once. You will bow to the Sanctum. You will bow to your opponent. You will not address the crowd. Now, because your opponent is an Imperial heir, the rules are a little different.” Nico looked up. “Only a little. You’ll both be carrying live comms for the Imperial channel, so the Empress and the provinces can monitor in real time. If you get a direct order from the Sanctum, you follow it. If there’s a breach or a forfeit, you stop immediately.” He paused. “If you kill her, you’re not to touch the body.”
Katherine stared. 
He didn’t blink. “We had…incidents in the past. The protocol is new.”
“You mean I’m supposed to let her kill me?” 
Nico’s face pinched. “No one’s asking you to die for optics. If you have the advantage, take it. Just…” He hesitated, the first crack in his perfect mask. “Don’t make it a spectacle. If you can avoid it.”
Katherine nodded. “I understand.”
“Good.” Nico almost smiled. “Try not to embarrass us.”
She snorted, and it sounded more like she was choking than laughing. “I’ll do my best.”
They left the box together. Security mages tracked their progress with blank expressions and glowing cuffs. The Sanctum sigil pulsed on every door.
Four turns, one lift, two more turns, and they were at the staging tunnel.
Beyond the reinforced glass, the arena floor gleamed under too-bright lights. The platform had been reset. No blood. No scorch marks. No cracked ice. If she didn’t know better, Katherine would’ve thought she’d imagined the last hour.
"And now—" The announcer's voice climbed, whipping the crowd back into a frenzy. "The moment you've all been waiting for."
“Representing the Imperial Province of London Town—Her Imperial Highness, Princess Melena Beatrix Elizabeth Lockwood.” He stretched every one of her names like it was candy on his tongue. “And representing the Province of Old Prosperity—Queen Piece Katherine Medina Celeste Nicholas.”
Katherine hated the way they gave the full names of the Queen and King Pieces. It reminded her of an eulogy for a condemned prisoner. She’d heard once there was an old tradition, from the wars before the Empire, where they’d read out your whole lineage before they put you in the ground. Like the more names you stacked behind a body, the harder it was to forget.
The door opened and Katherine walked. 
If she stopped to think she'd turn around and that wasn't an option, so she just moved. One foot in front of the other. The noise from the arena was a wall she had to push through—screaming, chanting, something that might've been her name but sounded more like static.
Twenty paces. The tunnel stretched them out until it felt like a hundred. The lights hit her first. White and gold and so fucking bright she had to squint against them. Then the platform rose under her feet.
Across the platform, Melena's section lifted at the same time.
Katherine didn't want to look but she did it anyway. 
Her first thought was that she looked composed. Which said a lot for someone who’d just watch their most powerful Piece almost die. The braids, the cape, the uniform — none of it looked out of place. 
The second thought almost knocked the air out of her lungs. 
She’s not okay. 
The announcer was saying something—rules, protocols, some bullshit about honor and glory—but Katherine couldn't hear it over the blood rushing in her ears.
This was it.
Melena or her.
One of them was walking off this platform. The other one wasn't.
“Circle,” Nico said in her ear. “Protocol.”
Katherine dropped into motion. She and Melena stepped onto the platform in sync, keeping to opposite sides as they curved around each other. The crowd volume dipped a little, like they knew this part was supposed to mean something.
Right. Protocol.
Katherine moved on autopilot, boots silent on the platform as she and Melena curved around each other in opposite directions. The crowd volume dipped slightly, like even they knew this part was supposed to mean something. Katherine kept her eyes forward but tracked Melena in her peripheral. The way she moved. The set of her spine. Looking for weaknesses, for tells, for anything that might give Katherine an edge when the timer started.
Except Melena was doing the same thing.
Their eyes met for half a second as they passed each other. Katherine felt something pull tight in her chest—that connection yanking at her ribs like a fishing line hooked somewhere vital. Melena's expression didn't change but Katherine saw her swallow. Quick. Like she was trying to force something down.
"Bow to the Sanctum," Nico's voice came through the comm.
Katherine's body moved before her brain caught up. Year Six combat protocols. Commander Reeves had drilled it into them until they could do it in their sleep—spine straight, eyes down, three seconds exactly. Any longer and you looked weak. Any shorter and you looked disrespectful. She'd practiced it a hundred times in formation, bowing to instructors and visiting generals and Old Prosperity officials who barely looked at them.
She'd never practiced bowing before she killed someone.
"Bow to your opponent."
This part was worse.
Katherine forced herself to meet Melena's eyes as they both dipped their heads. She’d learned the difference between a regular bow and an Imperial one on accident. Prince Consort Tobias had been visiting Old Prosperity for briefings with her mother—something about troop rotations along the southern border. Katherine had been in the hall when he'd stepped out of the war room, and she'd bowed the way Commander Reeves taught her. Spine straight, eyes down, three seconds.
Tobias had smiled and said, "That's a military bow. For Imperials, you dip your chin first. Shows you're acknowledging the crown before you acknowledge the person."
Her mother had looked embarrassed. Katherine had practiced the Imperial bow every night for a week after that, just in case. 
She wondered if he'd thought she'd use it like this.
The timer glyphs flared to life along the edge of the arena.
Ten minutes.
Katherine's hands curled into fists at her sides. Frost gathered at her fingertips without permission. She pulled it back, forced it down, tried to breathe through the tightness in her chest that wouldn't let go.
Six bodies. Atlas barely breathing. Tiran's blood still on the platform even though they'd tried to clean it.
Katherine looked at Melena one last time.
Then the world caught fire.
## Chapter 43: Katherine – The Sixth Challenge
One second she was standing on the platform, frost gathering at her fingertips like it always did. The next, heat slammed into her so hard she thought her ribs cracked. Not from impact but from proximity. As if the sheer force of Melena's element detonated across the space between them like she'd been holding it back this whole time and finally let go.
Katherine threw up ice on instinct. A wall formed in the half-second before Melena's fire reached her, and the collision was so loud Katherine felt it in her jaw.
Then the wall exploded.
Not melted. Exploded. Shards of ice turned to steam so fast Katherine didn't have time to process what happened before the heat was on her again, closer this time, hungry, and she was moving—boots scraping against stone as she dove right, shoulders hitting the platform hard enough to knock the air from her lungs.
Fire roared over her head.
She felt the heat of it singe the ends of her hair as she rolled onto her back and saw Melena standing where she’d been two seconds ago, hands still raised, flames dripping off her fingers like she was bleeding light.
She came in low and fast, fire spiraling up her arms in patterns Katherine had only seen in advanced combat texts. Katherine cursed under her breath. They didn’t teach Imperial formations like this until Year Twelve. Katherine pushed herself up, ice already forming along her forearms in defensive plates. She caught Melena's first strike—barely. The fire hit her shield and the force of it sent her skidding back three feet. Her boots scraped against stone. The crowd was screaming but Katherine couldn't hear them over the sound of her own heartbeat hammering in her ears.
Melena didn't let up.
She pressed forward, fire building with each step, and Katherine could feel the temperature rising. Could feel sweat starting to bead along her spine even though her ice was still active. She threw a blast of frost at Melena's feet, trying to trip her up, trying to get some distance.
Melena burned through it without breaking stride.
Shit.
Their eyes met.
For half a second, neither of them moved.
Katherine's training kicked in before her panic could. Watch their footwork. Everyone telegraphs. Commander Reeves's voice, drilled into her skull after years of combat evals.
Melena was telegraphing.
Her left shoulder hitched half a second before she threw fire. Small tell. Exploitable. And when she wound up for the big blasts, her right side opened completely—ribs exposed, no guard, committed to the attack with nowhere to pivot.
If Katherine wanted to kill her, she had the opening right now.
One ice spear. Straight through the ribs while Melena's arms were raised.
Katherine's frost responded to the thought, but she pulled her strike at the second and took the hit instead. She hit the platform hard, air punching out of her lungs, ice cracking beneath her. Pain bloomed across her ribs where Melena's hand had connected. Katherine threw her hand out and ice erupted from the platform—a wall, a barrier, anything to buy herself three seconds to breathe. Melena pulled back, flames gathering in her palms as she prepared to blast through Katherine's defense.
Katherine used those three seconds.
She got to her feet and steadied herself. Her ribs were screaming but she'd fought through worse. The downside to being on offense, was the fact that Melena didn't even realize how many times Katherine could've killed her. How many openings she'd given up. How sloppy her form was when she got angry. If Melena had formal training—if someone had taught her to control that fire instead of just throwing it—Katherine would be dead already. But Melena fought like wildfire. Everywhere at once, consuming everything, no strategy beyond burn.
“Lockwood’s output is off the charts,” one of the commentators was saying, voice barely cutting through the roar. “But Nicholas isn’t capitalizing—she’s got openings and she’s not taking them. What is Old Prosperity doing?”
She was holding back. Letting Melena hit her, again and again, just to see what she could take. Katherine could almost hear her mother's voice in the stands, spitting with disgust. Never let a Flamer in close. She’d read the manuals, done the drills, hell she’d even survived the first five Challenges. But fighting Melena was harder than she expected. Each strike from her landed too close to bone, each burst of heat stole another second off her clock, and every time, Katherine let it happen.
Melena’s fist caught her in the shoulder next, flames flaring at the last second so the impact sank straight through armor. Pain shot down her arm but it was off-center—muscle, not joint. It would bruise. It wouldn’t break.
Her frost could fix that.
Cold crawled under her skin, instinctively rushing to numb the ache, to keep the swelling down, and knit what it could as she moved. Katherine didn’t have to think about it. Her body had learned that part a long time ago so she didn’t lock herself up mid-fight.
She took a half step back, letting the momentum roll through her instead of stopping it.
Hit number five. Same side. Melena liked that shoulder.
Melena came for her ribs again—telegraphed by that same stupid hitch. Katherine could’ve slipped it easy, turned her hip, driven a frost-hardened elbow into that soft space just under Melena’s sternum.
She didn’t.
She dropped her guard just enough that the blast clipped her. Fire skimmed across her side, catching on the seam of her armor where ice plates met fabric. Katherine hissed, but she didn’t stagger. She pivoted with it, rolling her weight so the worst of the burn landed on the outer muscle of her ribs and not across her stomach.
Flesh wound. Surface level.
Her frost surged there next, a cold wash under the burn that slowed the damage. The pain blurred, flattening into pressure.
Hit number six. Melena came for soft tissue when she lost patience.
Noted.
The fire inside her pushed against her ribs in answer, like it didn’t appreciate the competition. Heat curled low in her chest, angry and restless. It felt like being too close to a bonfire and realizing the worst of it was coming from your own organs. Katherine shoved it down without looking at it. She couldn’t afford to. She focused on what was outside her body, not what was screaming inside it.
Watch their feet.
Measure the distance.
Count the breath between their tells and their strikes.
Melena’s breathing was getting louder. Not ragged, but no longer that steady pace from the start. Two-count inhale. One-count exhale on the big bursts. Her fire flared brightest when she exhaled hard.
Katherine knew she’d counted it perfectly. 
Melena swung wide with her right, fire trailing the arc like a comet. Katherine ducked late on purpose. The outer edge of the blast slammed into her upper back. Heat seared through armor, punching the air out of her lungs and driving her to one knee.
Her vision sparked. For a heartbeat, she tasted metal.
Then the frost slid up her spine, an icy hand bracing her, holding the line of bone and muscle in place. Cold wrapped the burn, hardening around it the way ice formed over cracked stone.
It would hurt later but that was a later problem.
She pushed up again.
Hit seven. Melena favored high, dramatic shots when she thought she had momentum.
If Katherine turned her body three inches to the left, that same swing would’ve taken her at the base of the skull instead but Old Prosperity hadn’t taught her to lose. 
Year One, Intro to Combat. Reeves standing at the front of the room, hands clasped behind his back as he clicked through holos of past Tournaments like they were case studies instead of graves. The test at the end of the unit wasn’t written. It was practical. Put the recruit in a sim with a “civilian,” give them a choice—kill the threat or watch the casualty bleed out. 
Unlike the PAE, Katherine hadn’t hesitated. 
Her frost tore through the targets throat in five seconds. She remembered the spray, the simulation's red pixels atomized across her gloves, and the cold satisfaction that came with a perfect score. Reeves had called it a perfect example. The medic examining her after said she’d been suffering from PTSD after the lake incident. The rest of her class didn’t look her in the eye for three weeks. She’d gone back to her room that night and stared at her hands until they blurred, waiting for the guilt to show up.
It never did.
That was the part that scared her.
She knew how easy it would be to do the same thing now. Adjust for height, speed, range. One good shot—spear to the ribs, frost-lock her lungs, finish it. Her body already knew the math. Her element was already leaning toward it, eager in a way that made her stomach twist.
She didn’t move.
Reeves would’ve ripped her apart for it—standing there with her guard open, taking damage just to study an opponent that sloppy. What are you doing, Nicholas? he’d snarl. You neutralize the threat, you don’t give it space to improvise.
Melena wasn’t the threat. 
What came next was. 
7:18.
Seven minutes left. Katherine could survive seven minutes. She could outlast Melena's fire, wear her down, wait for an opening that didn't require killing to exploit. She could—
Katherine saw it happen. Saw the moment Melena's eyes tracked down from Katherine's face to her chest, to the emblems on her wrists that were now exposed. 
Melena's eyes widened.
And Katherine knew.
She knew before Melena moved. Before the fire started tightening around her hands—not wild this time, not flaring for the cameras, but pulling in, condensing. Before the anger on her face twisted into something that looked a lot like determination.
“You can’t—”
Scars from a duel were honorable; emblems were sacred. Even in back-alley brawls, even in under-the-table matches, nobody touched another Elementalist’s emblems. They were the one thing that should never be bothered. 
To burn them was to erase the last proof you’d ever belonged to anything.
She'd backed herself into the edge of the platform—ten feet from the drop, nowhere left to retreat. Melena was between her and open space, fire building in both hands, and Katherine could see it on her face.
Melena was going to hit her with everything she had.
Move, Katherine's instincts screamed. Counter. Attack. Do something.
She knew what Melena was going to do before Melena moved. Knew it with the same certainty she knew her own name. Melena was out of options. Out of time. Out of everything except one desperate play. A wall—the biggest one she could manage with what was left of her frost. It formed between them in half a second, thick enough to take a hit, solid enough to buy her time to—
Steam flooded Katherine’s vision, scalding and thick. She lost sight of Melena in the instant the wall evaporated, but she didn’t need her eyes to know what was coming; she could taste copper in her teeth and heat prickled along her neck despite the ice crowding her veins. She dropped and rolled, arms locked over her head, and heard the sizzle as Melena’s fire whipped over her. The next moment she was scrambling to her feet, boots slipping on a platform now slick with condensation. The world was a frantic blur—shouts from the crowd, the shriek of evaporating water, her own ragged breath. Katherine’s right hand went numb from the cold, and she flexed it hard, willing the circulation back. She needed her arms to work.
She’d made it two feet before Melena was on her again, a knee to the gut and an uppercut that made Katherine’s teeth clack together. She tasted blood, warm and sharp against the inside of her mouth. She tried to counter, sweeping Melena’s legs with a blade of ice, but Melena anticipated it—jumped clear, twisted in midair, and came down with a fistful of fire aimed straight at Katherine’s face.
Katherine barely managed to angle her head aside, but the heat scorched her cheek, left a patch of skin roaring with pain. She threw her arms up on instinct, trying to shield her face from the blast, trying to—
Her guard dropped.
Just for a second. Just long enough for Melena’s steam to clear. Just long enough for Katherine to realize what she'd done.
The emblems were exposed.
Melena saw them.
Get out of there, Nicholas! Katherine turned to the left searching for Minho’s face knowing she wouldn’t be able to see him. 
Melena’s hands shot forward, fire coiling around her wrists in a blinding helix, and Katherine felt every old lesson vanish from her memory. There was nothing but reaction—she tried to shield her wrists, but Melena was faster, her hands locking around Katherine’s forearms with a grip that was all bone and will.
Heat. So much heat it was like being submerged in boiling water. Katherine’s skin screamed. She tried to freeze Melena’s fingers, tried to conjure a layer of ice between them, but the fire was already inside the effort, burning through the cold before it could even form. Melena’s grip tightened, and Katherine’s teeth snapped shut on a snarl.
She’d never felt pain like it. There was no room for thought, no room even for rage; just the singular, all-consuming agony of her element being burned out of her. The Frost emblems were the only thing she’d ever been proud of, and now Melena’s fire was melting them down to nothing. Katherine watched the blue lines in her skin turn white, then red, then black. She smelled her own flesh cooking and bile surged in her throat.
She tried to pull away. Melena didn’t let go.
