For a moment, Katherine thought she saw something—regret, maybe, or apology—in the way Melena’s eyes flicked up to meet hers. But the fire kept coming, and so did the pressure, until Katherine’s legs gave out. 
“Yield,” Melena whispered as if she wasn’t sure. “Please.”
Katherine couldn't.
Not because she was brave. Not because she had anything left to prove. She couldn't yield because her throat had locked around the word, because her body had stopped listening to her brain three seconds ago, because the pain was so absolute it had erased language.
"UNBELIEVABLE!" The commentator's voice cut through the noise, half-shriek, half-awe. "Melena Lockwood is going for the emblems—I have NEVER seen this attempted in Tournament history—"
"She's not just damaging them," the second commentator interrupted, voice climbing. "She's severing them. Do you understand what we're watching? If she succeeds, Katherine Nicholas will lose her primary element entirely—"
The crowd's roar reached a pitch that made the platform vibrate.
Melena's fire burrowed deeper.
Katherine felt it the moment the first emblem gave. Not broke—gave. Like a rope snapping under too much weight. The connection between her and her frost just... stopped.
She screamed.
She’d always wondered what it would feel like to die, and now she had an answer: it felt exactly like this, like heat so intense it became cold, like every nerve ending was being rewired to transmit only one message, over and over. Katherine’s vision shrank to a tunnel. Around the edges, the world dissolved into white static. There was no crowd, no platform, no air. The only thing that existed was the pain, and the certainty that it would never end.
But that was a lie. The pain did end. The second emblem broke and the pain ended so suddenly it was almost worse. Katherine slumped, boneless, her whole body shivering and dry-heaving on the stone. The sound of her own retching echoed up from somewhere far away. She tried to crawl, but her arms wouldn’t answer. She couldn’t feel them at all. Her wrists were ruined. Her hands hung useless, blue-black and twitching, skin cracked and wet.
She braced herself for a third wave of fire, for Melena to finish the job, but it never came. Instead there was just ragged breathing, and the strange, slippery sound of Melena backing away, shoes squeaking on the slick stone.
Katherine tried to focus on her hands, but her eyes wouldn’t cooperate. She blinked, hard, trying to see what was left. The marks—her Frost emblems—were ruined. The blue had gone black, lines split and twisted, the sigils broken mid-curve like someone had tried to erase her with fire and stopped halfway. She felt a scream building but nothing came out. She was empty.
Above her, the commentator was howling, giddy with glee. "—and there it is! Nicholas is out! Nicholas is—my gods, I think she's still conscious!"
Katherine heard it all like it was a radio tuned badly, volume spiking and fading, voices warping. Her body was somewhere below her, on the platform, still twitching. Her left hand twitched, spasmed, curled into a blue claw. She tried to make a fist and couldn't. She couldn't feel her element at all—just a numb, echoing blankness she’d only ever felt once before, the night the lake nearly killed her.
Katherine tried to push herself up.
Her arms wouldn't work.
She tried to call her frost—one last desperate attempt to feel that familiar cold respond.
Nothing.
Just nothing.
The absence was so complete it felt like dying. Like someone had reached into her chest and carved out everything that made her who she was and left her with just the shell.
Katherine's hands pressed flat against the platform.
And before she could blink, fire answered.
## Chapter 44: Melena – The Sixth Challenge

Black.

She took a step back. Another. Her boots squeaked and the sound was wrong. Everything was wrong. Five pieces dead and Tiran was—

Felt everything.
He wouldn't teach—
Unless—
(Don't worry about it, sweetheart.)
He came back different.
Why was she thinking about—
Katherine's fire was getting brighter and Melena couldn't—
She went down.
Hit the platform hard.
Her vision—
Above her Katherine stood and Melena saw—
Saw the shape of her—
The way she—
No no no that wasn't—
"Please."
Katherine hesitated.
The buzzer screamed.
Katherine dropped.
Someone shouted about her heart stopping.
And Melena—
She couldn't move.
Couldn't breathe.
Couldn't do anything except stare at the girl who moved like—
Like—
(Ours.)
# PART THREE: THE CONQUEROR
## Chapter 45: Katherine 
She'd died.
At least that's what ran through her head when she woke up.
She'd died.
Forty-three seconds. Long enough for her heart to stop. Long enough for the medics to panic and call the fight. Apparently, everyone was still shaken up from Tiran and Atlas to take any chances. 
A strip of lighting that buzzed just loud enough to make her want to claw at it.
Katherine stared at it instead.
Breath in.
Breath out.
Her lungs remembered how to work before the rest of her did. Everything else lagged behind, like her body was buffering.
She tried to swallow. Her throat burned. Something tugged at the inside of her arm. An IV line. Two, actually. One clear. One faintly blue.
There was a monitor somewhere to her right. Soft, steady beeping. She didn’t turn her head. Couldn’t. Her neck protested the idea before she even committed to it.
Okay. Inventory.
Fingers first.
She flexed them. Pain shot up both arms like someone had threaded molten wire under her skin and tugged.
She hissed through her teeth.
Left hand. Right hand. Ten fingers, all attached.
Her wrists throbbed.
Her breath stuttered as she thought about glancing at them.
No. Not yet. One thing at a time.
She pushed her attention down. Toes. She could feel sheets against her calves, the rough drag of hospital fabric against bare skin. There was weight at the end of the bed—bandages? Casts? She couldn’t tell. Her legs felt like they belonged to someone else. Someone who’d been hit by—
Fire.
It snapped across her mind fast enough to steal the air she’d just managed to drag in.
The arena. The Board. The sound the crowd made when they realized.
Her.
Melena.
Katherine’s chest tightened. The monitor chirped faster.
Traitor.
She squeezed her eyes shut.
Fragments, at first.
Ice. Smoke. Melena’s face when she saw the flame tattoos on Katherine’s wrists. 
Dualist.
She remembered the heat. Not just from Melena, but from herself. Furious. Broken. Angry. So damn angry. 
And then—
Nothing.
Like somebody had snapped something inside her. Like a door slamming shut.
She’d reached for frost and grabbed air. Reached for fire and caught the edge of a scream instead.
Katherine swallowed again. It hurt less this time.
“Okay,” she whispered, barely sound. “Okay. Move.”
“I wouldn’t do that if I were you.”
Katherine glanced to the right, making sure this time she didn’t move her neck. In the corner, sat Dax, still in his Tournament uniform. He didn’t lean forward, didn’t move at all, just let her notice him. The only proof he wasn’t a ghost was the way his boot tapped on the tile. 
“Dax.”
She tried to push herself up, and he was on his feet before she could get an inch off the pillow. He stopped at her bedside, careful not to touch her or the bed, hands tucked behind his back like he wasn’t sure what to do with them. Dax looked at her, and she saw why Atlas had always hated being compared to his brother. Atlas had the eyes of a man who read the backs of cereal boxes for pleasure. Dax had the eyes of someone who’d set the box on fire just to see something burn. 
“On a scale of one to ten,” he asked, “how bad is it if I tell you that you technically won?”
Her laugh came out as a wheeze. “Depends. Do I feel like I got hit by a transport?”
“You wish it were a transport,” he muttered. “Those usually leave your face intact.”
She hadn’t realized anything was wrong with her face until he said it. Now every nerve in her cheeks screamed.
“Don’t—mirror,” she said.
“Yeah, no,” Dax agreed. “They said that’d be… emotionally unwise.”
“You listened to a medic?” Her voice cracked mid-sentence. She swallowed and tried again. “You’re evolving.”
“I listened to five medics and one Sanctum rep who looked like she’d happily ice me to the wall if I breathed wrong,” Dax said. “Self-preservation is a skill.”
Katherine snorted, then winced. The monitor blipped high for a second. “How long?” she asked. “Since—”
“The fight?” His gaze flicked to the monitor, like he was checking if it could handle this conversation. “Six hours.”
Six.
That explained the buzzing lights. The way her body felt used up and hollowed out.
“The stands?” she asked.
He exhaled, slow.
“Cleared,” he said. “They evacuated everyone as soon as it… when it became obvious they couldn’t just… spin it as a regular match. They already had to scrap that shit Atlas and Tiran pulled.”
Katherine’s mouth felt dry again. “Couldn’t spin what, exactly?”
He gave her a look. The kind that said, You know, but fine, I’ll say it out loud for you.
“Oh, I don’t know. Maybe the Queen Piece from Old Prosperity manifesting a second element mid-fight that doesn’t belong to her,” Dax said. “You frying half the Board. The two of you nearly tearing each other apart on live broadcast. Take your pick.”
Her stomach lurched. For a second, she saw it from outside herself. Not as a girl on the ground gasping for air. As an image. A shot. A headline. Dualist on the Board, claws out, flames and frost in both hands.
“Dax,” she whispered.
He shook his head. “I know. I know. But listen,” he began, looking around the room. “We can speak freely here. After your little stunt, they’re too busy trying to fix this than monitor you. They even invoked emergency containment protocol. Called in the high Elite mages only. They shut down the feeds, locked the replay channels, and kept everyone in their seats.”
Her brow furrowed. “Kept—?”
“They didn’t let anyone leave until it was done,” he clarified. “Took them five hours.”
Katherine stared at him. “To do what?”
“Erase it,” he said simply. “Or… edit it. Whatever they call it. They scrubbed the stands. Everyone who was there will remember the Sixth Challenge. The casualties. The final bracket. But not that.”
He didn’t say what that was. He didn’t have to.
“So they just—” She swallowed. “Pulled it out of people’s heads.”
“They’re the Sanctum,” Dax said as if she should have already caught on. “This is kind of what they do. Official replay will show a brutal match. You holding your own. London Town breaking formation. Melena losing control of her element. You containing it. You going down. Medics call it. You win by Sanctum rule.”
She stared at the ceiling again.
“Sanctum rule,” she repeated.
“Last Piece standing,” Dax said. “Or closest enough that you can be stitched back together. Their language, not mine.”
“And Minho?” she asked. “Maxim?”
He shifted, jaw tight. “Never touched the Board. As soon as they realized what you and Melena were doing, they froze the bracket. No King match this year. They tagged the Sixth Challenge as decisive and ended it. My guess? They knew they wouldn’t be able to kill each other either. Can’t have that three times in a row. It’s bad for business. .”
Just like that.
Years of training. Months of blood and sweat and tears. Two provinces. Six Challenges. And then the Sanctum decided, mid-chaos, that they were done.
Her.
Melena.
It came down to them and then it didn’t, because some robed bastard in the back row decided to protect an Empire secret.
“Old Prosperity’s name is on the Conqueror roster,” Dax said. “So yeah. We won.”
The word felt wrong, almost like wearing a uniform that didn’t fit.
“Atlas,” she said. The name scraped its way up her throat. “He—”
“He’s alive,” Dax cut in, fast. Like he knew she needed that first. “In medical. Different wing. They weren’t going to put you two next to each other because apparently that would be ‘tempting fate.’”
Her eyes burned in a way that had nothing to do with lights.
“Extent?” she asked.
“Broken ribs. Concussion. Frostburn on his arms and shoulders,” Dax recited, like he’d memorized the chart. “Some internal bleeding but they stopped it. He’s… Katherine, he’s okay. It looks bad. But they said if he didn’t crash in the first hour, he wasn’t going to.”
She let out a breath she hadn’t realized she’d been holding since Tiran’s hand closed around Atlas’s throat.
“I should see him,” she murmured.
“You will,” Dax promised. “But not yet.”
She cut her eyes back to him. “Sanctum said no?”
“Sanctum said,” he mimicked, voice flattening, “that the Queen of Old Prosperity will be on her feet for the recognition ceremony. That she will stand for the closing rites. That she will accept the Empire’s gratitude like a good little Piece. Then, maybe, she can see her Bishop.”
She looked at her hands again. They were wrapped from palm to forearm in clean white bandages, no trace of blackened skin. No sign of the scorch that had crawled up her wrists. If she hadn’t felt the pain, she might have believed none of it happened.
“What about Melena?” she asked, before she could stop herself.
Dax hesitated. Just long enough.
“Alive,” he said finally. “In custody. Royal wing. Technically under medical observation, but there were two Blockers at her door when I passed.”
Of course, there were.
“Did they—” Katherine’s voice thinned. “Did they touch her head?”
“Sanctum?” Dax shook his head. “No. Not that I saw. The stands, yeah. The broadcast team, yeah. The handlers, probably. Anyone expendable enough, they did. But the Imperial Family? The Royal Family?” He huffed a humorless breath. “Pretty sure they want them to remember exactly what you are.”
The monitor’s beeping ticked faster again.
“I didn’t ask to be this,” she said, more to the ceiling than to him.
“I know,” Dax replied. “But you are, Little Flamer.”
Her muscles tried to flinch; the pain stopped them halfway.
“What happens now?” 
“We take our victory lap,” Dax said simply. “You put on something that doesn’t smell like burnt arena dust, they slap a crown on your head, and you stand on that stage while Sanctum tells the Empire how proud they are of you.”
“And then?”
“And then,” he said, “we find out how many of us they plan to keep.”
He didn’t say if he meant Old Prosperity, or Katherine, or both.
Silence stretched between them for a beat. Two. Three.
It would’ve been easy to stay there. To let the ache pin her down, to close her eyes and pretend the white ceiling was the gallery, that this was just another pre-fight medic check instead of whatever came after a fight like that.
Winning the Tournament had always been the finish line in her head. Survive the Board. Keep Old Prosperity intact. Don’t let them take Atlas. Don’t let them take Minho. Don’t let them take her.
No one told her what happened when you actually crossed it.
She shifted. The movement hurt, but she did it again anyway, nudging her heels into the mattress until she could use the bed’s controls to angle herself up. Her arms shook with the effort. Dax moved like he was going to stop her; she shot him a look and he thought better of it.
It felt like her skin was two sizes too small, like every breath stretched it farther. But she sat up.
“Tell me straight,” she said. “How bad do I look?”
He winced. “Bad-bad or… hero-bad?”
“Dax.”
He sighed. “You look like someone beat you with a flaming ice sculpture. But you’re still pretty. Just… in a terrifying way.”
“Good,” she said. Her lips cracked when she tried to smirk. “I’m glad there’s something of me left.”
Something like pride flickered across his face.
“That’s my queen,” he murmured.
It didn’t.
It hit something soft instead, something she’d been holding tight since the Board, and for a second the room went blurry in a way that had nothing to do with the lights.
Queen. 
Conqueror. 
Dualist.
Problem.
Katherine blinked hard. The ceiling refused to come back into focus and she realized too late that she’d already taken one too many shallow breaths in a row.
“Hey.” Dax’s voice softened. “Breathe with me, alright? In for four.”
She tried. It came out jagged.
“I’m fine,” she said, except it wasn’t steady and they both heard it.
“Sure,” he said easily. “You’re great. You just almost died on international broadcast and fought with an element that’s more my department than yours, but yes, you’re thriving.”
A tear slipped out before she could catch it.
Stupid.
She’d bled on the Board and kept her chin up. She’d watched Atlas go down, watched Tiran’s hand close around his throat, and she’d stood. But here, in a too-bright room with beeping that wouldn’t shut up and bandages that made her feel like she was wrapped in someone else’s skin, a single word from Dax cracked her open.
“What if it isn’t?” she whispered.
His brows pulled together. “Isn’t what?”
“Over.”
The ceiling blurred again. She kept staring at it anyway.
“What if this is just…” She searched for the word and came up empty. “The start. What if they come back in here and tell me it wasn’t enough? That I wasn’t enough. That I broke the rules and the Board and their nice little story and now I have to—”
Her voice snapped on the last word. The monitor betrayed her again, beeping faster.
“Katherine.” Dax moved closer, one hand braced on the rail, the other hovering like he wanted to touch her but wasn’t sure where it wouldn’t hurt. “You did everything. You kept Old Prosperity alive. You survived Melena Lockwood. You gave Sanctum a win they can parade for the next decade.”
“That’s not what I’m asking.” She squeezed her eyes shut. Tears spilled sideways into her hair.
There it was: the thing she hadn’t said on the Board, or in the viewing box, or even when Melena’s fire was eating the air out of her lungs. The part of her that was twenty and tired and so, so done fighting when all she wanted to do was run for her life. 
“If this is it,” she whispered, halfway wondering if Dax could even hear her, “if the Tournament is really over and they’re not going to throw us back on that Board… if I really did what they wanted…”
She swallowed. The next sentence felt heavier than any crown.
“Can you call my mother?”
Katherine had spent years trying to prove she didn’t need her.
That she wasn’t her. 
And right now? Michelle Nicholas was all she wanted. 
“If it’s not…” Her fingers curled in the sheet. “If this isn’t just them catching their breath before the next thing, I just— I need to see her. Once. Before I have to walk out there and pretend like any of this feels like victory.”
Dax didn’t answer at first.
Panic flared, stupid and instant. “If Sanctum said no—”
“They didn’t,” he cut in.
She opened her eyes.
He was looking at her like he could see straight through the bravado down to the part that had finally given out. The joking was gone now, stripped clean.
“I’ve been on comms since they pulled you off the Board,” he said quietly. “I called Atlas’s medic. I called Minho’s unit lead. And I called your mother.”
Something in her chest stuttered.
“You—”
“She was in the stands,” he went on. “They didn’t touch her head. Guess even Sanctum knows better than to try to scrub a Lieutenant with clearance that high. She saw everything.” His mouth tugged, not quite a smile, not quite not. “She’s been trying not to break down a door ever since they told her she had to wait until you were stable.”
Katherine’s hand flew to the rail, body screaming from the sudden movement. “Dax.”
He nodded toward the door.
“I told her I’d give you a minute first,” he said. “To wake up. To hear it from someone who wasn’t wearing a robe or a crown.” His eyes searched her face. “If you’re sure you want her in here, I’ll open the door and stand out of the way. If you’re not, I’ll send her to yell at a Sanctum rep for another ten minutes.”
A wet, broken sound slipped out of Katherine’s throat. Too close to a laugh and a sob at the same time.
“Winning the Tournament was supposed to be the hard part,” she said.
Dax’s mouth softened. “Yeah. Turns out, that was the tutorial level.”
“Let her in,” Katherine said.
Dax nodded once like she’d just given an order on the field. He squeezed the rail by her hand, a silent I’ve got you, then turned and crossed to the door.
The hallway noise swelled when it opened—voices, announcements, the hum of a hospital trying to pretend everything was normal. Dax stepped out of the way.
Michelle didn’t.
She filled the doorway like a storm front, shoulders squared in Old Prosperity uniform, hair twisted back in a knot that had clearly been redone three times in the last few hours. There were faint smudges under her eyes, the kind that only showed if you knew to look for them. Katherine always knew.
For a heartbeat, neither of them moved.
All the titles in the world—Lieutenant, Captain, Commander in Chief—fell away under the weight of that look.
Katherine’s throat closed as her chest finally gave away. 
“Mom.”
## Chapter 46: Melena
Melena came back to herself in pieces.
Heat first. A heavy, suffocating heat pressed over her skin like she was still on the Board, fire licking too close. Then cold, so sudden her teeth tried to chatter even though her jaw wouldn’t move. Her body couldn’t seem to pick one.
Then sound.
Not the roar of a crowd. No chanting, no stomping. Just machines. A steady beeping to her left. The quiet hiss of air somewhere above. Footsteps muffled by whatever they used for floors in this part of the arena.
Her eyes stayed closed.
Opening them meant deciding where she was. Who she was.
She wasn’t ready for that yet.
A voice floated in, too far away.
“Temp’s stabilizing,” someone said. “Mind-flare readings are still high, but she’s holding.”
Mind-flare.
She tried to move her fingers. Her toes. Nothing. Her hands felt distant, like they belonged to someone else. Her chest rose and fell on its own. The simple fact that she could feel it happening made her want to cry from relief and rage at the same time.
The Board came back in flashes.
Suppressor rods. The crack when they hit. Fire cut off in the middle of a scream. The moment she realized she couldn’t feel her element at all and the world went dark around the edges.
Katherine.
All that frost. All that fire. Hands black from emblems burning. Eyes she recognized a second too late.
Her heart rate jumped. The monitor told on her.
“She’s spiking again.”
“Get the sedative—”
No.
Melena clawed her way toward the surface of her own mind, fighting the drag of whatever they were about to push into her. She couldn’t move her hands, but she could drag her consciousness forward, teeth bared, refusing to drop.
Not yet.
If they knocked her out now, she’d see the Board again. She’d see Katherine standing up when she shouldn’t have been able to stand. She’d see—
The needle slid in anyway. She felt the sting in her arm, then the cold rush of it spreading.
Her body started to sink.
She tried to curse them, to tell them that she was not some unstable case for them to file and forget, that she was their Princess, their Queen on that Board, that she had not broken, she had not—
The world tilted.
The machines blurred into a low, distant hum.
Melena fell.
#
She was eight.
Her room was too warm. She’d kicked her blankets off hours ago. They lay in a heap on the floor, right under the line where the night wards began to glow against the wall. Elias sat on the edge of her bed, knees pulled to his chest. His hair stuck up at the back where he’d been yanking on it.
“Say it,” he whispered.
Melena blinked sleep out of her eyes. “Say what?”
He rocked a little like the motion might hold him together. “Her name. You said you’d remember if I forgot. That’s how this works.”
She knew this game. 
It wasn’t a game.
“Katherine Nicholas,” she said.
The wards flickered.
Elias’s shoulders dropped as he continued rocking himself. “Good,” he said. “Good. Okay.”
His eyes finally met Melena’s.
For a second, they weren’t Elias’s eyes.
They were Katherine’s. Frost and fire both reflecting back at her. Flames running under skin like bright veins. Wards sparking behind her like they were trying to hold her in and failing.
“Don’t forget her,” he told Melena. “You have to tell me her name so I don’t forget her.”
Melena opened her mouth to promise.
Snow slammed against the nursery windows.
No, not snow.
Ash.
The walls of the room turned black and white in a blink, squares stretching out into forever. The nursery floor flipped into the Board. Her bed became a sector. The night wards became suppressor rods hanging inches above her chest.
Katherine stood at the far edge, hands smoking.
Her wrists were black.
She lifted one palm and fire roared across the tiles, swallowing the discarded blankets, the cards Elias had left on the floor, the pieces of Melena’s childhood like they were kindling.
“Stop,” Melena tried to say, but it came out as nothing.
Her element didn’t move.
It wasn’t there.
The rods dropped.
She braced for impact.
.
When Melena woke again, the room was dark and someone was sitting in the chair by her bed.
At first, she thought it was a shadow. Then the shadow moved.
Melena’s fingers twitched against the blanket. “If you’re going to kill me,” she rasped, “you better get me on the first try.”
“I’m wounded.”
That voice did not belong in Remnia.
Her eyes snapped fully open.
Caelum sat slouched in the visitor’s chair like he’d grown there. Jacket rumpled, collar crooked, hair pulled back in a knot that would’ve made his Somnorian tailor weep. There was a faint bruise along his jaw and a line of exhaustion carved into the corners of his mouth.
“You’re not supposed to be in Remnia,” Melena said.
Caelum lifted one shoulder. “Good thing I’ve never been very good at doing what I’m supposed to.”
Her throat burned when she swallowed. “How did you even get past the border?”
“Very carefully,” he said. “And with the help of someone who still owes you three favors.”
Shadowmere. Maven. Of course.
Melena let her head sink back into the pillow. Her body felt wrong—heavy and hollow at the same time. Every breath dragged like it had to claw its way in.
“How bad is it?” she asked.
“For you?” He studied her for a long heartbeat. “Bad enough that half the palace thinks you almost broke.”
“Almost,” she murmured. “Didn’t stick.”
“That’s the only reason I’m sitting here instead of watching them wheel you into a ward with no doors,” Caelum said. “Your sister would’ve frozen the building before she let them take you, but that’s not the sort of family debate I want to witness up close.”
His attempt at lightness didn’t hide the tightness around his eyes.
“Let me guess,” Melena said. “Sanctum had rods ready.”
“They had rods in their hands,” Caelum corrected, voice going flat. “They were arguing over how much they could rip out before your ‘usefulness’ was compromised.”
He said the word like it tasted foul.
“And you?” she asked. “What did you do?”
“I told your sister,” he said. “She did the rest.”
Melena could picture it. Cordelia in a ward full of Sanctum robes, frost creeping up the walls while someone dropped a humming rod and pretended it was because their hand slipped.
“You’re not supposed to be here,” she said again, because it felt like the only solid fact in the room.
“Neither are you,” Caelum replied. “By all statistical models, you should be dead. Or mind-shattered. Or spending the rest of your life sedated in some very quiet, very expensive facility while people in robes argue about whether to pull the plug.”
“Comforting,” she muttered.
“Accurate,” he said.
She dragged her gaze away from him, up to the ceiling. White tiles. Tiny crack over the far corner that probably wasn’t a crack at all, just the sedative messing with her.
“What do they know?” she asked. “Out there.”
“Depends who you mean by ‘they,’” Caelum said.
“Start with the Empire.”
He folded his hands loosely in his lap. Even tired, he looked maddeningly in control.
“Officially?” he said. “The Sixth Challenge concluded with Old Prosperity conquering by Sanctum rule. The Queen of London Town lost control of her element under pressure. Intervention was required. Feeds cut for public safety. The Tournament continues.”
“That’s the story,” Melena said.
“That’s the story,” Caelum agreed.
She let that settle. Old Prosperity wins. Melena breaks. Sanctum saves the day.
“And Somnoria?” she asked. “What are they saying?”
Caelum’s mouth curved without humor. “We didn’t get the sanitized version you’re piping into your provinces. Half the foreign feeds cut a few seconds late. Long enough for every intelligence department with a recording crystal to see something they wish they hadn’t.”
Her stomach twisted. “So they saw her.”
“Yes,” he said. “Not clearly. Not enough for anyone to be certain. But enough for the right people to start asking very expensive questions.”
He paused, watching her face.
“And they know about Elias,” he added quietly.
Her chest squeezed. “They already knew about Elias.”
“They had theories,” Caelum said. “Rumors. Half-redacted files. A professor who showed up in Aetheria with the wrong kind of scars and the wrong kind of spellwork in his sleep. Now they have a name that keeps showing up in his episodes and a Tournament glitch to match it.”
Melena shut her eyes for a breath, then another.
“Your cousin,” she said. “Cassius.”
Caelum made a low sound that might have been a sigh. “He’s very good at his job. He doesn’t like loose ends. A Remnian prince tearing at the dream network qualifies as a loose end.”
“Elias is not the problem,” Melena said, voice rough. “I told you that in Shadowmere.”
“And I believed you,” Caelum answered. “Unfortunately, belief doesn’t mean much when half my Council wants to use him as proof that Remnia is finally unstable enough to push.”
Melena opened her eyes.
“So you came here,” she said slowly, “illegally. Through a closed border. To sit in my hospital room and tell me Somnoria wants to invade.”
“I came here,” Caelum said, “because you’re the only person who can give me enough truth to stop them from making the worst possible decision with incomplete information.”
Melena stared at him.
“In Shadowmere,” he went on, calm and steady, “I asked you for two things. Proof your brother wasn’t a spy. And intelligence on London Town’s Tournament preparations.”
“I remember,” she said. “I risked my grandmother’s entire security network to get you that.”
“And you did,” Caelum said. “You gave me more than enough to derail the first wave. Cassius lost his argument. Somnoria stayed on its side of the border. Elias was… contained.”
Contained. Not safe. Not alright. Just not dead in a Somnorian prison.
“Now?” Caelum continued. “Now I need the same thing on a different scale.”
“The same thing,” Melena repeated. “What, you want my battle notes?”
“I want your unedited memory,” he said simply. “Of the Board. Of her. Of exactly what happened when you reached for your element and it didn’t answer you.”
Heat shot through her—anger, not fire. Her element was still a hole in her chest.
“Sanctum has their version,” she said. “My grandmother has hers. Old Prosperity will write a heroic ballad of it by morning. Why do you think mine matters?”
“Because Sanctum is already scrubbing the stands,” Caelum said. “Because your grandmother has to think like an Empress. Because Old Prosperity wants a clean victory, not a complicated truth. And because the one person who walked out of that arena with all the pieces in her head is currently strapped to monitors while everyone else decides which story is safest to tell.”
He didn’t raise his voice. He didn’t have to.
“You are the only person,” Caelum said, “who can decide what version of this reaches Somnoria first. Your Empire will take months to argue itself into admitting what that girl is, if they ever do it at all. My country will not wait that long. They will fill the gaps with whatever suits them. If I don’t walk back with something bigger than a broken prince and some half-glitched footage, Elias becomes the easy answer again.”
Melena’s fingers curled in the sheet.
This was what he meant in the shrine, when he told her the Tournament would decide more than bragging rights. She’d heard him then. She hadn’t understood.
“You want a bigger monster,” she said.
“I want the right monster,” Caelum corrected. “The real one. So that when Somnoria looks at this, they see a problem too dangerous to poke at with a war—at least not yet—instead of a convenient excuse to burn your brother down and call it stabilization.”
Her first instinct was to say no.
Katherine had almost killed her. Had taken her fire and twisted it, had pushed her mind to the edge of a break it still hadn’t fully come back from. The girl’s existence made Melena’s skin crawl.
But hiding her didn’t make Remnia safer.
Hiding her made her a secret. And secrets were weapons. If Remnia didn’t control that weapon, someone else would.
Melena had spent years telling herself she was playing defense.
This didn’t feel like defense.
It felt like picking up a blade and choosing where to aim it.
“You think if Somnoria knows what she is,” Melena said slowly, “they’ll back off Elias.”
“I think,” Caelum said, “that if I can prove there is a dualist on your Board and that your Empire is already scrambling to cover it, my Council will be too busy arguing over what that means to push through an invasion plan based on a professor who cries about things that haven’t happened yet.”
He was right. 
She hated that he was right.
Her pulse thudded in her ears. She stared up at the ceiling for a long moment, watching the not-crack in the tile.
On the Board, power had come from fire.
Here, in this bed, power came from what she’d seen.
She thought of Elias, alone in Aetheria, brain fraying at the edges from too much dream magic. Thought of the way he’d whispered Katherine’s name long before the Tournament, clutching at a person no one remembered but him. 
“If I give you this,” Melena said, “I can’t take it back.”
“I know,” Caelum said.
“And you’ll use it,” she said. “However you need to. Not how I would. Not how my mother would. How Somnoria needs to.”
“Yes,” he said again.
“Good,” she said at last. “Then listen.”
She dragged the memory up, piece by jagged piece. She fixed her gaze on the ceiling as she spoke, because if she looked at him she might stop.
“I reached for my element,” Melena started. “ I hit her frost so hard I burned it out. I felt it go. One second, there was resistance; the next, it was just… gone. No cold. No pull. Nothing to push against. If she’d only had frost, that would’ve been it. She should’ve dropped. Instead, she stood up.”
Caelum didn’t interrupt. 
“That’s when I knew,” She finally turned her head, meeting his eyes. “The only reason Katherine Nicholas is alive right now is because when I burned her frost away, there was another element waiting underneath.”
His fingers closed briefly against his knee, the only sign he’d heard her.
“Descriptions,” he said. “Any prior indications from earlier Challenges, earlier years—”
“You’re acting like you haven’t already guessed,” she cut in. “You’re just waiting for someone more important than me to say it out loud. Katherine Nicholas,” Melena said, overpronouncing her words, “is a dualist.”
“You realize what you’ve done,” he said after a silence so long Melena was starting to forget she’d even spoken. 
“Yes, I’ve given you a monster big enough to keep my brother off your chopping block.”
“That’s one way to frame it,” Caelum replied.
“It’s the only way I have left,” she said. “You wanted leverage. There it is. Use it.”
“You may have bought him time,” he said. “Elias. Time, in my world, is more expensive than gold.”
“Then don’t waste it,” Melena replied.
“I don’t intend to.”
He stood, adjusting his jacket even though there was no sign that it had been messed up. His hand hovered for a second near the rail of her bed, like he was considering touching it, then thought better of it. Caelum turned toward the door and glanced back.
“You’re not the only one who chose a side tonight. Remember that.”
Then he stepped through the ward and was gone.
## Chapter 47: Katherine
The arena did not look like the place she almost died.
Someone had come through with magic and muscle and a mandate to erase, until the Board gleamed like it had never seen blood. The cracks in the tiles were gone. The scorch marks had been polished out. If she hadn’t been the one on her knees hours ago, Katherine might have believed nothing had happened here at all.
The emptiness made it worse.
Rows and rows of seats climbed into the shadows, every tier deserted. The sound that usually lived here—the roar, the chants, the ugly, electric hunger—was gone. Only the cameras moved, drifting over the Board on silent suspensor rigs, red recording lights blinking like eyes.
The access tunnel’s glass showed her reflection back: a young woman with deep brown skin still a shade too sallow from healing, tight coils smoothed and twisted up into an intricate crown of braids that made her scalp ache where the pins pulled. Someone had oiled her skin so the lights would catch the planes of her face just right, but the bruise at her temple still showed faintly under all their work. The formal Old Prosperity coat sat on her shoulders, this one the same blue of her mothers old En Guard uniform. Bandages hid her emblems under her sleeves, even though the skin beneath had already knitted.
“Old Prosperity,” the director’s voice boomed, echoing off stone and empty seats, “present your Conquerors.”
Someone at the far end of the arena tapped a control, and a low, prerecorded murmur of a crowd rolled in over the sound system, swelling up and fading back as if they were right back in the Sixth Challenge.
Katherine stepped out anyway.
The Board rose up before her, each square lit by wardlight. Just being on the edge of it made her skin prickle. Somewhere deep under the new stone, she could feel where it had remembered her.
Behind her, Old Prosperity fell into formation.
Minho took his place at her side, never once breaking step as she used his arm to help her walk. They’d pumped so much magic in her system, Katherine was sure she’d throw up later. The cameras slid sideways to catch their approach. The crowd track swelled—cheers, a long rising chant of Old Prosperity, Old Prosperity—all of it fake, all of it timed. She knew, because she’d sat through enough of these broadcasts in the gallery. She could practically hear the director counting beats in his head.
Four steps from the edge of the center square, Minho slowed, guiding her forward until she stood alone in the white-light center of the Board. The heat of the wards licked at her boots. Somewhere underneath, the stone remembered Melena’s fire, too.
“Hold there,” the director called. “Queens and Kings forward. Everyone else, half-step back.”
Minho moved up to stand level with her, his hand brushing hers for half a second. The contact grounded her more than the magic stabilizers ever had.
“You all right?” he murmured without moving his mouth.
“No,” she said from the corner of her lips. “But at least you’re here.”
He huffed a breath that might’ve been a laugh. “Indeed I am.”
“Quiet on the floor,” another voice snapped from the shadows. Wardlight dimmed, then flared, reshaping into the Imperial crest at the far end of the Board. A door hissed open.
The Empress stepped out first.
Aracelia’s presence always reminded Katherine of the weather. Today it was a cold front, dark green silk edged in frost-thread, crown heavy on tight black curls streaked with silver. Behind her came a cluster of aides and two Sanctum members in full ceremonial robes, faces hidden, hands folded in those deceptively gentle sleeves.
“Cameras one and three, on Her Imperial Majesty,” the director barked. “Four, stay on the Board. We’re live in five… four…”
The red lights on the rigs blinked in unison.
“…three, two—”
The crowd track surged. A triumphant fanfare blasted through the speakers.
“Citizens of the Remnian Empire,” the lead Sanctum voice intoned. “Bear witness.”
Katherine kept her chin up and her face neutral as the Empress crossed onto the Board. The wards shimmered around Aracelia, recognizing her and bending anyway. Must be nice, she thought, to have even magic know your name.
“Queen Katherine Medina Celeste Nicholas of Old Prosperity,” the Sanctum voice continued, “Conqueror of this year’s Chess Tournament. King Minho Augustine of Old Prosperity. Surviving Pieces of the Board.” A pause, perfectly timed. “The Empire honors your sacrifice.”
Aracelia stopped in front of Katherine. Up close, she looked exactly the same as she had the day Katherine stood in front of her at Delegation—impossibly composed, eyes like cut glass. If there was any flicker of nice under Empress, it didn’t show.
“Katherine,” Aracelia said, and for the cameras, her voice warm. “You have done what was asked of you.”
Katherine bowed her head. The movement made her vision tilt, but she forced her knees to lock. “By your command, Your Majesty.”
A page stepped forward with a cushion. The crown on it wasn’t the one from the Delegation Ceremony. This one was heavier; she could tell just by looking at it. Dark metal, inlaid with froststone and a single emberstone set dead center like a threat.
Of course.
The Empire never wasted a metaphor.
Aracelia lifted the crown. Katherine felt the weight of it hovering over her braids a heartbeat before it came down. The metal was cold, so cold it made her skin sting, but beneath it she felt the hum of emberstone like a pulse. 
Her pulse.
“Rise, Queen of Old Prosperity,” Aracelia said, for the cameras. “Rise, Conqueror of the Board.”
Katherine straightened. The crowd track roared on cue. One of the rigs swung in close enough that she could see her own face reflected in the lens—a dark-skinned girl in a borrowed coat, eyes ringed with bruised exhaustion and something harder underneath.
They were going to take that face and beam it into every province.
They were going to tell people this was what victory looked like.
Minho stepped up into frame beside her when the director barked his cue. His crown caught the light, simple silver over deep brown skin, shoulders filling out the formal jacket like he’d been born in it. To anyone watching, they would look like the portrait all those old propaganda murals had been waiting for: Old Prosperity’s Queen and King, whole and shining, backed by the Empire.
“Hold that,” someone called from the monitors. “Beautiful. Pieces, eyes front. Majesty, closing in three, two…”
Aracelia didn’t glance at the rigs. Her gaze fixed somewhere just beyond them, the way it did whenever she spoke to the Empire instead of the person in front of her.
“Remnia,” she said, voice wrapping cleanly around the word. “Tonight, the Board is cleared. The Tournament is concluded. Old Prosperity stands. London Town stands. Your Empire stands.”
The crowd track surged one last time, filling in the silence where real people should have been.
“Remember the Pieces who fell,” Aracelia went on. “Remember the King who held the line. Remember the Queen who rose from the fire.”
Heat prickled under Katherine’s bandages. They’d written a whole story in three sentences and none of it was true.
“aaaand… cut,” the director snapped. “Crowd track down. Kill cameras. We’re clear. Good work, everyone.”
The roar died mid-chant. The red lights on the rigs blinked out one by one. The fanfare cut, leaving only the low hum of the Board’s wards and the faint rustle of fabric as aides stepped forward to collect whatever needed collecting.
For the first time since she’d stepped onto the tile, the arena sounded like itself again: empty.
Katherine let out a breath she hadn’t realized she’d been holding. The crown sat too heavy on her braids, the metal biting into her scalp. Her body felt wrong in all its familiar places—knee, ribs, wrists, the thin buzzing ache behind her eyes where frost used to live.
“Your Majesty,” one of Aracelia’s aides whispered. “We can escort you back to—”
Aracelia nodded once then looked back to Katherine and Minho. 
“You will return to medical,” she said. “Once you’re all cleared, you will be transported back to Old Prosperity. My council will be in touch.” 
“Yes, Your Majesty,” Minho answered for both of them. Aracelia held her gaze for a moment longer—dark eyes, unreadable as ever—then turned and stepped off the Board. Her entourage closed in, swallowing her in a formation as they moved toward the tunnel.
Minho’s shoulder bumped hers. “Come on,” he said quietly. “We’re almost done.”
He turned to lead her off the Board.
The first cough sounded like nothing. Just a rough exhale, caught wrong in his throat after too-long ceremony posture. He pressed his fist briefly to his mouth and kept walking.
Katherine didn’t think anything of it until he stopped.
The second cough bent him.
“Minho?” 
He waved her off without looking at her, shoulders hitched. “I’m fine. Just—” Another cough cut him off. This one sounded damp.
A bad feeling slid down her spine. She reached for her fire out of instinct and hit the new, unfamiliar weight of it humming in her chest, still caged tight by the Board’s wards.
“Hey,” she said, moving in front of him. “Look at me.”
He did.
Up close, his olive skin looked a shade off—still warm, but with a grayness starting along his cheekbones she did not like. Sweat had sprung up along his hairline. His breathing came too fast, the crown on his head sitting just slightly crooked now.
“I’m just tired,” he said, trying for a smile that didn’t make it, “too much magic, not enough sleep. I’ll—”
He coughed again.
Something dark spattered the front of his glove and Minho stared at his own hand like it belonged to someone else. Slowly, he peeled his fingers back.
Blood.
It glistened on his glove, bright against dark fabric, streaked with something thinner and almost black where it caught the wardlight.
Katherine’s stomach lurched.
“That’s not—” she started.
Another cough ripped through him, harder than the last. He doubled over, one hand braced on his knee, the other clamped over his mouth as if he could force it to stop. When it passed, there were drops on the tile between his boots, small and obscene against the polished white.
“Medic!” Katherine’s voice cracked. She turned toward the tunnel. “We need—”
No one moved.
The techs at the far end were already busy breaking down equipment, talking in low voices, screens blinking through replay stills. Aracelia’s entourage had disappeared into the corridor. The two Sanctum figures still at the edge of the Board watched like someone had just changed the script and forgotten to tell them.
“Hey!” Katherine shouted, louder this time. “He needs a healer!”
Her voice bounced back at her, swallowed by stone.
Minho straightened by sheer force of habit, like he could pretend his lungs weren’t trying to tear themselves apart. He took one step toward the ramp. His knee buckled.
Katherine caught him.
For a second, she thought they would both go down. The weight of him slammed into her shoulder, driving another spike of pain up through her ribs. She dug her heels into the tile and held on. His breath rattled against her neck. Warmth soaked through the front of her coat where his mouth had slammed into her shoulder.
“Minho,” she whispered. “Stay with me. You hear me? Just breathe. We’ll get you to medical—”
He pulled back enough to cough again.
Blood splattered across her En Guard blue. 
The world narrowed to that color.
That deep crimson. 
“Katherine,” he said, and there was something wrong in his voice now, “I don’t… I think…”
He didn’t finish the sentence.
“Stop talking,” she said cutting him off, because the alternative was letting herself register the way his eyes were starting to unfocus. “You’re fine. This is just backlash. They pumped half of medical into us, remember? It’s—”
Her brain supplied another word she did not want.
Poison.
Her hands shook where they gripped his jacket. 
No. No. That wasn’t what this was. He was just reacting. He was—
“Please!” she snapped, turning her head without letting go of him. “Do something!”
One of the robed figures tilted their head, as if considering. “Post-combat complications are unfortunate,” they said. “But not unexpected. The strain on his system—”
“So help him!” Katherine’s voice broke. “You have healers, draughts, rods, I don’t care, just—”
“The Empress has already retired,” the other Sanctum said. “Emergency response teams have been reassigned. Medical is… occupied.”
Another cough wracked him. This time he didn’t cover it in time. A spray hit the tile, streaking red across the gleaming white square they’d posed them on for the closing shot. It started to drip into the seams. Katherine dropped with him as his legs went out, knees slamming hard enough into the tile that it stole her breath. She pulled him into her lap without thinking, one hand braced at the back of his head, the other pressed against his chest like she could hold everything in.
“Stay with me,” she whispered, the words barely making it past the tightness in her throat. “Come on, Min, don’t—”
His eyes found hers.
They were still Minho’s eyes. There was fear there, yes, but underneath it, the same stubborn calm that had let him walk onto this Board in the first place.
“Nicholas,” he said quietly. There was blood on his teeth. “Look at me.”
“I am looking at you,” she said. It came out harsher than she meant it to. “That’s the fucking problem.”
One corner of his mouth twitched. “You… won,” he said. “En Guard…transfers…to…you.”
His breath hitched again. Less a cough, more a stutter.
And she understood—finally, fully—that no one was coming. If she let herself, she could feel the cracks in him—the places the magic or the medicine or the blood was leaking out too fast for him to patch. 
Minho’s words slurred around the edges now. “Atlas…Sebastian… Zephyr… make sure they get home.” He coughed, winced, and tried to sit up. She caught his jaw, held his head steady, stared down at him with all the heat she had left.
“Shut up,” she said. “You’re not going anywhere. I’m not letting you.”
She pressed her palm to his chest, and the fire in her flickered. She didn’t know what she was trying to do. Maybe if she just burned hot enough, she could cauterize whatever was tearing him open from the inside. She tried to picture the diagrams from the medical manuals—arteries, veins, lungs, all those fragile tubes that could go wrong. The power in her hand wasn’t the thing it used to be. Her emblem under the bandage felt like it was about to split.
His hand found her wrist, fingers wrapping around it weakly. He squeezed once, like he could feel what she was trying to do and wanted her to stop before it cost her too.
“Don’t,” he breathed. “Not… here.”
“I can’t just sit here and watch you die,” she said.
His thumb brushed her skin, a small, grounding pressure. “You were never… good at… watching,” he murmured.
“Don’t you dare,” she whispered, tears welling in her eyes. “Don’t you dare leave me here alone.”
“Last move…” Minho managed, and his smile looked nothing like the ones he’d practiced in the mirror for the broadcasts. “Checkmate, right?”
She reached up and pulled the crown from her own head, the pins tearing free from her scalp. She set it down next to his. The weight of it left a cold band across her forehead, as if it had branded her.
Minho’s eyes rolled up, just for a second, and when they cleared he was looking through her, not at her. Blood bubbled at the corner of his mouth when he tried to say something.
“Minho,” she whispered, her voice hoarse and small. “Come on. Get up. You’re embarrassing me.”
He didn’t move.
“Minho,” she glanced down at him. “Minho!”
Minho’s chest stuttered, then jerked once, hard. His hand slipped from her wrist. She heard the sound he made and she tried to force her element into him anyway, fire and life and every scrap of herself that wasn’t already burned out. 
His body went still.
She waited for the next cough, the next flicker of Minho’s eyelids, the next smart-ass comment or shuddered breath, but nothing came. The wards hummed. The cameras, now offline, blinked their idiot red eyes at her and did not see. Katherine pressed her palm flat to his chest, counting down the seconds while she lied to herself. If she pressed hard enough, maybe the blood would stay inside. If she called loud enough, maybe the Empress would turn around. If she kept his name in her mouth, maybe he’d hear it and come back. 
Her hands shook. The fire in her flickered cold.
A faint crunch echoed from the tunnel. Steps on tile. Katherine whipped her head around, ready for a medic, a tech, anyone with enough authority to matter. Instead, the two Sanctum robes drifted forward, faces still shadowed. She glared at them over Minho’s body. The one on the left carried a black lacquered case. The other’s hands were empty, pale fingers barely visible inside their sleeves.
“Step away,” said the empty-handed one. “We will tend to the remains.”
“Consider this a lesson,” the taller one said quietly, as if they were offering her some kind of favor. “The Board isn’t a story about Queens alone. Every move has a Piece attached. Every Piece has a consequence.”
Their gaze slid to Minho’s body, then back to her.
“You spent the whole Tournament watching your opponent’s Queen. Next time, Corporal Nicholas, you should watch your King.”
 THE END!!!
